# English

## P1 What does hermeneutics study, and where does reception theory belong in it?

### From sacred texts to everything: special and universal hermeneutics

**Definition:** In its original sense, `hermeneutics` is the theory and methodology of interpretation and understanding: what meaning is, how one person understands another, how texts, utterances and works of art should be interpreted, and how understanding can bridge the distance between cultures and periods. As a theoretical concern it is as old as philosophy itself — Protagoras, Plato, Aristotle, the Stoics and the philological schools of Alexandria and Pergamon all discussed problems of interpretation. Modern hermeneutics, however, is to a large extent a German achievement.

The history of hermeneutics can be divided into three periods, each of which changed both *what* is interpreted and what interpretation *means*.

The first period took as its object sacred or near-sacred texts. If a text comes from a divine or quasi-divine source, ordinary human powers of understanding may systematically fail before it: a neighbour's reminder to tie up the ram requires no special art, an oracle does. Trained practices of interpretation therefore arose for reading a hidden sense out of the manifest one, and their results might circulate only among a few initiates. The Greeks developed allegorical interpretation to defend the theological and moral content of Homer against the charges of Plato and others, reading the stories of the gods as truths about nature or the soul; once Christianity admitted the pagan classics into education, it continued and developed allegory, and its interpretation of Scripture produced the doctrine of the `fourfold sense`: the literal sense teaches what happened, the allegorical what to believe, the moral what to do, the anagogical what to hope for. *Hermeneutics in this period was essentially a set of techniques for making particular texts yield an appropriate meaning that was roughly known in advance.*

The second period began in the eighteenth century and reached its height in the German-speaking world of the nineteenth. The **Protestant Reformation** shifted responsibility for interpreting the Bible from the Church to the individual, and this religious motive converged with the new historical and social sciences. Eighteenth-century Protestant theorists — **Johann August Ernesti**, **Johann Salomo Semler**, **Johann David Michaelis** — made a series of decisive advances: they recognized historical and cultural distance as an obstacle to interpretation; they demanded close attention to word usage in order to find a text's distinctive meanings; they required consideration of the wider social context; and they denied that the Bible could be interpreted by appeal to divine inspiration. **Johann Gottfried Herder** and **Friedrich Schleiermacher** went further, secularizing hermeneutics and extending it from religious texts to all texts, to oral discourse, and even to art. Hermeneutics thus moved from a `special hermeneutics` serving particular kinds of text to a `universal hermeneutics` whose object is linguistic understanding as such.

**Distinction:** Schleiermacher believed he had accomplished the turn from special to universal hermeneutics, and **Hans-Georg Gadamer** accepted this claim. Yet the idea of a universal hermeneutics predates Schleiermacher: Dannhauer had coined the modern term "hermeneutics" in the seventeenth century, and eighteenth-century German rationalists (Meier's *Attempt at a General Art of Interpretation*, for example) had already conceived a general theory of interpretation. What was distinctive in Schleiermacher was not that he first proposed universality but that he built universal hermeneutics into a systematically structured discipline (see P3).

The hermeneutics of the second period acquired enormous influence in the nineteenth century. It underpinned the flourishing of classics, law, history, biblical studies, the history of philosophy and, by the century's end, cultural anthropology; it stood at the centre of the debate, after the rise of the natural sciences, about the nature of history and the human sciences (with Droysen and Dilthey as its leading figures); it served as the methodological instrument of the `historicism` that swept through the human sciences; and it was extended by three radical theories — Marx's critique of ideology, Nietzsche's genealogy and Freud's psychoanalysis — which Ricoeur would later call the "hermeneutics of suspicion" (see P5).

The third period was opened in the twentieth century by **Martin Heidegger** and Gadamer. Understanding was no longer merely an art or a method but was defined as the human mode of being: all experience is already meaningful, and understanding is the condition under which this meaning unfolds (see P6, P7). Sartre, Ricoeur, Derrida, Barthes, Hirsch, Davidson, Skinner and others have since developed the field in various directions; analytic philosophy came relatively late to take the hermeneutic tradition seriously.

**Conclusion:** The changes across the three periods can be summarized as an expansion of the object (sacred texts → all meaningful expression → experience itself) and an elevation of status (art → method → condition of existence). *Each expansion brought a new problem: the more universal the object, the harder it becomes to fix standards of validity for interpretation. This is the difficulty to which the later parts keep returning.*

### Methodological versus philosophical hermeneutics: a map of the disputes

The most influential twentieth-century self-understanding of hermeneutics derives from two distinctions drawn by Gadamer in *Truth and Method* (1960).

The first is between `methodological hermeneutics` and `philosophical hermeneutics`. Methodological hermeneutics is understood as a critical-epistemological tool for the human sciences: it asks how best to make sense of human symbolic expressions within a particular field of meaning, communication and practice. Philosophical hermeneutics asks how such sense-making is possible in the first place. It describes not the procedures of a given discipline but the enabling conditions of human understanding and, ultimately, of experience as such. Gadamer states that he is concerned with something that methodological dispute serves only to conceal — something that does not so much limit modern science as precede it and make it possible.

The second distinction is between `special hermeneutics` and `universal hermeneutics`. Gadamer credits Schleiermacher with launching universal hermeneutics, but holds that Schleiermacher also set it on the wrong track: in moving from special to universal hermeneutics he remained caught in a methodological outlook inherited from Kant, tracing the conditions of understanding back to the interpreter, and failed to see that textual interpretation is sustained by a deeper hermeneutic responsiveness that precedes the very division between interpreter and text — namely `tradition`. On Gadamer's account, Schleiermacher imported into a field defined by participation in contexts of meaning the ideals of the natural sciences — value neutrality, disinterestedness, independence from context, a universal method as the condition of success — and thereby brought to an end the humanism that treated tradition as a source of truth and self-formation.

**Controversy:** After *Truth and Method* this narrative was long treated as the authoritative history of hermeneutics, but it has been challenged on several fronts. **Kristin Gjesdal** points out that Gadamer's project was first conceived in the neo-Kantian milieu of Marburg and that his retrospective history of hermeneutics was essentially polemical: to justify the ontological alternative, rivals had to be cleared from the field. In her view, Gadamer conflates *any* thought about methodology with *Kantian-Romantic* methodology and so fails to see other, non-Kantian ways of thinking about method. On **Wilhelm Dilthey**'s own genealogy, hermeneutics is to be traced not to Kant alone but to Herder's response to the formalism of rationalist school philosophy — a bottom-up, empirically oriented movement demanding individualizing, context-sensitive and sympathetic interpretation of historical and artistic material. **Michael N. Forster**, **Frederick C. Beiser** and **Fred Rush** support this revision from their different angles: the methodological tradition was neither naive objectivism nor mystical empathy.

The result is a map more complex than Gadamer's dichotomy. The real fault line may lie not only between "method and truth" but also between ontological hermeneutics and an empirically informed hermeneutics. Gjesdal even argues that Gadamer is committed to a softened, historicized transcendental claim: philosophical hermeneutics still takes as its task the search for a priori conditions of experience, merely relocating them from the transcendental subject to tradition. Gadamer himself later conceded that his accounts of science and of philosophical Romanticism had been too polemical.

*The map can be oriented by four axes of tension that run through the whole of this book: method ↔ truth; trust in tradition ↔ suspicion of tradition; the particular (the diversity of languages and cultures) ↔ the universal (universal grammar, universal pragmatics); determinacy ↔ indeterminacy of meaning. Almost every theorist occupies some position on each axis; what is peculiar to reception theory is that it occupies both ends of each axis at once, and is therefore riddled with internal tensions (see P10, P11).*

### Hermeneutics and poetics: asking about meaning versus asking about its conditions

In literary studies the distinction between hermeneutics and `poetics` is, in principle, quite simple. Given a text, hermeneutics asks what it means — its original meaning, or the meaning it has for readers in present circumstances, or some combination of the two: what the text is "really about". Poetics asks what rules and conventions enable the text to have the meanings and effects it does for those who participate in the relevant tradition. Poetics starts from texts and their significance and asks about their conditions of possibility or intelligibility rather than about what the text really means.

**Example:** Linguistics provides an exact analogy. Linguistics does not undertake to discover the true, deeper meaning of English sentences; it asks what rules and conventions of the language allow these sequences of sounds or letters to function as meaningful utterances. **Roland Barthes** distinguishes in the same way between criticism and a "science of literature": the former works out the meaning of a text, placing it in a context and applying a particular language to it; the latter neither attributes nor discovers meanings but describes the logic by which readers, following literary conventions, generate acceptable meanings — just as linguistics describes the sentences accepted by speakers' intuitions.

The two move in opposite directions: hermeneutics from text to meaning, poetics back from effects or meanings to their conditions. The distinction is clear in theory but often muddied in practice. Interpreters who want to propose a meaning often need to describe the conventions that make it possible, while those who claim to be doing poetics are frequently tempted to interpret along the way and to conclude that what the text is "really about" is literature itself. Barthes, in *S/Z*, declared that he would only describe the codes that make Balzac's novella intelligible, yet ended by offering an interpretation of it. Frye's *Anatomy of Criticism* aimed at a comprehensive theoretical framework for the possible forms of literature, but was absorbed as a method of interpretation, "myth criticism".

**Background:** In the Western tradition both poetics and hermeneutics originate in ancient Greece. Aristotle's *Poetics*, centred on mimesis and genre, supplied norms for judging drama that lasted for centuries; hermeneutics grew out of the allegorical defence of Homer. Medieval commentary provided rhetorical and structural information and stressed the pedagogical and moral uses of texts. From the Renaissance through the eighteenth century, the dominant mode of criticism was evaluative: Dryden defined criticism as "a standard of judging well"; Pope's *Essay on Criticism* discusses disputes over evaluation without ever mentioning the problem of what a poem means; Johnson scrutinized the pauses in Milton's blank verse, yet his writing too lacks a hermeneutic dimension.

**Turning point:** At the end of the eighteenth century the concept of literature shifted from "imitation of action" to "expression of an author". This undermined evaluative criticism based on generic norms and was closely bound up with the birth of modern hermeneutics: it was within this new conception of literature that Herder and Schleiermacher proposed a universal hermeneutics. What a work "expresses", however — the author's thought, the spirit of the age, a historical conjuncture, psychic conflict, or the working of language itself — admits many answers, and these later became the competing critical "schools". Only when Anglo-American `New Criticism` established the presupposition that the goal of literary study is to produce new and better interpretations of individual works did interpretation truly become the central activity of literary studies. Before that, students translated, imitated and memorized works and learned about authors and literary history, but were seldom asked to produce interpretations.

**Limitation:** The hermeneutic tradition itself offered little help to this "age of interpretation". **Peter Szondi** observed that hermeneutics was once exclusively a system of rules and is now exclusively a theory of understanding; twentieth-century philosophical hermeneutics concerns itself with the structure of understanding, not with deciding which interpretations are valid. **Jonathan Culler** accordingly argues that the aim of literary studies should be to understand how literature works rather than to produce new interpretations of individual works, and that it needs a dialectic of poetics and hermeneutics, or a special literary hermeneutics, rather than a general philosophy of understanding.

*The distinction offers a useful way of locating reception theory: **Wolfgang Iser** asks under what conditions a text has meaning for a reader, a question close to poetics in form; **Hans Robert Jauss** asks how works have been understood in history and seeks to rewrite literary history, a question close to hermeneutics. The two representatives of reception theory stand on opposite sides of this line (see P10, P11, P16).*

### "Reception" and "effect": the Constance School and the boundaries of reader-response criticism

"Reception theory" sounds rather odd in English; Jauss once joked that to foreign ears the problem of "reception" seems better suited to hotel management than to literature. But in the Federal Republic of Germany from the late 1960s to the early 1980s, "reception" and its many compounds were the key to theoretical concern. Hardly a literary field remained untouched, and the influence extended to sociology and art history.

**Distinction:** The most persistent terminological difficulty lies in the distinction between `reception` (*Rezeption*) and `effect` (*Wirkung*). Both concern the impact of a work on someone, and they may not be fully separable. The commonest suggestion is that reception relates to the reader and effect to the text — but this arrangement is in no way satisfactory. The ease of German compounding multiplies the problem. "History of effects" (*Wirkungsgeschichte*) has a long scholarly tradition in Germany, where it traditionally meant the study of an author's influence on later generations, especially on later writers; how it differs from "history of reception" (*Rezeptionsgeschichte*), and how an `aesthetics of effect` (*Wirkungsästhetik*) differs from an `aesthetics of reception` (*Rezeptionsästhetik*), were questions raised again and again over two decades.

To avoid confusion, this study edition adopts the following convention: `reception theory` is used as an umbrella term for the general shift of concern from author and work to text and reader, covering the projects of Jauss and Iser, empirical research and traditional studies of influence; `aesthetics of reception` is reserved for Jauss's early theory. Other compounds are to be understood from context.

Reception theory must also be distinguished from American `reader-response criticism`. The latter is likewise an umbrella term, embracing such widely divergent systems as Holland's "transactive criticism", Culler's structuralist poetics and Fish's affective stylistics, and it too designates a general shift towards the text–reader pole; Iser is usually included. But there are three important differences.

First, "reader-response criticism" is not a banner raised by any of its critics but a label attached after the fact to a group of writers who had little contact with, and little influence on, one another. They were scattered across the world, responded to very different predecessors and situations, did not meet regularly, and did not publish in the same journals; the two representative anthologies themselves testify to the divergence of positions. If it became a critical force, that was owing to the cleverness of the label rather than to a common effort.

Second, reception theory was a more cohesive, more self-conscious and more collective enterprise. In the broadest sense it was a response to social, intellectual and literary developments in West Germany in the late 1960s (see P8); it was a group undertaking at both the institutional and the critical level, with fruitful intellectual exchange among its proponents. Many of its supporters were associated with the **University of Constance** — as professors, as graduates, or as participants in the university's biennial colloquia. The proceedings of these colloquia, published in the series *Poetik und Hermeneutik* ("Poetics and Hermeneutics"), document the development and cohesion of the enterprise.

Third, apart from Iser, there has been almost no mutual influence between the two groups; if footnotes are any indication of influence, the exchange has been close to zero. The similarity of their general critical perspective is therefore too superficial and abstract to justify treating them together.

**Influence:** Within reception theory, Jauss and Iser formed a kind of division of labour. Jauss started from problems of literary history, was influenced chiefly by Gadamer's hermeneutics, and concerned himself with `reception` on a macro-level, at the scale of social history; Iser started from New Criticism and narrative theory, was influenced chiefly by phenomenology, especially Ingarden, and concerned himself with the micro-level `effect` between an individual text and its reader. *If Jauss deals with the macrocosm of reception, Iser deals with the microcosm of effect — a division that maps neatly onto the unstable terminological distinction just discussed.*

**Conclusion:** Reception theory stands at the point where the third period of hermeneutics "descends" into literary studies: it inherits the philosophical thesis that understanding is conditioned by historical situation, but tries to turn it into an operational theory of literary history and of reading. To understand both its strength and its difficulties, one must first understand the whole hermeneutic tradition on which it depends — the task of P2 through P7.

## P2 Why is understanding others difficult? Distance, language, and the metacritique of reason

### How was the Bible to be read "in a human way"?

**Background:** The Bible is a collection of writings in many genres, composed over almost ten centuries of antiquity, reflecting a wide range of religious cultures and scribal traditions in Israel and in the Jewish and Christian diaspora. At the same time it is a `canon`: an authoritative book through which a number of religious communities define their doctrine and practice. Its original languages are Hebrew (with a little Aramaic) and Greek; yet Western Christianity long used a Latin version, which thanks to Jerome's work had become largely standardized by about 400 CE and was declared the one "authentic" text by the Council of Trent in 1546 — ironically at the very moment when humanist scholars were returning to the Greek and Hebrew originals and reformers were producing vernacular translations in large numbers.

**Example:** Jeremiah 7:16–24 shows the many dimensions of the Bible as an object of interpretation. The passage takes the form of God addressing the prophet directly: do not pray for this people, do not intercede for them, for I will not hear you; see how the children gather wood, the fathers kindle fire and the women knead dough to make cakes for the "queen of heaven", and pour out drink offerings to other gods to provoke me; therefore my anger and wrath shall be poured out on this place and shall burn and not be quenched. It continues: when I brought your ancestors out of Egypt I did not command them concerning burnt offerings and sacrifices, but only this — "Obey my voice … and walk only in the way that I command you."

A devout reader will first be troubled by the idea of divine "anger and wrath": how can one relate to a God so characterized? What is the cause of this anger, and how might punishment be escaped? The text itself offers religious ideas about apostasy and prophetic intercession; but a reader may also take it simply as an episode from an ancient religious culture, as the opinion of some scribe, or even, paradoxically, as an invitation to sympathize with those who confidently worshipped their "queen of heaven" within a polytheistic framework. The second passage raises the relation between religion and ethics: the two parallel expressions "obey my voice" and "walk in the way that I command" deserve more attention than the historicizing rhetoric about the exodus and the covenant, and can be compared with the exhortations to justice, solidarity and the protection of life in the immediate context. A Christian reader will also set it beside the opening of Paul's Letter to the Romans, where the wrath of God is revealed from heaven, and beside his admonition to be transformed by the renewing of the mind so as to discern the will of God. *The example shows that the same passage can be read historically, theologically and ethically, and that there is no ready consensus on the criteria by which plausible interpretations are to be distinguished from implausible ones.*

**Mechanism:** The early modern period already had well-developed standards of interpretation. The Lutheran scholar **Salomon Glassius**, in his *Philologia Sacra* (1623–1636), required critical attention to the language and rhetoric of the biblical texts, comparison with related texts in near and wider contexts, and study of a text's historical origin — seven "circumstances": who is speaking, where, when, on what occasion, in what style, what view the text advocates (*scopus*), and whether it can serve as the basis of a formal doctrine (*sedes doctrinae*). The first five are driven by historical interest and anticipate later `historical criticism`; the last two require philosophical and theological judgement: determining the *scopus* calls for sustained reflection, and judging a text's doctrinal significance calls for weighing alternative possibilities.

The Enlightenment transformed the perspective on the Bible in four ways: (1) the biblical narrative of history "from the founding of the world" gave way to a "natural history" of humankind and of religion, within which the biblical tradition had to be placed; (2) the reading of Israel's history as prophetically and typologically pointing to Christ was no longer accepted, and the meaning of texts was confined to their original setting in the religious culture of the First and Second Temple periods; (3) miracles once used to "demonstrate" the truth of the Bible were ascribed to mythological imagination; (4) the religious significance of biblical history and revelation as a whole was called into question, and the emphasis shifted to reason and "natural religion". **Gotthold Ephraim Lessing** stated the epistemological problem most sharply: if no historical truth can be demonstrated, then nothing can be demonstrated by means of historical truths; contingent truths of history can never become the proof of necessary truths of reason. **Immanuel Kant** distinguished essential "religious faith" from "historical belief", which can be useful to religious faith as its sensible vehicle for certain people and eras but is no essential part of it. Earlier still, **Hugo Grotius** had distinguished "prophetic" books written under immediate divine inspiration from "historical" books written on a pious impulse, thereby opening a deep tension between the religious and scribal culture of ancient Israel and the doctrine of biblical inspiration.

The beginnings of `historical criticism` rest on a sharp distinction between what a given word can and cannot mean. The classical scholar Ernesti, in his *Instructions for an Interpreter of the New Testament* (1761), transferred the methods of classical philology to New Testament exegesis: since everything explained applies equally to divine and human books, the meaning of words in the sacred books can be sought, as far as human effort goes, in no other way than in human books, and fanatical enthusiasts who despise learning and refer everything to the power of the Holy Spirit must never be obeyed — although he granted that pious readers are aided by the Spirit on points of faith and morals. Ernesti acknowledged that linguistic usage and the character of discourse depend on time, social structure, political constitution, religion and school tradition, but he remained constrained by an oversacralization of the text and by the need to explain away differences and tensions within and between the books. Semler, by critically examining the formation of the canon, shed light on the historical conditions of the reception and production of texts in early Christianity; he did not abandon the idea of the Bible as the "Word of God" but sought to identify, within the diversity of the Bible, the religious ideas that sustain faith. Herder summed up these developments in an appeal to read the Bible "in a human way": it is a book written by human beings for human beings; its language is human; it was written and preserved by human means; and the mind by which it is understood, every interpretive tool that elucidates it, and all the ends and uses to which it is put are human.

**Mechanism:** The Enlightenment view of the Bible also had a philosophical foundation. Human reason was understood to aim at universal truths, so that whatever is transmitted only by contingent historical tradition cannot by itself satisfy the standard of rationality; religion could be acknowledged by philosophy only as universal, "natural" religion, directly connected with a universalist ethics. Kant defined religion as the sum of all our duties regarded as divine commands: in content religion does not differ from morality; it differs only in that reason uses the idea of God, derived from morality itself, to give morality influence on the will. Hence there is only one religion, though there are various kinds of belief in revelation and its statutory doctrines. This position continued the tradition of `natural law` — Grotius had famously said that the law of nature is so unalterable that God himself cannot change it.

The view implies an acceptance of the plurality of churches and doctrines: with regard to scriptural teachings that can be known only by revelation, faith is not in itself meritorious, and lack of it, even doubt, involves no guilt. From this Kant drew a hermeneutical rule: if certain texts seem to place faith in revealed doctrine above morally good works, they must be interpreted as referring to moral faith. Since the only thing that matters in religion is *deeds*, this final aim and a meaning appropriate to it must be attributed to every biblical dogma. Kant went further, holding that only a moral interpretation is "authentic", that is, given by the God within us; for we cannot understand anyone unless he speaks to us through our own understanding and reason, and so we can recognize the divinity of a teaching only through the concepts of our reason, insofar as they are pure moral concepts. The Bible, as the "organon" of religion, may contain much else, but the essential part of religion — what Kant calls its "canon" — can be found in it too. Grotius had already pointed to the Ten Commandments to show that natural law and the corresponding religion were anchored in Israel's sacred texts.

**Genealogy:** Kant here reasserted the position of **Baruch Spinoza** in the *Theological-Political Treatise* (1670). Spinoza's concern was the divine law as eternal truth, resting on natural law and the "natural light": the divine law is universal, deduced from universal human nature, and requires belief in no historical narrative, since love of God arises from knowledge of God, and knowledge of God must be drawn from universal notions certain in themselves and well known. Citing Jeremiah 9 and 22, Exodus 34 and 1 John 4, he argued that the only knowledge of himself God requires of men through the prophets is knowledge of his justice and love — attributes that people can emulate through a sound way of life. The Bible thus fully endorses the natural light of reason and the natural divine law.

**Controversy:** Spinoza and Kant are easily accused of reducing religion to morality and so failing to do justice to the Bible as a historical and religious text. Lessing, in his *Axiomata*, offered a more nuanced position. He insisted first that the Bible plainly contains more than religion: interpretation cannot begin from the unity of the text, only from the question of religion. Borrowing commercial language, he distinguished the Bible's "gross weight" from its "net weight" and asked: what if the gross weight includes wholly unnecessary "packaging" — material that illuminates or confirms not even the least religious principle? The crucial question then becomes how to identify the parts that convey religion, and whether revealed truths bear some "internal mark" distinguishing them from human additions. Lessing's answer was that the Bible can prove some facts, but not all Christian doctrines rest on facts; the rest rest on their `inner truth` — and how can the inner truth of a proposition depend on the authority of the book in which it is stated? He rejected the model in which the reader possesses the inner truth before ever encountering the Bible: whoever truly *explains* (rather than merely tries to explain) the inner truth of a revealed proposition has thereby proved that he has a correct idea of it. An honest believer holds these doctrines not because they are in the Bible but because he recognizes that they are worthier of God and more beneficial to humanity than any other religious teachings, and because he feels the consolation they bring.

Of these three criteria, the first two relate to natural religion but leave room for ideas that go beyond it without contradicting it; the third admits "feeling" into the hermeneutic process, allowing the individual a kind of immediacy in the encounter with religious ideas and the ability to communicate this experience to others. Schleiermacher continued this line in his hermeneutics lectures of the 1810s: one knows that the sacred books are sacred only after one has understood them.

**Conclusion:** Enlightenment biblical hermeneutics left a fourfold legacy: philological rigour (the same rules for divine and human books); historical criticism (texts belong to the religious and scribal culture of their time); a rational standard measured by universal morality; and a space reserved for the individual to "discover", in the encounter with Scripture, what goes beyond natural religion. *The tension among these four — historical distance, universal standards of reason, and individual experience — is exactly the problem that universal hermeneutics would later attempt to handle as a whole.*

### Differences of mind: French anti-universalism and the historicity of genre

**Background:** German hermeneutics rests on two revolutions against common Enlightenment assumptions. The first was a revolution in the philosophy of language: the Enlightenment usually assumed a sharp dualism between thought or concepts and language, holding that thought is in principle possible without language and that language is merely an aid to memory and a means of communication; the new philosophy of language held instead that thought is essentially dependent on and bounded by language and that meaning is word usage (see the next section). The second was the rejection of `universalism`: the Enlightenment tended to assume that beliefs, concepts, values and feelings are much the same in all times and places, whereas `anti-universalism` holds that they differ profoundly between historical periods, between cultures and even between individuals. The second revolution showed that the task of interpretation is far harder than Enlightenment thinkers had usually supposed, and so drove German theorists to seek solutions — which they found mainly in the first.

The first revolution was mainly a German achievement; the second was mainly French, exported from France to Germany. In *This Too a Philosophy of History* (1774) — the earliest, most radical and most famous statement of anti-universalism in the German tradition — Herder accused Montesquieu, Voltaire and Hume of holding or tending towards a false universalism, and thereby obscured his debt to the French. The true founders of anti-universalism were **Michel de Montaigne**'s *Essays* (1580) and the sceptical writings of his relative La Mothe Le Vayer (1646). The young **Montesquieu**'s *Persian Letters* (1721) emphasized profound moral differences between cultures, the young **Voltaire**'s *Essay on Epic Poetry* (1728) emphasized differences between literary genres, and **Étienne Bonnot de Condillac**, following Locke, carried the programme to the level of words and concepts in his *Essay on the Origin of Human Knowledge* (1746): complex concepts differ markedly between peoples and even between individuals. Universalist tendencies appeared only in the later works of Montesquieu and Voltaire — *The Spirit of the Laws* (1748) allows only three or four possible forms of government and moral outlook, and the *Essay on Manners* (1756) and *Philosophy of History* (1765) imply universalism in morality and other domains — and it was chiefly these later works to which Herder was responding; Hume joined this later universalist programme in his *Enquiry concerning the Principles of Morals* (1751) and "Of the Standard of Taste" (1757).

The French tradition also developed two refinements early on: first, the idea, already in Montaigne and exploited by Montesquieu in the *Persian Letters*, that studying others — especially how they see us — lets us understand ourselves more clearly; second, the idea embodied in Madame de Staël on Germany and Tocqueville on America, that such study should not be confined to "primitive" peoples but should extend to "civilized" nations.

**Genealogy:** The dependence was not only general but took specific forms. The first concerns `the two sources of originality`. In his *Instructions for an Interpreter of the New Testament*, Ernesti distinguished two kinds of deep variation in words and concepts: one arising from differences of culture or period, the other from exceptional individuals. Under his influence this distinction became foundational for the whole German hermeneutic tradition, appearing in Herder, Schlegel, Schleiermacher and Böckh. But the first to argue that different peoples develop different forms of language and complex ideas, and that different individuals do too, was Condillac. In the chapter on "the genius of languages" in his *Essay*, he argued that one can learn a person's language by studying him for a while — "his", because each person has his own language; that the character of a people shows itself more clearly than that of an individual; that the genius of a language is first formed by the genius of a people but develops only through great writers; and that a people has eminent talents only after its language has made considerable progress, while great talents in turn drive the language forward. Ernesti almost certainly took over this thesis from Locke and Condillac: he cites Locke explicitly when discussing national originality, and writes of "the genius of each author" in an entirely Condillacian manner when discussing individual originality.

The second concerns `genre`. In the *Critical Forests* (1769) and "Shakespeare" (1773), Herder recognized that understanding a work always requires correctly identifying its genre, that is, the set of purposes and rules that constitute it; and that this task is usually far harder than people assume, because genres bearing the same name ("epic", "tragedy") share only a few superficial features, while their constitutive purposes and rules differ markedly between periods, cultures, authors and even works by the same author. The interpreter therefore often has to (1) learn a genre not yet familiar to him and (2) resist the strong temptation to assimilate it falsely to a genre he knows better; this can be done only by rigorous empirical study — mainly of the works themselves, but also of their social context and the history of the genre behind them. The Schlegel brothers and Böckh adopted this view of genre and made it central to their hermeneutics. But the view was first developed in Voltaire's *Essay on Epic Poetry*, with Homer, Trissino, Camões, Tasso, Ercilla, Milton and his own *Henriade* as examples of epic, and Sophocles, Corneille, Racine, Shakespeare and Addison as examples of tragedy. Herder used epic and tragedy as his examples too, essentially taking over Voltaire's method.

**Distinction:** At first glance eighteenth-century France seems to have contributed little to hermeneutics: the ambitious systematic hermeneutics of Germany is absent there, and this might be explained by German Protestantism versus French Catholicism, German anti-dualism versus French dualism, German anti-universalism versus French universalism. *That impression holds only for "systematic hermeneutics"; as regards the problems on which hermeneutics was founded, the roots of German hermeneutics are to a large extent French.*

**Conclusion:** Anti-universalism created the difficulty of interpretation: if the beliefs, values and concepts of others differ profoundly from ours, understanding cannot be achieved by simply assuming a common human nature. The historicity of genre makes this difficulty concrete at every step of literary understanding. The next question is: what can overcome it?

### Why does thought depend on language? Meaning as word usage

**Definition:** The new philosophy of language on which German hermeneutics relied comprised two theses. (1) Thought is essentially dependent on and bounded by language — some German thinkers even held the two to be identical. (2) Concepts or meanings are not the kinds of items, in principle independent of language, with which the philosophical tradition had usually identified them — not the things referred to (Augustine), not Platonic forms, not the subjective "ideas" of Port-Royal, Locke, Hume and Condillac — but `word usage`.

**Genealogy:** Thesis (1) was put forward by **Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz**, accepted by Wolff and his school, and then absorbed and radicalized by Herder, Hamann, Schleiermacher and others; Condillac's version too was clearly borrowed from Wolff. Thesis (2) was first proposed by Spinoza, accepted by the German theorists of biblical interpretation Wettstein and Ernesti, and then borrowed and radicalized by Herder and Hamann.

**Mechanism:** Together the two theses offered a way out of the difficulty created by anti-universalism. If thought is bounded by language, differences of thought between periods and cultures show up as differences of language; if meaning is usage, a distant author's concepts need not be grasped through some mysterious access to his mind but can be reconstructed by examining how he uses words. *Usage is publicly observable — this is precisely the hermeneutic force of "meaning as usage": it turns the understanding of another mind into the study of evidence.*

In his prize essay *Treatise on the Origin of Language* (1772), Herder argued that language is of human rather than divine origin; since language shapes thought, each language and each era has its own spirit, which should not be judged by the standards of another. Grammatical or philological hermeneutics must therefore be supplemented by an effort to feel one's way into the author's thought. *This "feeling one's way in" is easily misread as mystical empathy; in the actual usage of Herder and Schleiermacher it is a conjectural activity that rests on evidence while going beyond it (see P3).*

**Influence:** **Friedrich Schlegel** developed this line into a principle of historical linguistics: thought depends on and is bounded by language, language is a historical entity, and therefore thought is historically conditioned. He did not believe that a universal grammar underlies all languages, and he held that more "indeterminate" modes of language such as poetry are more primordial and in a sense "better". The interpreter must therefore reconstruct a text's meaning within the social and historical context of its language and determine how the text exploits, in its own distinctive way, the possibilities that the structure of that language provides (see P3).

### The metacritique of Hamann and Herder: can reason be separated from language?

**Background:** Kant's *Critique of Pure Reason* (1781) was itself a critique of the rationalist "pure reason" that sought supersensible knowledge. Only three years later, **Johann Georg Hamann**, in a short text circulated among friends, *Metacritique of the Purism of Reason* (1784), argued that Kant's critique was by its own standards not critical enough and itself stood in need of a higher-order critique. Hamann's image has long been shaped by **Isaiah Berlin**, who portrayed him as the reactionary instigator of the "Counter-Enlightenment"; others have portrayed him as a "radical enlightener" opposing the illiberal aspects of the image of reason championed by other Enlightenment thinkers. Most agree that Hamann's challenge to the Enlightenment — his criticism of abstract reason lording it over tradition, and of philosophy over religion and literature — was closely bound up with his convictions about the relation of thought and language. Hamann's originality on this point has also been questioned.

**Mechanism:** Hamann identified three dimensions of Kant's "purism", three routes by which reason is "purified" into the empty abstractions of metaphysics: first, making reason independent of all inheritance, tradition and belief in them; second, making it independent of experience and its everyday induction; third, making it independent of language — which is the only, first and last organon and criterion of reason, with no credentials but tradition and usage. Kant, in the *Prolegomena*, had compared the architectonic of reason to the grammar of a language; Hamann took the analogy seriously. He went on to advance a thesis plainly parallel to Kant's "transcendental illusion": language is not only the foundation of reason but also `the centre of reason's misunderstanding of itself`. Empty metaphysics misuses the verbal signs and figures of empirical knowledge, treating them as pure hieroglyphs and types of ideal relations, and so turns the honesty of language into some indeterminate X, a rushing of wind, a magical shadow play.

**Distinction:** Hamann was a linguistic `particularist`: the dependence of reasoning on language meant that an individual's reasoning depends on his capacity to speak a particular language. He therefore opposed the idea of a "universal language" popular in the seventeenth century and later embraced by some of Kant's followers.

Hamann died in 1788 without developing this sketch. When the manuscript was published in 1800, it appeared shortly after his long-time friend and protégé Herder had published *Understanding and Experience: A Metacritique of the Critique of Pure Reason* (1799). Herder had been Kant's student in the 1760s, bore the imprint of his teacher's "pre-critical" thought, and had no sympathy for the turn that produced the critical philosophy; he had already, in the *Treatise on the Origin of Language*, treated language as the forerunner of thought in a manner broadly similar to Hamann's.

Herder inherited the core of Hamann's critique of Kant's transcendentalism: the human mind "thinks with words", thinking is merely "inner speech", and language is the criterion of reason and of all genuine science and understanding. The central part of the book tries to rewrite Kant's Transcendental Aesthetic and Transcendental Logic on a linguistic basis, so that the architectonic of thought is thoroughly historicized. The empty a priori systems of traditional metaphysics arose from inventing signs cut off from the empirical sources of their meaning; instead of attempting a "critique of pure reason", reason should turn to its natural origins and ask: how did you come to yourself, to your concepts? How do you express, use, connect and unify them? By what right do you give them universal and necessary certainty? Metaphysics thus becomes a philosophy of human language.

**Background:** Herder's *Metacritique* came at the end of a series of increasingly bitter exchanges with Kant, which began with Kant's negative review of the first part of Herder's *Ideas for a Philosophy of the History of Mankind* (1784). Kant saw Herder's historical inquiries as an exercise in the dogmatic metaphysics that the *Critique of Pure Reason* was supposed to have ended; he objected in particular to Herder's explanation of the development of human reason through its interaction with the natural forces that shape the continuum of living things, and to his treatment of humanity's progress from an animal-like state to reason and freedom as the result of factors external to reason. When the *Metacritique* appeared, Kant's followers — especially the idealists and Romantics who, under the influence of Reinhold and Fichte, saw themselves as heirs to the "spirit" rather than the "letter" of the critical philosophy — counter-attacked fiercely. It was in the course of this counter-attack that a Kantian published Hamann's manuscript, intending to accuse Herder of plagiarism; but the battle also created tensions within Jena Romanticism, many of whose members had been sympathetic to Herder's ideas.

**Influence:** The reaction revealed that by the late 1790s the relation between thought and language had become a central problem within the idealist camp itself. Its background was the collapse of the first phase of post-Kantian thought: Reinhold's attempt to give Kant's philosophy a psychological foundation in a theory of "representing consciousness" was attacked by Schulze, a Humean sceptic writing under the name "Aenesidemus"; Fichte's long response to this attack opened the next phase, centred on his *Wissenschaftslehre* (1794–1795). For Fichte and his followers, Schulze had exposed insoluble problems in the idea of "representing consciousness", and the Jena Romantics accordingly turned naturally to the necessity of language for thought.

**Conclusion:** The structure of metacritique is to apply critique to critique itself and to discover that the condition of reason is historical language. *This step set the basic direction of all later hermeneutics: if reason cannot be separated from a particular language, understanding others means understanding reason within another linguistic world, not measuring others by a universal reason.*

### The idealist response to language: Fichte, Bernhardi and Hegel

**Mechanism:** Among the idealists, the first to address the relation of thought and language directly was **Johann Gottlieb Fichte**, in his essay "On the Linguistic Capacity and the Origin of Language" (1795). Fichte defined language as the expression of our thoughts by means of arbitrary signs. At first sight this resembles the traditional view of Locke or even Aristotle: the representational capacity of language depends one-sidedly on that of thought. But Fichte did not treat language as an arbitrary labelling of the mind's "natural signs". He criticized Locke's treatment of images as natural representations that work by resemblance: a picture of a fish is as arbitrary as the word "fish", and whether there is natural resemblance between sign and signified is entirely beside the point. This seems to lead to thoroughgoing conventionalism and hence to accepting the determining role of "tradition" — but that would fall foul of Kant's critique of an origin of language external to reason. So Fichte proceeded, methodologically, as Kant had in responding to Herder: one must not resort to hypotheses or arbitrarily list the particular circumstances under which language "might" have arisen, but must deduce from the nature of human reason the `necessity` of inventing language.

**Example:** Fichte's deduction is in fact an origin story. It starts from natural signs in the sense of causal connection: a lion's roar is taken as a sign of the lion's presence. But what interests Fichte is not the roar that comes from the lion; it is the roar imitated by a human being to warn others that a lion is present. Such imitations can be combined: an imitated "roar" followed by an imitated "snore" signals that the lion is asleep. Combinations of this kind produce strings of sound with a certain syntactic differentiation, above all the distinction between subject and predicate; the degree of grammatical mediation is proportional to the degree to which individual sounds have been detached from their original particular referents. The "roar–snore" combination already constitutes a kind of "hieroglyphic" proto-language; what must then be explained is how a teleological self-elevation leads from this proto-language to a fully conventional "purely auditory language". Fichte held that conventional sign relations imply their maintenance by a collective act of will; the hieroglyphic, resemblance-based elements of the proto-language are progressively overcome, and language becomes the medium of the free expression of human thought, with everything involuntary in the choice of signs eliminated.

The core of this teleology is the central idea of the *Wissenschaftslehre*: the human being is essentially the `self-positing` "I = I", ideally free of external determination, seeking to subdue the forces of nature and to liberate itself from the determinations of the "not-I" given in experience. The approach is rationalist: one gains mastery over the external world by integrating one's judgements about it into a logical whole centred on the self-identical "I = I".

**Limitation:** From a hermeneutic standpoint, Fichte's origin story begs the question. A rational language requires not only linguistic counterparts of ordinary empirical concepts but also genuinely "universal" supersensible concepts — such as the substrate that persists beneath sensible change, or the "I" that applies to the representer itself. Here Fichte simply falls back on the traditional thesis that language is inessential to thought: the concept of something supersensible must exist before a sign is sought for it. He thus has difficulty accommodating Hamann's and Herder's critique of Kant.

Fichte also gave language a communicative motive: someone who recognizes the purposiveness of his own actions in others' responses to them will try to communicate his intentions to others, in order to prevent and correct their misunderstanding of a meaning expressed only in his actions. People easily misunderstand others' well-meant actions and repay them with ingratitude; the better one understands another's intentions, the more one wishes to correct the error, and so wishes to know his thoughts better in future. Hence arises the task of inventing fixed signs for communicating thoughts.

**Genealogy:** After the appearance of Herder's *Metacritique*, the official response from the idealist camp came from **August Ferdinand Bernhardi** — a Fichtean who tried to combine Fichte's thoughts on language with the core of the *Wissenschaftslehre* more successfully than Fichte himself, and who has been called the last and perhaps best representative of Kant's "critical" philosophy in the linguistics of the late Berlin Enlightenment. In a sharply worded review in the Schlegel brothers' *Athenaeum*, he attacked Herder on the ground of language itself, charging him with lacking a scientific understanding of language and with ignorance of the development of critical philosophy after Kant. In his *Grammar*, published the following year, he drew on an insight of Fichte's: in the *Wissenschaftslehre* Fichte had described the "circle" in which every finite understanding is enclosed — the representing capacity exists for and through the representing capacity — and in the *Foundations of Natural Right* (1796) he had discussed one subject's "recognition" of another's intentions in the other's actions. Bernhardi explicitly interpreted this circle as a `circle of communication`, in which the active speaker, the passive interlocutor and the state of affairs spoken about each receive a distinctive form of representation. *This conception already comes close to the later "hermeneutic circle" of Ast and Schleiermacher.*

Bernhardi added the idea of a `second language`. Herder had pointed out that the objects language first presents are sensible natural objects, and that the range of individual signs is entirely determined by their agreement with sensible impressions; but such a restricted "semantics" falls far short of carrying Kant's architectonic of reason. Bernhardi therefore proposed that out of a "first language" there develops a "second language" whose domain is produced and extended by the mind's own self-activity; the former is "bound presentation", the latter "free presentation". This was a version of Herder's own account of how the creation of new languages had led to the empty vocabulary of rationalist metaphysics, but Bernhardi saw no reason to draw only negative conclusions from it.

**Influence:** In 1807, after Napoleon's armies had occupied the German states, Fichte returned to the theme of language in his public *Addresses to the German Nation*, now asserting the primacy of language more clearly: it is not so much that language is formed by human beings as that human beings are formed by language. He asked under what conditions a language that originally spoke only of sensible things develops the resources to speak of supersensible, "spiritual" things. What distinguished the Germans from other European peoples, he held, was that they had continuously spoken their original language and cultivated it in speaking it. In this cultivation, words originally used for sensible objects were extended by metaphor to speak of the supersensible — as the Greeks conceived "ideas" through the metaphor of seeing — and the association between sensible and supersensible meanings helped people use abstract vocabulary. In contrast to this natural growth stood the case of a conquered people forced to use the conqueror's language: foreign words for spiritual things lack organic connection with the community's everyday life and become empty, dead abstractions. Here Fichte adopted Hamann's and Herder's linguistic critique of "empty" metaphysics, a critique that would reappear in mid-twentieth-century "ordinary language philosophy". The *Addresses* are often seen as a turn to linguistic nationalism, but on closer reading they also contain cosmopolitan elements.

**Mechanism:** **Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel**'s social thought is known for the concept of `objective spirit`: the way individuals think depends on the historically and culturally specific forms of life to which they belong. This idea goes back to Herder and later became central to Dilthey's distinction between the hermeneutic "human sciences" and the "natural sciences". Dilthey drew on the early Hegel, trying to separate the "good" Jena Hegel from the "bad" system-building metaphysician of the Berlin period; Habermas extracted near-hermeneutic ideas from Hegel's intersubjectivity mediated by "recognition"; and Gadamer also drew on Hegel within his Heideggerian philosophical hermeneutics.

Chapter Four of the *Phenomenology of Spirit* (1807) can be read as Hegel's critical appropriation of Fichte's theory of recognition rather than as a theory of recognition of his own. Hegel begins with the question of the "object" that satisfies a (self-conscious) desire: it seems that only another self-consciousness can serve as an adequate vehicle in which one recognizes oneself as self-consciousness. Fichte had imagined that a subject driven by the urge to subdue nature could recognize this rational urge in the actions of another subject, and the latter likewise in the former — each recognizing the other as an instance of the ideal "I = I" resisting external natural determination. Hegel exposes the contradiction in this scenario: each subject can recognize this attitude only in the other's empirically observable behaviour, but in Kant's transcendental idealism there is an unbridgeable gap between the transcendental subject and the empirical content given to it, so that for Fichte's finite subject any other person must necessarily be presented empirically as a piece of nature to be subdued.

**Example:** The master–slave scenario shows that the slave, who accepts the status of "non-human" natural objectivity relative to the master's subjectivity, unwittingly sets out on the road to true freedom that cannot be reached from the master's starting point. *This is less an anticipation of Marx's proletariat than a refutation of Fichte's conception of the subject.* The scenario also shows the necessity of language: for the institution of mastery and slavery to function, the master must at least communicate the content of his will to the slave. From the communicative point of view, the two roles differ in the speech acts available to each — only the master can issue commands, that is, perform a speech act whose result is that the other acts as the sentence prescribes. But to understand and carry out the master's will, the slave must show that he potentially possesses the master's intentional capacities, so the slave's "objectivity" is merely apparent. Indeed, to be a slave is itself a contradiction: the slave has chosen to trade freedom for escape from the immediate threat of death, so that to be a slave is to "will", continuously, a state of "will-lessness". From the hermeneutic point of view, the institution of slavery is self-contradictory and will be replaced in history by a more symmetrical one.

In the "Spirit" chapter of the *Phenomenology*, Hegel explicitly calls language `the existence of spirit` (*Dasein des Geistes*): language is self-consciousness existing for others, immediately present as "this" self-consciousness and at the same time universal; it is the self that separates itself from itself, becomes its own object as pure "I = I", remains "this" self in this objectivity, and at the same time merges immediately with other selves and becomes their self-consciousness. The formulation is aimed squarely at Fichte's "I = I": for Hegel, an intention of thought becomes a determinate thought only by being articulated in language; it cannot exist before language and be expressed afterwards.

**Controversy:** Hegel's philosophy after Jena is his "system" — logic, philosophy of nature and philosophy of spirit — and the latter two are held to presuppose the results of the *Science of Logic* (1812–1816). On the traditional "metaphysical" reading, this work, which claims to trace the self-unfolding of pure "determinations of thought", is from a hermeneutic standpoint precisely the "magical shadow play" Hamann spoke of, and the post-Jena Hegel seems to leave no room for a hermeneutic dimension. Since the mid-twentieth century this reading has been challenged: Findlay saw parallels between Hegel and the later Wittgenstein, calling Hegel's system one of the most anti-metaphysical and least unworldly of systems; Pippin stressed the "critical" dimension of Hegel's critique of Kant's residual metaphysics; Redding stressed its hermeneutic dimension.

**Evidence:** Two late texts support the latter readings. First, the preface Hegel wrote for the second edition of the *Science of Logic* in the year of his death (1831) states that the "received material", the known forms of thought, must be regarded as an extremely important template, even a necessary condition — apparently meaning the texts handed down by the logical tradition. Immediately afterwards he writes his most explicit statement on language and thought: the forms of thought are first set out and stored in human language, which permeates everything a person has internalized and made into a representation; although logic is human nature, languages differ in the degree to which they provide logical expressions for the determinations of thought — here he opposes a Humboldtian linguistic universalism and stresses the merits of German. Second, in 1828 Hegel wrote a long review of Hamann's *Writings*, summarizing the "concrete principle" of the "strange" and "witty" *Metacritique* thus: words, as visible and audible, belong to sensibility and intuition, and in the spirit of their use and meaning belong to understanding and concept. He likened Hamann's principle of language to Kant's transcendental idealism and, borrowing a simile going back to the Stoics, said that Hamann had made only a "fist", leaving to the reader the work of opening it into a "palm" — of revealing the real conceptual connections — indirectly suggesting that this was what his own *Logic* did. *The controversy must be left open: whether there is a "hermeneutic" late Hegel depends on how the *Logic* is placed, and both readings have textual support.*

**Conclusion:** Idealism offered three attitudes to language. The early Fichte treated language as an arbitrary labelling of pre-existing thought, with supersensible concepts prior to language; Bernhardi and the later Fichte acknowledged language's role in shaping thought, distinguishing bound from free language and living from transplanted language; Hegel treated language as the existence of spirit, so that recognition and self-consciousness themselves are mediated by language. *Together with the metacritique of Hamann and Herder, these three attitudes form the basic options for all later hermeneutic debate: is language the instrument of understanding, its shaper, or its very medium?*

## P3 How did understanding become a rule-governed art?

### Explaining the one through the other: Herder's contextualizing method

**Problem:** If minds differ profoundly between periods, peoples and individuals (P2), how can the interpreter avoid imposing his own standards on the object without drowning in countless particulars? Herder's answer constitutes the prototype of all later methodological hermeneutics.

**Principle:** Herder's hermeneutics rejects a priori theory and proceeds "bottom up", seeking to grasp the particularity of a culture and to understand it from within. In *This Too a Philosophy of History* he writes that each nation has its centre of happiness within itself, as every sphere has its centre of gravity; the interpreter's task is to grasp that centre. Two errors must be avoided at once: an a priori generalization that proceeds from the universal to the particular and so denies the particular as particular; and an immersion in particulars that can find no centre in them, no meaningful and coherent unity. *The two errors look opposite but share one feature: neither can mediate between universal and particular.*

**Example:** In his essay on Thomas Abbt (1768), Herder sets out to write the biography of a contemporary author. What is most needed, he thinks, is to distinguish in the author what belongs to his time or to the world before him from what he leaves to posterity. An author bears the chains of his age; he stands in his century as a tree stands in the soil in which it is rooted and from which it draws nourishment; whoever would strip the author of the "birthmarks of his time" strips him at the same time of his individuality. The point of the image is that the author, like the tree, does not come into being in isolation but becomes what he is in relation to a larger context; the context is neither artificially imposed nor an obstacle to understanding but the condition for recognizing the author's distinctive contribution. The interpreter must therefore "explain the one through the other": see how the context is reflected in the individual, and how the individual adds to or challenges the context. *The "unity" here is not a homogeneous whole but an internally differentiated whole constituted by the individual contributions of its members, which in turn becomes their condition — whole and part depend on each other.*

In a letter written for Gatterer's *Allgemeine historische Bibliothek* but never sent (1767/1768), Herder states the `perspectivity` of understanding still more explicitly: every human eye has its own perspective, and each projects the objects before it in its own way; two observers, other things being equal, may give roughly the same "description", but their "judgements" will differ widely according to their state of mind and reflective inclinations. Yet he also urges going beyond this fixity: freeing oneself from the idiosyncrasies one is born with and raised in, keeping a distance from an overly peculiar situation, and finally being able to savour, without the mediation of nation, period and personal taste, the beauty present in all ages, all peoples and all arts.

**Evidence:** "Shakespeare" (1773) is a landmark of historical hermeneutics. Against the French classicists who judged Shakespeare by Aristotle's theory of tragedy, Herder points out that the world in which Greek drama arose was fundamentally different from Shakespeare's: in Greece drama developed in a way impossible in the North, and in the North it is not and cannot be what it was in Greece. The differences are threefold. First, world-view: the Greeks had a strong sense of the unity of time and place, their peoples and polities were relatively simple, and they lacked modern historical consciousness and awareness of cultural difference; Shakespeare's world was made up of manifold estates, ways of life, beliefs, peoples and dialects, and to long for the old simplicity is futile. Hence Shakespeare's plays move from place to place and involve characters of every origin, and the "plot" is no longer a single action in the Greek sense but an "event" or "great happening". Second, purpose: Greek drama was a public institution and a religious event, whereas Shakespeare's drama has no religious motive — which is why his tragedies can contain comedy and need not observe the distinction between tragedy and comedy that goes back to Aristotle. Third, sources: Greek tragedy drew on the dithyramb, mimetic dance and the chorus, whereas Shakespeare drew on history, and his plays are themselves presentations of history — *Othello*, for instance, is the living history of the genesis, development, eruption and tragic end of a noble, unfortunate man's passion.

Herder therefore holds that genius too must be measured by its historical sources: Shakespeare's genius differs from that of the Greek tragedians; it resembles the genius of a historian — embracing a hundred scenes, arranging them at a glance, and breathing into them a soul that pervades and animates the whole. His contemporaries may have been stirred by the same emerging historical consciousness and the same increasingly differentiated world, but only Shakespeare could present this polyphonic world on stage *coherently*.

**Mechanism:** This demand for both the many and the one *implies the structure of the hermeneutic circle*. The interpreter reads the parts of a work one by one, that is, linearly; but the work extends in many directions — each part converses not only with the parts before and after it but with the beginning and the end, and with characters in other scenes. Only on reaching the end does the interpreter recognize what unifies the parts (it is not the sequence itself), and must then go back and see each scene, act and line in the light of that unity. The movement back and forth never comes to an end: as understanding of the connections among the parts deepens, the interpretation of the whole is continually revised.

**Influence:** Herder helped establish in German such terms as *Volksgeist* ("spirit of a people") and *Zeitgeist* ("spirit of the age") for the distinctive "living" spirit of a people or period; this inner spirit pervades all its products, from folk customs to polities and from musical forms to commercial contracts, and its highest and most revealing form is literature, especially poetry. The most crucial idea behind his hermeneutics is `organicism`: purposive human works have a structure resembling that of living organisms, whose parts are intelligible only in the whole; and by combining organicism with the idea of development ("epigenesis"), Herder could discern a cumulative ascent in history. From Dilthey onward, theorists of history have therefore regarded Herder as a founder; in the classic accounts of Meinecke and Stadelmann he is even called the "father of historicism" for his emphasis on individuality, development and "historical sense".

### Does nature need interpretation too? Classification, analogy and the concept of a "world"

**Problem:** Recent history of science has made two points: earlier histories projected the late-nineteenth-century division between natural and human sciences back onto earlier centuries and so overlooked their mutual influence; and it was chiefly the methods of the humanities — grammatical analysis of texts, collation, comparison, commentary, indexing, and above all detecting and correcting inconsistencies of form and content — that underlay many scientific practices in medicine and natural history. But did the influence run both ways? Did hermeneutics also influence the study of nature, bringing new insights and discoveries?

**Background:** In 1735 **Carl Linnaeus** classed human beings among the "quadrupeds" and created the order *Anthropomorpha*, which included monkeys, lizards and sloths, on the ground that they share the same arrangement of teeth. **Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon**, in his *Natural History* (from 1749), found such classification both arbitrary and narrow. Its fundamental error, he argued, was a "metaphysical error" — wanting to judge the whole by a single part: almost all classifiers use one part (teeth, claws, leaves or flowers) rather than all parts, and do not look for the similarities and differences between whole individuals. Such a way of knowing, he declared, is not science but a convention, an arbitrary language.

Buffon accordingly distinguished "physical truths" from "abstract truths": mathematical truths are inventions of the human mind and thus abstract, whereas physical truths really exist in nature and are the true object of human inquiry. His emphasis on real relations led to a historicized view of species and of nature: natural history must, after description, be organized around the relations between natural things and between them and us; the history of an animal should be the history not of an individual but of the whole species — its reproduction, the number of its young, parental care, habitat, food, and the services it can render us. Species thus ceased to be static, eternal entities and became the continuation of a group of individuals through reproduction in time. At a stroke Buffon gave a new definition of species, historicized nature, and redefined natural history.

**Problem:** Buffon also pointed out the difficulty of his own method: our mind proceeds linearly, one step at a time in one direction, whereas nature moves in all directions at once. Without an a priori theoretical foundation, how can unity be grasped in the infinite variety of nature? **Denis Diderot** raised the same problem in his *Thoughts on the Interpretation of Nature* (1753/1754): the mathematician's domain is a purely intellectual world, and what is taken as absolute truth there ceases to be so once applied to the world we live in; yet without a mathematical or a priori foundation, natural history seems incapable of unity — even if experimental science works century after century, the accumulated material will become too great to fit into any system, and the inventory will still be far from complete.

**Mechanism:** Buffon's and Diderot's solution was `analogy` and comparative analysis. Only by comparing different structures can similarities and differences between species be recognized; Buffon observed that anatomy gained real knowledge only after the human body began to be compared with animal bodies, since all science rests on comparing similar and dissimilar objects and we can judge only by the relations of things. Analogy offered a way of grasping the continuity of nature based neither on a single structural feature nor on a priori classification. But such an account differs from mathematical construction or axiomatic demonstration: it is necessarily revisable, analogical reasoning can err, and it cannot establish certainty, only probability. In Buffon's famous phrase, the frequent repetition and uninterrupted succession of similar facts constitutes the essence of physical truth; what we call physical truth is only a probability, but one so great that it amounts to certainty.

The way to an "interpretation of nature" was thus opened: natural history cannot produce certain and eternal knowledge, but neither is it a mere heap of isolated facts; it is observation guided by a literary instrument — analogy — to identify similarities and differences. The naturalist was no longer merely an "observer" of nature but its "interpreter". Yet neither Buffon nor Diderot provided a complete methodology for the "interpretation of nature".

**Distinction:** Herder knew and sympathized with many of Buffon's views — the critique of apriorism and mathematical universalism, the critique of abstraction in science, the view that human history must be seen as part of natural history — but his attitude to Buffon was complex. He feared that Buffon, despite intending a holistic account of the natural world, remained bound by the prejudices of his age, inclined to analysis and division rather than synthesis and unity. In the essay on Abbt he called Buffon the "unsystematic" mind needed to counter Linnaean system-building; but in the 1772 prize essay he criticized Buffon together with Condillac and Bonnet: all their dissection of the senses is abstraction; the philosopher, in following one thread of sensation, must neglect another, but in nature these threads are a single web. Buffon explained differences within a species (such as differences in human skin colour) by the two categories of "climate" and "nourishment"; Herder held that what is connected with us is a far larger reservoir of forces.

**Mechanism:** Against Buffon's "climate", Herder developed the concept of a "sphere" or "world" (*Kreis*). It seeks to encompass the many essential aspects of a natural (and cultural) environment, the relations among them, how they are reflected in and by individuals and species, and above all how they form an integrated unity. A sphere is not merely an external cause affecting a species' development but an inhabited world, to be understood in relation to its inhabitants and they in relation to it — just as an author's world not only affects the author but is affected by him.

**Example:** Herder first introduced the concept in the *Treatise on the Origin of Language*, in response to the Berlin Academy's prize question. He wanted to give a naturalistic account of the origin of language while solving the difficulty facing the naturalistic positions of Condillac and Rousseau — Rousseau, in the *Discourse on Inequality*, had pointed out that human language is artificial and involves the arbitrary and conventional, whereas animal language does not; if human language arose from natural language, this "leap" must be explained. Rather than speculating, as Rousseau did, about an imagined past, Herder began by observing and describing what was before him, asking: what is it like to be human, and what might it be like to be an animal?

He first noted that human beings are far inferior to animals in the strength and certainty of instinct, lacking many of the innate artistic capacities and drives that animals possess. Animals are born with specific powers and capacities that mirror their natural environment: every animal has its sphere, into which it is born, in which it remains all its life, and in which it dies. The sharper an animal's senses and the more marvellous its artistic products, the smaller its sphere and the more limited the range of its products. The bee's sphere is the hive, within which its drives and arts are precise and efficient; once outside the hive, these specialized capacities actually endanger it. The spider's "world" is its web; its capacities are perfectly attuned to that world but can hardly reach beyond it. Conversely, roaming animals have larger spheres and less focused capacities, and the power and efficiency of their senses relative to their environment generally decline: the more functions and tasks, the more attention is dispersed over many objects, the less constant the way of life, and the more sensibility is dispersed and weakened. This is clearest in human beings: they have no particular sphere and can inhabit all kinds of environment; around them is a world of occupations and destinies; their senses are not sharpened for one thing — they have senses for everything and therefore weaker, duller senses for each particular thing. The human being has no artistic drive, no artistic capacity and no animal language. Animal language is an innate instinct; human language must be acquired, or rather "invented".

**Conclusion:** By attending to context and seeing animals and humans in relation to it, Herder could sustain a naturalistic account of the origin of language without having to explain a "leap" from natural to artificial language.
