Introducing the concept of a sphere or world, and trying to understand the individual animal in its relation to its environment, is precisely "explaining the one through the other": seeing how the context is reflected in the individual and how the individual helps constitute the context. *This method relies neither on a priori explanation nor on the accumulation of material; it finds meaning in and through what is seen — an indivisible unity and reciprocity.*

In the *Ideas for a Philosophy of the History of Mankind*, Herder applied the method to the relation between species and natural environment: the bird flies in the air, and every deviation of its form from that of land animals can be explained by its element; the fish swims in water, its feet and hands have become fins and tail, and its limbs are little articulated. His point is not superficial correlation but that the animal's *structure itself* is in dialogue with its environment: the structure serves the needs of the environment and is made possible by it. By understanding how the bird converses with its context, and comparing it with fish and land animals, one can recognize the differences and similarities between the bird and other animals and so grasp a continuity of nature that does not imply sameness.

**Influence:** Herder is credited with a more "dynamic" view of nature — a relation of mutual determination between the natural world and its inhabitants — which ultimately contributed to the establishment of geography as a discipline in the nineteenth century. **Alexander von Humboldt** realized this view most fully: his biographer held that what Herder had enthusiastically attempted in the *Ideas* — connecting the development of human culture with its native soil — Humboldt sought to accomplish scientifically in *Cosmos*; the title of his *Ideas for a Physiognomy of Plants* and his conception of a "general geography of plants" have also been traced to Herder. Humboldt wrote that he would rather connect long-observed facts than pursue isolated new ones, and that the geographical relations of the plant world interested him more than the discovery of an unknown genus. *The method Herder developed for interpreting historical texts — the concept of a "world" — provided the most concrete answer to the problem posed by Buffon and Diderot and gave rise to a new natural history and a new geography; the influence between hermeneutics and natural science thus ran both ways.* Kant, by contrast, though he developed a kind of hermeneutics of nature in the *Critique of the Power of Judgment* through reflective judgement and the idea of "life", still insisted on mathematics as the basis of "proper science" and failed, unlike Buffon, Diderot and Herder, to integrate hermeneutic method into the study of nature.

### Irony, fragment and infinite interpretation: Schlegel

**Background:** Early German Romanticism was a loose circle whose centre of activity was the university town of Jena in the second half of the 1790s. Its core members were the Schlegel brothers and **Friedrich von Hardenberg** (writing as "Novalis"); the writer Tieck and the theologian Schleiermacher were peripheral figures. Jena became a centre of philosophical and literary activity because Fichte had succeeded Reinhold in the chair of "critical philosophy" there; Schelling was also there, and Hegel lived in Jena from 1801 to 1807 and may have attended Schlegel's lectures of 1800–1801, while keeping his distance from the Romantics. Wilhelm von Humboldt lived in Jena from 1794 to 1797 and was close to Schlegel.

German philosophy in this period was dominated by Kant — not so much by the details of his metaphysics, epistemology and moral philosophy as by his general intellectual stance: emancipation from notions of epistemic and moral authority derived from Christian theology, driven by a radical, self-authorizing idea of pure reason. The most powerful minds mostly saw themselves as heirs to the "spirit" rather than the "letter" of Kant and, from the 1790s, devised systematic programmes for putting the Kantian revolution on a better footing. Two problems stood out: Kant had unwittingly introduced a new, subtle self-scepticism, especially in moral philosophy; and he had seriously underestimated the philosophical significance of linguistic experience. Schlegel addressed both.

**Distinction:** The Jena Romantics received Kant's philosophy through Fichte. Novalis tried to radicalize Fichte's foundational idea in Romantic form: the structure of consciousness requires positing a pre-predicative, completely unified source (fashionably called the "Absolute") as the ground of the basic distinctions at the level of consciousness — subject and object, receptivity and production, will and cognition. Any attempt to represent it must fail, since representation requires a duality of terms; it can at most be "intimated" indirectly. What Novalis called `romanticizing` is just such a procedure: making the familiar strange and the strange familiar — thinking of a familiar thing as an object to which quite different predicates can be attached, while introducing almost fantastic experiences into standard thinking. Together these show that any single thought only partially fits the single unity of what is thought. Novalis had fascinating views about the epistemic and moral status of historical understanding, but hermeneutics was not his main concern.

**Definition:** Schlegel was not initially concerned with transcribing Fichte's categories into Romantic form; his starting point was the study of classical languages and literature. He was the first in a series of philosophers whose Kantianism was deeply tempered by ancient Greek thought; he had planned to translate Plato's dialogues with Schleiermacher (in the end he did not take part), and understanding texts across great historical and cultural distance, on their own terms, was central to his conception of understanding as such. This conception is closely tied to his theory of `irony`. Philosophical irony is a dialectical activity of mind with three elements: (A) identification with the truth, falsity or pragmatic force of some statement; (B) a distancing that counterbalances that truth, falsity or pragmatic force; and (C) an equilibrium between the two. The point is that the best attitude towards one's own subjectivity is to feel the pull of a statement under a given determination while regarding it as only partially given, open to other and even radically different determinations, thereby weakening its univocal force as a "claim".

**Distinction:** This idea must not be trivialized. To say that human beings may never exhaust meaning, or that all knowledge is provisional and more remains to be discovered, is close to common sense, and is not what Schlegel meant by irony. His point is that fundamental provisionality is *part of the very activity of claiming* — not merely because unrealized possibilities lie "out there", but because, as agents, we build the partiality of the claim into any fixed position about the world or the self. Provisionality is not a reservation added to a claim afterwards; it is the form of the claim.

**Mechanism:** Irony is plainly not an explanatory category; it has no predictive power. It is an `interpretive` category, aimed at maximizing understanding of self and others. Irony strengthens intersubjectivity, because the indeterminacy it introduces into a statement can always be supplemented by other (ironic) statements; ironic communication thus opens the prospect of endless mutual interpretation. Novalis and Schlegel coined a word for their collaboration: `symphilosophy` (*Symphilosophie*). Its product is a system of "fragments": each fragment is the responsibility of a single author, but the fragments interact kaleidoscopically, forming a dynamic "identity in difference" rather than being reduced to a common denominator. Schlegel compared this kind of unity to various things, the most familiar to philosophers being Leibniz's world of monads. Fragments are (1) objects carefully crafted for irony, (2) hence products of interpretation made for reinterpretation; (3) this interpretation and reinterpretation constitutes symphilosophy, (4) and thus intersubjectivity, (5) and thus sociality. This sociality consists of overlapping units of interpretation in which intelligibility — which Schlegel sometimes calls, negatively, `incomprehensibility` — is always at work. Understanding shared meaning is only a special case of the more obvious challenge of understanding distant cultures or historical forms of life: both are expressions of the "Absolute", that is, of the totality of possible human meaning.

**Principle:** For Schlegel, interpretation must take care not to assimilate the meaning of its object to the interpreter's prejudices; the practice of understanding involves the exercise of imagination in order to understand the object on its own terms, and the interpreter is precisely not to project himself into the field of interpretation. There is of course a process of revision in interpretation, which does include adjusting it in the light of what is understood; but there is no irremediable subjective "screen" that corrupts objectivity. When the Romantics say that "interpretation is infinite", they mean that any object of interpretation can always yield more meaning, not that the interpreter is inherently suspect. Empathy plays no role in Schlegel's hermeneutics, but inner engagement with the object is an ideal. In the exchange of ideas, understanding is mutual interpretation: each side tries to understand the other, adjusting its interpretation in real time and carefully putting itself in the other's place — while the other's place is itself variable and shifts relative to the other's attempt to understand oneself. Such a fragmentary understanding as symphilosophy requires continuous nested interpretation but need not converge on a single understanding to which both parties agree. It is like dancing: one adjusts one's steps to one's partner, not in order to arrive at a predetermined resting place but to dance a satisfying dance.

**Mechanism:** Schlegel held that thought depends on and is bounded by language, and he belongs to the line of German philosophy of language according to which more "indeterminate" modes of language such as poetry are basic and in a sense "better", because they express inexhaustibly plural meanings and are a more fitting vehicle for intimating the "Absolute". He was one of the founders of historical linguistics — besides Greek and Latin he mastered classical Persian and Sanskrit — and his interest in language, especially in the relation of language and thought, was always indexed to history. He recognized history's power to shape human practices on its own and so did not believe that a universal grammar underlies different linguistic practices. Since all thought is bounded by language and language is a historical entity, thought is historically bounded, and so is interpretation as a linguistic category. The interpreter must therefore reconstruct from within the social and historical context that governs a text's meaning and then determine the text's individuality within this structure, revealing how it exploits, in its own distinctive and even signature way, the possibilities offered by the structure of the language.

**Influence:** Schlegel's theory of irony might seem to place final authority over meaning in the author, but it does not: symphilosophy supports no such Cartesian conclusion, and its conception of the subject is deliberately fluid. For the Romantics no rule can prescribe my subjectivity to me — not even a self-given rule — because I am an ongoing process of becoming. Hence, even under the best conditions, the possibility of misunderstanding oneself always remains, and there is no stable vantage point of self-criticism; the interpreter may therefore understand what he interprets better than the author himself did. Likewise, interpretation must penetrate beneath the surface of a work to probe its latent `unconscious` meaning. "Unconscious" has two senses here: first, the author was not explicitly aware of this latent content when writing; second, even when confronted with it, the author could not acknowledge it. One might naturally think that meaning is unconscious in the first sense precisely because it is unconscious in the second, but it would be going too far to say that Schlegel already had a theory of the unconscious. In any case, the idea that texts worth interpreting shimmer with traces of buried meaning was to be of great importance for later hermeneutics.

**Mechanism:** The infinity of interpretation Schlegel speaks of is combined with another view from his linguistic writings: the meaning of an individual term or sentence depends on the whole structure of meaning — `holism`. There are many kinds of holism; Schlegel's peculiarity is that the part–whole relation is structured according to his theory of fragments: any whole is itself a fragment whose parts are fragments, so the constitutive power of the whole over its parts (and vice versa) is non-standard — the relative determination of a part by its whole is itself relative to that whole's relation to the Absolute (and to any larger whole). Holism and infinite interpretability thus limit each other. This in turn interacts with another important component of his theory of language: the grammatical features of a language, like its vocabulary, are developing aspects of meaning that the linguist must treat historically. Taken together, the three yield the conclusion that sustained deep interpretation of a work often uncovers meanings that depend on the interplay of part and whole — just as a language is meaningful neither as a mere accumulation of its constituent meanings (words, phrases, sentences) nor merely as a whole, but through the structural interrelation of parts and whole. The same thought governs his view of the importance of genre (and of mixtures of genres).

**Condition:** Even under the holistic constraint of seeking deep meaning, the interpreter must remain alert to incomprehensibilities in the text, whether digested or undigested. Schlegel's views on irony and the Absolute themselves foreshadow a certain incomprehensibility, since all texts must reach towards what they cannot fully represent or express. Explicitly ironic texts are more philosophical because they deliberately mark incomprehensibility and prompt the interpreter to mark it too. Pressing a work to expose its "confusion" (*Verwechslung*) is the entry point to its hermeneutic density, because this is very likely a basic form of meaning in the work: what the work most fundamentally expresses is the friction between the Absolute and emerging meaning. Incomprehensibility is by no means a sign of semantic or stylistic defect; it is a form of meaning, and a basic one.

**Distinction:** Rush warns that Gadamer's reception of Romantic hermeneutics exerts a retrospective shaping force: through Heidegger's phenomenology, Gadamer extended hermeneutics into a general theory of human self-constitution, in which the primary value of interpretive understanding lies in the transformation of the interpreter rather than in understanding others. To understand Romantic hermeneutics on its own terms, Gadamer's reading must be set aside. *Romantic hermeneutics did not sacrifice objectivity in the way Gadamer charges: what Schlegel calls "infinite" is the inexhaustibility of the object's meaning, not the insurmountability of the interpreter's subjectivity.*

### Grammatical and psychological interpretation: Schleiermacher's twofold structure

**Background:** Among the Romantics, Schleiermacher was the first to put forward a theory of interpretation recognizable as `hermeneutics`, though he never published a treatise on it. He lectured on hermeneutics many times during his quarter-century in Berlin, and the surviving lecture notes constitute our knowledge of his hermeneutics. Trained as a Lutheran theologian and pastor, Schleiermacher felt keenly the difficulty of understanding canonical texts written in ancient languages; but unlike his predecessors he sought to extend hermeneutics to the understanding of *any linguistic object of any period* — this is what he meant by the "universality" of hermeneutics. His paradigm case nonetheless remained the understanding of distant linguistic objects, where shared intelligibility is hard to establish and maintain. He held that the apparent smoothness of everyday linguistic understanding masks the difficulty of precise understanding, and that historically and linguistically distant texts merely make these difficulties more acute.

**Definition:** Schleiermacher distinguishes two main dimensions of interpretation, both of which must be examined for interpretation to be adequate. `Grammatical interpretation` infers from word usage the linguistic conventions that govern it and thereby determines the semantic content of the text; here the person disappears as an organ of language, and only language appears. `Psychological interpretation` (earlier called "technical" interpretation, *technisch*, from *techne*, "art") determines the author's intention in using particular expressions; here language with its determining power disappears and appears only as the organ of the person, in the service of his individuality. Only through psychological interpretation can non-semantic factors such as force and pragmatics be traced, and questions of style belong here too. The combination of the two yields an understanding of "thought" — always the thought of someone whose thinking is shaped by a concrete cultural and linguistic horizon. Examining both dimensions requires placing the text in its wider historical and linguistic environment, thereby establishing the range of possible meanings of terms and texts; attention to the author's psychology then marks out, against this general background of possible meanings, the particularity of the text's actual meaning.

**Distinction:** The word "psychological" has caused a long-standing misunderstanding of Schleiermacher's hermeneutics, as though it required introspective access to the author's mind. In fact psychological interpretation relies on public evidence to form hypotheses about authorial intention: it is concerned with which choices the author made among the possibilities offered by the linguistic community, and these choices are themselves embodied in the text. *This point is already present in Schlegel (previous section); Schleiermacher systematized it.*

**Genealogy:** Through his classic essay of 1900 and his biography of Schleiermacher, Dilthey established Schleiermacher as the source of systematic hermeneutics; but from Dilthey to Gadamer a story gradually took shape according to which Schleiermacher's thought, in his lectures of 1805–1833, tilted increasingly towards the psychological side ("divining" the author's intention) and away from linguistic critique, leading to a "bad metaphysics" — on which basis Gadamer declared Romantic hermeneutics "problematic". This story has been overturned: a fuller edition of Schleiermacher's writings and lecture notes shows that he retained both parts of the theory throughout his teaching. At the same time, Forster argues that almost everything distinctive and important in Schleiermacher's hermeneutics had already been developed by Herder. *The "founder" of hermeneutics should therefore be understood as a line running from Herder to Schleiermacher rather than as a single figure.*

### Misunderstanding, divination and the hermeneutic circle

**Principle:** Schleiermacher takes the possibility of `misunderstanding` as the starting point of hermeneutics: strict practice assumes that misunderstanding results as a matter of course. The strength of this thesis is disputed, and two versions must be distinguished.

**Distinction:** Schleiermacher's dialectics and ethics contain a transcendental, a priori component, and so does his hermeneutics. If this component is taken to be dominant, a strong thesis can be attributed to him: every act of interpretation adds a subjective filter that presents the object in the subject's own way; this veil is very hard, perhaps impossible, to detect or remove, so that misunderstanding is a constitutive and ineliminable component of understanding — a version that plays straight into Gadamer's hands. At times Schleiermacher seems to accept a weaker thesis instead: interpretive interaction is difficult, misunderstanding is an ever-present danger that must be reckoned with, but it can in principle be overcome. The strong thesis has ample textual support; the support for the weak thesis is mainly conceptual — Schleiermacher never draws the conclusion that would be almost self-evident under the strong thesis, namely that hermeneutics faces a paralysing problem of "incommensurability". On the weak thesis, interpretation is never complete and no text or discourse can be assigned a final meaning; but this is quite different from saying that at every step the interpretans seeps into the interpretandum. *Only the weak version is consistent with Schleiermacher's position as a whole, and with what Schlegel meant by "infinite interpretation".*

**Definition:** Schleiermacher also distinguishes the `comparative method` from the `divinatory method` (*divinatorisch*). The comparative method is relatively straightforward and concerns mainly linguistic questions: inferring from word usage the rules that give these usages their meaning — in modern terms, a kind of abductive inference. The divinatory method is more obscure; it operates mainly on the psychological dimension, interpreting particular authorial intentions in the text. Traditional scholarship took divination to involve empathetic projection rather than inference, but today's best scholars agree that this is anachronistic: it either assimilates Schleiermacher to the use of "presentiment" (*Ahndung*) in his Berlin-period dispute with Fries, or reads him retrospectively through Dilthey's "empathy" (*Einfühlung*).

**Mechanism:** Schleiermacher says that the divinatory method is the one in which one transforms oneself, "as it were", into the other in order to grasp his individuality directly. Note the qualifier "as it were". He later glossed the process as "guessing" (*erraten*). On what basis? Here the early Romantic idea of "symphilosophy" comes into play again: what Schleiermacher envisages is deep interpretive engagement throughout the target context until one can imagine that context from the standpoint of its participants. This establishes only a projective "co-habitation" with the author, not standing exactly in the author's place. Forster's judgement is that Schleiermacher's considered concept of "divination" is in fact an entirely secular process of guessing, conjecture or hypothesis: it rests on careful scrutiny of the available evidence while going well beyond it. This is the nature of empirical hypotheses — in the natural sciences too. If all communication came with transparent understanding, there would be no need for hermeneutics, just as there would be no need for scientific inquiry if the workings of nature were transparent. *Interpretive reconstruction is always a risky business; what must be discarded is philosophy's residual absolutism, which idealizes "science" as necessary and universal knowledge. Schleiermacher was not entirely free of such "scientific" ambition for hermeneutics either, and the tradition paid a price for it.*

**Mechanism:** Schleiermacher regards interpretation as holistic, and his holism operates at the metaphysical, epistemological and semantic levels at once. Strikingly, he denies that words can be truly polysemous: all the meanings of a sign or term share a common semantic root, which remains at work. Schlegel too was attracted to this idea. *If one holds that tracing a word back towards its etymology brings one closer to a more focused, more poetic sense, and that these etymologies still covertly shape present meaning, one may conclude that a word's meaning must be unified.*

**Definition:** Schleiermacher's holism yields a clear version of what was later called the `hermeneutic circle`: understanding any part of a text requires understanding it in its full context — a context reaching far beyond the single text, beyond all the author's texts, beyond the texts of his school, and even to the local social and historical conditions in which the text is set — while these contexts can in turn be understood only through understanding their parts. This did not trouble Schleiermacher: understanding is provisional, and the circle is merely a product of the interpreter's situation. The sensible course is to read the text openly, advancing step by step and building up contexts of meaning as far as possible, in the expectation that once a larger context is reached, one can return to adjust the details in its light, producing a new and better context, and so on.

**Conclusion:** The weak thesis of misunderstanding, divination as evidence-based conjecture, and holism with its circle together constitute a methodological hermeneutics different from the one Gadamer described: it acknowledges the provisionality of understanding and the interpreter's situatedness, but treats that situatedness as a condition that can be progressively improved through evidence, comparison and revision, not as an insurmountable filter.

### Language as the constitutive organ of thought: Humboldt

**Background:** **Wilhelm von Humboldt** was a polymath who made first-rate contributions to political theory, the philosophy of language and comparative linguistics, and his hermeneutic thought is scattered through these works. What sets him apart is that he carried out empirical research that supported what might otherwise have been mere speculation: he had a deep command of nearly twenty languages and built the richest library of the world's languages of his time; his linguistic interests reached far beyond the Indo-European family, to some native languages of North and South America and to the languages of the South Pacific; against prevailing opinion he argued that Basque is a language isolate, a conclusion that still stands; and his late study of the Kawi language of Java and Bali convincingly showed that it cannot easily be assimilated to Sanskrit, a view also still accepted. But the magnitude of his empirical achievements in comparative linguistics can easily obscure the philosophical core of his study of language.

**Genealogy:** Humboldt had studied Kant closely before coming to Jena, but the most important figures for him were Herder and Fichte. From Herder (perhaps also via Schlegel and Schleiermacher) Humboldt took over the idea of "reflection"; Herder's words are *Besonnenheit* and *Besinnung*, which may be rendered `reflective awareness`. Both are hard to translate: the verb *besinnen* in everyday German means "to reflect" or "to recollect"; the noun *Besinnung* often appears in fixed expressions about losing or regaining consciousness and is less cognitive in flavour than the philosophically more current "consciousness" (*Bewusstsein*); and the past participle *besonnen* carries an evaluative sense, "temperate" or "level-headed" — Schleiermacher used it to translate "temperance" (*sophrosyne*) in Plato's dialogues.

**Mechanism:** The concept has two dimensions, which are ultimately not separate. The first refers to the primary psychological process that distinguishes humans from non-human animals. Herder, Schleiermacher and Humboldt all rejected a purely additive view of human psychology — as if humans had the sub-discursive capacities of non-human animals "plus" a capacity for reflection; rather, human capacities are bundled together in a distinctively human way, without strict modularity, even in the most primitive forms of reflective awareness. At the same time one must avoid over-intellectualizing simple reflective awareness. It is the process by which a human being first becomes aware of discrete units of experience — the capacity to focus attention; as a basic mode of receptivity it is by no means passive: it is the capacity to punctuate and segment the flow of experience and so to have particular experiences. This is possible because a human being can distinguish himself, as subject of experience, from the experiences he undergoes. The subject forms itself as subject by forming experience. In formulation Humboldt is clearly influenced by Kant's third *Critique* and by Fichte, and sometimes uses *Reflexion* in place of *Besonnenheit*. Reflective awareness recognizes patterns in otherwise rough and seamless experience without fixing them under finite forms: when the manifold of intuition is synthesized according to rules in apperception, this is possible because the synthesis has a formal starting point in its performer, distributed in principle across the units of experience being combined.

The second dimension bears Fichte's imprint more clearly: reflective awareness is attuned to the vocation of freedom. Reflective awareness is the human being's original "doing", and this is the source of its connotations of temperance and composure. Human attention is tuned to a "measuring" of experience: once there are units of experience, a preliminary comparison of them takes place; by measuring unit against unit, one can experience the succession of units — a succession that, unlike the seamless continuum of animal sensation, is "made".

**Mechanism:** It is at this conceptual juncture that language enters. Kant had flagged a potential problem for his theory of cognition: intuitions are strictly particular, concepts wholly general — how can the two be combined in judgement? His answer was the Schematism of the first *Critique*: the productive imagination can write the universal into the particular and the particular into the universal. Humboldt faced a similar predicament: reflection provides comparative awareness of units of experience, and these units anticipate reflection's demand that they be comparable; but by virtue of what do sensible experience and reflection overlap to form a unified structure? His answer — *arguably a rewriting of Kant's Schematism* — is: by virtue of being embedded in language. Language is at once sensible (sound) and of thought (syntax and semantics), so wherever there is thought there is language, even when thought is internal to the individual. This seems strange: how is thinking to oneself speaking to oneself with sound? Humboldt was willing to develop a rather abstract concept of the phoneme for this purpose. *Here the pun on *Sinn* ("sense" in both the sensory and the semantic meaning) left when the inseparable prefix is removed from *besinnen* is productive: reflective awareness is precisely the activity of giving sense to sensation (*be-sinnt*).*

**Influence:** Thought thus depends on and is bounded by language. Humboldt's view of thought's dependence on language directly shaped his hermeneutics. His empirical work in comparative linguistics revealed an extraordinary diversity of languages; combined with the view that a language's vocabulary and syntax determine thought, this implies that thought, and with it culture, differs substantially both synchronically and diachronically. This brings back Schlegel's and Schleiermacher's concerns with misunderstanding and holism: where such differences exist, understanding the meanings of a language — and even of a culture — becomes an on-the-spot "fieldwork" reconstruction of context. Humboldt did not, of course, think there was a "measurement problem" of the kind that worried twentieth-century anthropologists such as Evans-Pritchard and Geertz — how to subtract the influence of the interpreter's "native framework" on the "target framework" — and his holism was not so severe as to become self-enclosing and make mutual understanding an insuperable problem. Even so, understanding remains an explicit reconstruction of meaning within the holistic context of a culture.

**Distinction:** Chomsky's *Cartesian Linguistics* tells the story of the idea of "universal grammar" from Descartes and Port-Royal to Humboldt. That Humboldt held there to be a universal grammar may be disappointing and sits in tension with his views on cultural plurality, but it is indeed part of his thought, and sharply distinguishes him from Schlegel. The seed of the idea probably lies in the theory of linguistic awareness just described: universal grammar may stem from the wish to rewrite Kant's forms of intuition and categories in linguistic terms. In any case, universality at this level leaves particular syntax and semantics (not to mention phonology) largely undetermined, and a great deal of more specific interpretive work remains to be done.

**Conclusion:** *The methodological tradition described in P3 — Herder's contextualization and concept of a "world", Schlegel's irony and infinite interpretation, Schleiermacher's twofold grammatical/psychological structure and evidence-based divination, Humboldt's view of language as constitutive — already contains contextual holism, empirical hypothesis, the sociality of dialogue, the mediating role of language and the historical variability of genre. It is neither the subjectivist formal method Gadamer described nor the mystical empathy analytic philosophy imagined. This fact will come into play again in the historiographical debates of P4 and in the assessment of Gadamer in P7.*

## P4 How is historical knowledge possible?

### Why source criticism is not enough to make history a science

**Problem:** Historians may be more uneasy about the scientific standing of their discipline than practitioners of any other academic field. **Frank Ankersmit** believes history has irretrievably lost what made it a discipline; Novick, in *That Noble Dream* (1988), declared that history no longer exists as a community of scholars with shared aims, standards and purposes; Martin conceded that we still do not know how historians do, or should, decide between competing historical interpretations. Historians are often accused of being incapable of theoretical self-reflection: absorbed in craft, enamoured of the archive, insisting that source criticism is method enough — the crudest empiricism — while claiming autonomy from philosophy and social theory and claiming at the same time to practise some kind of "science".

**Distinction:** To answer whether history is a science, two unreasonable conceptions of "science" must first be set aside. One is the ancient ideal of *scientia* or *episteme*, necessary and universal knowledge, which governed the Western philosophical imagination until modern times; the other is the idea of a "unified science" propagated in the age of positivism, from Comte (and Kant) through neo-Kantianism to logical positivism. Research over the past half-century has destroyed the theory of unified science that logical positivism projected onto the practice of the natural sciences: Quine raised sharp philosophical objections in the early 1950s, and after Kuhn's *Structure of Scientific Revolutions* (1962) the whole "received view" collapsed. In its place has come a less unified, more situated theory of empirical inquiry, more dependent on contingent conditions, which no longer begins by pronouncing history inherently inferior.

**Background:** For two hundred years philosophy has repeatedly appointed itself, uninvited, the conceptual guardian of history. Evans observes that very few historians possess a theoretical sophistication acceptable to trained philosophers, so that some have argued the nature of historical explanation is best left to philosophers. *But historians as historians are not philosophers; they need not become philosophers, nor despair because their project is not philosophy — which is not to say that the discipline may indulge in theoretical naivety.*

**Genealogy:** Long before history was a discipline rather than a genre of discourse, Aristotle ranked it below philosophy and even below poetry because it fails to reach the universal; from Aristotle to Wolff and Kant, `historical cognition` (*cognitio historica*), being particular, contingent and conjectural, was judged far inferior to philosophical knowledge derived from first principles. When disciplinary history arose in Germany in the late eighteenth century, it did not accept the tradition of *scientia* but sought to establish its disciplinary credentials through methods of empirical research — "source criticism". It borrowed methodological insights from three more mature disciplines: classical philology (especially Winckelmann's reconstruction of Greek sculpture and Heyne's archaeological notion of an "overall habitus"); biblical criticism (from Richard Simon and Spinoza to Michaelis's historicization of the Bible); and developmental linguistics, much influenced by Leibniz (the recognition of language families and a genealogical theory of linguistic relations). The new historical method was most forcefully formulated in the work of the Göttingen professor **Johann Christoph Gatterer** in the 1760s: in 1767, in the first issue of the *Allgemeine historische Bibliothek* of the new Royal Historical Institute at Göttingen, he set out the ideal of "pragmatic history", which became the consensus of a new generation of professional historians at the Institute.

Herder followed this emerging historical discourse closely from a distance. His letter to Gatterer stated the perspectivity of understanding (see P3); in a short essay of the same period he wrote that every philosopher sees things from his own perspective, and that it was dispiriting to have to prove that historical knowledge does not disgrace the philosopher. Herder insisted that history must treat periods, peoples or cultures as individuals formed according to their own principles, not measure them by abstract external standards taken from the historian's own time.

**Example:** History acquired its "paradigmatic" form in the mid-nineteenth century with **Leopold von Ranke** and his school. Ranke's seminar, philological method and archival research irreversibly shaped later historical practice, and two of his most famous dicta won general acceptance: that the task of history is to show "how it actually was", and his wish to "extinguish the self" and let things speak for themselves. Almost every important German historian of the nineteenth century passed through Ranke's seminar, which he led until 1871, producing a professional cohort who revered him as the "father of scientific history". But what they carried on was only part of his complex practice and theory: professional historians increasingly came to believe that access to archives and source criticism would resolve all problems of historical interpretation. The American reception of "scientific history" was similar and was canonized in the founding of the American Historical Association; for many professional historians, Ranke was even — quite wrongly — regarded as a "positivist".

**Distinction:** Ranke's actual monographs everywhere display the intrusion of his personal tastes and convictions — religious, political, aesthetic — however much he wished to efface his presence as author. There is no science without research, but no synthesis without intuition and imagination; historical practice was therefore for him always both art and science. It was the connection of individual events — their textured, integrated individuality and their immersion in synchronic and diachronic orders — that elicited a sense of the totality of history, even of the providence behind it, and this had to be imaginatively constructed and affirmed. The task of history, he said, is to present what is peculiar and essential in the individual, and the coherence and connection in the whole. **Frederick C. Beiser** sums this up in three points: Ranke believed history has an ineliminable artistic component; he believed metaphysics (and theology) form a necessary background to historical insight; and he had no interest in establishing general laws. Yet almost none of these personal views of historical practice were institutionalized in the emerging discipline: Ranke's narrative art and his gift for imaginative synthesis were not passed on to his "school".

**Genealogy:** Those who conceived historical method more systematically and more deeply than Ranke were, before him, **Wilhelm von Humboldt** and, after him, Droysen. Humboldt's lecture "On the Historian's Task", delivered to the Prussian Academy of Sciences in Berlin in 1821, has been called the most important short text of the nineteenth century on historical understanding and writing. Humboldt stressed the creative role of the historical interpreter, bringing the historian closer to the artist than to the philosopher, while arguing that the historian differs from the artist in tethering imagination to evidence. The historian's task is to examine accumulated sources rigorously while synthesizing them into an order they do not possess in their fragmentary particularity; this synthesis is not an artificial imposition but the recognition of an inner principle of order. Humboldt insisted that the historian can discern coherence in history; there are forces really at work in history, and the historian's task is to penetrate to them. The creative forces of world history — ideas — are not projected into history but are its very essence; for every force, living or dead, acts according to the laws of its nature, and everything that happens is inseparably connected.

**Mechanism:** Humboldt describes the process of historical cognition thus: only part of an event is visible in the sensible world; the rest must be supplied by intuition, inference and conjecture. This rightly affirms the actual, necessarily tentative process by which human beings construct in the face of the unknown — "science in the making". Daston and Galison have shown that the eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries had a quite different idea of scientific `objectivity`, one that linked scientific insight more closely to artistic imagination; Humboldt belongs to this historical moment of objectivity. His programme had much in common with Herder, whom he disliked, and with Hegel, who had not yet begun lecturing on the philosophy of history.

**Conclusion:** Source criticism is a necessary but not a sufficient condition of historical science. From Herder and Ranke's actual practice to Humboldt's lecture, historical understanding requires creative synthesis under strict evidential constraint; the question is how to theorize this synthesis without reducing it to subjective imagination.

### Droysen's investigative understanding and its four dimensions

**Background:** In 1857 the English historian **Henry Thomas Buckle** opened his *History of Civilization in England* by proclaiming a positivist mission for history — in effect a positivist manifesto. German historians took it as a hostile challenge. **Johann Gustav Droysen**, in a long review of 1863, spoke for the whole German historical profession in denying that Buckle's programme was the only way of "raising history to the rank of a science" — the very title of his review. Droysen's ambition was indeed to establish history as a self-sufficient science and autonomous discipline, but one must be clear what he meant by "science": the emphasis was on empirical research, and for him this meant hermeneutic inquiry — the essence of historical method is `investigative understanding` (*forschend verstehen*), an individualizing, "morphological" method pursued in a comparative and developmental context.

**Genealogy:** Droysen's thought was formed during his studies at the University of Berlin from 1826. He knew Humboldt's lecture of 1821 and called Humboldt "the Bacon of the historical sciences"; his doctoral supervisor was **August Böckh**, who applied the hermeneutic method he had learned from Schleiermacher to the broader problems of classical philology. Notably, the student Droysen attended Ranke's seminar only once but took six courses with **Hegel**. He never identified with the Ranke school and took a revisionist stance towards it: he regarded its self-styled "critical school" as naive and clumsy — it identified the essence of history with method and characterized that method as "source criticism", the presentation of "pure facts"; Droysen asked whether source criticism could lead to anything more than reproducing views once held, and whether it could lead to "pure facts" at all.

To put the discipline on a firmer epistemological and methodological footing, Droysen from 1857 onward worked on a systematic theory of history, which he called `Historik`, and continued to develop it throughout his career. But it found little echo among his colleagues, who were content to follow the archival source criticism and narrative reporting promoted by Ranke's institutionally decisive seminar. Only in recent times, in the search for theoretical grounding for disciplinary practice, has Droysen become an object of intensive study: Rüsen calls him "the greatest theorist of history in the German language", and Rothacker called the *Historik* "the most influential document of the logic of the historical sciences".

**Principle:** The key to Droysen's methodological self-awareness is the recognition that historical practice is always at once `presentist` and `historicist`. On the one hand, the historian is driven by concerns of his immediate context, which call forth the specific questions he investigates; on the other, this inquiry is constrained: one must learn from the historically distant object, listen to its own voice, and not merely read meaning into it — the historicist dimension of Droysen's hermeneutics. He therefore held that the historian must extend Schleiermacher's hermeneutics of *texts* into a hermeneutics of *events*: the part–whole dialectic at the core of textual hermeneutics must be extended into a "circle" of individuality and totality among agents, events, periods and peoples. The method of source criticism must always remain a means, not an end: finding and assessing sources is not enough; the historian's crucial task is interpretation, giving a coherent account — always a creative act, both discovery and making. The object must always be conceived in the basic context of dynamism and change, individuality and development.

**Mechanism:** Droysen distinguishes "methodics" (analysis) from "systematics" (synthesis), and divides investigative understanding into successive conceptual stages. It always begins from a focal question — a conjecture or concern rooted in the historian's present interest; from this a research design is formed, according to which the relevant "facts" are conceived and sought; once "sources" have been gathered, criticism assesses their adequacy and grounds; but this is not the end, for the historian's main work still lies ahead — `interpretation`, the discernment of coherent meaning. Interpretation has four dimensions:

1. **Pragmatic interpretation**: reconstructing the causal connections in the course of events, thereby locating and making intelligible the "ethical world" of that moment — a term Droysen explicitly took from Hegel to cover the domain of historical study: institutions, values, practices and traditions.
2. **Interpretation of conditions**: analysing the specific conditions, material and moral, that made action possible — the physical and structural environment in which events took place.
3. **Psychological interpretation**: determining the motives of individuals, accounting for individual identity and agency in the historical process.
4. **Interpretation of ideas**: at the most synthetic level, conceiving the larger currents of thought that drive the "ethical world" to realize itself — the general principles or ideals behind individual actions.

These four dimensions echo Aristotle's four causes while absorbing ideas Droysen drew from Hegel, Humboldt, Böckh, Schleiermacher and Herder. They are not optional perspectives but necessary components of a complete understanding of human action.

**Distinction:** Understanding as Droysen conceives it is fundamentally an intellectual activity involving concepts, judgements and reasoning. We understand an individual, he says, when we place it under a universal that explains why it happened; all understanding involves universality and necessity, which are the marks of thought. Droysen does describe understanding as a flash of insight, but this must be read in context: he distinguishes the "logical mechanism" of understanding from the "act of understanding", the latter being the result of the former; the flash presupposes conscious or subconscious processes of reasoning and inference in the reader or listener. He explains understanding holistically: the relation of individual and universal is that of part and whole; when we understand a person, we see his individual expressions as instances or parts of his whole character; we cannot understand a word or a sentence in isolation but must see it as part of a whole discourse, a historical context or an entire language. Yet he does not think holistic understanding merely places parts under wholes and individuals under universals — that is exactly what the covering-law model does; he stresses that understanding involves movement back and forth between part and whole, knowledge of each depending on and growing with the other. Because of this circle, the method of historical understanding is neither purely inductive nor purely deductive but a combination of both.

**Influence:** The problem of interpretation gradually led Droysen to add a third major part to the *Historik*, which he called "topics" (*Topik*): the problem of historical *writing*. Problems of presentation and communication needed more illumination within historical theory; in historical writing all problems of expression and persuasion come to the fore, and all the epistemological problems associated with the "hermeneutic circle" reach their highest intensity. Droysen's position here is extremely valuable: knowledge of the past is not given but created by the historian; coherence is made, not found — but made out of *what actually happened*, not fiction. Narrative exposition and hermeneutic interpretation contain both artistic and empirical elements. This is what Droysen could affirm as "science" and "objectivity" for the discipline: they lie in `intersubjective standards and methods`. In his view this was sufficient for the integrity of the discipline.

### Dilthey's grounding of the human sciences and the crisis of historicism

**Background:** By the second half of the nineteenth century the humanities had become extraordinarily well established in German universities, the envy of the Western world; but a new *epistemological* predicament arose, connected with positivism's hegemonic claim that there is a unified "scientific method" modelled on the natural sciences. The humanities might be established and professionalized, but the question became: can they really count as "sciences"?

**Principle:** **Wilhelm Dilthey** saw himself as confronting positivist philosophy of science and its hegemonic claims over the study of human beings. Trained as a philosopher, his interests and achievements in intellectual and cultural history made him one of the great masters of hermeneutic-historical practice; in the end he established himself in his time as a historical epistemologist. Explicitly emulating Kant, he aimed at a "critique of historical reason". He was a devoted student of Schleiermacher and saw clearly the central importance of hermeneutic method for humanistic study; more than Schleiermacher, he attended to psychology, especially to `lived experience` (*Erlebnis*). He insisted that the kind of general laws sought by the natural sciences can never capture the unique nature of lived experience — the first-person perspective, the agent's own beliefs and values; the proper study of lived experience can therefore only be interpretive, always acknowledging individuality and the holistic fusion of cognition, will and feeling. The distinctive task of historical hermeneutics, he stressed, is the constitution of individual agency, its outward expressions, and the intersubjective communities that both locate and embody those expressions.

**Mechanism:** Dilthey gives his fullest account of `understanding` in the late *Drafts for a Critique of Historical Reason*. Like Droysen, he regards understanding as fundamentally an intellectual activity. He attributes to "elementary understanding" a particular form of analogical or inductive inference, explicitly calling it an inference and seeking its "logical construction": (1) there is a uniform connection between expressions of type E and the mental states M they express; (2) in this concrete case C an expression of type E occurs; (3) therefore in C, E expresses M. In the most elementary understanding, we take a particular externalization — a word, an action or a gesture — and apply a more general rule to it, seeing it as an instance of that rule. More complex forms of understanding arise when we consider not one externalization but many of different kinds, and must take into account the varying circumstances and exact initial conditions under which general rules apply.

**Distinction:** Dilthey not only analysed understanding as a basic intellectual operation but explicitly denied that understanding is wholly a matter of intuition and imagination. He held it to be the result of careful methodical study, condemning understanding based on intuition alone as "aesthetic mysticism" and "enthusiastic obscurity". Though he granted that imagination and sympathy are important aids to understanding, he never thought them sufficient to establish an interpretation: the interpreter often begins with a feeling or insight but should not end with it; at the start of inquiry the interpreter's intuitions have only the status of hypotheses or conjectures, to be tested, revised or changed in the light of further evidence. The interpreter should not rely on flashes of inspiration but should follow a procedure combining induction and deduction: forming an idea of the whole after careful study of each part, then reinterpreting the parts in the light of knowledge of the whole. This is the "hermeneutic circle" he invoked again and again; he did not regard it as an "offence against logic" but as present in all inquiry, natural and human alike.

**Distinction:** Dilthey's famous distinction between the `human sciences` (*Geisteswissenschaften*) and the `natural sciences` (*Naturwissenschaften*) is phenomenological rather than metaphysical. It rests on the distinction between inner and outer experience, not between mind and body. In the *Introduction to the Human Sciences* (1883) he stressed that the distinction is not ontological, not a distinction between kinds of substance; he rejected mind–body dualism generally, holding that the division between mind and body is artificial, abstracted from the unity of human life, since human mental and bodily functions are inseparable. In his *Studies of Individuality* he explained further that the difference between inner and outer experience is fundamentally not a difference of mental acts or forms of consciousness but a difference of *content*, of the objects of awareness: the aim of the socio-historical sciences is not to describe mental events flowing through consciousness but to identify and analyse the content of inner experience.

**Mechanism:** For both Droysen and Dilthey, the method of understanding requires an inner unity of the mental and the physical, not a dualistic basis. Both say that understanding involves inferring a person's mental life from his words and actions, and both clearly recognized that if the mental and physical were heterogeneous, the mental could not be inferred from the physical. Both stress the concept of `expression`: actions and words are not merely the effects of inner mental causes (cause and effect being distinct events) but determinate forms of mental existence; without embodiment in word and deed, the mental is only an abstraction. *Their view of mind is closer to Aristotle than to Descartes: the soul is the form of the body, the general way the body acts and speaks in particular situations.* Nor did Dilthey ever doubt the value of establishing causal laws in the human sciences: in the *Introduction* he held that the socio-historical sciences, like the natural sciences, use abstract methods and seek general laws; that they need induction and generalization as the natural sciences do; and that, because human beings differ so greatly and interact in such complex ways, these laws may never have the precision of natural laws — but he did not doubt, indeed he stressed, the importance of general causal laws.

**Controversy:** Dilthey was a philosopher concerned with historical *practice*; **Wilhelm Windelband** was a historian of philosophy concerned with preserving the integrity of philosophy, which meant keeping history in its place. For Windelband, to historicize reason was to relativize it, to undermine the universal and necessary validity of its fundamental principles. His pioneering essay of 1883, "Critical or Genetic Method?", appeared in the same year as Dilthey's *Introduction* and was a direct challenge to it: validity stands above the historical domain; if historicism were true, philosophy would make no sense at all, so the standpoint of universal reason must be defended. In effect this meant that the neo-Kantians rejected the fundamental insight history sought. What Windelband began, his student **Heinrich Rickert** completed systematically: using the question of epistemological foundations to establish philosophy's disciplinary hegemony over history.

**Influence:** This led to the "crisis of historicism" that troubled the historical discipline for most of the twentieth century. In a set of key works of the 1920s — by Troeltsch, Meinecke, Mannheim and Heussi — the phrase became the canonical topic of anxiety, and has remained so to this day. Husserl, Windelband and others also accused Dilthey's psychology of blurring the line between philosophy and empirical science; but on another reading this is precisely the expression of his revival of the Herder–Schleiermacher line of hermeneutics.

**Conclusion:** Dilthey established understanding as the distinctive mode of knowledge of the human sciences, but his understanding is neither empathic intuition nor premised on dualism, and it does not exclude causal explanation. The roots of the "crisis of historicism" lie not in any defect of the method of understanding itself but in the neo-Kantian philosophical demand that validity must stand above history.

### Can the covering-law model swallow understanding?

**Background:** The division between "analytic" and "continental" philosophy is false and foolish, but it still does harm because some people still believe in it, and many believed in it for a long time. It erects artificial barriers that keep philosophers from talking to one another, and one of the fields that suffered most was the philosophy of history. From the 1950s, interest in the philosophy of history rose sharply among English-speaking analytic philosophers; stemming mainly from Popper and Collingwood, it produced first-rate work comparable in quality and quantity to the German discussions of the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. Yet these analytic discussions almost entirely ignored the German historicist tradition: Humboldt, Ranke, Droysen, Dilthey, Windelband, Rickert, Troeltsch, Simmel and even Weber might never have existed.

**Example:** Walsh's *Introduction to the Philosophy of History* (1951) marks the beginning of this postwar interest and was for decades the standard introduction in the English-speaking world. Walsh felt he had to defend the very existence of the subject, and attributed English neglect of the philosophy of history to "leading characteristics of the English mind and temperament" — caution and critical acumen — which made the English suspicious of speculative philosophies of history in the manner of Herder, Hegel and Spengler. His defence was to distinguish two kinds of philosophy of history: a *speculative* philosophy of history, whose object is "the totality of past human actions", and a *critical* philosophy of history, concerned with the logic of historical inquiry; the cautious English might prefer the latter. He wrote as if critical, epistemological philosophy of history were a new concern better suited to the English mind — completely ignoring that it had been a chief concern of German philosophy for generations: Ranke, Droysen, Windelband, Rickert, Dilthey, Burckhardt, Simmel and Weber, though German, were as suspicious of speculative philosophy of history as the most cautious English gentleman. To mention this fact would have wrecked Walsh's strategy: an English public that had just fought a second world war against Germany would not have been interested in the long-standing concerns of its enemy. *The root of this neglect was neither English temperament nor merely English insularity, but the tragic aftermath of war.*

**Definition:** The price of neglecting the historicist tradition was the poverty and futility of the ensuing discussion, above all the long debate about `historical explanation`. Since **Carl Hempel**'s classic 1942 paper "The Function of General Laws in History", analytic philosophers concentrated mainly on one question: is the `covering law` — the universal causal law — the only form of scientific explanation in history? Positivists said yes; their "idealist" opponents held that there is another form of explanation in history irreducible to covering laws, usually some version of Collingwood's theory of "re-enactment", according to which explaining the past means re-living or re-enacting past experience in the observer. The debate, which lasted decades, ended in deadlock: the positivists did not abandon the covering-law thesis but had to concede that it hardly reflects what historians actually do, and that most generalizations in history are of very limited scope, quite unlike natural laws, holding only for particular times and places; the idealists still could not accept the covering-law thesis but had to concede that merely re-living the past hardly counts as explanation. As a result the field lost the vitality it had in the 1950s and 1960s; the anthologies of those years have long been out of print, with nothing new to replace them.

**Mechanism:** In this debate philosophers seldom asked what was really at stake: what difference does it make whether there is one form of explanation and one methodology in history, or two, three or four? Behind the positivist campaign for the covering law lay an ideal that had fascinated them since Comte — `the unity of science`: there must be only one form of explanation and one methodology in all the sciences; whatever their terminology and subject matter, the human and the natural sciences must share the same logical structure. The main opposition to this ideal came from the historicist tradition, from the position of `the autonomy of history`: the consistent claim of historicists from Ranke to Weber was that history is indeed a science, but one with its own distinctive methods and forms of explanation, different from those of the natural sciences — including the methods of textual criticism and interpretation, and the method of `understanding` (*Verstehen*), which demands reconstructing past life from within, on its own terms.

**Evidence:** When the positivists later campaigned for the covering law, they seldom mentioned the historicists explicitly; on reading their polemics, the first impression is often that the only resistance came from metaphysics, which they equated with superstition and obscurantism. But at the end of his 1942 paper Hempel inadvertently revealed his real target: without naming names, he took aim at the historicist belief in the autonomy of history, arguing that even if there were distinctively historical laws (laws whose "terms" are historical), this would not justify the doctrine of the methodological autonomy of history, since the "form" of such laws would still fit the covering-law paradigm; he likewise criticized "the method of empathic understanding", clearly the method of understanding of the hermeneutic tradition. The reason he concealed his target was simple: it helped him win the argument. Had he explicitly named any leading historicist and work, it would have become clear that "the method of empathic understanding" was a straw man that nobody — least of all anyone in the historicist tradition — wished to hold, and that the methods and forms of explanation of history go far beyond covering laws.

**Mechanism:** The fundamental error of covering-law theory is exposed at once by a historicist commonplace: the past is gone and will never return. Precisely for this reason, historicists stressed that the past must be laboriously reconstructed through critical examination of sources, through sifting and ordering documents, texts, inscriptions and remains. The great sin of covering-law theory is never to have truly acknowledged this commonplace, instead *treating the past as given*: it is a thesis about the explanation of past `actions` and `events` that assumes we already know these actions and events and then tells us the logic of explaining them. But it does not answer the big question: by what methods does the historian reconstruct the past? The answer, of course, is critical methods — the study of the date, authenticity and provenance of documents — and methods of interpretation — techniques for determining the meaning of texts, often written in foreign or ancient languages. Taking these methods into account is crucial in determining the scientific status of history, because nineteenth-century history's claim to scientific status rested precisely on the rigorous and exact use of these methods: nobody claimed history was a science because it had discovered or applied covering laws. These methods sometimes employ general laws, but they do not consist only of them, because their domain is usually not actual events and actions but *statements about* events and actions in historical documents: people are assessing evidence, determining meaning and interpreting texts, not just proposing and applying covering laws.

**Conclusion:** Hence the first thesis: *even if covering laws were the only form of explanation of "actions" in history, Hempel's argument would not secure the ideal of the unity of science.* It applies only to history on its *object side* (history as the totality of past human actions), not to history on its *subject side* (history as the totality of what people have written about the past). How might Hempel respond? He might try semantic gerrymandering, claiming that the criticism and interpretation of documents are not "explanation" in the proper sense; but this will not work, because "explanation" has a perfectly legitimate sense that applies to criticism and interpretation — in assessing documents and interpreting texts we are also, quite straightforwardly, answering "why" questions. He might instead say that the methods of criticism and interpretation are not scientific methods; this too is unappealing: for one thing, these methods, rigorously applied, do meet many of the scientific standards the positivists themselves proposed — intersubjective testability, rigorous assessment of evidence, restricting conclusions to the evidence; for another, if he insisted on denying them scientific status he would have to admit that he was practising a kind of historical scepticism.

**Distinction:** Seen more broadly, positivists and their opponents alike committed a classic confusion: between history as past actions and events and history as the study or record of those actions and events. English *history*, French *histoire* and German *Geschichte* all share this ambiguity. The conflict between historicists and positivists arose partly because they meant different things by history: the positivists' covering laws apply to history in the sense of past actions, the historicists' techniques of criticism and interpretation to the records and documents about those actions. In fairness, the positivists were not the only ones to trample on this distinction: when Collingwood said that the essence of human action is thought, his characterization fitted history on the *subject side* better; history as past actions and events cannot be reduced to thought, since many factors enter into their causes, though their explanation must include thought among those causes. Collingwood identified history with thought because he rightly saw sources — history as knowledge — as the real starting point of history; but he wrongly inferred that, since sources consist of thought, the actions and events they are about also consist of thought.

**Counterexample:** The second thesis is that positivism made the claim to the unity of science look plausible only by caricaturing its main rival, the historicist method of understanding. When the positivists occasionally criticized the method of understanding, they did not examine the works of Droysen, Dilthey and Weber but produced a caricature: understanding is an intuitive method by which the historian empathizes, sympathizes or imaginatively re-lives the experience of someone in the past; it is opposed to the inferential use of concepts, judgements and reasoning; and it presupposes a dualist metaphysics, the method of understanding applying to the mental realm and the method of the natural sciences to the material world. This interpretation recurs in the positivists' writings, and analytic philosophers gave the same account, usually focusing on Collingwood's historicist-inspired theory of "re-enactment".

If the method of understanding were like this, the objections to it would plainly be strong. First, an intuitive method is unreliable, because we may read contemporary feelings, attitudes and values into the past: imagining *myself* as a soldier at Waterloo is one thing, having experiences like those of a French dragoon at the time quite another; however much I study the battle, I cannot have the same experiences as a participant, since I can neither fully abstract from my own historical conditions nor know deeply enough the historical conditions behind the dragoon's life. Second, even if we could have accurate intuitions about past lives, they would not constitute *explanations*: we would still have to confirm or test them, formulate them more discursively, propose hypotheses and generalizations, and test these against other empirical data. At most, the method of understanding is not a form of explanation but a heuristic device for discovering hypotheses; and the more we engage in formulating and testing hypotheses, the more we see that the method of understanding presupposes the same procedures as the natural sciences. The method of understanding thus faces a dilemma: the more it differs from the natural sciences, the more merely intuitive and the less reliable it is; the more reliable and accurate it is, the more it resembles the method of the natural sciences.

**Distinction:** But this is not at all the method set out by the leading proponents of understanding. The positivist interpretation contains three basic errors: that the method is wholly intuitive or empathic; that it presupposes a dualist metaphysics; and that it is meant to exclude covering laws or causal explanation. These are not deliberate distortions — the positivists knew too little of the texts even to distort them — but they are *serious* misunderstandings, because the historicists themselves had anticipated and explicitly, emphatically rejected them. **Max Weber**, in his outline of the theory of understanding at the beginning of *Economy and Society*, states clearly that although empathy greatly helps in interpreting the meaning of an action, it is not always possible, let alone necessary; being able to imagine oneself performing a similar action is not a necessary precondition of understanding — "one need not be Caesar to understand Caesar". He argues that any intuition about the subjective meaning of an action must be confirmed by placing it in a wider context and comparing it with the concrete course of events; rational understanding of motivation consists in placing an action in an intelligible and more inclusive context of meaning. Droysen's and Dilthey's positions have already been set out: understanding is an intellectual activity, and intuitions are only hypotheses to be tested; it requires the unity of mind and body rather than dualism; and it includes causal explanation as a necessary component.

### Interpretation and explanation as complements: Weber's ideal types and practical reasoning

**Problem:** At this point the positivists seem to have a plausible rejoinder: since the proponents of understanding must concede that intuition is unreliable and must be confirmed discursively, and since the chief discursive means are causal laws, the theory of understanding is just a disguised or mystified form of covering-law theory — what is good in it is already in the covering law, and what is new (intuition and feeling) is not good but merely a preliminary device. This rejoinder fails, because it wrongly assumes that all the work of conceptually articulating intuitions and feelings is done by covering laws. Examining the most sophisticated proponent of the theory of understanding — **Weber** — shows that what he calls the *interpretation* of human action has almost nothing to do with causal explanation, let alone with covering laws.

**Distinction:** In his later methodological writings, especially *Economy and Society*, Weber developed an important distinction between `interpretation` and `explanation`. `Explanation` for Weber is essentially the causal explanation of human action: in principle he affirms, as much as any naturalist, that all human action can be causally explained, and even denies a principled difference between purposes and causes — a purpose is just "the representation of an effect that becomes the cause of an action"; reasons act as causes when they are motives of action. This makes Weber look like a positivist, and he is often read that way, but that is a serious mistake. It is clear from *Economy and Society* and his earlier methodological writings that Weber regarded causal explanation as a *necessary but not sufficient* condition of a complete understanding of human action: besides causal explanation there is `interpretation`. Explanation involves causal principles; interpretation involves the application and assessment of norms: explaining an action by its causes is one thing, assessing its rationality according to norms is another. Here Weber returns to the neo-Kantian distinction between the question of right and the question of fact. He asks us to imagine that psychology had advanced to the point where it could accurately determine the neural causes of deriving a mathematical theorem; this would never suffice to determine whether the theorem is true, because there is a principled difference between establishing causes and assessing validity, which no amount of causal explanation can bridge. As he put it in *Roscher and Knies*, there is absolutely no bridge from the purely empirical analysis of given reality by means of causal explanation to the affirmation or denial of the validity of any value judgement. The distinction between interpretation and explanation therefore has nothing to do with any ontological distinction — mind and matter, inner and outer, noumenon and phenomenon, original and copy: everything belonging to the realm of being can be explained by causal laws, while questions of value do not belong to any realm of being at all.

**Mechanism:** The distinction is commonplace today and is much discussed in contemporary debate; its ultimate source is Weber's neo-Kantian predecessors Windelband and Rickert. What was new in Weber's method was not the distinction itself but how he formulated it, and his specific account of normative interpretation: *the normative interpretation of an action involves hypothetically attributing a process of practical reasoning to the agent.* We assume that the agent acted rationally and try to reconstruct the action according to a practical syllogism.

**Example:** Weber's own example: to understand the battle of Königgrätz in the Austro-Prussian War of 1866 (where the Prussian army decisively defeated Austria, establishing Prussia's leading role in German unification), one must reconstruct the ideal strategies of both sides — of the Prussian commander Moltke and his Austrian opponent Benedek; that is, one must imagine how each would have acted had he fully known his own and his opponent's situation, had full control of his troops, and so on. On the basis of this reconstruction one can compare what should have happened with what actually happened, and so better determine the real causes of Moltke's victory and Benedek's defeat. The point is that understanding an action is a hypothetical reconstruction of the agent's reasoning, attributing to him a pattern of reasoning and seeing whether and how it conforms to determinate norms — in effect, following a practical syllogism.

**Distinction:** Practical reasoning is not causal explanation. It does presuppose the application of causal laws, but it is not the same as explaining an action by applying a universal law to it. When I reason "I want X; Y is the only means to X; therefore I should pursue Y", I assume that Y is a sufficient or necessary cause of X, but the reasoning itself differs from covering-law reasoning: "whenever condition X obtains, Y occurs; condition X now obtains; therefore Y will occur". Weber accordingly attacked all attempts to conflate interpretation with causal explanation as the old vice of `psychologism`: when I reconstruct an agent's reasoning and make it intelligible to myself, I do not thereby assume that the agent actually reasoned in this way, consciously or subconsciously; to assume so would be like confusing the laws of logic, which have only normative validity, with processes of reasoning themselves. Weber put this forcefully in *Economy and Society*: the meaning of a piece of reasoning is not "psychological"; that an agent rationally considered whether a given action was necessary for achieving an end is not made more intelligible by any psychological investigation.

**Influence:** The ultimate fruit of Weber's theory of interpretation is his famous theory of the `ideal type`, which has become a pillar of sociology. The theory is notoriously complex, partly because there are several kinds of ideal type; but one of its main forms is precisely the one that attributes practical reasoning to an agent.

**Conclusion:** Hence the third thesis: *the covering-law thesis is actually false, because it is not the only form of explanation in history.* If practical reasoning cannot be reduced to causal explanation — and it is hard to see how it could be — then covering-law theory fails even as an account of human action itself, let alone as a model for the unity of science.
