**Influence:** The theory of interpretation Weber had developed by 1914 was far ahead of its time. Collingwood, who knew little of the German tradition and knew it in a form distorted by Croce, arrived only in the 1940s at a position close to Weber's, yet with all the weaknesses of the earlier doctrine of understanding that Weber had seen and removed in the early 1900s. The rational core of Collingwood's theory was rescued in the 1960s and 1970s by analytic philosophers — Donagan, Dray, Rex Martin — with a normative conception close to the one Weber had already proposed in 1914. *This shows that when philosophers do not talk to one another and ignore past discussions, they often end up laboriously reinventing the wheel.*

**Conclusion:** History needs hermeneutics. If positivist illusions about what "science" must be are abandoned, and the hermeneutic endeavour is understood as a contingent and fallible pursuit of what is intersubjectively discernible, theoretical confidence can be restored to historical practice. Several points follow. The hermeneutic historicist aims to "give an account"; this is a cognitive, not a mystical, activity: it involves not re-living but *understanding* — the very meaning of *Verstehen*; this does not deny a role to imagination, but it is imagination tethered to interpretation, not unleashed as fantasy. Historical practice cannot do without empirical evidence and hence cannot do without some reference to reality, however problematic that reference may be in the absence of the past; still less can it do without some claim to coherence, to meaningful organization, which can be differentially assessed. `Objectivity` is always the achievement of a community of inquiry, not of a solitary interpreter: it is the individuality of each inquirer's perspective that prompts and shapes the account he submits to the community, but when a practising historian submits a construction to the discipline, he submits it not as subjective artistry but as an attempted interpretation of an apparently common object, to be assessed by shared standards of validity. Veracity and coherence are indispensable to historical practice, even if the standards of assessment are disciplinary rather than absolute. Much remains underdetermined, to be sure; but, as **Willard Van Orman Quine** showed, this is the condition of all empirical understanding.

## P5 How did suspicion become a method of interpretation?

### What does the "hermeneutics of suspicion" name, and what does it obscure?

**Definition:** In *Freud and Philosophy* (1965) and *The Conflict of Interpretations* (1969), **Paul Ricoeur** coined the phrase `hermeneutics of suspicion` for three radical nineteenth-century lines of thought: the theory of ideology of **Karl Marx**, the genealogical method of **Friedrich Nietzsche**, and the theory of the unconscious of **Sigmund Freud** (he occasionally added La Rochefoucauld and Feuerbach). Its defining feature is roughly the thesis that the *surface* meanings and thoughts a person expresses (and perhaps also aspects of behaviour that at first seem meaningless, such as Freud's slips of the tongue) often act as proxies for certain *deeper* meanings and thoughts — both standing for and concealing them; these deeper meanings are to some degree hidden (even from the person himself), differ greatly from the surface meanings and are often their opposite, and the person has some motive for concealing them in this way (from others and from himself).

**Example:** The three paradigms are: Marx's view that ideologies such as Christianity are rooted in class interests; Nietzsche's view that Christian morality, while ostensibly emphasizing "love" and "turning the other cheek", in fact springs from hatred and *ressentiment*; and Freud's view that a vast range of apparently meaningful or meaningless behaviour expresses unconscious motives and meanings. The reason for classing these theories as *hermeneutics* is that they not only provide deeper explanations of surface meanings and other phenomena, but explain them in terms of underlying *meanings*.

**Influence:** These theories really are major developments in hermeneutics, and Ricoeur deserves credit for coining a concept that draws attention to their striking generic similarities and their potential to deepen hermeneutics. The three, Ricoeur says, clear the horizon for a more authentic word, for a new reign of truth, not only through "destructive" critique but by inventing an art of *interpreting*; their distinctiveness is that the "suspicious" reverse the work of the "cunning" falsifier — first locating where phenomena have been deliberately concealed, then trying to remove the distortions. *Ricoeur's own attempt to incorporate psychoanalysis into his hermeneutics is of doubtful benefit to hermeneutics; his main achievement in this field is the naming itself.*

**Controversy:** The label can also mislead. First, its distinctiveness is questionable. In one sense Nietzsche plainly takes a suspicious attitude towards phenomena that other philosophers take at face value, rejecting received interpretations of morality, agency and metaphysics as superficial and in need of correction; but being suspicious of previous philosophical interpretations is hardly distinctive — presenting oneself as correcting all one's predecessors' errors is a perennial philosophical trope. Ricoeur's talk of correcting "deliberate falsification" is also an exaggeration: Nietzsche does sometimes suggest that he is revealing how phenomena have been *deliberately* misdescribed, but this occupies only a small part of his work (a few sections of the *Genealogy of Morality* and *The Antichrist*); by volume, far more of his writing corrects errors, exposes misunderstandings, proposes new ideals and diagnoses cultural pathologies, and, with a few key exceptions, Nietzsche thinks distortion and concealment are seldom *deliberate*.

Second, another common formulation is equally simplistic: the hermeneutics of suspicion is understood as a stance that disregards an agent's conscious understanding of a phenomenon and reveals its *real and conflicting* causes — "what I really want is not money but the love I never got as a child"; "survivors have no moral claim to an inheritance; the interests of the ruling class merely lead us to believe they do". Yet that professed motives often differ from attributed ones is age-old common sense: if theologians stressing hidden human sinfulness, philosophers worrying that apparently altruistic acts are selfish, economists distinguishing stated from revealed preferences and novelists whose characters lack self-knowledge all counted as practising the hermeneutics of suspicion, the concept would lose its meaning.

Third, Gardner points out that the goal of the hermeneutics of suspicion — decoding and debunking — presupposes that texts do not say what they mean, or mean more than they say; but where does this anticipatory "distrust" come from? It cannot be prompted by anything on the surface of the text, since the surface is supposedly self-concealing; some *pre-hermeneutic* assumption must already be in place. For Marx and Nietzsche such assumptions may not be hard to find; but psychoanalysis does not presuppose the *falsity* of the mental (as ordinarily understood); its motive is that ordinary psychology cannot make sense of certain things it confronts — which generates a need for explanation, not grounds for suspicion. On this point Gadamer's rejoinder seems right: Ricoeur has not marked out a distinct subclass of hermeneutics, because *every* hermeneutics must overcome suspicious consciousness and seek the *true* meaning of a discourse.

**Conclusion:** The hermeneutics of suspicion is a valuable generic label picking out a class of theories that explain surface meanings in terms of deeper meanings; but understanding it as "interpretation that disregards the conscious level" or as "revealing any hidden cause" misreads its most important representatives — as the case of Nietzsche shows.

### Nietzsche's perspectivism: is everything interpretation?

**Problem:** The word "hermeneutics" appears only three times in Nietzsche's notebooks and never in his published works; yet his writings are a rich source of ideas, concepts and arguments that intersect with the hermeneutic tradition. Hermeneutics can be broadly understood as the interpretation of what is meaningful, which raises two sets of questions: what is the *scope* of hermeneutics — to which objects should the interpretive stance be directed? And what is the *nature and method* of interpretation?

**Principle:** To the first question Nietzsche's answer seems to be: everything. Not only texts and works of art need interpreting, but almost the whole of human experience. Within a few dozen pages of *Beyond Good and Evil* he tells us that physics is "only" an interpretation of the world; that Descartes's "I think" contains an interpretation of a process that does not belong to the process itself; that philosophical concepts and "world-interpretations" are constrained and guided by the grammatical structures of the thinker's language; that modern morality represents a peculiarly narrow interpretation; and that the intention of an action is only a sign and symptom that itself needs interpreting. To the second question his answer is equally broad: the philosopher must once have been critic, sceptic, dogmatist, historian, poet, collector, traveller, riddle-solver, moralist, seer and "free spirit" — almost everything — in order to traverse the whole range of human values and value-feelings and to see with many different eyes and consciences. As he writes in the *Genealogy*, there is only a perspectival "knowing"; the more affects we allow to speak about a thing, the more eyes, different eyes, we can use to observe it, the more complete will our "concept" of it, our "objectivity", be. Hence the need resolutely to reverse habitual perspectives and valuations, to *want* to see differently — no small discipline and preparation of the intellect for its future "objectivity".

Nietzsche also wanted to show that many experiences that seem uninterpreted, seem simply given, have in fact already been interpreted, and that some of these interpretations are harmful. As he writes in *The Antichrist*: wherever the theologian's instinct extends, value judgements are inverted and the concepts "true" and "false" are necessarily reversed — what is most harmful to life is called "true", what elevates, affirms, justifies life and makes it triumph is called "false". Much of his work is devoted to this exposure.

**Mechanism:** The first feature of Nietzsche's interpretive stance is his denial that there is any uninterpreted given on which presuppositionless philosophical or scientific accounts could be built. He mocks the notion of "immediate certainty" and of a knowing that grasps its object purely and nakedly. Even our most basic relation to the world, sense perception, is mediated by value judgements: as he writes in the notebooks, there is no doubt that all sense perceptions are permeated with value judgements. The world presents itself as attractive and repulsive, useful and resistant, threatening and fascinating, and these evaluative features derive from its relation to our activities and interests; we cannot get behind them to some perspectiveless access to the world. The same holds for the self: section 16 of *Beyond Good and Evil* points out that analysing the process expressed in "I think" yields a series of bold, hard-to-prove and even unprovable assertions — that it is *I* who think, that there must be something that thinks, that thinking is an activity of a being conceived as a cause, that there is an "ego", and that it has already been settled what thinking is and that I know what it is. What the philosopher finds here is not immediate certainty but a series of metaphysical questions: where do I get the concept of a "thing"? Why do I believe in causality? By what right do I speak of an ego, even of an ego as cause, as cause of thoughts?

**Mechanism:** Nietzsche's rejection of immediate certainty rests on `holism`, in fact two kinds of holism. One is a holism about *meaning*: the meanings of concepts are interdependent. As he writes in the notebooks, an isolated judgement is never "true", never knowledge; only in the connection and relation of many judgements is there certainty. Section 20 of *Beyond Good and Evil* illustrates this with philosophical concepts: individual philosophical concepts are not arbitrary or autonomously evolving things but grow up in connection and relationship with one another; however suddenly and arbitrarily they seem to appear in the history of thought, they belong to a system, just as all the members of the fauna of a continent do. The other is a *methodological* holism about physical and social phenomena: they can be grasped and understood only in their historical and social contexts (see the next section).

**Principle:** Nietzsche's `perspectivism` is best understood as a development of Kant. Kant held that the way we know the world depends on the pure concepts of the understanding or categories — particular forms of our most basic concepts and their relations — and that these concepts and relations are uniform for all rational beings. Nietzsche accepts the Kantian thesis that concepts structure experience but rejects the view that there is only one set of concepts and conceptual relations necessarily imposed on experience; he holds that there are many different, mutually incompatible conceptual systems, and that we have no way of assessing them from an external neutral vantage point to determine which is best. That is, "perspectives" — sets of relatively basic concepts and conceptual relations — vary over historical time; perspectives include both classificatory and evaluative concepts; some perspectives can be shown to be internally inconsistent, to conceal phenomena revealed by others, and so on, but none can be shown to be the best.

Yet we take these perspectives to be direct, unbiased presentations of their objects and fail to see that they are local and contingent, because perspectives are embedded in our language. Nietzsche writes that words and concepts are the most obvious reason why we believe groups of actions to be isolated from one another: we do not merely *designate* things with them but originally think we grasp what is *true* in things through them; through words and concepts we are continually misled into imagining things to be simpler than they are, separate, indivisible, each existing in and for itself; a philosophical mythology lies concealed in *language*, which breaks out again at every moment, however careful one may be otherwise.

**Distinction:** Since experience is partly constituted by these perspectives, Nietzsche holds that understanding physical and social phenomena requires attending to the perspectives to which they belong. This is where he comes closest to the debates of the hermeneutic tradition. Around the turn of the twentieth century there was a sustained debate about whether the human sciences require methods different from those of the natural sciences, and representatives of hermeneutics such as Dilthey argued that the human sciences are distinctive because they concern meanings that natural-scientific methods would miss or conceal. Nietzsche did not enter this debate directly; he regarded the natural and human sciences as continuous — but not for the usual reason. Not because the human sciences need not concern themselves with meaning, but because *the natural sciences also concern meaning*. Helmholtz and others held that the human sciences deal with values while the natural sciences deal only with "dead, indifferent matter"; Nietzsche thought the natural sciences covertly concern values too: the supposedly value-neutral distinctions and concepts used by physicists presuppose and reinforce evaluative outlooks. As he writes in *Beyond Good and Evil*, it is perhaps just dawning on five or six minds that physics too is only an interpretation and arrangement of the world (to suit *us*) and *not* an explanation of the world; the "conformity of nature to law" of which physicists proudly speak exists only thanks to their interpretation and bad "philology" — it is no fact, no "text", but a naively humanitarian adjustment and distortion of meaning that makes ample concession to the democratic instincts of the modern soul: "everywhere equality before the law — nature is no different". In *The Gay Science* he criticizes the faith of many materialist natural scientists in a world that is supposed to have its equivalent and measure in human thought and valuation, a faith that the only legitimate interpretation of the world is one that permits counting, calculating, weighing, seeing and touching, and nothing more. *Whether or not Nietzsche's claims about physics hold up, they illustrate his general thesis: no set of concepts or distinctions is wholly free of evaluative implications and assumptions.*

**Mechanism:** Nietzsche extends his reflections on concepts and language to an analysis of conscious thought. Section 354 of *The Gay Science* says that human beings, like every living creature, think continually without knowing it; the thinking that rises to *consciousness* is only the smallest, most superficial and worst part of it, for only this conscious thinking *takes place in words, that is, in communication signs*, and this fact reveals the origin of consciousness; the development of language and the development of consciousness (not of reason, but only of reason's becoming conscious of itself) go hand in hand. Here Nietzsche claims that conscious thought is organized by language, that is, by concepts; but, as noted, the meanings of concepts are not transparent to agents, and we do not see how our thoughts are guided by linguistic structures and concepts. He therefore holds that conscious (linguistic, conceptual) experience distorts and falsifies non-conscious thought: different conceptual frameworks reveal different aspects of non-conscious thought, and none can present it as it is prior to conceptualization. The world of which we can become conscious is only a world of surfaces and signs, a generalized, "vulgarized" world; consciousness is a "simplifying apparatus" bringing great and thorough corruption, falsification, superficiality and generalization.

**Distinction:** This brings us back to the correction of the "hermeneutics of suspicion". There is something in Ricoeur's claim that Nietzsche decided to regard consciousness as a whole first of all as "false consciousness": Nietzsche writes that actions are *never* what they appear to be, that all actions are essentially unknown; that we are necessarily strangers to ourselves, we do not understand ourselves, we *must* misunderstand ourselves, and for us the law "each is furthest from himself" holds forever. But it would be wrong to take this to mean that Nietzsche treats non-conscious meanings as the *true* meanings and disregards conscious interpretation. Section 44 of *The Gay Science* says that it may be important to know the motives from which humanity has so far acted, but it may be even more essential to know the *belief* in this or that motive — what humanity has imagined and told itself to be the real lever of its conduct; for people's inner happiness and misery have come to them according to their belief in this or that motive, *not* through the actual motives, which are secondary. Section 58 goes further: what things are called is unspeakably more important than what they are; the reputation, name and appearance of a thing, its usual measure and weight — originally almost always a mistake and arbitrary — grow into the thing through belief in them and their growth from generation to generation, until they become its very body; what began as appearance almost always becomes essence in the end and *acts* as essence; it suffices to create new names, valuations and probabilities in order, in the long run, to create new "things".

**Conclusion:** Human beings are interpreting animals, and interpretations often distort their objects, but these distortions are not idle: they affect the nature of what is interpreted. Conceiving of oneself as a sinner does not make it so, but it changes one's relation to one's own activities, the emotions one experiences, the cultural institutions one participates in and the values one embraces. *Nietzschean suspicion, then, does not lift the veil and throw it away; it asks how the veil has grown into the face.* Moreover, Nietzsche is concerned not with arbitrary causal forces but with how *normatively characterized* phenomena — moralities, social practices, customs, ideals — are misunderstood by those who hold them; the most striking thing about his occasional physiological explanations of particular actions is precisely their emptiness.

### Genealogy and the assessment of perspectives

**Problem:** If there are no simply given phenomena, and concepts, propositions and even experiences have meaning only within the perspectives to which they belong, how are these phenomena to be understood? Nietzsche's answer is well known: one must practise `genealogy`.

**Genealogy:** Nietzsche credits this general thesis to Hegel. He calls Hegel one of only three Germans to have made a major philosophical contribution, praising his "astonishing stroke" of daring to teach that species concepts develop *out of one another*, breaking through all logical habits. In other words, Hegel saw that concepts are not fixed and unchanging but have a history, and that grasping a concept's meaning requires placing it within its own conceptual framework. (The other two are Kant, for discovering that concepts structure experience, and Leibniz, for discovering the unconscious.)

**Distinction:** There is a crucial difference between Nietzsche and Hegel. Hegel has a vindicatory story: by and large, inadequate conceptual frameworks are superseded by increasingly adequate ones; on one reading the process is rational — the felt inadequacy of a framework prompts the introduction of a new one that resolves its tensions or contradictions. Nietzsche rejects this picture: transitions between conceptual frameworks are largely non-rational, and we should expect neither a rational progression in them nor that the replacing perspective will resolve the tensions of the replaced one. In the *Genealogy* he examines the transition from ancient warrior morality to Judeo-Christian morality and traces it not to the conceptual inadequacy of the former but to a series of social and psychological factors — the desire for political power, the desire for a vindicatory self-conception, revenge fantasies against an oppressive ruling class, self-deception about agency. These factors are highly contingent and probably unrepeatable, so the account is quite local; and the replacing moral framework did not resolve the tensions of the earlier one — Nietzsche goes out of his way to present it as more conflicted, more distorted and less adequate.

**Example:** Genealogy reveals that transitions between conceptual frameworks, and their persistence, are explained by non-rational social and psychological processes; it also helps us grasp the phenomena themselves. Without genealogy we might mistake morality for a unified phenomenon whose parts cohere and which has a unified aim and meaning; Nietzsche presents it as a composite of disparate parts welded together only by historical accident. His famous discussion of "punishment" illustrates this: the "custom", "act" and "drama" of punishment are relatively constant across societies, while the "meaning", "purpose" and "expectation" attached to them are fluid. The concept "punishment" in fact represents not one meaning but a whole "synthesis" of meanings: the previous history of punishment, its use for the most diverse purposes, finally crystallizes into a kind of unity that is hard to disentangle, hard to analyse and completely indefinable; today it is impossible to say exactly why people are punished; all concepts in which a whole process is semiotically concentrated elude definition — *only that which has no history is definable*. The same holds for other social practices: closely examined, apparently unified and stable phenomena reveal ruptures, mixtures of loosely related purposes and grafts of unrelated practices. Nietzsche accordingly calls one of philosophers' dangerous errors their lack of historical sense and hatred of the idea of becoming, their "Egypticism": they think they honour a thing by dehistoricizing it, making a mummy of it; for millennia philosophers have handled only "conceptual mummies", and nothing real has escaped their grasp alive.

**Mechanism:** Suppose we have carried out a careful historical and genealogical study of a perspective and laid it out clearly — what then? Nietzsche suggests that clearly laying out a perspective sometimes enables us to criticize it, and this is his real concern: more important than the hypotheses themselves is the *value* of morality. The most defensible reading is roughly Hegelian: Nietzsche appeals only to `immanent critique`, that is, a perspective must be shown to be defective by standards its own inhabitants would accept; if the inhabitants of perspective B can be shown that they have reason to move to perspective A, then A is superior to B.

**Distinction:** This standard is often read purely epistemically: moving from B to A brings some cognitive gain — A resolves contradictions in which B is entangled, explains phenomena B conceals, dissolves tensions within B. The *Genealogy*, for example, argues that Judeo-Christian morality has inconsistent values and incoherent conceptions of agency and responsibility, which can be seen from within that perspective. But what of two incommensurable perspectives, each defective, neither able to resolve the other's tensions? This is roughly the situation the *Genealogy* leaves us with: Judeo-Christian morality leads to sickness and self-deception and impedes human flourishing, while the old warrior morality appears oppressive, shallow and a curse for most of humanity. Epistemic standards are not enough to support moving from one to the other.

**Mechanism:** The key is that Nietzsche also appeals to *evaluative* standards: some perspectives should be rejected because they damage or conflict with "will to power". He often says that a perspective is "hostile to life" or "contrary to health", and both "life" and "health" are defined by will to power: modern morality "negates life", impeding the highest power and splendour actually possible to the human type. On one reading, Nietzsche argues for a theory of agency on which every action aims at power; moral interpretations that conceal this connection or lead us away from the goal should therefore be rejected. Critique by the standard of power thus remains immanent critique, since the standard of will to power is supposedly present in every perspective.

**Condition:** Immanent critique can show some perspectives to be superior to others, but it will not yield a single ultimate perspective: there will be different, mutually incompatible but equally justified perspectives. There are better and worse perspectives, but no reason to believe there is a *best* one. Such critique is also always historically situated: we do not generate and assess perspectives from nowhere but start from our own and move on from there (or not).

**Distinction:** Nietzsche advocates no particular hermeneutic procedure; apart from rejecting unhistorical methods, he always treats his own hypotheses as provisional. After giving his genealogy from Homeric to Judeo-Christian morality, he adds a note proposing a series of academic prize essays on the history of morality, open to philologists, historians, professional philosophers, physicians and physiologists, as though such studies might replace his own. Only a few generalizations can be made: he is concerned with normatively characterized phenomena, in which agents adopt norms, values and normative concepts such as obedience and guilt, and in which the agents' own perspectives help determine what the practices are; he seeks not the *best* interpretation but *better* ones — interpretations that reveal what another conceals, account for factors it neglects, understand its contradictions, reveal hidden connections and better understand agents' motives. Someone who knows a domain is someone who has a systematic understanding of a range of related facts and sees how they are connected; these connections are often not logical entailments, and seeing them requires historical sensitivity and interpretive skill.

**Influence:** These reflections help in understanding the best-known part of Nietzsche's philosophy: the death of God and the coming nihilism. Nietzsche sees human beings as fundamentally driven by one desire: the desire for interpretations that make experience meaningful. *The Birth of Tragedy* says that the Greeks felt the terror of existence and knew that their whole existence rested on a hidden substratum of suffering; to be able to bear it, they interposed between themselves and life the radiant dream-birth of the Olympians, seducing them to go on living. *The Gay Science* opens with the "teachers of the purpose of existence": the founders of moralities and religions proclaim that "life is worth living" and make what happens necessarily and purposelessly appear to have been done for a purpose. The *Genealogy* ends: man did not know how to justify himself, *he suffered from the problem of his meaning*; he did not deny suffering, he even sought it, provided he was shown a meaning for it; *the meaninglessness of suffering, not suffering itself, was the curse that lay over humanity*.

Nietzsche fears that we stand on the eve of a crisis: the systems that used to meet this need are becoming untenable. We have begun to value truth for its own sake; once this will to truth is strong enough, it hollows out the supposed grounds of our traditional values — the whole of European morality stands on the brink of collapse; the deeper one looks, the more our valuations vanish, and meaninglessness approaches. When ideals collapse we will enter a nihilistic phase, of which the relevant kind here is `anomie`: social pressure, custom and emotional attachment may keep us clinging to some values for a while, but they lack coherent justification and are no longer seen as overriding and authoritative, only as optional and dispensable. Nietzsche therefore tries out new interpretations: limiting the will to truth, an aesthetic justification of the world, affirming the eternal recurrence of one's life, creating new ideals. The common thread is to find ways of preserving meaning-giving interpretation under historical conditions that have made it questionable.

**Influence:** Simmel called Nietzsche the Copernicus of philosophical ethics. Those he influenced include almost every important German and French philosopher of the twentieth century, many of whom played an active role in the hermeneutic tradition: Adorno, Camus, Deleuze, Foucault, Heidegger, Horkheimer, Jaspers, Sartre, Scheler, Weber and others. **Michel Foucault** is especially close to Nietzsche: he shows that concepts, institutions and values regarded as necessary and fixed are historically contingent and fluid; that supposedly universal truths about human nature are contingent expressions of particular cultural valuations; and his critiques often reveal harmful and oppressive features of present conditions that only long and patient study can uncover. The differences are also clear: first, historical detail and erudition — Nietzsche spends a few pages on changes in the forms and meanings of punishment, Foucault more than three hundred pages, with detailed evidence, on the emergence of the modern penal system; second, Foucault's distinction between "archaeology" and "genealogy": archaeology reveals principles that operate unconsciously within a particular *episteme* and constrain thought, while the later genealogy shows the *effects of these contingent principles on the present* and tries to unsettle them.

### The double language of psychoanalysis: meaning and force

**Problem:** The relationship between hermeneutics and `psychoanalysis` is close but uneasy, and the crux lies in the distinctive and peculiar combination of conceptual commitments in psychoanalytic explanation. Freud gave the practice of interpretation a dominant role, sharply distinguishing psychoanalysis from other schools of psychology; but he also denied that mental life consists of meaning through and through. Interpretation may be the "royal road" to a new domain of psychological facts and a new therapeutic technique, but psychoanalysis does not confine itself to meanings and their relations.

**Mechanism:** This duality reflects another ambiguity. The primary explananda of psychoanalysis — dreams, symptoms, slips, affective disorders, perversions — lie precisely on the border between the personal and the sub-personal level: they belong within the orbit of a person's life and are not external in the way neural processes or the modules of cognitive science are; yet they mark the points where self-consciousness gives way to motivational forces that are no longer clearly personal but animal or even mechanical. Freud therefore requires a special receptive stance towards one's own mental life, suspending the stamp of the reflective personality in order to release the raw material from which unconscious meaning can be extracted.

**Example:** Freud's pioneering hypothesis about the meaning of dreams posits two processes, `condensation` (*Verdichtung*) and `displacement` (*Verschiebung*). They can be loosely compared with poetic-linguistic operations such as metaphor; but on Freud's account they also involve concepts of mental *force* and mental *location* — the "economic" and "topographical" points of view. The "dream-work" transforms latent content into manifest content, which means that thoughts and meanings, besides playing their familiar rational roles, are objects of quasi-mechanical operations in the mind. Behind the dream-work lie broader principles of unconscious mental life, which Freud collectively calls the `primary process`: the pleasure principle and the characteristics of the "system Ucs.", such as timelessness and the absence of contradiction. These assumptions are strictly necessary for his dream interpretation: without a mental apparatus that organizes thoughts by principles impossible in self-conscious thinking and shapes mental content independently of any purpose the agent could acknowledge, the lateral connections Freud traces between words, ideas and memory images would not add up, nor could the dream be conceived as an essential unity of latent and manifest content — translatable into each other like texts and regarded as expressions of affect and desire.

**Influence:** Individual psychopathology, and phenomena such as dreams that ordinary psychological understanding can describe but not explain, were where psychoanalysis first gained explanatory power, but they did not exhaust its scope. Having established what he took to be the basic principles of mental functioning, Freud turned to social and cultural life. Its distinctive features here are: (1) showing that socio-cultural phenomena are deeply rooted in mental life, so that social practices can maintain and reproduce themselves in ways public reason cannot explain; (2) making intelligible not only normal but also pathological social forms. This is why Freud attracted the critical theorists — Fromm, Horkheimer, Adorno, Marcuse, Honneth — who drew on psychoanalysis to address ideological subjection, voluntary servitude, fascism, alienation and related problems.

**Distinction:** On the one hand, psychoanalysis appears as an ally of hermeneutics: it defends and elaborates the concepts of meaning and understanding, showing their scope to be wider than previously thought and so resisting reductive naturalism; by contrast, a psychology that merely breaks ordinary psychological understanding down into functional elements has no comparable hermeneutic interest. On the other hand, it poses a challenge, because it implies that meaning and understanding are not as self-sufficient as strong hermeneuticists such as Gadamer held. Even in dealing with the most typically meaning-constituted objects — art, religion, normative social bonds — psychoanalysis aims to show that they ultimately derive from a source that cannot secure its own intelligibility in the way *Bildung*, tradition or objective spirit can. Freud had no general theory of interpretation, nor did he arrive at his idea of interpretation by reflecting on understanding as such. He valued language in only two respects — as the mechanism and mark of consciousness, and as the sole medium of the therapeutic technique; he conceived therapy as aimed at the simple hedonic goal of relieving suffering, and self-understanding as the ultimate goal of therapy was not his own idea.

**Controversy:** One conclusion is that psychoanalysis and hermeneutics are in fact irreconcilable: the lasting significance of psychoanalysis lies in showing that hermeneutic concepts are explanatorily shallow compared with robust causes that can be identified physically. Freud's self-professed positivism lends some support to this, but he also denied direct access to the organic substrate of mental activity, and in *The Interpretation of Dreams* made clear that his explanatory aims presupposed the autonomy of the mental, at least as a methodological principle. Proponents of the natural-scientific reading admit that their approach tends to reduce psychoanalysis to a precursor of cognitive science and neuropsychology, but argue that the hermeneutic reading does worse at salvaging its truth content: by separating meaning from the control of causal reality, hermeneuticists supposedly cannot explain what the correctness of a psychoanalytic interpretation consists in beyond subjective satisfaction. Hence the proposal to split Freud's legacy in two: a genuine scientific research programme, which has largely left Freud's ideas behind; and a loosely formulated psychological wisdom, of humane value but lacking strict truth and subject to scientific regulation.

**Rejoinder:** This charge rests on a confusion. What the hermeneutic reading assumes is only that interpretation provides psychoanalysis with its core means of delineating mental phenomena — an epistemological claim to which questions of underlying causal ontology are secondary or even irrelevant; to refute it, one would have to damage the whole of ordinary psychology as well. Some hermeneutic readers do question the general metaphysics of scientific realism, but the fact that psychoanalysis sharply raises the problem of the relation between meaning and causation does not mean that it must contain the resources to solve that wholly general philosophical problem. The real question is whether psychoanalytic interpretations are arbitrary in ways that ordinary psychological interpretations are not; the critics have not shown this.

**Counterexample:** Early criticisms by Sartre and Wittgenstein identified, from different angles, the same deep conceptual problem in hermeneutically construed psychoanalysis. **Jean-Paul Sartre**, in his 1939 theory of the emotions, argued that psychoanalysis treats a conscious phenomenon as the symbolic fulfilment of a desire repressed by the censorship; but for consciousness this desire is not implied in its symbolic fulfilment — if we had even implicit consciousness of our real desire we would be in "bad faith", which is not what the psychoanalyst means. The signification of the conscious act is thus entirely external to the act itself; the relation of the conscious fact to what it signifies is like that of the ashes of a fire on a mountain to the person who lit it, whose presence is not contained in the ashes. Freud wanted to conceive the conscious phenomena he interpreted as essentially unified with their underlying unconscious motives, as ordinary mental life presents intelligible wholes; but this is impossible, Sartre argues, because the essential principle of any such unity — the subject's "taking X to mean Y" — is necessarily absent; were it present, we would have pretence or bad faith. In *Being and Nothingness* Sartre argued further that Freud uses two inconsistent models of the subject at once: one affirming the absolute unity of the subject, at work when Freud asserts that X *means* Y; the other distributing the subject's contents among several mental parts, with only a "many" and no unifying "one", at work when Freud explains what X's meaning Y *consists in*. Sartre did not think Freud's enterprise failed wholesale: psychoanalysis revealed the paradoxical character of human motivation, an explanandum that gave Sartre's own "existential psychoanalysis" its theme.

**Ludwig Wittgenstein**, in conversations of the 1940s, focused on two points in *The Interpretation of Dreams*: first, the question of criteria — when should interpretation stop, and what entitles one interpretation to exclude others? Second, whether the concept of meaning can coherently be applied to dreams at all, together with the ideas of dreams as thoughts, as using symbols and as fulfilling wishes. He noted that Freud's dream symbols lie between the natural and the conventional: a symbol that is not understood seems not to be a symbol at all; but if I accept an interpretation and superimpose it on the dream, I can say "the table obviously corresponds to that woman". This might be seen as an indirect way of justifying talk of dream symbols — the meaning of a dream as a function of later reflection on it. What Wittgenstein made clear was that dreams do invite interpretation, but what fundamentally underpins Freud's ideas is a demand for complete explanation, a belief that mental events are fully determined; only this philosophical prejudice could justify his auxiliary ideas, especially the "mental forces" supporting the concept of wish fulfilment. The result is a compromised style of theorizing: Freud was drawn to the possibility of a deeper understanding of the mind not by experiment but by the kind of non-propositional pattern-making familiar from aesthetic contexts (dream interpretation resembling judgements of correctness in interpreting music or painting); but he tried to turn his interpretive proposals into *doctrine*, and so had to turn to scientism to underwrite speculations that outran their grounds, ending with a powerful "mythology".

**Rejoinder:** The force of Sartre's argument is limited not by his Cartesian commitment to mental transparency (which he suspended for the sake of argument) but by his strong assumption about the mind's "architecture": he assumes that mental life is fully determinate and mental connections fully transitive, as though the whole mind were assimilated to a single practical syllogism; ordinary psychology does not agree, and Freud can point out that Sartre question-beggingly relies on a rationalist or Kantian idealization of the mental. The reply to Wittgenstein is that a conception of meaning requiring symbols to be regarded merely as *products* of interpretive activity is too strict, too driven by empirical anchoring, and gives sceptical worries too much weight. If the task of psychoanalysis were to establish mental ascriptions from scratch, or to make them criterially secure against other-minds scepticism, it would indeed violate Wittgenstein's conditions; but psychoanalysis accepts the ascriptions of ordinary psychology, neither questioning nor re-justifying them, and aims to elaborate, deepen and build on them; there is no reason why this "second wave" of interpretation must be governed by the same rules as the ground floor of interpersonal understanding. *These replies weaken the Sartre–Wittgenstein objection without fully answering its challenge: they show only that certain strong conceptions of psychological understanding must be rejected before psychoanalytic inquiry can begin, not what alternative conception could positively serve Freud's purposes.*

### Is the unconscious privatized language or psychical reality?

**Distinction:** **Jürgen Habermas**'s *Knowledge and Human Interests* and Ricoeur's *Freud and Philosophy*, both published in the 1960s, are landmarks of the philosophy of psychoanalysis, but they arose from different backgrounds: Habermas belonged to the Frankfurt School, Ricoeur to Husserlian phenomenology. Their main contrast is this: Habermas tries to turn psychoanalysis into an unambiguously hermeneutic discourse, eliminating its ambiguity altogether; Ricoeur places the double commitment of psychoanalysis in a wider philosophical context, so that what looks like a problem peculiar to psychoanalysis is seen as the recurrence of a more fundamental philosophical opposition, which Freud cannot be expected to resolve on his own.

**Mechanism:** Habermas's discussion is explicitly reconstructive: sifting out what is defensible in Freud and discarding what belongs to his "scientistic self-misunderstanding" (on this point he agrees with Sartre and Wittgenstein). But this does not mean rejecting metapsychology; on the contrary, Habermas finds in it the deepest point of contact with his project of reconstructing critical theory. Metapsychology, he says, is "a general interpretation of self-formative processes": the model of the three agencies — id, ego, superego — allows the structures of linguistic deformation and behavioural pathology to be systematically presented. His reconstruction takes `communicative action` as the measure of rationality and gives priority to the dialogical situation: one person cannot make himself understood to another, and what is artificially reproduced in the therapeutic situation is that the inability to make oneself understood to others corresponds to, and arises from, an avowed inability to make oneself understood to oneself. Habermas's key claim is not only that intrasubjective disturbance can be modelled *on* intersubjective breakdown, but that it *consists in* communicative failure: abandoning a realist conception of the unconscious as a set of phenomena defined by the difficulties they cause for cognition, and regarding it instead as *constituted by* failed communicative action.

The object domain of `depth hermeneutics`, he writes, comprises all those places where, because of internal disturbances, the texts of our everyday language games are interrupted by incomprehensible symbols — incomprehensible because they do not obey the grammatical rules of ordinary language, norms of action and culturally learned patterns of expression. Symptoms are signs of a specific self-alienation of the subject; the gaps in the text are places where an interpretation produced by the self, yet alien to it, has forcibly prevailed; because the symbols interpreting repressed needs are excluded from public communication, *the communication of the speaking and acting subject with itself is interrupted* — the privatized language of unconscious motives becomes inaccessible to the ego. He even thinks the psychoanalytic search for general laws — the feature that makes its discourse most like scientific causal hypotheses — can be salvaged: these laws supply a "narrative background" or "dramatic model" with reference to which an interrupted self-formative process can be completed into a whole history.

**Definition:** For Habermas, the unconscious need not generate meaning *ex nihilo*, as Sartre and Wittgenstein assumed; it distorts meanings already in circulation. He can therefore agree with Sartre that Freud's partition of the mind fails to elucidate intrasubjective incoherence, while maintaining that its correct explanation lies in distorted self-communication; and he can reply to Wittgenstein that the usual conditions of meaning are indeed unmet, not because language is absent but because it is in a defective state. It follows that apparently pure forces in mental life are only apparently so, consisting really in a failure or refusal to articulate meaning; the act of repression is reduced to "the banishment of the need-interpreting", "the splitting-off of individual symbols from public communication". Habermas thus holds a `privative` conception of the unconscious. *This is the root of his disagreement with Ricoeur. Lacan challenges Habermas from another side: he agrees that language is the key to the unconscious, but holds that human beings are necessarily alienated in and by language — entering the "symbolic order" and joining in communication with others, the subject at the same time splits itself.*

**Mechanism:** Ricoeur's discussion has three salient points: (1) placing psychoanalysis alongside Marx and Nietzsche as hermeneutics of suspicion (discussed above); (2) claiming that psychoanalysis has direct philosophical significance; (3) attributing to Freud a non-hermeneutic "realism". Freud's ideas strike directly at two high-level assumptions of modern philosophy: that consciousness and self-consciousness are irreducible, and that the individual human subject possesses deep autonomy and a privileged place in philosophical reflection. Ricoeur places psychoanalysis on the same level as Descartes and Kant: reading Freud is also a crisis of the philosophy of the subject, demanding a "dispossession" of the subject as it first appears to itself in the form of consciousness; it makes consciousness not a given but a task; the true *cogito* must be won through the false *cogito* that masks it.

Where does this "dispossession" lie? In what Ricoeur calls Freud's `realism`: by characterizing the unconscious economically and topographically, Freud attributes to the mental the same *degree* of reality as external physical objects, and a partly similar *kind*. At the least, some of the entities that make up the mind are unconsciously experienced as quasi-objects — they have, *for the subject*, the features picked out by the economic-topographical description. The image of the unconscious that Habermas eliminates — as composed of a kind of affective matter — is thereby restored: mental opacity differs from the obscurity of a text, because the mind is depicted as *opposed to itself*, which is quite different from mere cognitive deficiency. On this basis Ricoeur can say that consciousness discovers its immediate self-certainty to be only presumed, and must lose itself in order to find the subject.

**Distinction:** Ricoeur does not give Freud a one-sided victory over traditional philosophy. His claim is that the `antinomy` between the objective reality of the mental and the subjectivity of self-consciousness manifests itself *within* psychoanalysis — this is what its mixed discourse commits it to. As to what to do once the antinomy is acknowledged, his answer is that it has no purely theoretical solution but is a task for reflective practice, guided by a "limit idea" of its overcoming. Its radicalism lies here: the usual discussion assumes that psychoanalysis poses a problem requiring theoretical solution; Ricoeur suggests that what appears in reflection as a *conceptual* problem of its own actually represents a *real opposition* in its object — not merely that phenomena have first-order properties of different kinds, but that this heterogeneity reflects a deeper division in our constitution. The riddle of Freud's discourse thus becomes a constitutive "paradox of reflection", rooted in the nature of human existence.

**Evidence:** Two considerations support this proposal. First, it will appeal to anyone who shares the claim of classical German philosophy that human reality stands under a total opposition of freedom and nature, spirit and nature — though this runs against the mainstream of philosophical hermeneutics, which largely follows Herder in regarding Kant's dualism as a mistake. Second, the antinomy seems confirmable a posteriori: the ambiguity of the primary explananda of psychoanalysis contains an internal discordance — the reflective perplexity of the "Rat Man" or "Wolf Man" involves an active grasp of inner incoherence, not merely a failure of rational capacity.

**Mechanism:** Returning to Freud himself shows more clearly the philosophical factors driving his theory-building. Two nineteenth-century legacies lie behind his innovation: classical German philosophy supplied the bare concept of a dynamic unconscious; empirical psychology (widely, though not universally, accepted in the late nineteenth century) supplied the idea that ascriptions of particular unconscious mental states could have strict empirical grounds. In the metapsychological papers of 1915, Freud presented the possibility of a psychoanalytic science as hinging on an essentially simple consideration: our right to posit unconscious mental states is disputed, but the posit is both *necessary* and *legitimate*, because the data of consciousness have a great many `gaps`. Once unconscious mental states are granted to be conceptually possible, the evident gaps in consciousness give sufficient reason to posit causes outside consciousness; these causes must be mental rather than physical, because they serve to restore the continuity of consciousness, which descriptions of brain and physiological states cannot do; but since conscious contents themselves cannot fill in these links or explain why the gaps exist, the interpolated causes must have their own nature and causality — the mental has several varieties, of which the familiar conscious instance is only one.

**Distinction:** Freud was confident he had shown the concept of unconscious mental states to be unproblematic, though it had previously been fiercely disputed. The only philosopher who had a direct personal influence on him and whom he admired, **Franz Brentano**, explicitly rejected the concept as both unnecessary and incoherent. Brentano's *Psychology from an Empirical Standpoint* (1874) aimed to make psychology autonomous and raise its scientific standing, holding that psychology should occupy a foundational place in the order of justification of human knowledge. His definition of mental phenomena is often said to be `intentionality`, but strictly that is inaccurate: he defined mental phenomena as having both intentionality and *real*, not merely phenomenal, existence, and supported this psychological realism by our certainty about our own mental states — all other sciences depend on theoretical *inference* from empirical data (the merely phenomenal data of the outer senses) to their *unobservable* causes, whereas psychology reaches explanatorily ultimate reality merely by *describing* what is perceived (the data of inner consciousness). Accordingly he attacked at length the hypothesis of unconscious mental phenomena, holding that talk of them can be fully reduced to talk of dispositions of conscious states.

When Freud asserted the existence of unconscious mental states, he explicitly understood them in an unqualifiedly realist way; this is crucial, because a dispositional analysis would have strangled psychoanalytic theorizing at birth, making it impossible to posit a source of mental agency independent of the conscious subject. Freud therefore abandoned Brentano's thesis of the explanatory ultimacy of consciousness, holding that our epistemic relation to inner psychical reality and to outer physical reality is *symmetrical*. Brentano had argued that if the principles of inference leading from sense impressions to physical substances and forces were reproduced inwardly, what we would be led to, if anything, would be something not only unconscious but *non-mental*: if inner perception were inner *observation*, the phenomena observed would no longer be mental; the symmetry of inner and outer perception would reduce mental phenomena, like physical ones, to "signs" of the reality producing them. *Brentano's objection has the same structure as Sartre's: both hold that loosening the relation between the mental and its presentation in consciousness leads to absurdity.*

**Mechanism:** Freud's reply — where his hermeneutic side shows itself — is that Brentano held a mistaken or at least incomplete picture of what it means for something to be "given" in consciousness. Conscious mental states are not without a quasi-perceptual aspect, but where this acquaintance is present it necessarily involves understanding: a feeling of joy or sorrow involves taking this mental event as having that determinate meaning. If this is right, then in interpretively extrapolating from conscious data to the unconscious we do not leave the domain of psychology. It is reasonable to suppose that different kinds of mentality have different proportions of interpretable content and perceptual presence, and that the mental states making up the contents of the "system Ucs." lie at one end of this spectrum — they interpret self and world in ways that preclude their own direct manifestation or reflective recovery. They do present objects (more or less phantasmatic) to the subject, but they *themselves* do not become objects of higher-order presentation and so are accompanied by no *judgement*. These complex features are not shared by all mentality; Brentano built them into his analysis because he conflated the general reality of the mental with the certainty of its conscious instances. For Freud, a correct psychological realism leads to the unconscious by default.

**Example:** Freud's short 1925 paper "Negation", in which he implicitly continues his dialogue with Brentano, shows the side of his realism that departs from the hermeneutic stance. He begins with recurrent clinical material: a patient producing an association in analysis says, "Now you'll think I mean to say something insulting, but really I've no such intention", which Freud takes as the repudiation, by projection, of an idea that has just come up. Hence the interpretation: the content of a repressed image or idea can make its way into consciousness on condition that it is *negated*; negation is a way of taking cognizance of what is repressed, indeed already a lifting of the repression, though not, of course, an acceptance of what is repressed. Then comes a speculative hypothesis about judgement itself: affirming or negating the content of thought is the task of the intellectual function of judgement; a negative judgement is, at bottom, saying "this is something I would rather repress"; it is the intellectual substitute for repression, its "no" the hallmark of repression, a certificate of origin — like "Made in Germany"; by means of the symbol of negation, thinking frees itself from the restrictions of repression and enriches itself with material indispensable to its proper functioning. Judgement is the purposive continuation of the original process by which the ego, following the pleasure principle, took things into itself or expelled them; the polarity of judgement corresponds to the opposition of the two groups of drives — affirmation, as a substitute for uniting, belongs to Eros; negation, as the successor to expulsion, belongs to the destructive drive.

**Distinction:** Freud agrees with Brentano that affirmation and denial in judgement are acts of acceptance and rejection, continuous with the affects of love and hate; but he then departs from Brentano in proposing that (1) judgemental and conative-affective negation (and affirmation) are *the same* kind of mental act; (2) the judgemental form *depends on* and derives from its original conative-affective form; (3) both have a single *source*, which can be conceived at the level of (phantasies of) bodily processes or at the level of drives. The hypothesis is attractive because it deepens the original interpretation and coheres with the rest of psychoanalytic theory; it is especially significant in showing Freud trying to "get behind meaning": he does not think that the logical function of negation itself poses a problem of understanding requiring a psychologistic solution; his aim is fundamental insight into the "proper functioning" of conscious intellectual life — a unified concept of what grounds and *contains* meaning. Freud's ultimate theoretical concern is thus the "shape" of the `mental apparatus`, guided by his realism; grasping it, we would break out of the hermeneutic circle, or at least reach its outermost ring — the unmoved mover of the mental. This might also resolve the Sartre–Wittgenstein problem: if Freud's conjectures about the underlying shape of the mind allow us to conceive *new ways* in which a subject can mean something, his claims should not be measured by any previously formed concept of meaning; the "depth" interpretations he offers do not merely extend the scope of hermeneutics — their founding claim is that the mind *has* depth in a previously unrecognized sense.

**Controversy:** One might object that Freud ultimately remains within meaning, since his life and death drives have "aims", and the bodily processes and phantasies expressing them have a rudimentary teleological organization. This raises a difficult and broad question about the boundary between meaning and its other, on which hermeneutic thinkers differ. Here too a possibility arises that Ricoeur mentions but does not develop: if drives and bodily life not only ground and contain meaning but are themselves saturated with it, meaning extends all the way down into nature — as the *Naturphilosophie* of the late eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries held. In sections 5 and 6 of *Beyond the Pleasure Principle* (1920), Freud shows himself willing to entertain and defend this plainly *naturphilosophisch* idea: our drives are inherent in organic life itself, even in the very differentiation of organic from inorganic matter. He thereby distances himself from hermeneutic orthodoxy; but one may also say that he returns to an early, later abandoned juncture in the history of hermeneutics: in 1808 **Friedrich Ast**, deeply influenced by Schelling, proclaimed the unity of the modern hermeneutic project with *Naturphilosophie* — without the unity and identity of everything spiritual, without the original unity of all things in spirit, no understanding or interpretation of the alien would be possible. *In his speculative moments, Freud agrees with Ast: to understand human beings, one must give an interpretation of nature as a whole.*

**Conclusion:** Here the hermeneutics of suspicion reveals its deepest challenge to hermeneutics. Nietzsche shows that interpretations at the level of consciousness, though distorting, help constitute their objects, so suspicion cannot simply discard the surface; psychoanalysis shows that interpretation may have to presuppose something non-interpretive — force, location, drive — for meanings to add up. Habermas tries to dissolve these non-hermeneutic elements into distorted communication, preserving the self-sufficiency of hermeneutics; Ricoeur acknowledges an irreducible antinomy and hands it over to reflective practice. *This disagreement will return in P13: it is precisely on the model of psychoanalysis that Habermas argues that hermeneutics needs a critical vantage point beyond tradition.*

## P6 Why is understanding a way of being? Heidegger's hermeneutics of facticity

### Life is already meaningful: facticity and hermeneutic intuition

**Background:** Heidegger's intellectual legacy remains deeply controversial, and not only because of his involvement in the cultural politics of the Third Reich; but he was undoubtedly a giant of twentieth-century hermeneutics. Working in an atmosphere shaped by the historicist tradition, he creatively transformed the thought of Dilthey, Husserl, Nietzsche and others, developing a new, *phenomenological* conception of hermeneutics that profoundly influenced some of the century's most important figures. In 1919 he named his philosophical approach "phenomenological hermeneutics", and went on to develop a "hermeneutics of facticity" — familiar to readers of his magnum opus *Being and Time* (1927) as the "analytic of Dasein". A minimum of background is needed: **Edmund Husserl**'s phenomenology understood the `horizon` (*Horizont*) as the margin of meaning, open to further unfolding, within which every object of experience stands, and analysed inner time-consciousness into the `retention` of what has just passed and the `protention` of what is about to come; Heidegger and Gadamer both transformed these concepts. The word "hermeneutics" disappeared from Heidegger's philosophical vocabulary soon after *Being and Time*, but its basic ideas and practices did not; and it was during this most explosively creative period in the early 1920s that Gadamer witnessed the formation of the new hermeneutics: in autumn 1922 Natorp showed him the "Natorp Report" Heidegger had written in applying for a professorship; in summer 1923 he attended all of Heidegger's courses, including the lecture course "Ontology: The Hermeneutics of Facticity" and the "Phenomenological Exercises for Beginners in Connection with Aristotle's *Nicomachean Ethics*".

**Definition:** Most accounts hold that by 1919 at the latest Heidegger had discovered the philosophical project that, in various forms, would occupy him for the rest of his life. Over the years he used many names for his theme: factical life, life-experience, Dasein, human being-there, the worlding of the world, Being itself, the truth of Being, the fourfold, the place of Being. What these names try to capture is the most basic level of human experience, where intelligibility is first constituted and made possible. For Heidegger this deep structure is a complex pattern of historically evolving ways of dwelling within a network of `significance` (*Bedeutsamkeit*); the pattern is itself made up of category-like structures — the "existentials" familiar to readers of *Being and Time* — which make all meaningful dealings with things possible. These structures are not concepts or schemata by which a subject organizes experience, nor the "synthetic forms of consciousness" of the neo-Kantianism then dominant in German universities; they "live in life itself" originally, so as to "form" life according to themselves.

**Principle:** This leads to Heidegger's deepest philosophical conviction: life itself is *not* irrational — which, he adds pointedly, has nothing to do with "rationalism". That is, life as a whole is already a meaningful configuration (*Gestalt*) long before the theoretical attitude usually adopted by scientists and philosophers arises. Life is *always already* meaningful, and we quite literally *find* ourselves involved in it; only because of this can we analyse it scientifically, shape it through our activities and express it in art. Every "life-world", every concrete form of life in general, has its own "determinations of meaning". For Heidegger, ordinary human life is meaningful, and this meaning is *not conceptual*.

**Problem:** Why call the project of articulating this basic level of meaning `hermeneutics`? Heidegger's explanations are often fragmentary or provisional. In the 1919 "War Emergency Semester" lectures he says: the life-experience that grasps life and is carried by it is an understanding intuition, a `hermeneutic intuition`, the original phenomenological back-and-forth formation of concepts from which all theoretical objectification and all transcendent positing fall away. He does not pause to explain why this intuition is "hermeneutic". *Perhaps the thought is this: just as a serious reader immerses himself in the work he reads, trying to let it speak in its own way without distorting it by his expectations, so one who investigates the original level of human existence must immerse himself in his theme and articulate it, so to speak, "from within", without committing in advance to what will emerge.* The 1921 summer lectures on Augustine's *Confessions* call these categorial structures "hermeneutic concepts", again without explanation. The 1923 summer lectures are relatively explicit: in section 2 he sketches the history of hermeneutics and, as Gadamer would later do, goes back to an earlier period — especially the theology of Augustine, the Church Fathers and Luther — when hermeneutics was seen in a comprehensive and living way. Despite the inadequacy of the name, he says, with respect to its "object" it indicates that this object is something that can be interpreted and needs to be interpreted, and that being in some state of having-been-interpreted belongs to its very being; one must understand from facticity itself in what way and when it demands the interpretation in question; and such interpretation is possible because "interpretation is itself a being of factical life". This hermeneutics is therefore *phenomenological*: an interpretation that lets its theme show itself according to its own meaning.

**Condition:** Heidegger here advances a thesis that anticipates several of Gadamer's key insights: the possibility of hermeneutic error belongs in principle to its ownmost being; the evidence of its explications is fundamentally "labile"; and to demand of it an extreme ideal of evidence such as "intuition of essences" is to misunderstand what it can and should do. In this period he repeatedly stressed a key feature of the new hermeneutics: the categorial structures of life do not emerge from observing the life-world from a detached, neutral, uncommitted standpoint; hermeneutics participates in the "historical self". This means in part that, as in the traditional art of textual interpretation, one must form an initial hypothesis about the meaning of what one encounters. The 1923 lectures say that the initial framework proposed at the start of inquiry, the "as what", is like a card staked in a game — an "opening move". Crowell's research points out that the Heideggerian phenomenologist must stake himself, must invest himself in the work of articulating the deepest structures of life. Heidegger usually calls this the `hermeneutic situation` of any phenomenological attempt: the complex of cultural tendencies, contemporary problems and ways of seeing the past, individualized in a deeply personal way by the phenomenologist himself. More fundamentally, hermeneutics helps realize facticity's own tendency to understand and speak about itself, rather than imposing some conceptual framework "from above", which would only alienate facticity from itself.

**Mechanism:** The "Natorp Report" of late 1922 offers more clues about the name. The investigation is an interpretation of the meaning of this being with respect to its basic categorial structures, which together constitute the ways in which factical life temporalizes itself, unfolds itself and, in temporalizing and unfolding, *speaks about* itself. Heidegger stresses the origin of the word "category" in Greek law, where the *kategoros* was the accuser or prosecutor who speaks *to* the court *about* the defendant. The categories of life are the ways in which life expresses itself, makes itself known, speaks to itself; these structures of intelligibility can be interpreted or made explicit like speech acts or written words. As an ontology of facticity, philosophy is therefore at the same time an interpretation of the categories of this speaking and interpreting; this "fundamental research" can properly be called a "phenomenological hermeneutics of facticity". Heidegger's enterprise is thus one of *articulating* or making explicit the structures of immediate, meaning-laden experience of the world: bringing "into the light of day" the categories that originally "live in life itself".

### The fore-structure of understanding and the legitimacy of the hermeneutic circle

**Definition:** All understanding — including the understanding achieved by the hermeneutics of facticity — involves a prior engagement with and familiarity with the matter at issue, which *Being and Time* calls a "fore-meaning" (*Vormeinung*). On the basis of this projected significance we can develop a more explicit understanding, in which we actually articulate and conceptualize the matter; Heidegger calls this explicit working-out of what understanding has already opened up for us `interpretation` (*Auslegung*).

**Mechanism:** In the hermeneutics of facticity, as in any other interpretation, one must first gain an initial grasp of the phenomena in question, which is constitutive and decisive for interpretation. This initial grasp is a projection, a sketch — as Aristotle says in *Nicomachean Ethics* I.7 — and so neither fixed nor final; it can be questioned, abandoned or revised as the situation requires, but it is indispensable. The whole complex that makes any interpretation possible, the `hermeneutic situation`, has three elements: `fore-having` (*Vorhabe*), `fore-sight` (*Vorsicht*) and `fore-conception` (*Vorgriff*). All three "fix in advance" the possibilities available for understanding. Whenever something is interpreted as something, he writes, the interpretation is essentially grounded in fore-having, fore-sight and fore-conception; interpretation is never a presuppositionless grasping of something presented to us. The explicit appropriation of something already understood always proceeds from some "point of view": fore-sight makes the "first cut" in what has been taken into fore-having, with a view to a definite way in which it can be interpreted.

**Example:** *Supplement:* In §34 of *Being and Time* Heidegger illustrates this structure with hearing: what we "first" hear is never pure sounds or complexes of noise but the creaking wagon, the motorcycle, the column on the march, the north wind, the woodpecker tapping, the crackling fire; to hear a "pure noise" requires, on the contrary, a very artificial and complicated attitude. What is heard is always already understood *as* something — this is the "as"-structure: understanding precedes and supports all explicit interpretation, and so-called presuppositionless perception is a product of subsequent abstraction. *The example carries over directly to reading: a reader never first sees a string of neutral marks and then assigns them meaning; he always already reads them as a sentence, a poem, a testimony — the "fore-having" of genre and expectation is at work from the first glance. This is the phenomenological premise of Jauss's "horizon of expectations" and Iser's "gestalt" (see P10, P11).*

**Distinction:** All this means that understanding has a *circular* structure — the back-and-forth movement Heidegger had already hinted at in 1919 when he first called his enterprise hermeneutic. The crucial point is that this circularity does not vitiate the understanding achieved but *first makes any understanding possible*. Handled properly, the circle conceals "a positive possibility of the most primordial kind of knowing". *This differs importantly from Schleiermacher's circle: for Schleiermacher the circle is a product of the interpreter's situation, which can be improved by gradual revision; for Heidegger it expresses the ontological structure of understanding itself, and there is no position "outside the circle" from which understanding could be achieved.*

### Historicity, tradition and having-been-interpreted

**Principle:** The reality of the "hermeneutic situation", and the circular structure of understanding that depends on it, point to the historical conditionedness of human existence. For Heidegger this conditionedness is not an accidental hindrance to reflective transparency, nor something to be overcome for the sake of objectivity; rather, `historicity` is a defining feature of Dasein at the ground of its being. All meaning, and the processes by which meaning is created, discovered and transmitted, are historical. How he understood this historicity, and what it implies for interpretive understanding, deeply shaped his own hermeneutics and became what Gadamer valued most.

**Definition:** At a first level, "history" refers to the structure of our unfolding sense of ourselves, the basic familiarity with our own life that "stretches" between birth and death. "History" here is not in the sense of "source criticism" or "historiography" but "life lived in common", the way life is intelligible from itself. To have a "self" is to have a unified understanding of life-experience, but this understanding is not available at any moment; it is enacted in the course of life: our basic familiarity with ourselves unfolds in time through the continual interpretation of particular experiences in the light of expectations. Life always has some "direction", into which it "grows" and which it nevertheless gives itself. Unified meaning is achieved through two moments that are not successive but jointly constitute this basic "history": first, the generation of a "horizon of expectation" from previous experience and from the active "projection" of possibilities taken over from cultural inheritance; second, the interpretation of one's "past" in the light of this horizon. The result is a fluid self-interpretation that gives a person's life pattern and "connectedness": past and future are woven into a coherent "rhythm", which is the "horizon" within which I am in fact intelligible to myself. In his Kassel lectures on Dilthey, Heidegger says: we are history, we are our own past; our future is lived out of the past; we carry our past with us.

**Definition:** Yet the self's "past" does not end with the events that make up the whole story of a life; the past is also "there" as `tradition`. We always already find ourselves within a communal tradition — made up of practices, concepts, norms and linguistic usages — that constitutes the meaningful content of individual life. This is the second sense of "history" stressed in the hermeneutics of facticity. The possibility (or set of possibilities) with which a person identifies, and which gives his life pattern and meaning, is not created from nothing but found in the complex network of ideals, practices, models and concepts that make up a tradition; the coherence of an individual life depends on its connection with tradition. Put more bluntly: a person has a self only *as* a member of a community, as heir to a particular cultural inheritance — and this includes all his activities, including the activity of reaching understanding. *Being and Time* says that Dasein has grown up both into and in a traditional way of interpreting itself, which discloses and regulates the possibilities of its being; its own past — which always means the past of its "generation" — does not *follow* Dasein but always already goes ahead of it. The "Natorp Report" says that factical life moves at every moment within a certain `having-been-interpreted` (*Ausgelegtheit*) that has been handed down to it and that it reworks or remakes. This understanding is "sedimented" in language (in the broad sense), not only as the way people in the past understood things, available for our study, but as "the present understanding of being" and as possibilities and horizons available for reinterpretation and conceptual articulation.

**Limitation:** Heidegger also viewed history as tradition from a critical angle.
