Contrary to a common view, careful readers find little of his supposed conservatism in the texts: what attracted generation after generation of students was not a defence of the status quo or nostalgia for the glories of the Second Empire, but a truly revolutionary call — to free the present from the burden of tradition while opening up radical possibilities missed in its contemporary reception. Tradition, in its present operation, is not something simply to be followed or blindly accepted; hermeneutics, as an activity unfolding in present history, is necessarily a *critical* enterprise, because the historical process inevitably involves what Heidegger calls the "fading of significance" (*Verblassen der Bedeutsamkeit*). Tradition hardens into a self-evidence in which other conceptions or interpretations appear as idle "chatter". In 1921–1922 he illustrated this with the very concept of "history", with concepts such as "definition" and "philosophy" — even with "the attempt to bring oneself to an understanding in one's conditionedness" itself. Hence a disjunction: either we live, work and do research according to unexamined needs and artificially induced tendencies, or we are prepared to grasp a radical idea concretely and realize our existence in it.

**Mechanism:** Tradition makes things familiar and available to us, while at the same time concealing and closing off possibilities. In 1922 he wrote that life is always caught up in, and driven along by, this or that inauthentic tradition and custom, from which arise certain desires whose paths of fulfilment are already marked out in its concern; life conceals itself from itself. The problem is not that we are historically conditioned but that this conditionedness is usually enacted along pre-laid tracks. Some elements of tradition acquire a "shadowy life", hardening into a long, degenerate pseudo-tradition that was never originally appropriated; life is "weighed down" by tradition. In the 1923 summer lectures he formulated this phenomenon as "today" (*das Heute*): the immediate atmosphere into which tradition has congealed at a given moment, which affects intellectual discourse and even a culture's relation to its own past.

### Destruction and repetition: how to free the past from tradition

**Definition:** For this reason, the project of reaching self-understanding in the hermeneutics of facticity must include an essential *negative* moment. The "Natorp Report" says that hermeneutics can accomplish its task only by way of `destruction` (*Destruktion*); this is the authentic way in which the present encounters itself in its own basic movement, making it a permanent question to what extent what has emerged from its history is concerned with appropriating radical possibilities.

**Distinction:** Destruction, this critical hermeneutics, was designed to unite the creative, "systematic" path of phenomenological research with revolutionary readings of some of the most important texts in the history of philosophy. In this respect it was conceived as an alternative to two approaches then current: historicist reconstruction, and the neo-Kantian practice, dominant in German universities in the first decades of the twentieth century, of reading traditional texts as responses to eternal philosophical problems. Phenomenological method, Heidegger said, already works by critically dismantling the objectifications that constantly pile up on the phenomena; because of the historical nature of human existence, this enterprise necessarily means confronting tradition; the "basic meaning" of phenomenology includes "carrying out a destruction within the history of spirit".

**Mechanism:** The aim of this confrontation is not "to extend knowledge of the past" but to force the present back upon itself, "to heighten its questionableness". Understanding does not merely take over the past for the sake of knowledge that only records; it `repeats` (*Wiederholung*) in an original way, for and from one's own situation, what was understood in the past. In his first Marburg course he said: destruction is indeed critical, but what is criticized is not the past — the past is precisely opened up by destruction; the critique falls on the present, on our contemporary Dasein, because it is covered over by a past that has become inauthentic; what is criticized is not Aristotle or Augustine but today; destruction makes visible what was authentically, originally positive in the past. He also said that the task of giving Dasein to itself and explicating it is bound up with the task of "shaking" contemporary Dasein: this proceeds by way of "dismantling", by seeing into the origins of basic categories such as consciousness, person and subject, showing that they stem from entirely different ontological experiences whose conceptual tendencies are unsuited to the Dasein we want to see. Gadamer and other students recalled an atmosphere of "hostile takeover" when Heidegger arrived in Marburg, and that he made clear his intention to blow apart the way the history of philosophy was then studied.

**Example:** The Kassel lectures put the point most clearly: philosophical research is called upon to become a critique of the present; when the past is disclosed in an original way, it is no longer merely another present preceding ours; the past can be freed so that we find in it the real roots of our own existence and bring it into the present as a living force; historical consciousness frees the past for the future, and the past thereby gains force and becomes fruitful. Heidegger's famous and controversial readings of Augustine's *Confessions*, Aristotle's *Physics* and *Nicomachean Ethics*, Kant's *Critique of Pure Reason*, Nietzsche's *Nachlass*, the Presocratic fragments and Hölderlin's hymns exemplify this hermeneutics. In *Kant and the Problem of Metaphysics* (1929, originally planned as an important part of Part Two of *Being and Time*), for instance, he read the temporal synthesis in the A-deduction of the *Critique of Pure Reason* as the foundation that makes experience and judgement possible, and held that Kant's real commitment was to "general metaphysics", that is, ontology — an argument that shaped both *Being and Time* and Gadamer's hermeneutics. These readings are often far from what historians of philosophy accept as scholarship, but they succeed in showing that the philosophical tradition is neither a string of mistaken views nor the period expression of dead cultures, but *things worthy of thought* (*Denkwürdigkeiten*).

**Limitation:** Heidegger was notoriously scornful of the traditional crafts of philologists and historians, yet his phenomenological hermeneutics did not exclude continued reflection on the art of understanding. On the other hand, Gjesdal points out that, apart from his earliest lectures, Heidegger took almost no interest in the human sciences as an object of philosophical reflection; his "ontologizing" of hermeneutics is also the starting point of the equation "philosophy = hermeneutics, hermeneutics = ontology" that she criticizes (see P16). In later life he attempted dialogue with Japanese scholars and occasionally cited the *Daodejing*, but never revised his central ideas in the light of other traditions (see P16).

**Conclusion:** Heidegger opened an entirely new dimension of hermeneutic inquiry. Herder, Hegel and Dilthey had sown the seeds, but by conceiving hermeneutics as a phenomenological enterprise that, like Husserl's phenomenology, aimed to be the "primordial science" of human experience as a whole, he took hermeneutics far beyond its traditional scope and overturned the terms of the debate set off by the rise of historicism. Understanding is no longer one cognitive act among others, distinguished only by its subject matter and methodological limits; the human being is shown to be through and through an *understanding animal*. Life in its immediacy is meaningful, and the structures that make it meaningful can be articulated by an engaged interpretation that largely bypasses the epistemological puzzles that troubled his predecessors. *But this "bypassing" has a price: once understanding is defined as a way of being, the question of standards for distinguishing good from bad interpretations, and for well-grounded critique of tradition, is not solved but shifted to another level — the focus of the dispute among Gadamer, Habermas and Gjesdal (see P7, P13, P16).*

## P7 Why is understanding an event within tradition? Gadamer

### Truth and method: Gadamer's retelling of the history of hermeneutics

**Background:** Gadamer (1900–2002) belonged to the remarkable group of students who witnessed the formation of Heidegger's phenomenological hermeneutics in the early 1920s. From his earliest work on Plato and Aristotle he developed his own approach, stressing, like Heidegger, the ubiquity of understanding in human life. After more than thirty years of teaching in Marburg, Kiel, Leipzig, Frankfurt and Heidelberg, he published *Truth and Method* in 1960 and became the most influential living exponent of the hermeneutic tradition. Unlike *Being and Time*, published under pressure before its author's thought had settled, *Truth and Method* was the mature fruit of decades of reflection. Over a long career Gadamer defended his position in debates with Habermas, Derrida and others, and influenced English-language philosophers such as Rorty, McDowell and MacIntyre as well as many fields beyond philosophy.

**Principle:** Gadamer's influence on reception theory carries a certain irony: in *Truth and Method* he set out to discredit precisely what many reception theorists most wanted — a "method" that would not only study and analyse literature but arrive at the truth about texts. The "and" in the title should be read not as a conjunction but as a disjunction. For Gadamer, method is something a subject applies to an object to produce a predetermined result; in the natural sciences this result is — wrongly — identified with truth. He revived hermeneutics precisely to resist this identification: against the tendency of modern science to absorb hermeneutic reflection and make it serve science, he proposed hermeneutics as a corrective, metacritical orientation that overcomes the limits of every methodological endeavour. Earlier hermeneutics had concerned the exegesis of scripture (biblical and humanist texts), the psychology of understanding (Schleiermacher) or the methodology of the human sciences (Dilthey); Gadamer claimed a `universal` status for hermeneutics: he set out to explain not understanding relative to some discipline but understanding as such, conceived as the essence of our "being-in-the-world". His work is best seen as an attempt to mediate between philosophy and science, going beyond "the limited horizon of interest of scientific, theoretical methodology".

**Mechanism:** To make this renewed task of hermeneutics more plausible, Gadamer embedded two philosophical narratives in the book. The first, deeply influenced by Heidegger, tells the Western philosophical tradition as a story of a fall from grace and possible future redemption. In some pre-Cartesian age — above all ancient Greece — scientific method did not yet govern the idea of truth, and subject and object, being and thought, had not yet been sharply separated; with the arrival of Cartesian dualism, the estrangement of mind and matter became the cornerstone of Western philosophy. Gadamer writes that scientific activity always has something Cartesian about it, being the result of a critical method that admits only the indubitable; Kant's *Critique of Pure Reason* provided the final epistemological justification of natural science. To highlight the hegemony of scientific method he devotes the whole first part of the book to a critique of `aesthetic consciousness`: art was excluded from truth and the aesthetic reduced to mere "semblance" as a result of "the dominance of the epistemological model of science", which discredited every possibility of knowing outside the new method. Art is thus the domain most devalued by the privileged method and the domain where its deficiencies are most conspicuous; Gadamer is concerned to reveal resistance to scientific method and to tell the story of the breakdown of its tyranny — German philosophy from Leibniz to Hegel kept trying to supplement the new physics with a speculative science reviving the Aristotelian heritage, and Goethe's opposition to Newton was shared by Schelling, Hegel and Schopenhauer. The completion of the narrative involves both returning to the ontological question Heidegger raised at the start of *Being and Time* and tracing the vestiges of resistance to the distortions of modern science; Heidegger and Gadamer, as revivers of the universal concern with understanding, are cast as the chief authors of the last chapters of this philosophical story.

**Mechanism:** The second narrative is the history of hermeneutics. It has a similar ending but a slightly different plot, since hermeneutics is generally linked by Gadamer with opposition to dominant scientific thinking; it is also related to art, which serves as a paradigm of the general concept of understanding. The story begins with biblical exegesis and the humanist tradition before Romanticism: the origins of hermeneutics are bound up with the concern to discover the correct meaning of texts, and together with legal interpretation it is presented as a threefold capacity — `subtlety of understanding` (*subtilitas intelligendi*), `subtlety of explication` (*subtilitas explicandi*) and `subtlety of application` (*subtilitas applicandi*). This too is a story of loss: in its uneven development hermeneutics forgot its threefold capacity and was finally stripped of its explicative and applicative functions. Gadamer details the pioneering contributions of Schleiermacher, Ranke, Droysen and Dilthey, then shows that each in his own way failed to escape "methodological" thinking, illegitimately appealing at some point to objectivity and objective knowledge and so retaining the subject–object dualism inherent in scientific method. The resolution of the hermeneutic predicament, like the redemption of Western philosophy, involves Heidegger's overcoming of the last metaphysical obstacle — Husserl's phenomenology. Husserl too thought his philosophy opposed to objectivism and metaphysics, but for Gadamer his critique of the objectivism of all previous philosophy was in fact a methodological continuation of the modern tendency; Heidegger's project was to "go back to the beginnings of Western philosophy and revive the long-forgotten Greek argument about 'being'". Gadamer reduces the thesis of *Being and Time* to the claim that being itself is time. Unlike Husserl, who posited a transcendental, timeless subject or "primal ego", Heidegger placed all being in the world; the life-world can no longer be "bracketed", and finitude and temporality must stand at the centre of philosophy. For scientific method, historicity had been an obstacle to the ideal of objective knowledge; now it is transformed into what makes understanding possible: understanding is no longer a methodological concept but "the original character of the being of human life itself".

**Distinction:** Gadamer stresses that his philosophical hermeneutics is not primarily a prescription for interpretive practice but a description of how interpretive understanding is achieved; it does not aim to develop a theory and then, in the form of application, put hermeneutic practice on a "technically correct" track; its goal is self-understanding, with at most indirect benefits for the art of understanding. Even so, in the course of his long argument he pauses from time to time to reflect, in a more practical tone, on the art of interpretation; more interesting still are the moments when he practises hermeneutics himself (such as his reading of Aristotle; see the section on "application" below).

**Controversy:** Gadamer's two narratives have already been challenged in P1, P3 and P4: Gjesdal points out that they were formed polemically against a Marburg neo-Kantian background; Rush points out that Gadamer retrospectively distorted Romantic hermeneutics; Beiser and Forster show that the methodological tradition was neither objectivist nor empathy-based. Gadamer himself later conceded that his accounts of science and of philosophical Romanticism were too polemical. *The theory reconstructed below should therefore be read alongside these revisions: its theoretical insights do not depend on the correctness of its historical narrative.*

### Rehabilitating prejudice: authority, reason and tradition

**Background:** Gadamer's chief adversaries were the twin nineteenth-century legacy of `historicism` and `positivism`. Historicism can be broadly understood as a view of history that acknowledges the limited, conditioned outlook of every period and aims at accurate, objective reconstruction of the cultural products of past periods; positivism shares the view that correct method guarantees correct results, often adding that only the methods of the natural sciences qualify. This legacy was a product of what Gadamer calls the "historical Enlightenment", the flourishing of nineteenth-century German historiography (represented by Ranke and Droysen). In his view, historicism reflects this legacy in a "prejudice against prejudice itself": it denies the power of tradition, treating past cultures as naively self-enclosed and best understood through the supposedly superior objective lens of present methods.

**Definition:** Gadamer tries to dismantle this underlying prejudice. German *Vorurteil* (`prejudice`), like its English counterpart, etymologically means a prejudgement, a judgement formed in advance, but later came to mean a negative bias that precludes accurate judgement. He recalls that prejudice originally meant a provisional legal judgement rendered before the final verdict, not necessarily a false one. The Enlightenment's disparagement of prejudice stems from rationalism: Cartesian methodical doubt suspends assent to all preconceived opinions in order to "start from scratch" with a method that grounds all judgement; this more basic rationalist move, backed in the end by the brilliant polemics of Hume, Voltaire and Diderot, rose to an a priori distrust of tradition and became, in nineteenth-century historical consciousness, a rupture with or estrangement from tradition.

**Mechanism:** Gadamer's thesis is that the Enlightenment's comprehensive demand to overcome all prejudice itself proves to be a prejudice; removing it opens the way to a proper understanding of the finitude that governs our humanity and our historical consciousness. He argues by turning a key insight of historicism against it: reason is always historically conditioned. In fact, he says, history does not belong to us; we belong to history. Moreover, acknowledging authority is itself an act of reason, so the stark opposition between "the authority of tradition" and "the authority of reason" is a false dilemma. The work of scholars in the human sciences cannot be artificially isolated from their historical existence, from their belonging to the unfolding of the very tradition they study. Hence the need for a fundamental rehabilitation of the concept of prejudice, recognizing `legitimate prejudices`, in order to do justice to the finite, historical mode of human being. This claim continues Heidegger's "fore-structure of understanding": earlier theories demanded the removal of preconceptions, whereas Heidegger held that it is precisely our being-in-the-world with its prejudices and presuppositions that makes understanding possible.

**Influence:** Valorizing "prejudice" can be seen, on the one hand, as a device for provoking liberal readers; but the serious thesis behind it should not be overlooked: one's prejudices and preconceptions are a basic part of any hermeneutic situation. Unlike earlier hermeneutic theories, the interpreter's historicity is not an obstacle to understanding; genuinely hermeneutic thinking must "take its own historicity into account".

### Effective history and historically effected consciousness

**Definition:** Hermeneutics, Gadamer says, is "adequate" only when it demonstrates the effectiveness of history within understanding itself; he calls this `effective history` (*Wirkungsgeschichte*). Note its difference from the earlier sense of "history of effects" (the study of an author's influence on later generations; see P1): for Gadamer it is not an auxiliary discipline studying an object but the structure of understanding itself. The effect of living tradition and the effect of historical research must form a unity of effect, and analysing it reveals only a texture of interactions. His thesis is that modern historical research is itself not only research but the transmission of tradition; we do not see it only in terms of progress and verified results; every time the past resounds with a new voice, we have a new experience of history. Historical understanding is not the activity of an isolated subject striving to understand an alien object from the distant past but "a process of transmission in which past and present are constantly mediated". History (*Geschichte*) is an event (*Geschehen*), not an object of research or a particular methodology for understanding it. Historical and cultural studies both *presuppose* tradition (the prejudices that make understanding possible) and *produce* it.

**Mechanism:** Gadamer is quick to explain that he is not promoting a new method that takes account of effects, nor calling for "a new independent discipline ancillary to the human sciences"; he is calling for a new consciousness — `historically effected consciousness` (*wirkungsgeschichtliches Bewusstsein*) — that recognizes what is already happening when we encounter the past. Whether one approves of effective history or not, it is intertwined with our understanding, and historically effected consciousness simply makes us aware of this reality: "historically effected consciousness is first of all consciousness of the hermeneutic situation". The label is meant to point to two levels at once: a consciousness affected by and determined within the historical process, and a consciousness of this very affectedness and determination.

**Condition:** Historical consciousness must realize that in its apparently immediate approach to a work of art or a transmitted text there is also another kind of inquiry at work, unrecognized and unregulated: when we try to understand a historical phenomenon from the historical distance characteristic of the hermeneutic situation, we are always already affected by history, which determines in advance what seems to us worth inquiring about and what will appear as an object of research; when we take the immediate appearance of the phenomenon as the whole truth, we forget half of what really is — indeed we miss the whole truth of the phenomenon. Historicism, with its overly naive reliance on method, covers up the formative role of tradition. But this awareness does not mean complete transparency: borrowing from Hegel, Gadamer describes how historically effected consciousness reveals the "substantiality" presupposed by a particular historical form of consciousness, the limiting horizon a situation provides; at the same time he rejects an ideal of historical knowledge corresponding to Hegel's "absolute knowledge" — nineteenth-century historians and philosophers of history conceived the goal of historical knowledge as "the thoughtful fusion of the whole of tradition with the present". For Gadamer such transparency is impossible: historically effected consciousness is so fundamentally finite that our whole being, effected in the totality of our destiny, inevitably exceeds its knowledge of itself.

**Example:** Habermas illustrates effective history with the "Thirty Years' War": the war became the "Thirty Years' War" only after it ended in 1648; its meaning depends on the historian's changing interests and on the different contexts in which it is understood — the political collapse of the German Empire, the delayed development of capitalism, the end of the Counter-Reformation, the subject of the Wallenstein drama, and so on. Because history does not end, the possible contexts and historical narratives on which an event, action or text depends are inexhaustible: to exhaust its meaning one would have to know all later events and the interests of all future historians, and even the last historian of history could not narrate his own final act of narration. Habermas therefore sees one merit of Gadamer's hermeneutics in its linking the understanding of historical tradition with "action-orienting self-understanding": every historian is in the position of the last historian; what he can know historically cannot be grasped independently of the framework of his own life-practice (see P13).

### Horizon and the fusion of horizons

**Definition:** To explain what the hermeneutic situation means, Gadamer first says that it represents "a standpoint that limits the possibility of vision", then borrows and transforms a term from Husserl's phenomenology, introducing the central concept of `horizon` (*Horizont*) as "an essential part of the concept of situation". A horizon is "the range of vision that includes everything that can be seen from a particular vantage point". It describes our situatedness in the world, but should not be conceived as a fixed or closed standpoint; it is "something into which we move and that moves with us". A horizon can also be defined by the prejudices we carry at a given moment, since prejudices represent a "horizon" beyond which we cannot see.

**Mechanism:** A text speaks to us from its own horizon-giving situation, into which we must place ourselves; this does not mean agreeing with or endorsing the meaning the text conveys. But Gadamer thinks it wrong to isolate the text's claim methodologically and draw a rigid line between its horizon and ours, because this rests on the false picture of horizons as *closed*. To regard the horizon of a past culture as closed, an insurmountable boundary to understanding, or to regard the past as superseded from our supposedly superior viewpoint, Gadamer mocks as "a Robinson Crusoe dream of historical enlightenment". Playing on the metaphor, he says that for a moving person the horizon changes; likewise the horizon of the past, out of which all human life lives and which exists in the form of tradition, is always in motion.

**Definition:** Understanding is not transposing oneself into a wholly alien world, whether a past culture or the depths of a genius's soul; the "transposing" involved in understanding is "rising to a higher universality that overcomes not only our own particularity but also that of the other". Our horizon (made up of prejudices) is not "a fixed set of opinions and evaluations" and hence not an obstacle to grasping the meaning of the past; on the contrary, in confrontation with the historical other our own prejudices (in both the usual and Gadamer's more neutral sense) come under pressure and undergo continual formation and reformation. The conclusion is that understanding is always the fusion of these horizons supposedly existing by themselves — `fusion of horizons` (*Horizontverschmelzung*).

**Distinction:** Gadamer concedes that the idea of a separate horizon for a literary text and the like is illusory: no line divides the horizon of the past from the horizon of the present. When our historical consciousness transposes itself into historical horizons, this does not mean entering alien worlds unconnected with our own; together they constitute one great horizon that moves from within and, beyond the frontiers of the present, embraces the historical depths of our self-consciousness — in fact there is only one single horizon containing everything historical consciousness contains. Yet this illusion, "the projection of a historical horizon", is a necessary "phase" in the process of understanding, after which historical consciousness recombines what it has distinguished in order to become one with itself again. Fusion of horizons does take place, but it means that the historical horizon, as soon as it is projected, is at the same time superseded. *Almost in Hegelian fashion, understanding seems to be historical consciousness becoming aware of itself.* Understanding the past does involve projecting a historical horizon that allows us to account for the real differences between past and present; but understanding also involves *transcending* that horizon.

**Mechanism:** *Supplement:* Part Three of *Truth and Method* takes this analysis a final step: the medium in which the fusion of horizons takes place is `language`. Gadamer's dictum "Being that can be understood is language" does not mean that everything is language; it means that understanding always proceeds linguistically — tradition is handed down in linguistic form, the interpreter responds to it in his own language, and what mediates between them is a conversation carried on in language. Language is therefore not an instrument the one who understands could exchange for another, but the very experience of the world within which the one who understands already stands; this is why translation can serve as the model of all understanding (for Habermas's development of this point, see P13). This step connects Gadamer with the philosophy of language of Hamann, Herder and Humboldt in P2: the thesis that thought depends on language and that language constitutes a world-view is raised here to the ontological structure of understanding. *It also explains why Gadamer can treat the experience of art, historical understanding and dialogue as one and the same kind of event — all three are an unfolding of meaning in and through language; and this is exactly where his critics press (see P13): if distortion lies within language itself, language cannot correct it by its own means alone.*

### Application: legal hermeneutics and practical wisdom

**Definition:** Gadamer held that the traditional hermeneutic concept of `application` (*Anwendung*) captures precisely the crucial aspect of understanding that transcends the historical horizon. This is perhaps his most original contribution to modern hermeneutics: taking legal hermeneutics as paradigm, he insists that every interpretation is also an application. Recovering the *subtilitas applicandi* is not merely a gestural return to the original function of interpretation but an affirmation and logical consequence of the principle of historically effected consciousness: to understand is to apply for the present. Historical understanding, he writes, always already contains the idea that the tradition reaching us speaks to the present and must be understood in this mediation — indeed as this mediation; legal hermeneutics is therefore not a special case but is fitted to restore the full scope of the hermeneutic problem and so to retrieve the former unity of hermeneutics, in which jurist, theologian and philologist met. Traditional disciplines such as legal and biblical hermeneutics provide the model, showing that "historical understanding" in the modern or historicist sense is precisely *not* what one aims at when facing the past.

**Distinction:** The controversy over Gadamer's concept of application seems another unnecessary dispute provoked by provocative wording. Holub points out that application should not be understood as praxis in the Marxist sense, or as carrying out some physical action in the real world; it does not mean taking something from a text and implementing it in reality. It is closer to what Ingarden called "concretization" (see P9), realization or making-present for the interpreter. In this sense one can compare a theatre director's interpreting a play and realizing it in performance with a reader's understanding of a text: both involve application in Gadamer's sense. Application can also be understood within the framework of Gadamer's central analogy between understanding and conversation: encountering a text, we enter a dialogue with the past, with question and answer, give and take, openness to the other, leading to understanding; application can thus be described as "mediation between then and now, between Thou and I". *Seen as concretization or mediation, the concept loses some of its initial appeal; Gadamer's retrieval of the lost dimension of hermeneutics is far less radical than it first appears.* Moreover, **Ralf Poscher** argues that Gadamer's concept of application equivocally mixes at least four theoretically distinct aspects — rule-following, legal construction, the exercise of discretion and judgements of significance — which must be kept apart (see P15).

**Example:** In a section on Aristotle's *Nicomachean Ethics* — harking back to his formative experience in Heidegger's Aristotle seminars of the early 1920s — Gadamer focuses on how `practical wisdom` (*phronesis*) exemplifies a kind of knowledge that belongs to a process of becoming rather than standing over against it as a detached objective observer. The process in question is the domain of human practice, and Aristotle's understanding of it anticipates the conception of history put forward by the nineteenth-century "historical school" and by Dilthey and the Baden neo-Kantians (Rickert, Windelband): the practical domain, like the historical one, is the domain of changeable human institutions and modes of conduct. Especially important for Gadamer is that practical wisdom is concretely engaged in a particular, temporally located situation; because of the nature of its subject matter and its involvement in becoming, ethics cannot attain the extreme precision of mathematics. Philosophy on the Aristotelian model offers no decision procedure or moral methodology but aids moral consciousness by "outlining phenomena", promoting self-reflection in the present context of action. This knowledge is not separate from being (or from its object) — just as, in historically effected consciousness, the interpreter belongs to tradition and understanding is an event in the unfolding history of tradition.

**Evidence:** This analysis is itself an example of Gadamerian hermeneutics in practice (in the traditional sense of reading texts). Gadamer's own "horizon" consists of questions about understanding shaped mainly by the dynamics of late-nineteenth- and early-twentieth-century historiography and philosophical reflection on it; Aristotle's horizon is late classical Greece, the dissolution of the polis and the question of the relation between knowledge and ethical practice, a philosophical preoccupation since Socrates. The "fusion" here consists in articulating a distinctive intellectual activity situated in a context of continual change: like historical understanding, the acquisition of moral knowledge is conditioned by custom and education; and like historical understanding, the full meaning of a concept emerges only in a concrete situation.

### The negativity of hermeneutic experience and openness to the Thou

**Definition:** After Aristotle, Gadamer explains historically effected consciousness in more detail. As a consciousness, he stresses, it shares the reflexivity characteristic of all conscious activity; this leads him to Hegel and his account in the *Phenomenology of Spirit* of the `experience` (*Erfahrung*) of "spirit" — the reflexive structure of consciousness driving a dynamic process of self-criticism and revision along a "path of despair". **Axel Honneth**'s reconstruction of this part is that Gadamer saw in Hegel's dialectic an insight into the importance of *negative* experience. Positive experiences are crucial to the natural and social sciences because they confirm hypotheses; but Gadamer stresses the kind of experience we "go through" or "undergo" — the cases in which we can say we "had an experience": experiences that interrupt our routines, frustrate our expectations and expose the falsity of assumptions we previously held. Their importance lies in extending our knowledge: when we "stumble" over assumptions that experience proves untenable, we learn more both about the event and about our prior assumptions.

**Distinction:** Hegel saw the gradual accumulation of negative experiences as a reflective process involving reason's capacity for self-correction, leading ultimately to absolute knowledge. Gadamer draws two different conclusions: first, repeatedly undergoing negative experiences leads not to absolute knowledge but to insight into our own continuing fallibility — through experience we develop a readiness for further experience; second, since we extend our knowledge through negative experiences we go through and suffer, knowledge is something that *happens to us*, not something we can control. For Gadamer the true result of experience is not self-transparency or certainty about the correctness of one's findings but an openness to what is different and new; this openness is won through the hard lessons of failure. Experience is thus the experience of human finitude, in which one discovers the limits of the power and self-knowledge of planning reason.

**Mechanism:** This `hermeneutic experience` is first of all the experience of *tradition*. Tradition is not an object of historical curiosity or a puzzle from an alien world, but a "Thou" that speaks to us like a genuine partner in conversation; it therefore demands a non-objectifying understanding, quite different from the historicists' "naive faith in method". Honneth points out that Gadamer here builds a parallel: he examines three possible modes of relation between I and Thou and connects them with three ways of understanding historical tradition, showing two to be deficient and only one to allow genuine encounter.

1. **The objectifying relation**: treating the other as an object of interest only as an obstacle or aid to one's own ends. This is both a cognitive and a moral error: the "I" restricts its understanding of the other to the aspects that serve its ends, treating the other merely as a means rather than an end in itself; more importantly, the "I" imagines itself a wholly independent subject, entirely unaffected by the other's previous actions, as though there were no intersubjective past. The corresponding historical consciousness reflects the positivist self-understanding: treating historical tradition as an external object, devoting itself to tracing its causal laws, and imagining itself distanced from and uninvolved in tradition.
2. **Authoritative solicitude**: treating the other as the same as oneself and so claiming to know him better than he knows himself. The cognitive error is substituting "I" for "Thou"; the moral error is withdrawing from the reciprocal relation — the empathic response that supposes it already knows the other's feelings is in fact related only to itself and does not respond to the other at all. The corresponding form of understanding is scientistic hermeneutics or (in Habermas's terms, Wittgensteinian) linguistic analysis, which Honneth connects with Dilthey: it assumes that its understanding of tradition is unconstrained by its own historical starting point and that it can understand any historical situation from within as well as it understands its own.
3. **Radical openness**: "I" and "Thou" both allow themselves to enter a genuine relation with each other, a mutual encounter in which both are directly involved rather than reflectively withdrawing. The corresponding historical understanding acknowledges our prior bond with tradition and opens itself to tradition and its claims.

The common problem of the first two relations is that they deprive the other, or tradition, of its "surprise value", making it impossible for us to be challenged by it and leaving us complacent in our own presuppositions. Only at the third level are the "surprise value" of tradition and the experiences it can provoke possible. This openness requires an act of self-limitation; it does not require freedom from all prejudice — that would be impossible anyway — but makes genuine understanding possible.

**Mechanism:** This openness takes concrete form in `conversation`: participants consider the challenges their interlocutors raise to their understanding of the subject matter and, together with them, rethink what they thought they understood. Gadamer even insists on a "discipline of questioning and inquiry", calling it "a discipline that guarantees truth". But for him questions are not products of critical reason; they arise from experiences — including experiences of others — that make us "stumble".

**Example:** Gadamer takes our experience of truth in encounters with great works of art and literature as the model for the insight characteristic of the humanities. He characterizes the experience of art as `play` (*Spiel*): the structure of play is independent of the individual subjectivity of the players, who are caught up in the game rather than its masters. Just as in the experience of art we are moved, shaken and absorbed in ways no method or procedure can induce or enhance, so our experience of tradition and of others has the power to change us, unsettle our presuppositions and conceits, and transform what we thought we knew.

### The problem of the classical and the limits of Gadamer's hermeneutics

**Controversy:** One of the central controversies in contemporary German hermeneutics concerns the role of tradition in Gadamer's work, especially his treatment of the "classical" as the paradigm of effective history. For Gadamer the `classical` is simply what has been preserved because it was deemed worth preserving; but critics have reason to charge him with neglecting the power relations inherent in any socially mediated textual or social exchange. Since language itself is not a neutral instrument, Gadamer's dialogue model — an ideal communication between past and present as two speakers — both misrepresents what actually happens in understanding and is itself an ideological device masking the concrete social relations within which communication takes place. Gadamer did not lack answers to his critics, but his failure to integrate a social perspective into his general theoretical framework remains a weakness of his work (for Habermas's and Honneth's critiques and Gadamer's replies, see P13). Like Heidegger, he seems able to acknowledge historicity only at the level of abstract theory: when he analyses texts himself — whether a poem by Rilke or a novel by Immermann — the potentially radical notion of "being-in-the-world" yields philosophical criticism resembling the most unhistorical New Critical readings.

**Distinction:** Another notable tension appears in Gadamer's writings on Celan, where he takes a surprisingly anti-intentionalist position: all that matters is what the poem actually says, not what its author intended and perhaps did not know how to say; a hint from the author about the raw material of his subject matter may be useful and guard against misunderstanding, but such hints remain a dangerous crutch. He also says that a written text is accessible in its full meaning, across space and time, to anyone who knows its script and language. *This does not fully square with the claim that understanding is always a fusion of horizons and always application: if a text is accessible in its full meaning to anyone who knows its language, the hermeneutic significance of historical distance is greatly reduced.*

**Limitation:** Gadamer's influence on reception theory is, in Holub's view, the product of a misunderstanding. However useful the "horizon of expectations" and effective history later proved for examining literary texts, in Gadamer's theory they belong to a more abstract philosophical domain; although any hermeneutics can be said to imply a methodology, Gadamer plainly disagreed. Reception theorists, especially Jauss and his students, most often appropriated precisely "effective history" and "horizon" (see P10).

**Controversy:** Gjesdal points to the limits of Gadamer's hermeneutics from another direction. Because his reading of Schleiermacher rests on a misreading of Kant's concept of "genius", he missed another possibility within the methodological tradition; his ontological framework leaves him almost uninterested in the different challenges arising within the various fields of the human sciences (which matter to him chiefly as studies of interpretation), and he does not even acknowledge different branches of philosophy or different approaches to textual interpretation; his work shows a worrying tendency to identify philosophy with hermeneutics and then to treat hermeneutics as the master discourse of the human sciences (see P16). Rush sees Gadamer as a follower of Heidegger who extended hermeneutics into a general theory of human self-constitution, in which the primary value of interpretive understanding lies in the transformation of the interpreter rather than in understanding others; he can even be seen as drafting the methodology for the planned but unwritten Part Two of *Being and Time*.

**Conclusion:** After Heidegger's revolution, Gadamer's hermeneutics rethought the distinctive role and value of humanistic inquiry in an age that prizes precision and results. Absorbing Heidegger's insights into the ubiquity of understanding and the way history pervades the present and sketches the future, he raised humanistic understanding from a mimic forever striving and failing to achieve scientific precision and objective validity to an individual and collective event: one in which meaning emerges from obscurity and human life proceeds along an endless path of self-understanding. His readings of Plato, Aristotle and the Presocratics, and of German poets such as Celan and Rilke, inside and outside *Truth and Method*, conversed with other scholars' work while opening wholly new ways of understanding these thinkers and their relations, overcoming the objectifying isolation in which the classics had been imprisoned while preserving their genuine otherness. *But its core concepts — prejudice, effective history, fusion of horizons, application — are an ambiguous legacy for literary studies: they gave reception theory philosophical legitimacy for the idea that readers and history participate in meaning, but provide precisely none of the method and standards of validity that reception theory sought. This tension runs through P8–P12.*

## P8 Why did literary studies need a "turn to the reader"? The rise of reception theory

### Jauss's narrative of paradigms and its rhetoric

**Background:** In a 1969 essay, "The change in the paradigm of literary scholarship", Jauss sketched the history of literary methods and declared that contemporary literary studies stood at the beginning of a "revolution". Borrowing the concepts of `paradigm` and "scientific revolution" from **Thomas S. Kuhn**, he presented literary scholarship as an undertaking analogous to the procedures of the natural sciences. The study of literature, on this view, is not a gradual accumulation of facts and evidence that brings each generation closer to knowing what literature really is, or to understanding individual works correctly; its development is marked by qualitative leaps, discontinuities and new points of departure. A paradigm that once guided literary scholarship is discarded when it no longer satisfies the demands made on it; a new paradigm, better suited and independent of the older model, replaces it until it, in turn, proves unable to perform the function of explaining past works of literature for the present. Each paradigm defines not only the accepted methodological procedures of the academic community — "normal" literary scholarship — but also the accepted literary canon. In other words, a paradigm creates both the techniques of interpretation and the objects to be interpreted.

**Mechanism:** Jauss sketched a sequence of three earlier paradigms and the one now emerging. After a "pre-scientific" phase came the `classical-humanist` paradigm: works were compared with the approved models of the ancients; those that successfully imitated the classics were judged good, those that broke with time-honoured conventions bad; the critic's task was to measure present works against fixed rules. The breakdown of this paradigm in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries was part of the "scientific revolution" of historicism, which arose in the wake of the founding of nation-states and the striving for national unity across Europe. Under political change and ideological necessity, literary history became an idealized moment of national legitimation; activity centred on source studies, reconstructing the prehistory of standard medieval texts and editing critical editions of the national tradition, and the generally accepted positivist approach produced the celebrated national literary histories of Gervinus, Scherer, De Sanctis and Lanson. Methodologically, this `historicist-positivist` paradigm is often identified with a mechanical handling of literary texts and a narrow, almost chauvinist outlook; Jauss notes that remnants of it survive today, citing official examination questions and the bulk of Marxist scholarship as evidence; but by the First World War it had outlived its usefulness for productive research. Out of the "growing discontent with positivist asceticism" emerged a third paradigm, which Jauss calls `aesthetic-formalist`. It embraces Leo Spitzer's stylistic studies, Oskar Walzel's *Geistesgeschichte*, Russian Formalism and New Criticism: what unites them is a turn away from historical and causal explanation towards concentration on the work itself. The precise description of linguistic technique, literary devices, composition and structure gave scholars an array of analytical tools, while raising the literary work to a self-sufficient object of research legitimized a preoccupation with literature as such.

**Mechanism:** Since the end of the Second World War, Jauss argued, this paradigm too has shown signs of exhaustion: the rehabilitation of philosophical hermeneutics, calls for more socially relevant criticism, and the appearance of alternatives such as Northrop Frye's archetypal criticism and structuralism are all symptoms of a crisis in the third paradigm. Structuralism (and presumably the varieties of "post-structuralism") may look like a strong candidate for the new scholarly model, but because it originated in opposition to the old philological-historical school and has split into many critical directions, Jauss excludes it from consideration for the moment; its chief value lies in challenging literary scholarship to incorporate into the analysis of works the categories and procedures developed by linguistics. The precise orientation of the new paradigm cannot yet be determined, but Jauss outlines the demands it must meet. First is the requirement every previous paradigm has met: the interpretation, mediation and *actualization* of past art — wresting works of art from the past by new interpretations, translating them into a new present, making the experiences preserved in past art accessible again; in other words, posing the questions that each generation poses anew and to which the art of the past can speak and again give answers. Second, the growing importance of the mass media compels any new paradigm to incorporate methods for dealing with a whole range of previously unforeseen "aesthetic and quasi-aesthetic effects". Accordingly, he sets out three methodological requirements for a fourth paradigm:

1. the mediation of aesthetic/formal and historical/reception-related analysis, as well as of art, history and social reality;
2. the linking of structural and hermeneutic methods (which scarcely take note of each other's procedures and results);
3. the exploration of an aesthetics of `effect` (*Wirkung*) — no longer merely descriptive — and of a new rhetoric, able to account equally for "high" literature, popular literature and the phenomena of the mass media.

Tentative steps towards such a paradigm, he claims, can be seen in the reforms initiated by the literature department at the University of Constance, where he and Iser held positions.

**Limitation:** Jauss never names reception theory in the essay, but it is clearly his favoured candidate for the fourth paradigm. The two main competitors that made significant inroads into German academia in the late 1960s are disqualified on rather dubious grounds: Marxism is taken to consist only of mechanistic procedures and can thus be conveniently consigned to the historicist-positivist dustbin; structuralism is granted a degree of legitimacy but is finally discredited for lacking the unity paradigmatic status requires. Only reception theory seems able to meet the three demands — and although this is never stated, the attentive reader will no doubt draw the conclusion unaided.

**Distinction:** Appropriating Kuhn's model of scientific revolutions to write the history of literary scholarship was also a way of enhancing reception theory's appeal. By defining scholarship as discontinuous, Jauss could better emphasize the novelty of his own work: continuity with earlier attempts to elucidate the nature of aesthetic response was severed, and reception theory's programmatically innovative character secured. In the tumultuous climate of German universities in the late 1960s, the appropriation also had a built-in appeal to students and younger scholars: by adopting Jauss's position on reception they found themselves in "opposition" to the establishment and were assured of the pioneering status of their work. Yet Jauss had to evade several important problems in transplanting Kuhn's theory. Kuhn posits the "scientific community" as a special social grouping relatively independent of other organizations, and he holds that there are long periods of "normal science" in which scientists pursue routine investigations according to established practices, competing paradigms are relatively scarce, and a single paradigm dominates and is almost universally accepted. These notions have limited application in literary studies. Literary "communities" — if the term may be extended to the literary sphere — are more often embroiled in "paradigm" controversies of a more complex kind: the continuity of Marxist criticism or structuralist thought, the penchant for neo-positivist methods in some European circles, the persistence of New Critical premises in many American universities all suggest that it is difficult to speak of a period of "normal" scholarship. No physicist would be taken seriously for advocating a return to Newton, but literary scholars still receive a fair hearing when they propose phenomenological or immanent methods as the most adequate way to interpret texts. Looking at the footnotes in scholarly works — for Kuhn a sign of an accepted paradigm — one is tempted to conclude that in cultural studies we must speak of quasi-independent communities, each with its own paradigm, rather than a linear succession of clearly differentiated models.

**Conclusion:** In retrospect, Jauss's "paradigm" essay can itself be judged paradigmatic, though in a way he would never have suspected when writing it: in the context of the late 1960s it is the prototype of a scholarly manifesto. It uses rhetoric as much as logic and persuades by analogy rather than coherent argument; adopting Kuhn's popular theory of scholarly change, it sets up a "plot" whose outcome must favour reception theory; refusing to name that outcome, it seeks to convince readers that they are freely arriving at the right conclusion; implicitly presenting reception theory as the resolution of a crisis and a "scientific revolution", it suggests at once a return to normalcy and the overthrow of existing dogma. Like many theoretical manifestos of the era, it might long since have been forgotten had the method it promoted not turned out to be precisely the answer German scholarship was seeking. For all its contradictions and confusions, this essay — itself a paradigm of its genre — has borne more legitimacy than its argument would seem to allow.

### Crisis and the canon debate in 1960s West Germany

**Evidence:** In retrospect, reception theory hardly seems to have needed such a "hard sell": it proved one of the most marketable products the scholarly community has ever produced. Whether one sees its appearance as a change of paradigm or, more modestly, as a shift of emphasis, no one today can seriously question its enormous impact on the interpretation of literature and art. A few examples show the magnitude of the effect. In 1977 Gunter Grimm's *Rezeptionsgeschichte* appeared with a bibliography of over sixty pages, most of its entries published in the previous decade; in the following fifteen years at least five readers or collections dealt with reception or reader-oriented problems; the *Zeitschrift für Literaturwissenschaft und Linguistik* (1974), the *Amsterdamer Beiträge zur neueren Germanistik* (1974), *Der Deutschunterricht* (1977), *Œuvres et Critiques* (1977–8) and *Poétique* (1979) devoted entire issues to the subject; *Poetica* published two provisional "balance sheets" (*Zwischenbilanzen*) of the "aesthetics of reception" in 1975 and 1976; the 1972 conference of German teachers in Stuttgart devoted two entire sections and parts of others to the new development; and in 1979 the ninth congress of the International Comparative Literature Association met under the general title "Literary Communication and Reception", its proceedings published the next year in a 436-page volume. In concrete application almost no field was left untouched: troubadour lyric, the English novel tradition, the *nouveau roman*, surrealism, the *Nibelungenlied*, Lessing's *Emilia Galotti*, Goethe's *Werther*, Hauptmann's *The Weavers*, Celan's "Thread suns", Brecht's *Keuner Stories*, Grass's *Local Anaesthetic* … From Marxists to traditional critics, from classicists and medievalists to modern specialists, virtually every methodological perspective and literary field responded to the challenge of reception theory.

**Distinction:** It is easier to document the scale of the response than to explain why it occurred. The "exhaustion" of old methods and a general "discontent" may have played a part in its rapid acceptance, but makeshift psychology of this sort raises more questions than it answers, and it confuses symptoms with causes: if a generation of scholars grows dissatisfied with current critical practice, these attitudes are themselves responses to change, not its causes. The origins of this purportedly new method must ultimately be sought in the context of German social, intellectual and academic life in the period. If we see the emergence of reception theory as an answer to the methodological crisis of literary studies in the 1960s, we can grasp at least one facet of the interface between literary theory and the wider social sphere.

**Mechanism:** That crisis was in turn the product of a nexus of factors penetrating almost every area of German life. Economically, the end of the "economic miracle" with its promise of unlimited growth and prosperity, and the first signs of recession in the mid-1960s, fostered a more questioning attitude in West Germany towards systemic and institutional structures. Politically, the end of the Adenauer era in 1963, the Grand Coalition of 1966 and the Social Democrats' rise to power on a non-socialist basis were both symptoms of change and causes of further reflection (and action); the extra-parliamentary opposition began precisely in this atmosphere of economic and political transformation. Many further factors could be listed that were at once results of and impulses for change: the Eichmann trial of 1960–1 and the first sustained attempts to come to terms with the Third Reich historically; the final realization, with the building of the Berlin Wall, that hopes for German unity were futile; the recognition that West Germany belonged to an "imperialist" alliance waging a brutal war of destruction in Vietnam; the rise of the student movement; and the coming of age of the first post-war — and hence post-Nazi — generation. All of these played a part in forming a new consciousness in West German society and contributed to the altered mentality of the late 1960s.

**Evidence:** The most visible manifestations of the crisis appeared in German language and literature. Young scholars, reacting both against the vestiges of National Socialist methodology and against the uncommitted, supposedly neutral scholarly practices introduced after the war, tried to rethink the possibilities of future research. One of the best-known documents of the crisis, *Ansichten einer künftigen Germanistik* (1969; *Views of a Future Germanistik*), called German studies a "much maligned discipline" and sought a way out of its prevailing "misery"; the collection closed with a "Memorandum for the reform of the study of linguistics and literature" proposing sweeping changes to academic programmes. Not coincidentally, some of the most important critics of reception theory's early phase — Jauss, Iser and Siegfried J. Schmidt — were co-signatories. Its sequel, *Neue Ansichten einer künftigen Germanistik* (1973), is even more telling: its first group of essays bears the heading "Problems of a social and reception history of literature", and the other two groups, "Critique of linguistics" and "Literary scholarship and communication research", also touch in part on categories associated with reception theory. The founders of reception theory were thus involved in both the institutional and the methodological restructuring of literary studies in West Germany.

**Mechanism:** The turn to reception theory as a possible resolution of the crisis in literary method was part of a more general concern with methodology arising from the attacks in and on the academy. Young scholars began to question the hidden and not-so-hidden assumptions of their predecessors; proseminars devoted to examining methodological procedures became an essential part of the curriculum; and the academic market was flooded with books on approaches to literature. Most of these methodological primers served a double function: first to warn beginners of the pitfalls of earlier methods, then to suggest alternatives in a thinly concealed undercurrent. The "formalist" methodology of Wolfgang Kayser's *Das sprachliche Kunstwerk* (1948) and Emil Staiger's *Die Kunst der Interpretation* (1955) was dismissed as obsolete or elitist, and the plea for reappropriating and redirecting a more historically grounded scholarship is unmistakable. In this climate of reconsidering past practice and searching for new paths, of methodological turmoil and revolt, it is easy to see why reception theory attracted a large following, and why proclaiming a "scientific revolution" was a timely and apt way of declaring opposition to the status quo.

**Mechanism:** A reception-oriented approach also seemed to answer another major concern of literary studies. As noted, one of the central tasks Jauss assigned to a successful paradigm was the mediation and reformulation of the canon, and in the second half of the 1960s just such a rethinking of the standard works of German literary history began. Influenced in part by scholarship in the German Democratic Republic — which from its inception had a very different idea of the literary heritage — and propagated by young scholars inside and outside West Germany, the accepted canon changed noticeably: Goethe, Schiller and the eternal perfections of German classicism were confronted with the revolutionary pamphlets and radical theatre of the German Jacobins; conservative mid-nineteenth-century writers such as Mörike and Stifter were largely supplanted by the committed lyric poets of the *Vormärz*; and the twentieth-century giants Kafka, Rilke and Thomas Mann had to share their laurels with newly discovered proletarian and socialist writers. Authors admitted to the old canon grudgingly, often on apolitical grounds, were re-examined too: Hölderlin descended from Heideggerian heights to Jacobin reality; Jean Paul was seen as part of a political opposition; Büchner was released from existential pessimism and steered towards the revolutionary avant-garde; Heine's Romantic lyrics became less important than his critical journalism and his friendship with Marx.

**Distinction:** Reception theory claimed competence to deal with this canonical dilemma in two ways, which often led to contradictory results. On the one hand, it offered a way of looking at the old canon anew and re-evaluating the past, thereby rescuing the old standards from this onslaught of insolent plundering; on the other hand, as Jauss's reference to mass media and popular literature makes clear, it provided a basis for analysing works traditionally excluded from the canon and the reasons for their exclusion. In the context of these canon struggles, the promise of a revised and continuously revisable canon was no small part of its appeal.

### Audience and reader in contemporary literature

**Evidence:** Perhaps more important than the promise of renewing the past was the ability to connect with the present, and here too reception theory was not wanting. If reception theory is understood as a general redirection of attention to the pole of the reader or audience, then several clear parallels appear between German literature of the 1960s and the shift in critical perspective. The rise of `documentary theatre` is perhaps the most obvious example: from Rolf Hochhuth's *The Deputy* (1963) and Heinar Kipphardt's *In the Matter of J. Robert Oppenheimer* (1964) to Peter Weiss's *The Investigation* (1965), one sees a development from concern with production and presentation to concentration on effect and response. In one documentary drama, Hans Magnus Enzensberger's *The Havana Inquiry* (1970), members of the audience took part in discussions after the performance and the whole evening was broadcast on national television, with the aim of maximizing audience involvement and effect. Two early plays by Peter Handke, *Offending the Audience* (1966) and *Kaspar* (1967), likewise sought to break down the traditional barriers between spectator and stage action with a variety of unconventional techniques.

The novel of the period also shows a growing concern with reader response. Alfred Andersch's *Efraim* (1967) tries to engage the reader by various devices, including specific and frequent references to the "presumed reader"; Jurek Becker's *Jakob the Liar* (1968) toys with reader expectations by offering two endings — first a happy ending that did not happen, then the actual course of events, which takes the protagonist to the gas chamber. Both works incorporate the reader into the narrative structure by anticipating responses and reflecting on the traditional ways in which texts communicate with an anonymous audience.

**Condition:** Structural changes in the literature of the 1960s, like any other isolated factor, cannot explain the rise of reception theory. The social, scholarly and literary considerations above offer at most an insight into the climate that made a change of method possible. The various aspects of German social life are not causes of which reception theory was the inevitable consequence, but `conditions of possibility` for the formulation, acceptance and popularity of this purportedly new perspective.

### Reception theory itself as a "reception"

**Principle:** "Methods", Jauss wrote at the beginning of the paradigm essay, "do not fall from the sky, but have their place in history." Reception theory is no exception: it developed in a conflict-ridden situation in German literary and political life and so took its place in complex dialogue and debate with other methods and traditions; it had not only to fend off competitors by declaring them obsolete or incomplete but also — especially in its early phase — to adopt a posture of rebellion and novelty.

**Mechanism:** The emergence of a new method claiming paradigmatic status inevitably generates a series of studies that trace its roots and thereby tarnish its claims to originality; in a sense a theory creates its own forerunners, and reception theory is no exception. "Precursors" in a general sense are not hard to find: Aristotle's *Poetics*, with catharsis as a central category of aesthetic experience, may count as the earliest theory in which audience response plays a major role; the whole tradition of rhetoric and its relation to poetics can likewise be seen as a precursor by virtue of its attention to the effect of oral and written communication on listeners and readers; in the German tradition, Lessing's exegesis of Aristotelian categories is the best-known instance of the effect of drama on the individual spectator being accorded substantial weight; and more broadly the whole aesthetic tradition of the eighteenth century, from Baumgarten's *Aesthetica* (1750) to Kant's *Critique of Judgment* (1790), relies as much on notions of what the work of art *does* as on what it *is*.

**Condition:** Establishing "influence", however, is more complex, and the principles involved are undoubtedly those reception theory itself developed. That a theory is appropriated at a particular time in a particular way is not simply a matter of the availability of material: the editorial policies of publishers and the acquisitions of libraries may have some effect on the development of theory, but they are never enough to determine influence; there must also be a readiness on the part of the intellectual community to rethink its methods, and only this readiness, coupled with an influx of theoretical impulses, can lead to a productive reception. What are called the "influences" or "precursors" of reception theory are therefore those theories or tendencies that appeared or reappeared in the 1960s and defined the intellectual climate in which reception theory could flourish. On this basis five influences are marked as precursors: Russian Formalism, Prague structuralism, Ingarden's phenomenology, Gadamer's hermeneutics and the "sociology of literature". They were chosen either because they had a noticeable impact on the theory's development (as the footnotes and sources of the leading reception theorists show) or because they refocused attention on the text-reader relationship and so helped resolve the crisis in literary studies just described. In most cases they directly influenced the so-called "Constance School" — a name covering not only those who taught or studied at the University of Constance but also the regular participants in the biennial colloquia held there since the mid-1960s. Concentrating on this group's precursors is justified because its members did the most to shape reception theory's international reputation, and because this school seems responsible for the principles that sparked the critical direction in various circles. Marxist or empiricist variants of reception theory could of course point to other significant influences.

**Condition:** Two warnings must accompany the discussion of these precursors. First, they cannot be treated in their entirety: each contains a wealth of views extending well beyond our topic, and only the aspects relevant to reception theory, or that contributed to a changed perspective in literary studies, are treated at length. Second, even with respect to the Constance School the survey makes no claim to completeness: many works are omitted, either because their influence was confined to a small branch of literary studies or because they are themselves less directly concerned with literature. What is included are the theories that dominated the literary scene of the late 1960s and early 1970s — the most frequently cited works of aesthetic or literary import. Gadamer's hermeneutics was discussed in P7; the other four are the subject of P9.

**Conclusion:** *The rise of reception theory can be described in reception theory's own terms: a "horizon of expectations" formed by economic, political, generational and disciplinary crisis made a method claiming to centre on the reader and on history look like the answer to the questions of the age; and its "precursors" were selected retrospectively by this very act of reception. The same logic will explain its later fortunes: once the student movement ebbed and the universities no longer sought innovation and upheaval, Jauss's more mature theory of aesthetic experience met with indifference (see P10).*

## P9 Theoretical sources of the turn to the reader

### Perception constitutes art: Shklovsky's device and defamiliarization

**Background:** To say that `Russian Formalism` contributed a great deal to reception theory may puzzle English-speaking readers: in the English-speaking world this major early-twentieth-century movement is more often associated with New Criticism or structuralism. The associations are not unfounded. The Formalists' concern with the construction of the literary work and their detailed analyses of metre and poetic form seem to ally them with the New Critics, who likewise privileged textual analysis; their reliance on the linguistic features of texts and their general concern with the nature of language have led to their identification with Parisian structuralism — a connection repeatedly stressed by the structuralists and their expositors. Yet the importance of Formalism for German criticism of the late 1960s is hard to overlook. From the German vantage point of the 1970s, what mattered was not the concentration on the work itself or the linguistic roots and offshoots, but the shift of viewpoint to the relationship between text and reader: by expanding the notion of form to include aesthetic perception, by defining the work of art as the sum of its "devices", and by directing attention to the process of interpretation itself, the Formalists fostered a new mode of interpretation closely related to reception theory; and in literary history, their postulate of "literary evolution" — schools contending for dominance — has a strong echo in recent German theory. One must therefore acknowledge that, alongside the structuralist lineage from Moscow to Paris, intellectual history contains a parallel line of descent from the Formalists to the modern German reception theorists.

**Principle:** The shift from the author-work pole to the text-reader relationship is perhaps clearest in the early writings of **Viktor Shklovsky**. Refuting Potebnya's dictum that "art is thinking in images", he appeals almost at once to a principle of perception: imagery is not what constitutes literature; it is merely a means of creating the strongest impression, one poetic device among many for maximizing effect. In examining art we should begin not with symbols or metaphors — themselves instruments of effect — but with the general laws of perception. It is in this domain that he finds the guiding principle for analysing and evaluating works of art: everyday perception, bound up with practical language, tends to become habitual or `automatized`; the "algebrization" or automatization of perception necessarily leads to a failure to "see" the object — one merely "recognizes" it, perceives it in a habitual way.

Art, by contrast, functions to dehabituate our perception and bring objects back to life. The role of the recipient therefore becomes paramount: in a sense, it is the perceiver who decides the artistic quality of a work, since an object may be created as prosaic and perceived as poetic, or vice versa. Shklovsky writes that the poetic artistry attributed to an object is the result of our perception; artistic objects in the narrow sense are those created by special devices whose purpose is to ensure that they are perceived as artistic as surely as possible. Despite the circularity of the definition, the drift is clear: only objects perceived as art — as departures from habitual, automatized perception — deserve the epithet "artistic". *What constitutes art is perception rather than creation, reception rather than production.*

**Definition:** Given this epistemological-aesthetic foundation, broadly shared by the early Formalists, it is easy to see why the `device` (*priem*) became the central tool of literary analysis: the device is the means by which we become aware of objects, the technique that makes things perceptible and turns them into art. Hence **Roman Jakobson**, in a 1919 lecture on contemporary Russian poetry, placed the concept at the centre of literary interpretation: the object of literary scholarship is not literature but `literariness` (*literaturnost'*), the quality that makes a given work a work of literature; and since literariness is defined by devices, attending to devices is the task of criticism — if literary study wants to become a science, it must accept the "device" as its only "hero".

**Distinction:** The meaning and use of the concept varied considerably among the Formalists. In that lecture Jakobson counted as "devices" the play of suffixes, the juxtaposition of two units (parallelism, comparison, metaphor) and a variety of other linguistic features; Boris Eikhenbaum, analysing Gogol's "The Overcoat", spoke of the dominant device of *skaz* and sought to show how the individual devices are linked; **Yury Tynyanov** warned against a static view of the device, whose importance lies not in its mere presence but in its function within literary evolution. Three factors seem to unite these diverse uses. First, the device is considered only as a formal element, a matter of how the work is made: its domain is construction, not content. Second, it operates against a particular background, whether practical language or literary tradition. Third, and most importantly, it is the element that bridges the gap between text and reader, making the work a valuable, genuinely aesthetic object.

**Definition:** The concept most often associated with the device, however, is Shklovsky's `defamiliarization` (*ostranenie*, "making strange"). From his early essays it is hard to tell whether defamiliarization is one type of device or the defining feature of devices as such; in any case it denotes a special relationship between reader and text, removing the object from its usual sphere of perception, and in that sense it is constitutive of all art. As he writes, the device of art is the device of "making strange" objects and of making form difficult, increasing the difficulty and length of perception, because in art the process of perception is an end in itself and must be prolonged.

**Example:** Shklovsky illustrates the device with a series of examples from Tolstoy. In the essay "Shame" Tolstoy does not name flogging but describes the procedure — stripping people who have broken the law, throwing them to the floor and rapping on their buttocks with switches — and asks why precisely this stupid, savage means of causing pain should be used, rather than, say, pricking the shoulders or another part of the body with needles; by changing its form without changing its nature, a familiar institution is made to appear absurd. In "Kholstomer" the story is told by a horse, who is puzzled that humans apply the word "my" to horses, land and even other people — an indictment of private property. In *War and Peace* battles are depicted in a strange light to heighten perception. These examples actually involve two functions of defamiliarization.
