On the one hand, the devices illuminate linguistic and social conventions and compel the reader to see them in a new, critical light — and although Shklovsky draws no conclusions from this method of exposing injustice, this side comes closest to what Brecht called the "alienation effect". On the other hand, the devices direct attention to form itself, in a sense forcing the reader to disregard social consequences and to focus on the process of defamiliarization as an artistic element. From the standpoint of reception theory, what matters most is that Shklovsky has formulated a basic component of the reading process: defamiliarization, though introduced by the author for manipulative or perceptual purposes, is a process that establishes the relation between reader and text — and literature as art is defined by this activity.

### Mechanisms of literary evolution: laying bare the device, system and dominant

**Definition:** Attention to the devices of defamiliarization naturally drew the Formalists to authors and movements that deliberately reveal the nature of literature by `laying bare the device` (*obnazhenie priema*). Avant-garde poets, satirists and parodists were especially important, because their writing functions to make readers aware of the work as art. Laying bare the device can be seen as defamiliarization raised to the second power: defamiliarization merely exposes conventions and directs attention back to form; laying bare reveals the function of the device itself in constituting art. Jakobson invokes the notion in discussing the Futurist poets Mayakovsky and Khlebnikov, two lyricists who play with conventions in unusual ways so that technique itself becomes visible. A similar idea appears in Shklovsky's famous study of *Tristram Shandy*: Sterne, a "revolutionary of form", differs from his predecessors in presenting artistic form as such, without any motivation. Shklovsky sees a parallel between Sterne and the Futurists, and his claim that *Tristram Shandy* is "the most typical novel in world literature" is simply the logical consequence of the Formalist theory of the device.

**Mechanism:** Tynyanov took a more differentiated and more evolutionary view. He recognized that laying bare the device can itself become automatized — as happened with Sterne's imitators at the end of the eighteenth century — at which point what is needed is to *conceal* the device once more; only thus can the dynamism of literary history be explained. But here too he relies on an orientation consonant with reception theory: his revision of Shklovsky's concept still depends on the perception of the work of art — the device must be concealed only once readers become aware that baring has become habitual.

**Example:** The reader's role in the literary process extends beyond attention to the formal nature of devices. Boris Tomashevsky's essay "Literature and biography" is a case in point. Given the Formalists' rejection of traditional scholarship relying on the "life and works" method, one would expect biography to be banished from critical study — and so it is, if the scholar uses biography to illuminate the author's life, but not if one examines its *literary function*. For Tomashevsky the relation between biography and literature is a matter not of the work's genesis or description but of its reception: before answering the question we must remember that creative literature exists not for the literary historian but for the reader, and we must examine how the poet's biography operates in the reader's consciousness. The actual life may be of interest as a cultural phenomenon, but for the literary historian only the legend of the author's life — the `ideal biography` — matters: the image of Pushkin, Rousseau or Voltaire in readers' minds is instrumental in interpreting and evaluating their works. Not every author, of course — not even every author who is not strictly anonymous — has a relevant biography: there are "writers with biographies" and "writers without biographies", for in some periods readers take no interest in the artist's personality; only with the "individualization of creation" do the author's name and personality begin to function in our perception. In these cases the legend created by the author (or, one might say, by posterity) becomes a "literary fact". From the reader's viewpoint, the ideal biography is a necessary mediating element between text and reader.

**Mechanism:** Another area in which Formalist theory strongly influenced reception theory is literary history. For the Formalists, the process of art can be seen as the development and application of the device. Since Shklovsky defines the device by its capacity to defamiliarize perception, and since what is familiar is determined in part by current literary practice, artistic change comes about through the rejection of prevailing artistic models. The result, as Fredric Jameson put it, is a "permanent revolution of art", successive schools replacing outworn techniques with conspicuous and provocative formal innovations. Yet the political metaphor misses an important aspect of Formalist literary history: a certain continuity can be established within this "revolutionary" process, one that does not consist merely in negating predecessors. Shklovsky stresses that a new literary school necessarily draws on a forgotten or non-dominant heritage for its basic principles; inheritance passes not from father to son but from uncle to nephew. Literary history thus does "progress", but along a broken rather than a straight line. He also concedes that matters are less simple in practice: a new hegemony is usually not a pure restoration of earlier forms but includes features from other emerging schools, and even features (now subordinate) inherited from the predecessor on the throne. On the whole, though, the "dialectical self-production of new forms" defines the nature of literary history and explains the rise and fall of literary schools.

**Mechanism:** Tynyanov again considerably revised Shklovsky's rather one-sided view, though he shared most of his fellow Formalist's basic tenets: he seems to retain the notion of formal innovation as the decisive factor in literary history (and hence the inner-literary character of the whole process), as well as the thesis of development by leaps and struggles. But by 1929, in his important essay "On literary evolution", he adopted two theses that take him far beyond Shklovsky. The first concerns the *systemic* and *functional* character of literature: with these concepts he can conceive of literary evolution as the "replacement of systems", and by distinguishing "auto-functions" (elements interrelated within a literary system) from "syn-functions" (elements interrelated within a single work) he can explain in a more differentiated way the struggles, replacements, distortions and parodies that characterize changes in artistic technique. The second concept grows out of this functional approach: the `dominant`, the element or group of elements foregrounded in a given work or period. Literary succession can then be seen as the continual replacement of one set of dominants by another; what is replaced does not leave the system entirely but recedes into the background, to reappear later in a new way.

**Influence:** The fruitfulness of this account for reception theory is evident: it helps explain not only changes in the literary canon but also shifts of critical emphasis in judging "great" works in different periods — if a work's interpretive potential is rich enough, a change of dominant will simply lead to the recognition and appreciation of features previously unnoticed. These ideas about the dynamics of literary history bear on Jauss's "horizon of expectations" as well as on Iser's "blanks" and "indeterminacy" (see P10, P11).

### The stratified structure of the literary work and places of indeterminacy: Ingarden

**Background:** The fortunes of **Roman Ingarden**'s work, as varied as those of Russian Formalism, could themselves serve as a case study in reception theory. A student of Husserl concerned chiefly with philosophical questions, Ingarden turned to the literary work of art out of more theoretical interests. He wrote in 1930 that the literary work of art offered him a perfect object: its purely intentional nature is beyond doubt, and through it one can study the essential structures of the mode of being of purely intentional objects without being misled by suggestions arising from the consideration of real objects. His ultimate motive for turning to literary theory was bound up directly with his investigation of the problem of idealism and realism, since in his view the literary work of art falls outside this dichotomy; the subtitle of *The Literary Work of Art* — "An investigation on the borderlines of ontology, logic and theory of literature" — underscores this philosophical aim.

**Distinction:** Yet the two most important responses to Ingarden's work, while not denying its phenomenological ties, both downplayed his principal philosophical concerns. **René Wellek**'s reception linked him with the principles of New Criticism: though Ingarden's name rarely appears in *Theory of Literature* (1949), his work was clearly important to Wellek's thinking, especially the discussion of the literary work's "mode of existence". What tied Ingarden to Wellek and to Anglo-American New Criticism as a whole was less the realism-idealism problem than his insistence on "intrinsic" analysis of literature — both held that the work itself should be the focus of study. Another dimension of Ingarden's theory emerged in Germany over the past fifteen years: his earlier influence there was likewise associated with the "intrinsic" approach, but he has more recently become known for his studies of the reading process and of the cognition of the literary work. The divergence stems partly from the reception of two different books: Wellek and the New Critics knew *The Literary Work of Art*, whereas the 1968 German publication of *The Cognition of the Literary Work of Art* made Ingarden's concern with the relation between text and reader much clearer to future reception theorists. But neither the New Critics nor the reception theorists seem to have been interested in the larger philosophical questions that drew Ingarden to the subject.

**Definition:** Ingarden's analysis of cognition — the part of his work most influential in recent German criticism — rests on his conception of the literary work of art. He considers the literary work a `purely intentional object`, or heteronomous object: one that is neither determinate nor autonomous, as real and ideal objects are, but dependent on acts of consciousness. It consists of four *strata*, each affecting the others, and two distinct *dimensions*. The first stratum comprises the "raw material" of literature, the "word-sounds" and the phonetic formations built upon them, including not only the sound configurations that carry meaning but also the potential for special aesthetic effects such as rhythm and rhyme. The second includes all "meaning units", whether words, sentences or units of several sentences. The third and fourth consist of the "represented objects" and the `schematized aspects` through which these objects appear. The totality of the four strata — the first dimension of the literary work — produces what Ingarden calls a "polyphonic harmony" associated with aesthetic value; the second, temporal dimension comprises the sequence of sentences, paragraphs and chapters in the work.

**Mechanism:** Especially important for Ingarden's theory of cognition is that these strata and dimensions form a skeleton or "schematized structure" to be completed by the reader. This is easiest to see in the third and fourth strata, which concern represented objects. Real objects are "unequivocally, universally determined" in every respect — there is no point at which a real object is not fully determined — whereas the objects represented in a literary work exhibit `places of indeterminacy` (*Unbestimmtheitsstellen*). As he writes, wherever it is impossible, on the basis of the sentences in the work, to say whether a certain object or objective situation has a certain attribute, we find a place of indeterminacy. On phenomenological theory every object has infinitely many determinations, and no act of cognition can take account of all the determinations of any particular object; but a real object must have a specific determination — it cannot simply be "coloured", it must be some particular colour — whereas objects in a literary work, being intentionally projected from meaning units and aspects, necessarily retain some degree of indeterminacy.

**Example:** Reading the sentence "The child was bouncing a ball", we confront countless "gaps" or places of indeterminacy in the represented object: whether the child is ten or six, a boy or a girl, brown-skinned or white, red-haired or blond — none of these features is contained in the sentence, and so each constitutes a gap. Every child must have an age, a sex, a skin colour and a hair colour; but even if this or later sentences determined these attributes, others would necessarily remain undetermined. A text can of course limit, or at least suggest, the range of indeterminacy without mentioning details: if the sentence occurred in a novel set in Sweden, we might be inclined to imagine a fair-haired white child. But no number of details or hints can eliminate all indeterminacy; in theory every literary work, indeed every represented object or aspect, contains infinitely many places of indeterminacy.

### Concretization and concretion: the standard of adequacy and its classical bias

**Mechanism:** In reading we interact with the literary work in many ways; on Ingarden's account our cognition is active on every stratum of the work. The stratum of word-sounds can be made manifest by reading aloud, or by realizing sounds and sound configurations in silent reading; any reading worthy of the name will realize most of the meaning units; and gaps in the work's temporal structure (its second dimension) must also be bridged for the text to be intelligible.

**Definition:** But perhaps the reader's most important activity is removing or filling in the places of indeterminacy, gaps or schematized aspects of the text. Ingarden usually calls this activity `concretization` (*Konkretisation*), although (especially in *The Literary Work of Art*) he also uses the term to distinguish the apprehended literary work from its skeletal structure, the aesthetic object from the artefact. Concretization in the narrow sense denotes any "supplementary determination", any active step the reader takes to fill a place of indeterminacy. Though often unconscious, this activity is, for Ingarden at least, an essential part of apprehending the literary work of art: without it the aesthetic work and its represented world would never emerge from the schematic structure. In concretizing, the reader also has occasion to exercise imagination: filling in what is undetermined requires creativity, and Ingarden implies that it requires skill and insight as well. Because concretization is the activity of individual readers, it can vary enormously: personal experience, mood and a host of other contingent factors affect each concretization, so that no two are ever exactly alike, even by the same reader.

**Distinction:** In a wider sense Ingarden uses the word for the *result* of actualizing potentialities in a given text, objectifying meaning units and concretizing indeterminacies: it occurs when the aspects acquire concreteness and are "raised to the level of perceptual experience (in the case of drama) or imaginative experience (in reading)". To avoid confusion, concretization in this sense may be called `concretion` (*Konkretion*). Ingarden is careful not to identify the concretion with the apprehension of the work or with any psychological state: although its existence is conditioned by the reader's corresponding experiences, it is co-determined by the literary work, and with respect to the experiences of apprehension it is just as "transcendent" as the literary work itself. The number of concretions is infinite, while the work itself remains unchanged; Ingarden thus draws a strict theoretical distinction between the stable structure of the work and what readers do when they actualize it.

**Distinction:** Moreover, concretizing a literary work may involve aesthetic experience, but this is only one possible experience of literature. Ingarden first distinguishes non-aesthetic or extra-aesthetic experiences — a classicist reading the *Iliad* to teach ancient Greek customs, say — from aesthetic experience proper; he also discusses at length two further, more scholarly ways of relating to literary works: the *pre-aesthetic cognition* of the work, and the *reflective aesthetic cognition* of a concretion. The latter is bound up with evaluation; the former yields a "reconstruction" of the literary work, an examination of its skeletal structure establishing the "artistic values" that make concretization — and, in genuine works of literature, aesthetic value — possible. In theory, scholars should be able to agree on the reconstruction of a literary work of art.

**Counterexample:** It is precisely here that one should begin to challenge Ingarden's system. Even granting that concretions of the same work necessarily differ from reader to reader and even from reading to reading, why suppose that absolute agreement can be reached about the structure that makes these concretions possible? We may concede some stable structure — at the level of the written marks on the page it is hard to avoid that conclusion — but it does not follow that this structure is wholly identifiable, that it can be defined in the terms Ingarden prescribes ("places of indeterminacy"), or that it is immune to the contingent factors that affect concretization. If indeterminacy in a text is always infinite, the theoretical agreement Ingarden hoped for is hard to imagine, even using the "sober analytical procedure" he advocated. One gets the impression that, faced with empirically verifiable indeterminacy at the levels of concretization and evaluation (*de gustibus non est disputandum*), Ingarden simply shifts determinacy from the level of concretization to that of the schematic structure. The shift may solve the theoretical problem of differences in interpretation and taste, but it brings us closer to determinate statements about texts only in the realm of pure theory.

**Limitation:** Claiming determinacy at the skeletal level has further consequences for Ingarden's view of the literary work and of the reader's activity. Since gaps and places of indeterminacy can be described precisely, he holds that it should also be possible to set limits to concretization, and so he posits `adequate` and inadequate concretizations. The distinction is not unreasonable in itself — especially if outlandish and arbitrary interpretations are to be curbed in some way — but Ingarden's linking of adequate concretization to traditional literary norms is a serious problem for his theory. His normative or "classical" bias can be traced to his very description of the work of art: though he occasionally tries to fit more modern movements such as Expressionism into his framework, the overwhelming weight of traditional terminology and traditional canonical examples suggests that he neglects, if he does not entirely exclude, non-realist, non-mimetic and experimental works; his literary work of art is repeatedly associated with loaded terms such as "harmony", "polyphony", "crystallizing centre" and "unity" — concepts that define the individual work and literature as a whole in terms of traditional poetics. This tendency to set "classical" norms for the work and its reception is most evident in his discussion of `metaphysical qualities`: his conviction that "the literary work of art attains its culmination in the manifestation of metaphysical qualities" has drastic consequences both for his view of literature and for the role of the reader. On the one hand, despite the stratified structure, it leads him to assume an "organic unity" of the work in which the strata must cooperate harmoniously in definite ways and satisfy specific conditions; on the other, metaphysical qualities become the controlling force in the reader's concretization: an inadequate concretization is equated with the reader's inability or unwillingness to realize the work as a whole possessing the corresponding metaphysical qualities.

**Conclusion:** Ingarden thus conceives the reader as an ideal individual, detached from and independent of any larger collective; political or class questions are regarded merely as obstacles to concretization, as unwelcome and to be avoided as inattentiveness to the text. *The main weakness of Ingarden's phenomenological perspective, then, lies less in his insistence on adequate concretization than in his failure to account for the fact that works and their recipients are always situated.* It is precisely this fundamental weakness that the theorists of the Prague School avoided when they turned to semiotics.

### The work as social sign: Mukařovský and Vodička

**Background:** Like the writings of the Russian Formalists, the work of **Jan Mukařovský**, the most important literary theorist of the Prague structuralist school, was virtually unknown in Germany before the late 1960s; apart from a few essays written in French and German in the 1930s, the West gained access to his main achievements only relatively recently. In the English-speaking world the harmful effects of this underexposure persist: despite two recent volumes of essays in English translation, he has received far less attention than he deserves, and even those who promoted French structuralism — in many respects the intellectual heir of the Prague School — have shown little interest in his work. In Germany, by contrast, his work became one of the most dominant theoretical forces of the late 1960s and early 1970s: between 1967 and 1974 many of his most important writings were translated into German and immediately became a focus of critical discussion from many angles; in those years any mention of reception theory or structuralism almost inevitably brought Mukařovský's name with it.

**Counterexample:** This popularity may stem partly from his being seen as a continuator of the Formalist tradition, particularly in developing the reception-oriented side of that school. Until the early 1930s he seems to have accepted the basic Formalist tenet that literary analysis should never transgress the boundaries set by the work itself; but by the mid-1930s his theoretical position had clearly shifted — he came to recognize that immanent analysis of the text, and even an evolutionary literary history of Tynyanov's kind, are inadequate to the complexity of the literary work, above all to the relation between literature and society. In a 1934 essay on the Czech translation of Shklovsky's *Theory of Prose* one can see his cautious but principled dissent from his Formalist predecessor. He first takes care to explain the necessity of Formalism, excusing Shklovsky's occasional "one-sidedness" by the state of criticism when he wrote: Formalism was merely Shklovsky's "battle cry" against traditional literary theory; Formalism in the strict sense never existed, and did not correspond to reality even when it was accepted as a programmatic statement. He thereby salvages part of Shklovsky's work, arguing not only that the theory was historically necessary but that the Formalists' real achievements were not formalist at all — from the outset Shklovsky tended towards structuralism: by understanding the structure of the work of art as a complex semantic construction he destroyed the harmful antinomy of form and content, and so took the first step towards elucidating the sign character of art. A "pure" formalism that recognizes only autonomous development within literature is the mirror image of traditional criticism, which stresses only external forces; what Mukařovský found in Shklovsky, and would place at the centre of his own theory, is the mutual permeation of social reality and literary text. His conclusion is that structuralism represents the overcoming of the opposition between formalism and traditionalism.

**Definition:** His stimulus to reception theory is clearest in his conception of art as a dynamic `sign system`. On this conception every work of art is a structure that inherently refers to what preceded it; structure is therefore not independent of history but formed and determined by a diachronic series. Nor is structure limited in size or scope: the individual work is only one instance of structure — an author's oeuvre, contemporary art forms, even national or international literatures can be studied structurally. Nor should structures be conceived as independent, self-contained entities: any change in a single structure — the discovery of a lost work by an author, for example — necessarily alters the perception of other related structures.

**Mechanism:** More importantly, these structures function as *signs*. Mukařovský calls the work of art itself a complex sign, a "semiotic fact" mediating between artist and audience (viewers, listeners, readers). Viewing the work semiotically allows him to escape both psychologizing theories that identify art with the mental states of the artist or perceiving subject, and theories that treat art as a mere reflection of social reality; with these two obstacles removed, the work of art is placed in a context favourable to the study of aesthetic response. He goes on to argue that the work's sign character operates in two ways. As a *communicative* sign the work resembles *parole*, an actual utterance within a given language system — though he cautions that the whole work should be understood as the "message", not as "content" poured into a meaningless container called "form". As an *autonomous structure* the work has three components: the `work-thing` (the artefact, the sensory sign, corresponding to Saussure's "signifier"); the "aesthetic object, lodged in the social consciousness and functions as 'meaning'" (corresponding to the "signified"); and the relation to the thing signified, the referential aspect of the sign.

**Distinction:** Both functions bring the recipient into the analytical framework and so foster a reception-oriented perspective; but the second component of the work in its autonomous function — the aesthetic object — is perhaps the most illuminating for literary theory, because the emphasis on `social consciousness` is precisely what distinguishes Mukařovský from Ingarden (who likewise separates the artefact from its aesthetic concretization) and from the Russian Formalists (for whom social reality was an unwelcome intruder on critical territory). In Mukařovský's theory the perceiving subject is not an autonomous, idealized individual — neither the abstract phenomenological subject nor an ideal perceiver thoroughly versed in literary history; the recipient is itself a product of social relations. Although his writings still speak of "the viewer" and "the perceiver" in the singular, they stress the collective processes involved in the reception of art. In his pioneering essay *Aesthetic Function, Norm and Value as Social Facts* (1936) he affirms the social nature of both sign and recipient: by viewing the work of art as a social sign and the viewer as "a social being, a member of a collective", he avoids aesthetic subjectivism as well as the idealizing tendencies of the phenomenological and Formalist positions. *His semiotics can incorporate sociology not by positing a mechanical reflection of an already constituted reality but by presupposing reality's prior permeation of artistic structures and their recipients.*

**Mechanism:** The turn to a sociologically informed aesthetics of reception is most clearly marked in his discussion of artistic `norms`. The sociological aspect, he argues, cannot be separated from the study of norms: approaching aesthetic norms through sociology is not merely one possible or auxiliary route but, together with the epistemological side of the problem, a basic requirement of research, because it allows a detailed study of the dialectical contradiction between the diversity and variability of aesthetic norms and their claim to constant validity. Ingarden defined his standards by classical ideals, and the Formalists conceived norms within the purely literary realm of form; for Mukařovský the social interaction and movement of norms are what matter most. Though closer to the Formalists in relying on perceptibility and the disruption of habit, he recognizes that social classes and extra-aesthetic social relations play an important part in establishing and changing norms; and unlike the Formalists, who attended only to the avant-garde or "high art", he observes how "high art" penetrates the various strata of society and how folk art influences the so-called avant-garde. His two most important conclusions about artistic norms are that norms are not static, eternal constructs, and that the coexistence of several different, even conflicting, norms is a common phenomenon. *With some justice, his concept of the norm can be said to historicize Ingarden's rigid classicism and to socialize the Formalists' closed notion of "literariness".*

**Example:** His orientation towards the perceiver's role in constituting the aesthetic object has far-reaching consequences for a whole range of traditional literary terms, of which `intentionality` is one. Like Wimsatt and Beardsley in the New Critical movement, he denies the relevance of the creator's mental states and declines to treat intentionality as a psychological matter concerning the conscious and unconscious components of the creative process. In keeping with his view of the work of art as an autonomous sign, he first removes the work from any goal-directed activity: since the creator necessarily adopts a practical attitude towards the work — whether overcoming technical difficulties in its production or simply aiming to finish it — he is not in a position to grasp intentionality. The perceiver, free from considerations of purpose, can view the work as an autonomous sign, and so only the perceiver can confer on it the semantic unity subsequently identified with intentionality. He thus arrives at a paradoxical observation: for understanding the artistic intention inherent in the work, what is basic or "unmarked" is not the creator's attitude towards the work but the perceiver's; the author or producer can grasp intentionality only by assuming the role of perceiver — by ceasing to view the work as a task to be completed or a product.

**Example:** His contribution to the problem of reception also extends to what is now called the sociology of art: the establishment of aesthetic norms, the evaluation of works and even the function of art in a given society cannot be separated from the conditions of artistic production, the possibilities of distribution and a series of factors bound up with the state of the market. By 1934 he had recognized the importance of these considerations, as his rejoinder to Shklovsky's position on the sociology of literature makes clear. Shklovsky had likened the literary text to a fabric, saying he wished to study only "the kinds of yarn" and "the methods of weaving", not "the state of the world cotton market". Mukařovský, without retreating to the old biographical or vulgar-sociological "milieu" approach, replied from a structuralist standpoint: literature belongs to the realm of social phenomena made up of many series or structures; one can affirm the relative autonomy of any one series, but none is completely independent — "the state of the cotton market", through its connection with the finished fabric, is intimately bound up with the problem of weaving. Hence the role of patronage, the book market, publishers, museums and book clubs are all legitimate concerns of literary study.

**Mechanism:** Mukařovský's ties to the problem of reception are manifold, but they are scattered across various contexts of his work; he never treated reception as a unified field of study. His student **Felix Vodička** did more to establish reception as a legitimate area of research: among the three basic tasks he outlined for literary history, reception-related questions form the third group. Vodička's own contribution lies in his attempt to reconcile Ingarden's phenomenological approach with his teacher's structuralist model. He adopted Ingarden's concept of concretization but rejected the idea of an ideal concretion, linking the term to the development of aesthetic norms so as to overcome its ahistorical limitation; he also widened its scope — where Ingarden stressed the concretization of schematized aspects, Vodička insisted that when time, place or social conditions change, the structure of the whole work acquires new qualities. A concept of concretization of this kind copes better with empirically observed differences of taste, or with changes in the popularity of individual works and even of whole canons.

**Limitation:** His focus on actual literary history, however, tells against him when he introduces the critic as the *arbiter* of appropriate concretization: for Vodička, the critic's function is to fix the concretizations of literary works and integrate them into the system of literary values. With such a model of the critic, one can say he simply replaces Ingarden's norm of the ideal, adequate concretization with an ideal figure who formulates the concretization for a particular age; the two theories differ only in that Vodička acknowledges the potential diversity of responses. What is lost in both cases is the most appealing part of Mukařovský's semiotic model — the sociological situating of the work and its recipients.

### Fame, taste and fantasy: reception studies in the sociology of literature

**Background:** The social situating of literary texts that Gadamer's ontological hermeneutics lacked would seem to be the task of the so-called "sociology of literature". Before the Second World War this branch of literary study was poorly developed in Germany, so sociologically oriented precursors of reception theory are few; and the handful of studies in the field share a recurring theme — complaints about the previous neglect of the area and calls for more research on the effect and reception of literature.

**Example:** In 1932 **Leo Löwenthal** could still note the lack of interest in this side of the critical enterprise, and he took the lack itself to be an important indicator of the state of literary studies: it is sociologically noteworthy, he wrote, that a task so important and central to research as the study of the effect of literary works has been almost entirely neglected, although newspapers, letters and memoirs offer endless material on the reception of literature by particular social groups and individuals. Though he stressed the wealth of readily available information, his chief interest lay not in empirical research — which would merely perpetuate the "mere philology" and "data collection" that reception studies were meant to replace — but in a more thorough exploration of the psycho-sociological features of social structure. Psychology supplies the link that makes aesthetics possible: there is no poetic aesthetics without a psychology of art, without the study of the unconscious stimuli in the psychological triangle of writer, literature and recipient; Freud's psychology is indispensable for dealing with the sociology of reception and for examining the relation between work and recipient, which had repeatedly been pushed into the background. His contribution to a socio-psychological theory of reception was a pioneering study of the reception of Dostoevsky in Germany before the First World War. By examining essays and books on the Russian novelist, he was able to show his appropriation by, and theoretical significance for, particular groups in Wilhelmine Germany: Dostoevsky supplied the German middle class with a series of myths, becoming an ideological crutch for individuals caught between a powerful upper class and a rapidly rising proletariat.

**Principle:** More important than the conclusions of this case study are the general theoretical statements that underpin it. Löwenthal insists on the first page that the effect of a literary work belongs to its very existence: what it is is essentially determined by how it is experienced. And human experience is itself largely predetermined, so analysing the reception of an author's work involves understanding the social "life process". Literature intersects with society in complex ways. On the one hand, it satisfies for particular social groups psychological needs that would otherwise threaten the social order — art in general can contribute to `fantasy gratification`, in which an inherent process of internalization substitutes for real social satisfaction. Studying the reception and consumption of literature is therefore not only the study of an essential literary problem but a contribution to social analysis, since it examines factors that "perform socially conservative and retarding functions through their psychological force, outside the mere apparatus of power". On the other hand, it would be undialectical to reduce art's function to ideology and psychological pacification; art also contains resistance to society: in the social process as a whole it does serve to reconcile people to the existing order, but it also contains an element of dissatisfaction that has to be reconciled; its very texture in principle holds resistance to and contradiction of the existing order. *For Löwenthal, reception is both socially conditioned and psychologically conditioning; both ideology and resistance to ideology; both the satisfaction of needs and the displacement of satisfaction.*

**Example:** This socio-psychological approach to reception produced not only an exemplary case study but also several revolutionary implications for general literary theory. But Löwenthal concentrated on a particular case in a particular period of German history (1880–1920); a theoretical framework covering the problem of reception as such must include not only sociology and psychology but also reflection on basic questions of historiography — for, as Jauss and others have pointed out, reception is closely tied to the writing of history: why a work or an author becomes famous, how fame endures over time, what factors increase or diminish reputation — these questions concern historians as much as sociologists and psychologists. A notable exception in early-twentieth-century Germany is **Julian Hirsch**'s *Die Genesis des Ruhmes* (1914; *The Genesis of Fame*), with the telling subtitle "A contribution to historical methodology" (not to be confused with E. D. Hirsch, the theorist of interpretation discussed in P15). His task was to examine how and why judgements of fame arise, and this way of posing the question was itself a radical break with earlier practice. The usual question had been how outstanding individuals arise and what effect they have on their own and later times — a formulation implying that eminence is an objective fact and that the individual determines his effects, rather than that the conditions producing the effects determine the individual's eminence. By shifting the emphasis from the object (the eminent individual) to the subject of evaluation or perception (the individuals or groups who confer fame), Hirsch posed the question from the standpoint of reception theory and so avoided the objectivist trap.

He examines the factors that make up the conditions under which an individual is judged famous. Fame for him means recognition by the masses, so the most important factors are the institutions that foster general recognition of "eminence": schools, newspapers, popular magazines, as well as more traditional vehicles such as works of art. These institutions do not merely shape the subject's views; in some cases, even when a dissenting view happens to form, it is rejected for deviating from the norm. He gives a hypothetical example: a Shakespeare scholar told from childhood that Shakespeare is the greatest English writer, who later reads in magazines of Shakespeare's genius and mastery of dramatic technique, can hardly be expected to adopt any attitude towards the English poet but admiration; "the power of social heredity has already weighed so heavily on the future researcher that he can no longer escape it"; and should he happen to give a negative judgement, other experts would regard it as foolish or sacrilegious. This miniature of a scholarly career reveals not only that the effect of a work or a person is inseparable from the history of its effects, and that social conditions predetermine our evaluations and their influence, but also that we judge past individuals by their phenomenal form — by how they appear to us — rather than by what they are or were. He therefore proposes to supplement biography with what he calls *Phänographik* ("phenography"), the study of individuals as phenomena: if the essence of an individual's life includes his influence and fame, the historian or biographer cannot do without phenography — any individual who has "appeared" in some way, that is, produced any mass effect, whether artist or man of action, can be known only when the development of his phenomenal form is presented. This demand resembles many statements of the reception theorists and implies a historiography that replaces historicism with what Gadamer, in an ontological context, called "effective history": instead of an objective portrayal of events and individuals, a history of how events and individuals became events and individuals "for us".

**Example:** Suggestive as Hirsch's study was for literary history, it had no influence on the next generation of literary scholars: literary history continued largely to be seen as the production and description of works rather than their reception. One of the few alternatives was proposed by **Levin Schücking**. Unlike Hirsch, who focused on individual fame, Schücking held that the key to understanding literary history lay in examining `taste`, a realm previous scholarship had neglected. "Taste" for him meant a general receptivity to art, a relation to art that mirrors a person's whole philosophy of life or at least touches his innermost being; it is not a constant quality or a universal faculty but varies across time, cultures and even within a society; it is related to the *Zeitgeist* and is responsible not only for the evaluation and canonization of works and authors but also for the literature written at the time. The history of taste, or the formation of taste, is therefore the literary historian's primary task: what the various strata of a nation read in a given period, and why — that, he wrote in 1913, should be the first question of literary history. Posing the problem this way signals a fundamental shift of emphasis: the creative process and the examination of the literary quality of works give way to an exploration of the public, its preferences and the causes of its reading habits; no longer the author and his work but the consumer and the conditions of consumption stand in the foreground.

Schücking thus envisaged a wide range of tasks for his followers: the forces that maintain taste (such as schools and universities), the organizations that try to influence trends (literary clubs, libraries), bookshops and publishers are all treated as essential elements of literary history. He also attended closely to the factors that change taste, pointing to events as disparate as revolutions, the introduction of coffee into Europe and the democratization of German society after the First World War as causes of altered attitudes towards art and literature. His most important theoretical contribution concerns how the dominant taste of a period is determined: he introduced the notion of a `culture-bearing stratum` in society, made up of the propagators of taste. Art ultimately depends on these propagators, and the ability of these groups to maintain themselves depends in turn on the degree of power they wield in the social structure — a power determined mainly by the extent to which they control the "machinery of artistic life". Control, he stresses, should not be understood only materially (actually owning a publishing house, say), but neither is it entirely immaterial: the relation between ownership and ideas is complex. What is ultimately important in this analysis is the recognition that even what we inherit as the taste of past ages — what Gadamer called "the classical" — is only the selection of a small group; what has been canonized are the works chosen by individuals in positions of power. In Schücking's words, it is not that the good establishes itself through tradition, but that "what has prevailed will thereafter be regarded as good". *Although the specific connections remain undeveloped, this approach to literary history places power relations in society at the centre of aesthetic evaluation — the most direct rejoinder to Gadamer's concept of the classical.*

**Influence:** Unfortunately, the implications of Schücking's, Hirsch's and Löwenthal's work for literary criticism went almost unnoticed. Even after the Second World War, when German society was supposedly making a fresh start in literature and scholarship, there were few attempts in the West to examine literary history from the standpoint of the sociology of literature. Only in the 1960s, as young scholars sought alternatives to outdated methods, did sociological concerns revive: many of these studies concentrated on the traditional problems of author and work, but some placed the emphasis on the book market, the public and the sociology of readers. It is probably no coincidence that the flowering of sociological studies coincided roughly with the first manifestos of reception theory: although reception theorists often drew neither on the pre-war sociology of literature nor on the many sociological studies of the 1960s, they were concerned with the same questions, above all the connection between literature and its public. The relation between the sociology of literature and reception theory is thus probably not one of direct influence or simple causation; but the growing attention to sociological research almost certainly helped create the climate in which reception theory became possible and flourished.

**Conclusion:** *Each of the five sources gave the turn to the reader one thing and left it one limitation. Formalism supplied "perception constitutes art" and "deviation drives evolution", but equated value with novelty; Ingarden supplied the schematic structure and concretization, but bound them to classical norms and an ideal reader; the Prague School supplied the social sign, variable norms and the collective recipient, yet its student returned to idealization through the critic-arbiter; Gadamer supplied effective history and the horizon, but refused method and favoured the classical; the sociology of literature supplied institutions, power and psychological function, but was never absorbed by literary theory. The theories of Jauss and Iser can be read as different combinations of these resources, inheriting their respective limitations (see P10, P11).*

## P10 How does literary history become a history of reception? Jauss

### The fourfold predicament of literary history

**Background:** Unlike the "precursors", whose concerns were mainly philosophical, psychological or sociological, Jauss came to the problem of reception out of a concern with the relation between literature and history. His early theoretical writings deal most often with the disreputable state into which literary history had fallen and with its remedy: he saw German and international scholarship of the 1960s growing increasingly indifferent to the historical nature of literature, as scholars turned to sociological, psychoanalytic, semantic, Gestalt-psychological or aesthetic approaches; his declared aim was to help restore history to the centre of literary study.

**Example:** In April 1967 Jauss gave his inaugural lecture at the University of Constance under the title "What is and for what purpose does one study literary history?" — merely substituting "literary" for "universal" in the title of Schiller's first lecture as a historian at Jena in 1789, "What is and for what purpose does one study universal history?" The choice evokes his famous predecessor in at least two ways. First, it creates a sense of urgency in a field that seemed moribund and in need of new direction — Schiller lectured on the eve of the French Revolution, and Jauss must have had this in mind in choosing his title; the intent was to shock, to announce a "revolution" in the making and the end of the *ancien régime* of literary studies. Second, just as Schiller had argued 178 years earlier, reviving literary study requires restoring a living connection between the artefacts of the past and the concerns of the present; for Jauss this connection could be established only when literary history ceased to be relegated to the margins of the discipline. The revised title of the lecture, "Literary history as a challenge to literary theory" (1970; German *Literaturgeschichte als Provokation der Literaturwissenschaft*), stresses its active side: to make literary study relevant again, the establishment must be challenged by this quasi-Schillerian call to arms. It should be remembered that Jauss's essay is a *provocation*, not a plea for restoration: the allusion to Schiller and the explicit intention to restore literary history's integrity and centrality should not be read as a conservative or reactionary critical strategy — for Jauss, it was precisely the obsolescence of the old conception of literary history that had produced the present predicament.

**Mechanism:** Jauss diagnoses a fourfold predicament of literary history.

1. **The teleological predicament**: the German idealist view of history — the dominant paradigm in Schiller's day — relied on a goal or guiding principle to order events and facts. The most celebrated literary history in this tradition is Gervinus's *History of the Poetic National Literature of the Germans* (1835–42). Like most nineteenth-century historians, Gervinus tied his guiding idea to national identity, in contrast to the more cosmopolitan goals, such as freedom or humanity, posited by earlier universal histories. Once the teleological model is questioned, the problem with this kind of historiography becomes apparent: the historian must either project the end point into some future moment and read events backwards from that hypothetical end, or regard the goal as already achieved, implying that subsequent events are insignificant or belong to a general decline. Gervinus chose the latter when he narrated German classicism as the culmination of German literary history, though his intention was of course to cultivate and project a national unity Germany had not yet achieved.
2. **The predicament of historicism**: nineteenth-century historicism was the main alternative to teleological historiography, and its most famous advocate, Ranke, preached complete objectivity — that is, complete relativity — in a celebrated dictum: every epoch is immediate to God, and its worth rests not on what issues from it but on its own existence. But the dignity and self-sufficiency of each age were bought at the cost of destroying the connection between past and present that Schiller and Jauss so passionately defended; to avoid speculation about final ends, historicism sacrificed the relevance of history to a dubious ideal of objectivity.
3. **The predicament of positivism**: imitating the methods of the natural sciences, positivism treated literary works as effects of verifiable, measurable causes; applying the principle of pure causal explanation to literary history disclosed only external determinants, inflated source studies out of all proportion, and dissolved the specific character of literary works into an arbitrarily expandable collection of "influences".
4. **The predicament of intellectual history**: the German reaction to positivism — `intellectual history` (*Geistesgeschichte*) — likewise failed to reconcile literature and history: it opposed an aesthetics of irrational creation to causal historical explanation and sought the coherence of literature in the recurrence of timeless ideas and motifs.

**Evidence:** The literary histories actually written under these methodological influences have been justly criticized for their theoretical shortcomings. To avoid becoming a mere chronicle of dates and works, the literary historian had two options. One was to organize the canon by general tendencies, genres and similar categories, assigning individual works to them in chronological order; the result was mostly a cultural history with literature as illustration. The other was to treat only the great authors, block by block; the result was in effect a series of abridged "life and works" essays held together only by loose chronology and nationality. Neither was satisfactory. Jauss further notes that both kinds of traditional literary history are unable to deal with evaluation: oriented towards the dubious objectivity preached by historicism and positivism, they practise an `aesthetic abstinence` that refuses judgements of quality.

### Marxism and Formalism: reconciling two one-sided approaches

**Distinction:** Another way of seeing the problem of writing literary history — the problem of uniting history and aesthetics — is through two opposed methods: Marxism and Formalism. As noted (P8), Marxism represented for Jauss an outdated literary practice belonging to the historicist-positivist paradigm. In the "provocation" essay he singles out the concept of "reflection" (*Widerspiegelung*) as regressive and idealist, and criticizes Lukács and Goldmann — somewhat unfairly — for treating literature as a mere passive mirror of the external world. Yet though he rejected most earlier Marxist theory, he also acknowledged that in its insistence on the historicity of literature, and in certain theoretical remarks by less orthodox critics such as Werner Krauss, Roger Garaudy and Karel Kosík, Marxism is not a monolithic dogmatic system; what mattered most to him were the remarks that showed a sensitivity to effect and reception.

The Formalists, on the other hand, are credited by Jauss with introducing aesthetic perception as a theoretical instrument for examining literary works. Their shortcoming lay above all in the aesthetic tendency of "art for art's sake": in Formalist theory the process of perception in art appears as an end in itself, the "perceptibility of form" as its specific mark and the "discovery of the device" as the principle of theory; this theory made art criticism a rational method by consciously renouncing historical knowledge, and in doing so produced critical achievements of lasting scholarly value. But even when Tynyanov or Eikhenbaum ventured into writing literary history, they tended to confine their view to literature: they succeeded in elucidating a concept of evolution valid for the literary series, but could not connect this literary evolution with more general historical development. The task of a new literary history was therefore to fuse the best qualities of Marxism and Formalism: to satisfy the Marxist demand for historical mediation while retaining the Formalist advances in the domain of aesthetic perception.

**Definition:** Jauss's attempt to overcome the Marxist-Formalist dichotomy was to view literature from the perspective of the reader or consumer. The theory he called the `aesthetics of reception` (*Rezeptionsästhetik*) in the late 1960s and early 1970s holds that the historical essence of a work of art cannot be elucidated by examining its production or by simply describing it; literature should be treated as a dialectical process of production and reception. As he writes, literature and art become a history with the character of a process only when the succession of works is mediated not only by the producing subject but also by the consuming subject — by the interaction of author and public. He satisfies the Marxist demand for historical mediation by placing literature within the larger process of events, and preserves the Formalist achievement by placing the perceiving subject at the centre of concern; history and aesthetics are thereby united.

**Mechanism:** The aesthetic implication, Jauss writes, lies in the fact that a reader's first reception of a work includes a test of its aesthetic value in comparison with works already read; the obvious historical implication is that the understanding of the first reader will be sustained and enriched in a `chain of receptions` from generation to generation, thereby deciding the work's historical significance and making its aesthetic value evident. This shift of perspective also has implications for a new kind of literary history: Jauss envisages a historiography that plays a self-conscious mediating role between past and present — the historian of reception should not simply accept tradition as given but should continually rethink the works of the canon in light of how they affect, and are affected by, present conditions and events. To move from the reception history of individual works to literary history, the historical consequences of works must be presented as they determine and illuminate the coherence of literature — insofar as that coherence is meaningful for us — namely as the prehistory of its present experience. *Practised in this way, literature becomes a meaningful source of mediation between past and present; and literary history — in keeping with Jauss's Schillerian ideal — becomes the centre of literary study, because it allows us to understand the meaning of the past as part of present practice.*

### The horizon of expectations: concept, objectification and theoretical difficulties

**Definition:** The integration of history and aesthetics, Marxism and Formalism, is accomplished chiefly through the concept of the `horizon of expectations` (*Erwartungshorizont*) — in Jauss's own words the "methodological centrepiece" of his most important theoretical essay. As noted (P7), "horizon" was a familiar word in German philosophical circles: Gadamer uses it for "the range of vision that includes everything that can be seen from a particular vantage point", and his predecessors Husserl and Heidegger had introduced the concept in similar contexts. The compound with "expectations" was not entirely new either: the philosopher of science Karl Popper and the sociologist Karl Mannheim had already used the term; it even had earlier ties to cultural matters — the art historian Ernst Gombrich, under Popper's influence, defined the "horizon of expectations" in *Art and Illusion* as a "mental set" that registers deviations and modifications with exaggerated sensitivity. "Horizon" and "horizon of expectations" thus appear in contexts ranging from German phenomenology to art history.

**Limitation:** The trouble with Jauss's use of "horizon" is that it is defined so vaguely that it can include or exclude any previous meaning of the word. In fact he never defines precisely what he means: discussing its sources later in the "provocation" essay, he cites his own earlier uses in 1959 and 1961, but consulting those works yields no more specific account. Moreover, the term appears in many compounds and phrases — "horizon of experience", "horizon of lived experience", "horizonal structure", "change of horizon", "material horizon of conditions" — whose relations to one another are as obscure as the category of "horizon" itself. Jauss seems to rely on his readers' common sense to understand his main term: the "horizon of expectations" appears to denote an intersubjective system or structure of expectations, a "system of references", or a mental set that a hypothetical individual might bring to any text.

**Mechanism:** This provisional definition does not relieve the central difficulty in its use. One of Jauss's important methodological postulates concerns the `objectification` of this horizon. The "ideal cases" of objectification are works that parody or reflect on literary tradition. He names Cervantes's *Don Quixote* (whose hero, driven mad by reading chivalric romances, evokes the expectations of the romance only to dismantle them one by one), Diderot's *Jacques the Fatalist* (whose narrator constantly interrupts the story and argues with the reader about "what should happen next", thus exposing the conventions of fictional narrative) and Nerval's *Les Chimères* — and *Tristram Shandy*, Shklovsky's "typical" novel, would fit perfectly well. These works are ideal because they evoke the reader's horizon of expectations, formed by conventions of genre, style or form, only in order to destroy it step by step: here the literary horizon is objectified, because the work itself makes it an object of the reader's perception. For works that evoke expectations less directly, Jauss proposes three general ways of constructing the horizon:

1. through familiar norms or the immanent poetics of the genre;
2. through implicit relations to familiar works of the literary-historical environment;
3. through the opposition between fiction and reality, between the poetic and the practical function of language — an opposition always available in reading to the reflective reader as a possibility of comparison.

**Limitation:** **Robert C. Holub**'s critique points out that the problem lies not in these procedures — they are largely what many literary scholars do when they relate works to literary tradition and social structure — but in the notion of "objectification" itself. Although Jauss sometimes tries to preserve the transcendental character of the horizon, positing that it can be objectified implies an empirical procedure; and the methods he indicates for objectifying the category presuppose a neutral position from which these observations can be made: the "familiar standards" of an era are verifiable only if we assume that we can judge objectively, from a present standpoint, what those standards actually were. Contrary to the historicity Gadamer insisted on, we are asked here to ignore or suspend our own historical situatedness. *Despite Jauss's efforts to escape the historicist-positivist paradigm, adopting objectivity as a methodological principle seems to lead him back to the very errors he criticized.*

**Limitation:** A similar difficulty arises when Jauss tries to show how the horizon of expectations avoids the "threatening pitfalls of psychology" and "circular recourse to a general 'spirit of the age'" — the concept's two strongest selling points. On the first point, he seeks to overcome the limitations of I. A. Richards's psychologically based aesthetics of response, endorsing Wellek's critique that such an approach is inadequate for examining the meaning of a work of art and at most reduces to a sociology of taste. Against this reduced notion of reception, and to go beyond individual response, he turns to `text linguistics`: the psychic process in the reception of a text is, in the primary horizon of aesthetic experience, by no means merely an arbitrary series of purely subjective impressions, but the carrying out of specific instructions in a process of directed perception, which can be comprehended according to its constitutive motivations and triggering signals and can be described by text linguistics. Drawing on Wolf-Dieter Stempel's work, he redefines the process of reception as "the expansion of a semiotic system that takes place between the development and the correction of a system"; in this scheme, examining "the process of the continuous establishment and alteration of horizons" through textual and generic cues would eliminate the variability of individual response and, at least in theory, establish a "transsubjective horizon of understanding" that determines the text's "influence". The problem, Holub argues, is that Jauss is still operating — evidently against his hermeneutic intentions — with an objectivist model: signals appear as signals only within some mode or framework of perception; genres, as conventional categories for dividing literary works, are "facts" only because we, or some author of a poetics, claim that certain features of works warrant grouping them together. There is nothing wrong or unusual in doing this; the objection is that Jauss's theorizing runs counter to his stated intentions: he avoids psychology only by reintroducing an objectivist moment that conflicts with his hermeneutic premises. He is therefore also driven into the vicious circle he attributed to *Geistesgeschichte*: as long as he insists on the possibility of "reconstructing the horizon of expectations" and carries out that reconstruction with evidence or signals from the works themselves, he is measuring a work's effect or influence against a horizon abstracted from the work.

**Influence:** In the revised and expanded version of *Aesthetic Experience and Literary Hermeneutics* (1982) Jauss reconsidered the "horizon of expectations", tying it more firmly to phenomenological theory; but Holub holds that he still has not entirely overcome the objectivist dilemma described above.

### Can aesthetic distance measure value?

**Definition:** The circle Jauss could not fully escape also affects his account of aesthetic value, since the contrast between work and horizon lies at the heart of his theory of evaluation. Borrowing heavily from the Russian Formalists, he argues that a work's artistic character can be determined "by the kind and degree of its influence on a presupposed audience". `Aesthetic distance` — defined as the disparity or separation between the horizon of expectations and the work, or the "change of horizon" (*Horizontwandel*) — can be measured by "the spectrum of the audience's reactions and the judgement of criticism".

**Limitation:** This mechanical approach to evaluation lands Jauss in several difficulties. The least of them is how to specify when a work "disappoints", "surpasses" or "destroys" expectations. Even if a measure of "disappointment" could be found, the hypothesis that value equals difference would have to be supplemented by a notion of "similarity that permits recognition". **Counterexample:** If a chimpanzee's random typing were published as a novel, it would certainly stand at a great distance from the reading public's expectations; but for contemporary readers or any critic to treat this "literature" as a serious endeavour, it would have to come closer to some recognizable literary norms. In other words, the distance between horizon and work is insufficient as a criterion of literary value.

**Limitation:** This one-sidedness also forces Jauss to place "culinary" art (popular entertainment literature) in dangerous proximity to the classics: both form the background of normal expectations and so should both lack any "artistic character". Jauss himself concedes that, according to the aesthetics of reception, the beautiful form of so-called masterpieces, which has become self-evident, and their seemingly unquestionable "eternal meaning" bring them dangerously close to the irresistibly convincing and enjoyable "culinary" art. But if a special effort to read masterpieces "against the grain" is needed to recognize their greatness, one may reasonably ask why such a reading should not apply equally to popular literature. And if it does not — presumably Jauss's position — what other features constitute a work's "artistic character"?

**Mechanism:** The root of the problem is an almost exclusive reliance on the Formalist theory of "perception through defamiliarization" (*ostranenie*) to establish value. Novelty here seems to be the sole criterion of evaluation; although Jauss elsewhere tries to treat "the new" (*das Neue*) as both an aesthetic and a historical category, he often universalizes its function in determining aesthetic value. Given his extensive work on medieval literature, this emphasis on innovation is strange: most Formalists were influenced by a contemporary literary practice that stressed the destruction of conventions, whereas Jauss surely knew that many past ages valued works as much for their similarity to traditional norms as for their difference. **Counterexample:** In the Middle Ages the demands of artistic production centred on repeating certain structures rather than breaking them; looking back we may prize works that deviate from the norm, but how long such judgements can be sustained is doubtful.

**Distinction:** The emphasis on novelty looks like a modern prejudice, quite possibly tied to the penetration of market mechanisms into the aesthetic sphere. Originality and genius are latecomers to the list of evaluative categories; the general change in the system of production — from a feudalism that stressed hierarchy, rule and repetition to a capitalism with originality as its ideology and a demand for constant innovation in production — may well have played a large part in the creation and reception of art too. *In other words, our preference for the new and the unique may have more to do with our own "horizon" than with the thought and expectations of past ages.* This critique corresponds to the later Jauss's self-criticism of the "aesthetics of negativity" (see below in this part).

### A new literary history: diachrony, synchrony and the socially formative function

**Mechanism:** Jauss also relied heavily on Russian Formalism in another important area: in sketching a new kind of literary history he adopted the Formalist notion of the `diachronic literary series`. Three points in the Formalist discussion particularly attracted him. First, a literary history applying the principle of the evolutionary series maximizes the possibility of linking aesthetic categories, which traditional literary histories rarely connected by anything but general chronology. Second, Formalist literary history can eliminate the teleological control on which most earlier literary histories relied: instead of reading events backwards from a hypothetical end, the evolutionary method posits a "dialectical self-production of new forms", and it also simplifies the criteria for selecting works — the criterion is the work as a new form in the literary series, not the self-reproduction of worn-out forms, artistic devices and genres, which recede into the background until a new moment of evolution makes them "perceptible" again. Third, because novelty is posited as both aesthetic and historical criterion, Formalist literary history combines artistic and historical significance, thereby reconciling the opposition that most concerned Jauss. **Limitation:** The advance may be illusory: to overcome the separation of history and aesthetics, history is simply defined in aesthetic terms.

**Distinction:** Jauss's own dissatisfaction with Formalist literary history concerns another problem: merely describing changes of structure, device or form is not enough to outline a work's function in the historical series. To determine this function — to recognize the problem left behind to which the new work in the historical series is the answer — the interpreter, he writes, must bring his own experience into play, because the past horizon of old and new forms, of problem and solution, is recognizable only in its further mediation, within the present horizon of the received work. This consideration may well describe how we understand texts, but as a foundation for historiography it is impractical: introducing the subjective and vague criterion of "experience" (*Erfahrung*) in effect builds instant obsolescence into the writing of literary history — if the past is recognizable only in its further mediation within the present horizon, and the present horizon is presumably changing constantly, it is hard to see how this revised diachronic model could ground anything but the most ephemeral literary history. Still, the perspective marks a clear change from the earlier postulate that past horizons must be objectified: here Jauss appeals to the interpreter's experience rather than neutrality and, in Gadamerian fashion, defines the writing of literary history as a "fusion of horizons" rather than an objectivist description of the temporal succession of horizons.

**Mechanism:** The diachrony Jauss adapted from the evolutionary model is supplemented in his new literary history by a `synchronic` dimension. He proposes that the literary historian examine selected "cross-sections" of literary life to determine which works stand out from the horizon at any given moment and which remain inconspicuous; this yields the multiple structures operating at a particular historical moment, and by comparing one synchronic cross-section with earlier or later structures one can determine how structural change in literature was articulated at any time. He relies on two conceptual tools. The first is Siegfried Kracauer's notion of the mixture or coexistence of "contemporaneous" (*gleichzeitig*) and "non-contemporaneous" (*ungleichzeitig*) features in any historical moment: in any period the avant-garde is only one movement on the literary scene; a "kitsch" work or an "ordinary" literary work of the time can be classified by its degree of non-contemporaneity, while contemporaneous works are presumably tied to the horizon of expectations; works that truly "belong" to the past are always mixed with truly timely ones in every historical cross-section. Adopting this thesis lets Jauss explore a work's historicity as a function of the intersection of diachronic and synchronic planes: the historicity of literature appears precisely at the intersection of diachrony and synchrony. The second tool comes partly from structural linguistics, especially Jakobson's and Tynyanov's application of it to literature: literature is treated as a system analogous to language; the researcher uses synchronic methods for heuristic purposes, but a purely synchronic description is impossible, since any depiction of a system implies a before and an after; the synchronic method is useful only insofar as it helps locate the constant and variable constituents within the system. Literature too, Jauss says, has a grammar or syntax of relatively fixed relations — the arrangement of traditional and uncanonized genres, modes of expression, kinds of style and rhetorical figures — over against the far more variable realm of "semantics": literary subjects, archetypes, symbols and metaphors. Selecting cross-sections for study thus offers a way of reading literary history that goes beyond statistical correlation while avoiding subjective arbitrariness; the decisive guide is `effective history`: what has issued from the "event" (*Ereignis*), and what, from the present standpoint, constitutes the coherence of literature as the prehistory of its present manifestation.

**Problem:** A literary history built on these diachronic and synchronic moments is still deficient, because it remains within the literary sphere and may encourage the notion that literary history can be understood and written independently of social development. Jauss's final proposal in sketching a reception-oriented history therefore seeks to connect literary production with general history — in fact another version of the central problem of the "provocation" essay, since even the evolutionary series remained for him essentially an aesthetic construct. He formulates it thus: if, on the one hand, literary evolution can be grasped in the historical change of systems and, on the other, pragmatic history can be grasped in the processual connection of social conditions, must it not be possible to place the "literary series" and the "non-literary series" in a relation that comprehends the relation between literature and history without sacrificing the artistic character of literature and forcing it into a function of mere copying or commentary?

**Counterexample:** Answering the question involves two points. The first concerns the difference between literary and historical events. Comparing two roughly simultaneous events, Chrétien's *Perceval* and the Third Crusade, Jauss claims that the literary event lacks the "lasting, inevitable consequences" attached to the political event: it can continue to have effects only if those who come after still or again respond to it — if there are readers who reappropriate the past work, or authors who want to imitate, surpass or refute it. Holub points out that this distinction between literature and history is too simple: the consequences of historical events, and indeed the very recognition of any occurrence as an "event", likewise depend on the judgement of "readers" or observers; from the standpoint of reception, this is no difference between literary and historical events. Jauss seems to have recognized his error in the essay "History of art and pragmatic history" and tried another strategy: literary works differ from mere historical documents precisely because they do not merely record a particular era; insofar as they attempt to solve problems of form or content they still "speak", and so go far beyond the mute relics of the past. Holub finds this line too exclusive: that literature still "speaks" to us does not rule out its documentary character; it may even be its documentary character that enables it to speak. *Drawing the boundary this way, Jauss again tends to place literature in that closed realm where eternal values speak to the eternal human condition.*

**Definition:** The second point marks one of the most important contributions of Jauss's theoretical work. He notes that earlier aesthetics of production and of representation tried to connect literature and history by subordinating the former to the latter: literature became either a passive reflection of history or an illustration of more general tendencies. Against this, Jauss stresses the `socially formative function` (*gesellschaftsbildende Funktion*) of literature. In this context the horizon of expectations acquires a new meaning: as a social construct it contains not only literary norms and values but also desires, demands and aspirations. The literary work is then received and judged "against the background of other art forms as well as against the background of the experience of everyday life"; in this capacity it can play an active role in its reception, questioning and changing social conventions through its content and form.

**Example:** Jauss illustrates this with the famous trial of *Madame Bovary*. In 1857 Flaubert was prosecuted for offences against public morals and religion on account of the novel (he was eventually acquitted). Among the passages the prosecutor Pinard cited as evidence was the one in which Emma, after her first adultery with Rodolphe, looks in the mirror, repeats to herself again and again "I have a lover!" and abandons herself to rapture; he took these words as the author's own praise of adultery. The defence counsel Sénard replied that this was the character's consciousness, not the narrator's judgement.
