Jauss argues that the source of the confusion lay in the narrative technique itself: the character's inner discourse is presented in the form of third-person narration, the narrator no longer separates himself from the character by explicit moral comment, and the reader cannot be sure whether the words belong to Emma or to the author. Flaubert was accused of promoting immorality at least partly because of a misunderstanding of his innovative use of `free indirect discourse` (*style indirect libre*). The significance of this artistic device lies in the fact that it enabled and demanded the questioning of social morality: because the new device broke with an old novelistic convention — a moral judgement of the represented characters that was always explicit and confirmed in the description — the novel could radicalize or raise new questions about the practice of life, so much so that in the course of the trial the original grounds for the charge, the alleged lasciviousness, receded entirely into the background.

**Influence:** With this example Jauss wanted to draw attention to an essential aspect of literature: its effect on society — a question most scholars have unfortunately neglected but writers have cared about intensely. Research in this area accomplishes more than filling a scholarly gap: it can illuminate the `emancipatory function` of literature while linking the pair of opposed terms Jauss strove to reconcile. The gap between literature and history, between aesthetic and historical knowledge, he writes, can be bridged if literary history does not simply describe the course of general history once again as reflected in its works, but discovers in the course of "literary evolution" that socially formative function which belongs precisely to literature — as it competes with other arts and social forces in emancipating humanity from its natural, religious and social bonds. Thus Jauss's "provocation" once more follows its Schillerian model and ends with a call to emancipation.

**Evidence:** The "provocation" essay occupies so much space here partly because of the main lesson of reception theory itself: no other essay in literary theory in West Germany over the past two decades received so much attention — it prompted countless rejoinders, critiques and sequels, generated scholarly studies and journalistic responses, and its terminology was even adopted by non-specialists. Jauss mentioned in an essay of the mid-1970s a football report stating that "the interest of the fans rests on a very high horizon of expectations". *Taking the degree of its own reception as the main indicator of importance, one would have to regard the "provocation" essay as the most important document of German literary theory in recent decades.*

### The critique of the aesthetics of negativity and the rehabilitation of aesthetic enjoyment

**Background:** The essay's far-reaching effect may have influenced other scholars more than Jauss himself: the publication of *Literaturgeschichte als Provokation* in 1970 marks a turning point in his theoretical work. He remained concerned with history and continued to stress the study of the interaction between public and text; but in the 1970s the role of the Russian Formalists clearly diminished — their views on perception and defamiliarization and their model of evolutionary literary history receded into the background and were rarely mentioned. The seminal concept of the "horizon of expectations" also largely disappeared: though he still used the category in some analyses of particular works and their reception, it seldom appears in the central theoretical essay of the period's major work, *Aesthetic Experience and Literary Hermeneutics* (1977; English translation 1982). If there was a major shift in Jauss's overall position, it is already visible in the 1972 booklet *Kleine Apologie der ästhetischen Erfahrung* (*Small Apology for Aesthetic Experience*), where the horizon of expectations and the Formalist influence are already markedly reduced.

**Definition:** This implicit repudiation of his early theoretical work seems tied to his more general disparagement of what he called the `aesthetics of negativity` (*Ästhetik der Negativität*). Reacting to Adorno's posthumous *Aesthetic Theory* (1970) — for him the paradigm of a negative theory of art — Jauss also reconsidered the implications of his own "negativity".

**Counterexample:** What troubled Jauss about Adorno's theory was that it allows art a positive social function only when the work negates the particular society that produced it, and thus leaves no room for affirmative, progressive literature, since literature is defined in general by its opposition to social praxis, by its "ascetic" character. The theory also promotes an elitist view of avant-garde art by extolling the non-communicative function of genuine culture: only art that stubbornly asserts its autonomy in the face of reified culture, that becomes a "schema of social praxis" by withdrawing from praxis, and that severs its ties with "normal" language and images can be true art; even artists who seem not to fit the paradigm are forced into the mould of social alienation — in artists of the highest rank, such as Beethoven or Rembrandt, the sharpest awareness of reality is joined with estrangement from it. Perhaps even more repugnant to Jauss was the radical separation Adorno posited between art and enjoyment or happiness: subjective pleasure in a work of art approaches the state of being released from empirical reality rather than experience itself; happiness in encountering works of art is a state of sudden escape, not the bit of stuff from which art comes; it is always accidental and less essential to art than the happiness of knowing it; the notion of "artistic enjoyment" as a constitutive concept ought to be abolished. Jauss held that Adorno's view of art evades confronting art's primary function throughout history: it cannot appreciate the artistic value of a vast body of literary works, from the medieval heroic epic to the classics of "affirmative" literature; caught in the machinery of perpetual negativity, Adorno's aesthetics ends by preaching an elitist puritanism that effectively cuts art off from all but the most indirect formative role.

**Distinction:** Adorno shares this negativity with many other twentieth-century theories of art and literature. Jauss also criticizes, more briefly, what he identifies as the aesthetics of the *Tel Quel* group: the one-sided legacy of the nineteenth century, the formula of "art for art's sake", lives on in this group's "materialist" aesthetics as the celebration of works in irreconcilable contradiction with social hegemony; but this uncompromising posture is bought only at the price of complete ineffectiveness — one cannot see how a recipe of pure negativity, of refusing to identify with social conditions, could ground a schema for a new social praxis. The same one-sidedness is now attributed to the theory of the Russian Formalists: their exclusive concern with innovative achievement and defamiliarized perception, while not necessarily severing literature from social effect, assumes that literature is perceived and evaluated only against a normal or automatized social and/or literary background, and so perpetuates a variant of the negativity of Adorno and *Tel Quel*.

**Conclusion:** The same holds for a theory of reception grounded in the breaking of the horizon of expectations. Jauss acknowledged the one-sidedness of his earlier account of aesthetic experience: first, the aesthetics of reception excluded primary aesthetic experience, sharing an artistic asceticism with other contemplative, self-reflective modes of speculation; second, being oriented towards autonomous art (roughly, post-Romantic art), it neglected not only the important role of pre-autonomous art but also the many functions art has had and may have. According to this theory, he writes, the essence of a work of art rests on its historicity, that is, on the effect arising from its continuous dialogue with the public; the relation of art and society must be grasped in the dialectic of question and answer; the history of art gains its distinctiveness in the change of horizons between natural tradition and understanding reception, between deferred classicism and continuous canon formation. It shares with Formalist evolutionary theory, with the aesthetics of negativity and with all theories aimed at emancipation (Marxism included) the conviction that the "new as event" takes precedence over "what has come to be in process", negativity or difference over affirmative or institutionalized meaning. These premises are adequate to the historical and social role of art after it attained autonomy, but they cannot do justice to the practical, communicative and norm-forming functions of art in the domain of pre-autonomous art. *By 1972 the central thesis of the "provocation" essay retained only limited validity; Jauss had in effect moved the horizon of expectations away from the centre of his aesthetics: if only autonomous art can be adequately understood through negativity, the breaking of expectations represents not an aesthetic norm but merely a special case within the longer and more complex history of aesthetic experience.*

**Definition:** Against the aesthetics of negativity Jauss sets primary aesthetic experience. He reminds us of a simple fact: most encounters with art are prompted by `aesthetic enjoyment` (*Genuss*). The German word has two senses, and Jauss wants to include both in his concept: in its most common present-day use it can be rendered "pleasure" or "enjoyment"; but an older sense places it in the semantic field of "use" or "benefit" — in the eighteenth century the verb *geniessen* often meant "to make use of something". In both senses enjoyment is the fundamental impulse behind interest in art, even though the aesthetic tradition has all but ignored it. Perhaps most important for us is its loss of standing over the past two centuries: aesthetic experience was once thought to have legitimate cognitive and communicative functions, but more recent art and theory have stripped it of these roles, relegating pleasure to a cultural attitude associated with a narrow-minded, affected middle class. Aesthetic pleasure, now deprived of cognitive and communicative efficacy, he writes, appears either as the sentimental or utopian counterpart of alienation in the three-stage model of the philosophy of history, or, in contemporary aesthetic theory, as the essence of an attitude regarded as philistine towards classical art and simply excluded towards modern art. Jauss's ambition was to challenge this tradition and restore primary aesthetic experience to its rightful place at the centre of literary theory.

**Distinction:** Twentieth-century theory did not, of course, entirely ignore the question of pleasure and art. Perhaps the best-known positive treatment is **Roland Barthes**'s *The Pleasure of the Text* (1973); but Jauss takes issue with Barthes for his alleged adherence to the aesthetics of negativity. Barthes is credited with rebutting the simple notion that all aesthetic pleasure is an instrument of the ruling class and hence to be condemned; but by dividing aesthetic experience into "pleasure" (*plaisir*) and "bliss" (*jouissance*) — corresponding to affirmative and negative aesthetic pleasure — he reintroduces the ambivalence towards art that has run through criticism since Plato. Jauss holds that Barthes's defence ultimately supports only the pleasure of the scholar: he reduces aesthetic pleasure to delight in commerce with language, fails to open the self-sufficient universe of language decisively enough to the world of aesthetic practice, and his highest happiness remains in the end the eros rediscovered by the contemplative philologist and its undisturbed sanctuary — "the paradise of words".

**Mechanism:** To avoid the harmful consequences of the aesthetics of negativity, Jauss takes a different route. Pleasure, understood as the opposite of labour but not necessarily in contradiction with action or cognition, must be distinguished phenomenologically from aesthetic pleasure. Drawing on Ludwig Giesz's appropriation of Moritz Geiger's work on aesthetic pleasure, and on Sartre's discussion of the imaginary, Jauss determines that aesthetic pleasure involves two moments. The first applies to all pleasure: the unmediated "surrender" (*Hingabe*) of the self to the object. The second is specific to aesthetic pleasure: "taking up a stance that brackets the existence of the object and thereby makes it aesthetic". The aesthetic attitude thus involves a distancing of observer from object, and this aesthetic distancing is at the same time a creative act of consciousness: in aesthetic contemplation the observer produces an imaginary object. Giesz describes the experience of the aesthetic as a "suspension" between primary pleasure and its object; in Kantian terms one may imagine a movement between subject and object in which we gain "an interest in our disinterestedness". Jauss tries to capture aesthetic enjoyment in a formula: `self-enjoyment in the enjoyment of something other` (*Selbstgenuss im Fremdgenuss*).

### Poiesis, aisthesis and katharsis: three categories of aesthetic experience

**Definition:** With this expression Jauss means to stress not only the to-and-fro movement between subject and object but also "the primary unity of understanding enjoyment and enjoying understanding". In other words, pleasure should not be separated from its cognitive and practical functions; on this conception of aesthetic experience Jauss undertakes a historical analysis of three "basic categories" of aesthetic pleasure: `poiesis`, `aisthesis` and `katharsis`.

**Mechanism:** **Poiesis** denotes the productive side of aesthetic experience, the pleasure derived from exercising one's own creative capacity. What interests Jauss most in this concept is its evolution from antiquity to modernity. In Aristotle's hierarchy of knowledge, poiesis as productive activity remained subordinate to practical activity (*praxis*) throughout the ancient and medieval world, though it was a capacity that could be perfected. The changed worldview of the modern era brought a corresponding change in the notion of poiesis: it no longer meant the capacity to imitate a pre-existing perfection but came increasingly to be identified with creating a work that itself produces perfection, or at least the "beautiful appearance" (*schöner Schein*) of completion. Jauss takes Leonardo da Vinci as presented by Valéry as the prototype of this notion of poiesis and points to its constructive side, which goes beyond mere imitation of truth: poiesis here is "a cognition dependent on what man can do, a form of action that tries and tests, in which understanding and producing can become one". The final step towards autonomy — creating perfection rather than representing it — was taken by the "literary revolution of the eighteenth century"; by the nineteenth century the work of art had become the paradigm of non-alienated activity, especially in the early writings of Marx. The twentieth-century change Jauss identifies is that poiesis became as much a function of the public as of the artist. With the decline of the metaphysics of eternal beauty, art came to be defined by ambiguity and indeterminacy; the work of art became an "ambiguous object" (Valéry's term) whose construction and cognition depend on the recipient or observer as well as on the producer. *In this way Jauss historicizes his earlier aesthetics of reception once again: it is portrayed as the theory suited to, and needed for, dealing with this altered notion of poiesis.*

**Mechanism:** **Aisthesis**, aesthetic perception, denotes the receptive side of aesthetic experience. The dependence of poiesis on reception in modern art means that in recent times it has converged with aisthesis. To outline the history of aisthesis, Jauss examines exemplary texts in which observation and perception, especially the contemplation of nature, figure prominently; he hopes in this way to circumvent, or at least mitigate, a hermeneutic problem — trying to perceive what the history of perception is.

1. **Antiquity**: aesthetic and theoretical curiosity were not yet separated, and aisthesis meant "presenting" events in the double sense of depicting and making present; since the ancients drew no ontological distinction between earlier and later occurrences, this presentation involved no ordering or hierarchy of moments. In the scene of Achilles' shield that Jauss cites, each image has its own beauty; as pleasurable lingering in the presence of a perfect appearance, aisthesis here expresses its highest concept.
2. **The Christian Middle Ages**: aisthesis was subsumed under the formula of a "poetry of the invisible", since the difference between image and meaning constituted the primary experience of reception.
3. **Petrarch**: the beginnings of a new aisthesis can already be detected — on the one hand the inner movements of the soul, on the other the beauty of the natural world, increasingly important to the receptive side of aesthetic experience. The most important impetus for this change came from the separation of science and aesthetics: science, in its modern role of classifying natural phenomena, no longer dealt with the experience of nature as a whole, and this function was ceded to aesthetics.
4. **Romanticism**: the culmination of this development. With the reversal of the Augustinian renunciation of the world, landscape and autobiography became perhaps the two poles that best capture the span of the new aisthesis. But the increasingly important contemplation of external nature should not be understood, as in antiquity, as "unmediated enjoyment of what lies before one's eyes"; as Schiller's concept of the "sentimental" indicates, it is now a nostalgic feeling for lost naivety. Romantic aisthesis thereby produced a new experience in the form of remembrance, and the initial discovery of the perceptual capacity of memory became a bridge to the most recent changes in the experience of reception.
5. **Modernity**: Jauss presents modern aisthesis in two competing variants: one with a language-critical function, associated with Flaubert, Valéry, Beckett and Robbe-Grillet; the other with a "cosmological" function (one might also say a "communal" or "generic" function), associated with Baudelaire and Proust. The former tends to destroy or call into question all perception and communication in general, and so potentially to annihilate any possibility of aesthetic experience; out of his overall dissatisfaction with the aesthetics of negativity, he seeks to downgrade this branch of the modern tradition while holding up Proust's theory as exemplary and preferable. What attracts him in Proust is the reintroduction of the cognitive function of aisthesis by way of the category of memory mediated by Romanticism.

**Influence:** Jauss in effect sees a basic tendency since the mid-nineteenth century to regain the cognitive role of art. Contrary to the popular view that art's importance has declined, he holds that the task assigned to aesthetic experience has never been greater: in this process aesthetic experience at the level of aisthesis, confronted with the growing alienation of social existence, has taken on a task never before assigned to it in the history of art — to oppose the atrophied experience and submissive language of the "culture industry" with the language-critical and creative function of aesthetic perception; and, faced with the pluralization of social roles and scientific perspectives, this perception is also to preserve others' experience of the world and so safeguard a common horizon, which art is best able to sustain now that the cosmological totality has vanished. *In Jauss's scheme, art and the aesthetic experience it permits contain not only an inherent moment of social critique but also an aspect that holds together a society that has lost touch with its own experience: by providing common perceptions, aisthesis becomes the glue binding the most diverse and alienated elements of the modern technological world.*

**Mechanism:** **Katharsis**, understood as the communicative component between art and recipient, is the third and final category in the history of aesthetic experience. In his central theoretical essay Jauss traces the fortunes of Aristotle's controversial term from antiquity to Brecht; but perhaps a more illuminating discussion of the communicative side of art is found in his essay "Interaction patterns of identification with the hero". For Jauss an important aspect of communication lies in the transmission of models of conduct, so katharsis can be examined in part through an analysis of aesthetic identification.

### Five modes of identification with the hero

**Principle:** It would be a mistake to regard aesthetic identification as passive reception by the spectator. Like every communicative process, it involves "a to-and-fro movement between the aesthetically liberated observer and his unreal object, in which the subject, in his aesthetic enjoyment, can run through a whole range of attitudes". The heuristic model of hero types Jauss constructs should be seen as part of a larger project on aesthetically mediated patterns of identification. Equally important, the very variety of patterns of interaction between art and public points once again to one of the main defects of the aesthetics of negativity: by excluding identification from primary aesthetic experience and allowing only the ironic breaking of norms or the banal mass-cultural affirmation of expectations, negative theories overlook a whole domain of aesthetic phenomena. Jauss is concerned with the full spectrum of patterns of identification; his project here can be understood as filling the wide space between the two extremes sanctioned by the aesthetics of negativity. Unlike Northrop Frye's typology of heroes in *Anatomy of Criticism* (1957), these categories are based on modalities of reception rather than on the actions or speech of characters.

| Mode of identification | Reference | Receptive disposition | Norms of conduct (+ positive / − negative) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Associative | Game / contest (ritual) | Placing oneself in the roles of all the other participants | + pleasure of free existence (pure sociability) / − collective frenzy (regression to archaic ritual) |
| Admiring | Perfect hero (saint, sage) | Admiration | + emulation / − imitation; + exemplarity / − edification or entertainment through the extraordinary (need for escape) |
| Sympathetic | Imperfect (everyday) hero | Compassion | + moral interest (readiness to act) / − sentimentality (enjoyment of suffering); + solidarity for a definite action / − self-confirmation (consolation) |
| Cathartic (a) | Suffering hero | Tragic emotional shock / inner liberation | + disinterested interest, free reflection / − sensational fascination (pleasure in illusion) |
| Cathartic (b) | Beleaguered hero | Sympathetic laughter / comic inner release | + free moral judgement / − derision (ritual of laughter) |
| Ironic | Absent hero or anti-hero | Alienation (provocation) | + reciprocal creativity / − solipsism; + refinement of perception / − cultivated boredom; + critical reflection / − indifference |

**Mechanism:** The typology is roughly chronological, but each mode can be found in every society, and several may occur in a single work. `Associative identification`, though generally found in less developed social organizations, also appears in forms such as medieval courtly ceremony or, more recently, the Living Theatre and happenings; its decisive features are the spectators' active participation and the breakdown of the boundary between actor and audience — it means "assuming a role in the closed imaginary world of a dramatic action". `Admiring identification` involves a perfect hero whose actions are exemplary for a community or part of one; the most obvious instances are the medieval heroic and chivalric epics, but there are many examples from other periods, and Jauss mentions *La Nouvelle Héloïse* and *Werther*. `Sympathetic identification` often grows out of initial admiration: the spectator puts himself in the hero's place and so expresses a kind of solidarity with a character who is usually suffering. `Cathartic identification` is marked by its liberating function for the spectator; it occurs in tragic and comic situations and involves aesthetic distance: tragic emotion or sympathetic laughter is permitted only when the spectator can withdraw from the immediacy of identification and rise to judgement and reflection on what is represented. Finally, `ironic identification` means that the expected identification is disappointed, broken or denied; this familiar and recently celebrated mode of interaction occurs most often in parody and modernist literature, and it is the model not only of the aesthetics of negativity but also of Jauss's own early aesthetics of reception. *That it occupies only a small part of the "heuristic model" here shows once more how far Jauss's later work had moved from his original "provocation".*

**Influence:** Jauss's general concern with aesthetic experience can be read as a revision of his original reception hypotheses: many of the more dubious claims are eliminated or altered, and the newest theoretical framework partially absorbs several aspects of the horizon of expectations. This self-critical attitude is not only admirable but also strengthens his theoretical position. Yet compared with the aesthetics of reception, the newer work has provoked nothing like the same degree or intensity of response. The relative indifference cannot be wholly attributed to a less provocative hypothesis: after all, the main argument for turning to the study of aesthetic experience is that much speculation about art is far removed from the basic ways art functions and is enjoyed in real life, and the sharp critique of the aesthetics of negativity in Adorno and French theory seems designed to provoke partisan reactions. Part of the reason may be formal: in *Aesthetic Experience and Literary Hermeneutics* the style becomes drier and more academic, and the astonishing range of its literary citations and theoretical sources may hinder rather than help its reception. But the main reason for the lack of response is presumably that the composition and atmosphere of the community had changed dramatically: students were no longer interested in taking part in a theoretical revolution, literary scholars were no longer eager to make literary study relevant again, and the university system was no longer caught up in innovation and upheaval. In this climate a theory of aesthetic experience, however novel and necessary, was just another academic undertaking. *Its reception, however provocative its posture, was limited — as Jauss himself had earlier shown — by factors beyond its control.*

**Conclusion:** Jauss's theoretical trajectory can be summed up in two phases. The early aesthetics of reception: the dialectic of production and reception → horizon of expectations → aesthetic distance → change of horizon → chain of receptions → literary history as the prehistory of the present, plus the socially formative and emancipatory functions; its difficulties lie in the objectivist contradiction of the horizon of expectations, the circularity of abstracting the horizon from the work, the modern prejudice of novelty as the sole criterion of value, and the failure of the distinction between literary and historical events. The late theory of aesthetic experience: critique of the aesthetics of negativity → aesthetic enjoyment (self-enjoyment in the enjoyment of something other) → poiesis, aisthesis, katharsis → the spectrum of modes of identification; it reduces the "breaking of expectations" to a special case of autonomous art and restores the standing of pre-autonomous and affirmative art. *What remains constant across the two phases is Jauss's pursuit of the "unity of history and aesthetics"; what changes is his judgement of what lies at the core of aesthetic value — a shift from "deviation" to "enjoyment and communication".*

## P11 How does reading produce meaning? Iser

### Meaning as effect: from Ingarden to Iser

**Background:** The reception of the work of **Wolfgang Iser** was likewise shaped largely by general cultural factors, in some respects paralleling the response to Jauss. His most successful early work, *Die Appellstruktur der Texte* (1970) — translated into English as "Indeterminacy and the reader's response in prose fiction" — began as a lecture at the University of Constance, where he too taught; although the lecture and its printed version did not have as intense and lasting an impact as Jauss's "provocation", it established Iser as one of the most important theorists of the "Constance School". His major theoretical work, *The Act of Reading* (1976), like Jauss's *Aesthetic Experience*, provoked far less controversy than the less mature but more explosive lecture.

**Distinction:** But the similarity in the German reception of reception theory's "twin stars" should not obscure the fundamental differences between them. Both sought to reconstruct literary theory by shifting attention away from author and text and refocusing it on the text-reader relationship, but they carried out the shift by very different methods. Jauss, a Romanist, came to reception theory from a concern with literary history; Iser, a scholar of English literature, came from the interpretive orientation of New Criticism and narrative theory. Jauss initially relied on hermeneutics, above all Gadamer; the strongest influence on Iser was phenomenology, especially Ingarden, from whom he took his basic model and several key concepts. Even in his later work Jauss was most often concerned with broad social and historical questions — his history of aesthetic experience unfolds across a vast historical span, with individual works serving chiefly as illustrations; Iser was concerned mainly with the individual text and how readers relate to it, and although he does not exclude social and historical factors, they are clearly subordinated to, or absorbed into, a more detailed textual examination. If Jauss deals with the macrocosm of reception, Iser deals with the microcosm of `effect` (*Wirkung*). A further difference bears on how this part is presented: Jauss seems to have abandoned, or at least shelved, most of his early ideas, whereas Iser's theory is best understood as an extension of his original premises — most of the central ideas and examples of *Die Appellstruktur* are incorporated into *The Act of Reading*; his contribution can therefore be discussed as a coherent, continuous development, an elaboration rather than a revision of his early work.

**Problem:** The question that concerned Iser from the outset was: how, and under what conditions, does a text have meaning for a reader? Unlike traditional interpretation, which seeks to elucidate a meaning hidden in the text, he wanted to treat meaning as the result of an interaction between text and reader — as "an effect to be experienced" rather than "an object to be defined". Ingarden's conception of the literary work of art thus provided a useful framework for his inquiry: if the aesthetic object is constituted only through the reader's acts of cognition, the focus shifts from the text as object to the `act of reading` as process.

**Mechanism:** The literary work is neither wholly the text nor wholly the reader's subjectivity, but a combination or fusion of the two. Accordingly, Iser marks out three areas of inquiry. First, the potential in the text that permits and governs the production of meaning — like Ingarden, he sees the text as a skeleton of "schematized aspects" to be actualized or concretized by the reader. Second, the processing of the text in reading — central here are the mental images the reader forms in trying to build a consistent, coherent aesthetic object. Third, the communicative structure of literature, the conditions that give rise to and govern the text-reader interaction. By examining these three areas Iser hoped to elucidate not only how meaning arises but also what effect literature has on its readers.

### The implied reader: transcendental structure or conceptual ambiguity?

**Definition:** To describe the interaction of text and reader, Iser introduced a number of concepts borrowed or adapted from other theorists, of which the most controversial is perhaps the `implied reader`. The term, which gave its title to a collection of his essays on prose fiction, is clearly a counterpart to the "implied author" developed by **Wayne Booth** in *The Rhetoric of Fiction* (1961). In that book the implied reader is defined both as a textual condition and as a process of meaning production: the term covers both the text's prestructuring of potential meaning and the reader's actualization of this potential through the reading process. Iser hoped thereby to distinguish it from the various categories and typologies of readers that had appeared in recent years.

**Mechanism:** In *The Act of Reading* the reasons for this peculiar definition are easier to see. Iser wanted a way of accounting for the reader's presence without having to deal with real or empirical readers, or with abstract readers whose character is already predetermined — such as Riffaterre's "superreader" or Fish's "informed reader". In other words, he sought a "transcendental model", which might also be called a "phenomenological reader": one that embodies all the predispositions necessary for a literary work to exercise its effect, while excluding empirical interference.

**Limitation:** But in bracketing the real reader and any predispositions, Iser often comes dangerously close to defining his "construct" in purely textual terms. The implied reader, he tells us, has his roots "firmly planted in the structure of the text"; he even writes at one point that the concept is "a textual structure anticipating the presence of a recipient without necessarily defining him". But if the implied reader is purely textual, it is identical with the literary work's `response-inviting structure` (*Appellstruktur*), and calling it a "reader" is pointless, even misleading. The concept's double function as "textual structure" and "structured act" is therefore indispensable if it is to escape a purely immanent meaning. Yet by introducing this double definition Iser may also have failed to achieve his aim: his supporters can explain the duality as the inevitable result of a sophisticated effort to grasp a process, but his critics can just as well point to the consequences of an ambiguous designation — defining the term this way lets him move back and forth between text and reader without ever clarifying the composition and contribution of either partner. *The implied reader may show a want of rigour rather than a plenitude of sophistication.*

**Influence:** Iser may have been aware of the implied reader's problems in *The Act of Reading*: after being introduced in the second chapter as an alternative to the competing constructs of other reader-oriented theories, the term is seldom mentioned in the rest of the book; he turns instead to the two poles the implied reader was meant to span — the text and the reading process.

### How fiction speaks about reality: speech acts, repertoire and strategies

**Principle:** Iser equates literature with fiction and holds that it is not opposed to reality but is "a means of telling us something about reality". It does so by placing the reader in a communicative situation compared to the `illocutionary act` described by **J. L. Austin** and John Searle in speech act theory. This division of linguistic utterances is defined by performance rather than meaning: in Austin's theory illocutionary acts can involve many common activities — promising, informing, ordering, threatening, warning and so on; what matters is that they have a potential effect, an "illocutionary force", and invite an appropriate response from the recipient. Assuming the speaker's sincerity, and assuming that speaker and recipient share the same conventions and procedures, the recipient can grasp the force of the speech act, and hence its "meaning", from the situational context.

**Distinction:** Iser sees a clear parallel in literary language, which resembles the illocutionary act but has a different function. The success of a linguistic action depends on resolving indeterminacy through conventions, procedures and guarantees of sincerity, which form the frame of reference within which the speech act can be translated into a context of action. Literary texts also call for the resolution of indeterminacy, but by definition fiction has no such given frame of reference — on the contrary, readers must first discover for themselves the code behind the text, and this amounts to revealing the meaning. The distinguishing feature of literature therefore lies in its different handling of conventions: "normal" speech acts *confirm* conventions as they draw on past communicative practice in order to be understood, whereas fiction *calls them into question*. Speech acts organize conventions "vertically" from past to present, whereas literary speech acts combine conventions "horizontally"; as a result these conventions are lifted out of their social context and deprived of their regulative function, and so become objects of scrutiny in their own right. Literature "tells us something about reality" by arranging its conventions so that they become objects of our reflection.

**Definition:** Iser calls these conventions the text's `repertoire`. It is the "familiar territory" on which text and reader meet to initiate communication; but if the repertoire were wholly familiar, the text could not fulfil its function of conveying something new to the reader. Literary texts therefore use the repertoire to reorganize social and cultural norms and literary traditions, enabling readers to reassess their function in real life. The text should be understood as "a reaction to the thought systems which it has chosen and incorporated in its own repertoire", since literature supplies possibilities "excluded by the prevailing system" and lets readers "see what they normally cannot see in the ordinary process of day-to-day living". The repertoire thus has a double function in Iser's model: it reshapes familiar schemata to form a background for the process of communication, and it supplies a general framework within which the text's message or meaning can be organized.

**Limitation:** With the concept of repertoire Iser comes closest to Jauss's early aesthetics of reception: defining a "prevailing system" — social or literary — against which the reorganized repertoire is perceived and takes effect recalls his colleague's hypothesis of a horizon of expectations broken or disappointed by the literary work. Holub points out that if Iser really maintains that literature "takes its selected objects out of their pragmatic context and so shatters their original frame of reference", he too falls victim to what Jauss called the "aesthetics of negativity" — though he himself criticized the "deviation model". He may wish to conceive the text as allowing an "extension or broadening" of the reader's reality, but the text can do this only because it is set against reality, as deviation, innovation or negation. His theory therefore shows the typical difficulty in accounting for "affirmative" medieval literature and finally has to treat much of this tradition as "trivial". He also has trouble determining why modern readers are interested in earlier works and what they get from them: since his system is built around readers contemporary with a given text and its repertoire, he is left with intellectual curiosity as the only motive — contemporary readers gain insight into the organization of their own world, while the present-day "observer" merely "grasps something which has never been real for him". *By this standard a cheap popular novel of the past and a "masterpiece" would perform exactly the same function, which shows that Iser's speculation on this point remains unresolved.*

**Definition:** The repertoire consists mainly of elements traditionally regarded as "content", so it needs a form or structure to organize its presentation; Iser uses the term `strategies` for this function. Since he is concerned with the interaction of text and reader, he does not want strategies seen as mere structural features: they comprise both the arrangement of material and the conditions under which that material is communicated, "the immanent structure of the text and the acts of comprehension thereby aroused in the reader". Strategies should not be understood as a complete organization — otherwise the reader would have no organizing role — nor as traditional narrative techniques or rhetorical devices, which for Iser are only surface phenomena of the text. What he means by strategies are the structures underlying these surface techniques that enable them to take effect. Bearing in mind that the "ultimate function" of strategies is "to defamiliarize the familiar", Iser outlines two dominant structures.

**Mechanism:** The first is `foreground and background`: the relation by which some elements stand out while others recede into a general context, similar to the figure-ground distinction of Gestalt psychology. It guides the reader's perception and is responsible for the "meaning" of the literary work — the foreground-background relation is a basic structure by which textual strategies produce a tension that sets off a series of different actions and interactions, finally resolved by the emergence of the aesthetic object. The second is `theme and horizon`, a pair of terms taken from Alfred Schütz's phenomenological theory, concerning selection among the text's multiple perspectives. In most narratives Iser sees four vantage points: the perspective of the narrator, of the characters, of the plot, and the perspective marked out for the reader. When readers attend to any one of these, their attitude is conditioned by the horizon established by previous reading and by the other perspectives; the tension between theme and horizon thus forms a "mechanism for regulating perception", though the "ultimate meaning" of the text necessarily transcends any single perspective.

**Example:** Iser also holds that a typology of perspective arrangements can be built on the various combinations of theme and horizon. In premodern literature he identifies a *counterbalancing* structure, typical of didactic and devotional literature, whose strategy is to restrict the options so that the reader is compelled to make simple and obvious choices. Somewhat more sophisticated is the *oppositional* arrangement: shifting perspectives force the reader to question the norms of each viewpoint, a strategy generally found in liberal Enlightenment novels such as *Tom Jones* or *Humphry Clinker*. More common in the past century, by contrast, are *echelon* and *serial* arrangements: both preclude positive orientation by continually undermining any perspective or guidance; the serial structure — which Iser associates with Joyce's *Ulysses* and the *nouveau roman* — accelerates the alternation of theme and horizon to the point where reference itself becomes problematic. *These different uses of the theme-horizon arrangement represent textual strategies that arrange the reader's perception of the elements of the repertoire — even when their aim is to disorient the reader — so that an appropriate aesthetic object can arise.*

### A phenomenology of reading: the wandering viewpoint, consistency-building and ideation

**Definition:** To complement the repertoire and strategies that make up his functional model of the literary text, Iser developed a corresponding phenomenology of reading, whose central concept is the `wandering viewpoint`. It is conceived as "a means of describing the way in which the reader is present in the text", and is meant to overcome an external relation between reader and text: for Iser, what is distinctive about literature is that its object is grasped from "inside".

**Mechanism:** The journey of the wandering viewpoint is best understood through what Iser calls the "dialectic of protension and retention". Taken from Husserl's discussion of temporality, the terms denote the "modified expectations" and "transformed memories" that make up the reading process: as we read a text, we continually evaluate and perceive events in light of expectations about the future and against the background of the past; an unexpected event leads us to reformulate our expectations accordingly and to reinterpret the significance of what has already happened. The wandering viewpoint thus "permits the reader to travel through the text … unfolding the multiplicity of interconnecting perspectives which are offset whenever there is a switch from one to another".

**Mechanism:** The assumption underlying this description of reading is called `consistency-building`. Faced with the text's various signs or schemata, readers try to establish connections among them that give their activity coherence; Iser assumes that readers form "gestalten" in taking part in the production of meaning, and if something arises that conflicts with the imagined gestalt, they restore consistency through a series of corrective efforts. Indeed, the "dialectic between illusion-building and illusion-breaking" and the related "oscillation between involvement and observation" are crucial to the constitution of the aesthetic object and explain why the text is experienced as a "living event". Even when a text sets out to deny consistency — as in some modern novels — readers taking part in this production of meaning can arrive at that conclusion only by way of the principle of consistency-building.

**Distinction:** The second area Iser explores in the reading process is the reader's activity of `ideation`. As we read, we continually and unconsciously construct images, which Iser calls "passive syntheses"; these images are to be distinguished from perceptions in our encounters with empirical reality, since images "transcend" the sensory — they are something that accompanies reading and is "not yet fully conceptualized". Here Iser draws on the German distinction between "perception" (*Wahrnehmung*) and "ideation" (*Vorstellung*): the former occurs only when an object is present to be perceived, whereas the latter presupposes the object's absence or non-existence. Reading involves ideation because, apart from the marks on the page, the reader must produce or ideate the "object" — usually conceived as a world suggested by the text's "schematized aspects". Ideation is thus an essential part of the creative imagination that finally produces the aesthetic object. It is not always accomplished in a straightforward way; on the contrary, in most non-trivial works images are produced and recede, modified and reconstructed in a complex temporal process. Meaning, as the final result of this process, consists of a synthesis of its phases; and since images can never be exactly reproduced, meaning is never quite the same.

**Influence:** Perhaps the most suggestive part of Iser's account of reading concerns its effect on the subject. Unlike Georges Poulet's "substantialist conception of consciousness", which relies on the subject-object division and identifies each text with a consciousness, Iser posits a `split` in the subject during reading: as we appropriate the alien experience foregrounded in the text, we simultaneously background our own previous experience; in assimilating the other we alienate a part of ourselves — "the division is not between subject and object, but between subject and himself". But since it is we who produce this "alien" meaning, it is more accurately seen as a hitherto unrecognized part of our own consciousness. So understood, reading actually brings about "the heightening of self-consciousness which develops in the course of reading". It is this therapeutic, almost psychoanalytic, consequence of our encounter with the text, Iser holds, that makes meaning production meaningful: the constitution of meaning means not only creating a totality out of the interacting textual perspectives but also, by formulating this totality, enabling us to formulate ourselves and so to discover an inner world of which we had hitherto been unaware. *Reading becomes the medium through which consciousness realizes itself.*

### The asymmetry between text and reader: blanks, vacancies and negation

**Definition:** Having analysed the text functionally and the reading process phenomenologically, Iser turns to the communicative structure of fiction. Taking as his starting point the "normal" face-to-face communicative situation — two equal partners striving to reach understanding — he defines what he calls the `asymmetry` between text and reader, consisting of two departures from the norm: first, the reader cannot test whether his understanding of the text is correct; second, there is no regulative context establishing intention between text and reader, and this context must be constructed by the reader from textual clues or signals.

**Mechanism:** Yet the encounter with literature shares a fundamental similarity with other dyadic interactions: both involve an asymmetry that is corrected or overcome. The communicative structure Iser outlines is indeed built on a model in which imbalance is adjusted or readjusted through the give and take of interaction. In text-reader communication, of course, this give and take has its own character: the reader must to some extent be guided and controlled by the text, since the text cannot respond spontaneously to the reader's remarks and questions. How the text controls the dialogue is therefore one of the most important aspects of the communicative process, and Iser assigns this central regulative function to the structure of the `blank` (*Leerstelle*).

**Genealogy:** The blank has occupied a central place in Iser's speculation since *Die Appellstruktur*. There it was defined, like Ingarden's "places of indeterminacy", as a "no-man's-land of indeterminacy" between schematized aspects; and although much of the early essay discusses blanks and indeterminacy, what exactly constitutes a blank was never made clear. One can imagine that the lack of definition was deliberate: replying to criticism of his undifferentiated category of "indeterminacy", Iser said that he agreed that indeterminacy is an extremely undifferentiated category and therefore at most a universal of a theory of communication; to define it would be to eliminate its status as a universal determining communication. The same reasoning evidently applies to the communicative unit governing indeterminacy — the gap or blank.

**Mechanism:** *The Act of Reading* likewise offers no satisfactory definition, but the blank, while retaining its central communicative role, is given more complex functions, which can be divided into three levels.

1. **Blanks of connection**: they are first of all still a matter of connecting different segments of the text. This is easiest to see at the level of plot: in most narratives the storyline breaks off abruptly and continues from another perspective or in an unexpected direction; the result is a blank the reader must fill in to connect the unjoined segments. Iser gives an example in *Die Appellstruktur*: nineteenth-century novels published in serial form (Dickens's, for instance) were often found more gripping by contemporary readers than the later single-volume editions — the enforced interruption between instalments created blanks, and readers, speculating and discussing as they waited, took part themselves in completing the story.
2. **Blanks in the referential field**: the juncture of two or more segments in turn forms a "field of vision of the wandering viewpoint". This referential field is the smallest organizational unit in the process of comprehension; the segments within it are structurally of equal value, and their confrontation produces a tension that must be resolved by the reader's ideation: one segment must become dominant while the others temporarily recede. Iser conceives the resolution of this tension also as the filling of a blank, since the reader must supply an abstract framework to accomplish this ordering of segments.
3. **Vacancies**: once a determinate structure has been established, blanks appear at the level of theme and horizon: whenever a segment becomes thematic, the previous segment must lose its thematic relevance and become a marginal, thematically vacant position — which can be, and usually is, occupied by the reader, enabling him to focus on the new thematic segment. Since this kind of blank involves the wandering viewpoint moving into a thematically empty position, Iser here prefers the term `vacancy`.

**Distinction:** Blanks, then, denote "suspended connectability in the text", and vacancies "non-thematic segments within the referential field of the wandering viewpoint". With their shifting positions, blanks and vacancies organize the reader's participation, marking out a route through the text while compelling the reader to complete the structure and so produce the aesthetic object. The journey they mark out remains on the *syntagmatic axis*; that is, it traces a path "within the text".

**Definition:** On the *paradigmatic axis* Iser finds another important kind of blank, produced by what he calls `negation`. In the course of reading, readers often become aware of the norms of the social system they inhabit; most literature — especially the kind Iser's theory prizes — has the function of calling these norms into question. By filling blanks at the syntagmatic level, the reader gains a vantage point from which previously accepted norms appear outmoded or invalid; at this point a "negation" occurs, producing a "dynamic blank" on the paradigmatic axis of reading.

**Influence:** Negation is an important category for Iser because it is central both to his theory of literary evaluation and to his brief remarks on literary history. Iser implies that good literature is marked by the negation of particular elements and the subsequent search for an unformulated "meaning" intended by the text; when the expectations or dispositions negated do not go beyond the reader's horizon, the work in question is assigned to the category of "light reading". The quality of negation is therefore the determinant of literary value. *The resemblance to Jauss's early position on evaluating "culinary" literature is evident, and its shortcomings need not be repeated (see P10).*

**Mechanism:** Iser's brief foray into literary history concerns what he calls `secondary negations`: they are not "marked out in the text" but "arise out of the interaction between textual signals and the gestalten produced by the reader". Eighteenth-century novels seem to contain more primary negations, the various perspectives in the text being continually problematized as viewpoints shift; in the twentieth century, by contrast, Iser sees an increase in the proportion of secondary to primary negations. The self-reflexivity of Beckett's prose is taken as a perfect example of the subordination of primary to secondary negation: the comprehensive cancellation of all images built up in reading has the effect of directing our attention to the communicative activity in which we are engaged.

### Negativity: the deep structure of literary communication

**Definition:** Negations and blanks are thus the basic means by which communication takes place. They form a nexus that originates in the text but is not identical with it; in a sense this framework can be conceived as an "unformulated double" of the "formulated text". Iser calls this double `negativity`. As "the basic force in literary communication", negativity has three important aspects.

**Mechanism:** The first is its *formal* character. Unlike negations and blanks, which receive concrete formulation in the text, negativity cannot be defined or precisely determined; it can only be experienced. It is like a deep structure of the text, an organizing principle whose "abstract manifestations" are the blanks and negations the reader perceives.

Second, negativity also operates at the level of *content*. Literature from Homer to the present, Iser holds, has always contained manifold instances of misfortune and failure, "the negativity of human endeavour and the deformations of existence". Deformation and failure, however, are only "surface signs" of a "hidden cause", alerting the reader to something unformulated. Since the reader must ideate this "hidden cause", negativity has a double function: it is at once the conditioning cause of deformation and its potential remedy, Iser writes; it transforms the position of deformation into a propulsive force that enables the unformulated cause to become the theme of the imaginary object ideated by the reader; negativity thus mediates between representation and reception — it initiates the constitutive acts necessary for realizing the unformulated conditions that produced the deformation, and in this sense can be called the "infrastructure" of the literary text. In this scheme meaning is conceived as "the reverse side of what the text depicts": it is the unformulated — because unformulable — experience that emerges from the double structure of negativity.

Third, from the standpoint of *reception*, negativity is "the unformulatedness of what has not yet been comprehended". It is the structure that enables readers to transcend the world in order to "formulate the cause underlying the questionable nature of the world". By enabling us to step temporarily outside our own lives, negativity allows us to assimilate the views of others, and so is the most fundamental component of communication.

**Distinction:** The appeal of Iser's theory is perhaps best seen in his handling of negativity. Usually regarded as a destructive concept akin to decadence or nihilism, negativity becomes in Iser's hands an instrument of education: it is enlisted in an Enlightenment project long abandoned by the tradition of theorists of "negativity" — from Nietzsche and Adorno to Heidegger and Derrida. This curious ability to fuse modernism with more traditional views is indeed the hallmark of Iser's work. On the one hand, the degree and quality of indeterminacy in a text are treated as the defining feature of literature in general: in *Die Appellstruktur* the course of literary history is described as an increase in indeterminacy, and in *The Act of Reading*, in more sophisticated form, as the growth of secondary over primary negations; Iser even evaluates this development with an organic metaphor — "in modern literature negations may be said to come into full flower" — and writes that "a fictional text must by its very nature call into question the validity of familiar norms".

**Example:** On the other hand, while extolling what better suits the poetics of modernism and the creed of the European avant-garde, Iser does not entirely reject the more traditional functions of literature. We are repeatedly reminded of the "lessons" we should draw from a given work. Fielding's *Joseph Andrews* (1742) is itself a parody of Richardson's *Pamela*: Richardson's Pamela, a maidservant who resists her master's advances through chastity and finally marries into wealth, is replaced here by her brother, the footman Joseph, while the seducer becomes his widowed mistress, Lady Booby. Iser analyses the scene in which Lady Booby tries to seduce Joseph: her social decorum conflicts with her desire for a servant, and her speech alternates between the hauteur of a mistress and flashes of lust; recognizing the hypocrisy of her behaviour and comparing it with parallel scenes in the novel, the reader is asked to draw the appropriate, enlightened conclusion — "in seeing through the social pretence, he discovers the basic dispositions of human nature". Even modern novels whose lesson might be that "there is no lesson" are placed in this didactic context: in Faulkner's *The Sound and the Fury* the "constellation of mental images stimulated by the blanks" is to give us "the key to the novel's meaning", namely "the meaninglessness of life"; in Beckett's prose we arrive at an understanding of "finiteness as the basic condition of our productivity". In each case, provided we read correctly, we grasp the appropriate lesson: literature, Iser implies, helps us lead better, more productive lives, even if its educational method is conceived as springing from negativity. *Schiller's "aesthetic education" found in Iser an advocate who could recast the vocabulary of modernism to suit Enlightenment ideals.*

### The liberal reader and the shadow of New Criticism

**Limitation:** Holub points out that Iser can achieve this uneasy union only by tilting his model towards the inclinations of his most likely audience. His theory is full of statements that most teachers and students of literature would like to believe true but which lack analytical argument or empirical proof; his treatment of the reader is a case in point. Although Iser posits a "transcendental construct", in practice his reader approximates the ideal of the educated European. Throughout *The Act of Reading* one finds a competent, cultivated reader who, contrary to Iser's wishes, is predetermined in character and historical situation: he must be familiar with the social and literary norms of the time — in the eighteenth century he must know Locke's philosophy; in the twentieth he should, like Iser, prefer the works of the traditional avant-garde. Statements such as this are telling: "The thesis novel tends to bore us nowadays because it gives the reader just enough room to think he is voluntarily accepting an attitude that is in fact being imposed on him." Who is this "us"?

**Evidence:** The reader Iser extols should also be a model "liberal": unless he strives to free himself from ideological "prejudices", he will be unable to read texts properly. The more committed the reader is to an ideological position, Iser writes, the less inclined he will be to accept the basic theme-and-horizon structure of comprehension that regulates the text-reader interaction; he will not allow his norms to become thematic, because once thematic they are automatically exposed to the critical gaze inherent in the virtualized positions that form the background; and if he is induced to take part in the events of the text only to find himself asked to adopt a negative attitude towards values he is unwilling to question, the result is often an open rejection of the book and its author. Commitment to an ideological position thus impedes proper understanding; indeed, the point of reading modern texts such as Joyce's or Beckett's is to assimilate "the openness of the world" into "the reader's conscious mind". *Iser, Holub comments, once again casts post-Enlightenment literature in an Enlightenment mould, giving modernism a mainstream morality better suited to the liberal tradition of the academy.*

**Distinction:** Embracing a liberal worldview does not mean that Iser has purged his theory of all prejudice. In light of Gadamer's discussion of the subject (see P7), the thesis that prejudices are obstacles to understanding rather than enabling conditions is itself contestable; extolling an "open" reader may be not only a prejudice but also an idealistic conception of social interaction — the line between knowledge and interest is never drawn from a disinterested standpoint.

**Limitation:** Besides the "liberal" reader, Iser's model depends on certain kinds of text and response. A successful literary work, for instance, must not present its elements too obviously, or the reader will lose interest: if a literary text organizes its elements too obviously, Iser writes, we as readers will probably either reject the book out of boredom or resent the attempt to render us completely passive. Here and in similar passages one has the impression that the text or author is covertly manipulating the reader's response — in Iser's benevolent system, for therapeutic and enlightening purposes. Moreover, as noted, Iser's texts function only as negations of norms: we are told that literature would be "barren" if it offered only "what is already familiar"; familiar territory is "interesting" only because it is "to lead in an unfamiliar direction"; fictional texts present elements to us in unexpected combinations "so that they begin to be stripped of their validity". A particular view of the text and its reception is thereby sanctioned — one most educators would presumably like to believe. But whether it suits the literature of premodern periods, or the reading experiences typical of history, is by no means certain. Nor is it easy to agree with Iser's assertion that "the reader's enjoyment begins when he himself becomes productive": within Iser's system the statement is either a truism — since the reader is always productive — or a discrimination against popular literature and cultural forms that make no heavy demands on the consumer. We may feel that readers *should* enjoy only when productive, or that literature *should* actively engage the reader, but Iser seems to smuggle this hope, wish or ideal into the description of his supposedly phenomenological model. *Like the "universal assent" of Kant's* *Critique of Judgment, Iser's model oscillates between normative presentation and utopian projection.*

**Mechanism:** Iser's predicament stems from his ahistorical phenomenological starting point. He conceives the text-reader relationship in constant or timeless terms, thereby precluding any integration of historical information other than the superficial; when he ventures evaluations or discusses actual texts, the result is often the inconsistencies described above. Iser wants to speak historically about literature and literary norms, but his phenomenology prevents him: a historical perspective cannot be tacked on afterwards as illustrative material or as the mere content of the empty structures of the repertoire; it must be integrated into the conceptual apparatus of the system itself. Separating the timeless forms of text, reading and interaction from their historical content repeats a familiar pattern of idealist philosophy: like Kant and Husserl, from whom his system ultimately derives, Iser cannot conceive the categories themselves as products of historical mediation.

**Distinction:** The phenomenological basis may also be responsible for Iser's unwitting proximity to New Criticism. Though he tries to define his central terms so as to avoid a "formalist" appearance, his interpretive practice often belies his claims to innovation: since he regards the search for meaning in literature as an "extrinsic approach", he appeals, like the New Critics, only to "the functions operating within the work"; even his implied reader is ultimately an immanent construct. What we get is, on the one hand, a model premised on textual properties such as gaps and vacancies, and on the other, the response of a reader who, because Iser avoids historical readers, is the product of an abstract performance. We thus get either textual analysis guided by indeterminacy or speculation about how an ideal reader or interpreter is affected by, reads or analyses various authorial or textual strategies. Neither conflicts with the best traditions of Anglo-American New Criticism and close reading: searching texts for indeterminacy and elucidating how they are put together — whether or not to elicit response — is hard to distinguish from the activity of more traditional critics looking for ambiguity, paradox or irony; and speculating on how readers respond, "filling the gaps" as it were, is little different from interpreting the text. Insofar as Iser's project can be converted into analysis, it remains essentially within the bounds of textual criticism. As Wallace Martin put it, Iser is in effect an ally of those "who want to revive the basic assumptions of New Criticism"; his reception in America, which has so far exceeded that of his colleague Jauss, is due largely to his compatibility with this familiar critical heritage.

**Limitation:** Iser is also able to mask his New Critical tracks by introducing a wealth of terms drawn from different disciplines. One critic wonders whether this terminological overload serves any purpose other than short-circuiting the reader's intellectual system: it is unclear why concepts from speech act theory, phenomenology, systems theory, existentialism, communication theory and poetics are needed to make his points, and far from certain that these terms form the neat system Iser hopes for. After one set of terms is discussed and the next taken up, it is not always obvious how the two relate or whether they actually overlap: how does the implied reader differ from the wandering viewpoint or the reader's role? What is the relation between repertoire and theme, between gestalt and image? Moreover, many concepts appropriated from other contexts carry implications alien to an essentially phenomenological enterprise: speech act theory assumes a human agent performing utterances with some intention, whereas Iser is clearly reluctant to discuss directly the author's intention in producing a literary work, with the result that he vacillates between attributing intention to words independent of human agents and attributing it to a traditional author. *By tearing terms from their usual contexts and forcing them to operate in his "transcendental model", Iser adds a layer of ambiguity to his vocabulary that makes his theory harder to understand than it need be.*

### Where is determinacy? The Fish–Iser debate

**Problem:** The most serious objection to Iser's theory turns on the question of indeterminacy and determinacy. In a sense this strikes at the heart of his enterprise, since the primary issue is the production of meaning: who or what is responsible for it, and to what extent is it constrained? The two extreme positions on these questions are associated with objectivism and subjectivism: the former holds that each work has only one correct and determinate meaning, often identified with the author's intention; the latter that meaning is entirely the product of the individual reader's mind. Iser tries to take a middle position: the text allows different meanings while limiting the possibilities; its meaning is seen as constituted by the reader under the guidance of textual instructions, so readers can evidently concretize it differently and create different meanings.

**Limitation:** In several passages, however, Iser seems to retract this freedom. At one point he defines the meaning of the text as the "meeting point" of the various narrative perspectives: the reader's role is to place himself in "a prestructured activity" and to "fit the various perspectives into a gradually evolving pattern"; in a similar description he asks the reader to "grasp the pattern underlying the connection between the blanks", thus severely limiting the "subjective" contribution; discussing segments, he speaks of "their determinate meaning", implying that meaning belongs to the domain of the text rather than the reader. These inconsistencies might be reconciled with his stated intention if perspectives, patterns and segments are themselves conceived as products of the reader's activity — the determinacy of these constructs would then not be inherent in the text but already the result of text-reader interaction. But even excusing these apparent lapses, it is hard to see what Iser can mean when he speaks of the work's "message" or "the ultimate meaning of the text". At the very least, Iser shifts determinacy from the construction of meaning to the level of the text's potential: as in Ingarden's system, the "instructions for meaning production" are evidently treated as "verifiable" on an intersubjective basis. *There is thus a point in his theory beyond which we are no longer permitted to interpret or analyse unconstrained by the standard of a single determinate element.*

**Controversy:** This is precisely the point at issue in Iser's most recent and most heated theoretical exchange. **Stanley Fish**'s most provocative comment on Iser's theory goes to the legitimacy of the determinacy/indeterminacy distinction itself. From his metacritical perspective, the distinction is possible only if one can conceive of a pure text — something that can be perceived without mediation. Since every encounter with the world, whether called "reality" or "text", involves conventions of perception, he argues, indeterminacy and determinacy are irrelevant categories: there can be no indeterminacy, because readers cannot place themselves outside all assumptions and so free of the possibilities built into a system of intelligibility; by the same token determinacy is not a meaningful designation either, since all creation of meaning depends on the "subjectivity" of readers operating within conventions. Fish is not saying that analysing texts with Iser's model is impossible — any work can be explained by distinguishing what the text gives from what the reader contributes; rather, the distinction is itself an assumption which, when it informs the act of literary description, produces the phenomena it claims to describe. That is, every component of such an account — determinacies or textual segments, indeterminacies or gaps, and the adventures of the reader's "wandering viewpoint" — will be products of the interpretive strategy that demands them, and so none of them can constitute an independent given grounding the interpretive process. In other words, what we see or understand is always already determined by a prior perspective or framework that makes seeing and understanding possible; Fish implies that Iser's system rests on a false foundation.

**Mechanism:** Iser's rejoinder is interesting because it clarifies some possible misunderstandings of his earlier statements, though it does not really answer the central objection. First, he corrects Fish's equation of the determinate with the given and the indeterminate with what is supplied, making this distinction: the words of the text are given, the interpretation of the words is determinate, and the gaps between given elements and/or interpretations are the indeterminacies. Second, he agrees with Fish that there is no unmediated given, but insists that there is still "something" that limits interpretation: this "something" to be mediated exists prior to interpretation, acts as a constraint on interpretation, has repercussions on the anticipations operative in interpretation, and thus contributes to a hermeneutic process whose result is both a mediated given and a reshuffling of the initial assumptions.

**Distinction:** These two points show well the difference between Iser and Fish: both agree on the mediated nature of all perception, but Iser insists that at some level there is control exercised by a "something". Holub notes that Iser at the same time misses, or is unwilling to address, Fish's real point. The false equation may impair the accuracy of Fish's description, but it is not central to his argument; Fish does not take the Berkeleyan position Iser attributes to him — he is not saying that only what is perceived exists; he would presumably concede the existence of words or marks on the page, and of a "something" existing prior to interpretation. His point, though, is that these "givens" are meaningless — cannot even be "pointed to" as "givens" — until we endow them with meaning *as* "givens". Iser's first statement thus conflates two uses of "given": first to denote existence ("the words of the text are given"), then to denote the intersubjective identifiability of what exists ("the gaps between given elements"); for elements are given in the latter sense only within a system that can perceive elements, and before they can be called "elements" they must be interpreted as elements. Likewise, Fish does not seem to question that, once we work within conventions, elements or a "something" in the text exert constraints on interpretation; what he claims is that perceiving constraints, or their capacity to constrain, is possible only because the interpreter is already operating within a convention or under a set of assumptions.

**Counterexample:** The real problem with Fish, of course — one Iser does not raise — is: by what conventions is *he* determined in his metacritical stance? In presenting his argument as valid or true, Fish must assume that his statements have a special status that somehow escapes assumptions; he therefore either contradicts his own metacritical creed or offers a theory just as "conventional" as Iser's (see P14 for further discussion of Fish). Fish's "victory" by no means settles the debate: if he gained the upper hand in this critical exchange, it was only because Iser retreated to old positions instead of attacking the weakest point of his opponent's polemic. Granted metacritical privilege and subjected to conventional attacks, Fish's theoretical fortress is impregnable; but once the internal contradiction in its foundations is exposed, it collapses under its own weight.

**Example:** For Iser, however, even defeating this opponent would not absolve the deficiencies in his treatment of determinacy. His brief discussion of film and novel brings out another set of problems with the concept. Iser notes that the film version of *Tom Jones* differs from the novel in the determinacy of the optical image in the former medium; if we have read the book before seeing the film, this determinacy is often experienced as disappointment or impoverishment. No one would deny that film works with visual images, but the comparison and evaluation are still revealing of Iser's biases in treating reading and determinacy. Leaving aside that this is a very unsophisticated view of film — film is more "textual" than Iser is willing to admit — the discussion indicates that vision plays the primary role in eliminating indeterminacy. In imagining Tom Jones while reading the novel, Iser writes, we have to put together the various facets revealed to us at different times, whereas in the film we always see him as a whole in every situation. But do we really see Tom Jones "whole" merely because we see his image on the screen? In what sense is he "whole"? Does the visual image really relieve us of our productive role, as Iser later implies? Like Ingarden, who regarded a theatrical performance as a concretion, Iser strongly implies here that determinacy depends on sensory perception, which in turn eliminates the need for ideation — for Iser, it seems, "seeing is believing", and visual images make us passive consumers. *In other words, Fish's supposedly false equation may not be entirely false after all.*
