**Limitation:** Perhaps more troubling for Iser's enterprise is that here and elsewhere freedom is granted to the reader only where it does not matter: whether Tom Jones is a pound or two lighter, an inch or two taller, or his eyes a deeper blue — these are left to the reader, and in these respects we are permitted some freedom in filling the blanks; but when it comes to the meaning of parts of the novel or of the whole work, Iser leaves no room for deviation from the "message". What is indeterminate seems often to concern only trivial, inessential details; where meaning is produced, readers either follow the prescribed path or misunderstand the text.

**Conclusion:** For all its "liberal" biases and argumentative inconsistencies, Iser's significance and value should not be underestimated. He is, after Jauss, the most important German theorist to have emerged in the past fifteen years, and his project complements Jauss's well: his attention to the details of interaction with the text may bring him closer to traditional interpretive activity, but it also gives us a hitherto underexplored approach to actual texts; his system helps elucidate the complexity of our most basic encounter with literature — the reading process itself — which, unlike most earlier theories, he treats not as a simple appropriation of words printed on the page but as a matrix of complex experiential and intellectual activities. Iser's merit lies in forcing us to recognize that to understand what literature is about we cannot forgo analysing our own involvement with texts; nor can we any longer ignore that texts are constructed to be read and that they set the conditions of their own readability — conditions that are enabling constructs rather than dogmatic constraints. Moreover, Iser has done more than any other contemporary German critic to introduce important European theorists to the English-speaking world: under his influence phenomenology was pried loose from Wellek's New Critical grip, and the Russian Formalists and Czech structuralists received favourable and wide exposure in his work. *Iser is thus doubly linked to communication: he advanced the entry of continental theory into the English-speaking world, and his work stimulated a necessary discussion of long-neglected problems.*

**Influence:** *Supplement:* Iser's later work went beyond the reach of the criticisms above. In *The Fictive and the Imaginary* (1991) he extended his theory of reading into a `literary anthropology`: literature is conceived as the interplay of three elements — the "real", the "fictive" and the "imaginary". Acts of fictionalizing operate through three operations — `selection` (picking elements out of extratextual fields of reference and detaching them from their original contexts), `combination` (rearranging the selected elements within the text) and `self-disclosure` (the text marks itself as fiction and so places the represented world "in brackets") — thereby giving form to an otherwise formless imaginary, while letting reality appear within that form as something that can be overstepped. Human beings need literature because they cannot fully know themselves and can only "stage" through fiction what they might be. *This turn can be read as a partial response to the criticisms above: it no longer centres on the individual reader's reading process but asks about fiction itself as an anthropological phenomenon; yet it too starts from a universal anthropological structure, and so continues the ahistorical tendency of the earlier phenomenological model.*

## P12 Should reception be studied sociologically, communicatively or empirically?

### From reception to literary communication: four versions of the communication model

**Background:** Perhaps the most important question surrounding reception theory is its relation to a general theory of communication — a topic much in the intellectual air in the years of reception theory's rise. Those who dealt most with communication at the time were German social scientists: the three best-known figures in social theory — Karl-Otto Apel, Niklas Luhmann and Jürgen Habermas — each incorporated the practices and parameters of human interaction in some essential way; Habermas's theoretical concerns in particular, from his early work on the public sphere to his 1981 magnum opus *The Theory of Communicative Action*, can be understood within the framework of an emancipatory theory of communication: he was always thinking about the possibility of establishing meaningful dialogue with democratic content in contemporary society. In this climate, theoretical impulses that contributed to a model of communication were especially welcome; almost every field was placing a previously isolated discipline within the larger framework of human interaction, and reception theory itself could easily be seen as a contribution to this broad enterprise. It is no coincidence that Iser and Jauss both ended their most persuasive theoretical reflections with chapters on communication — Iser through blanks, negation and negativity, Jauss through katharsis. It was easy to conclude that reception theory must culminate in, or be absorbed by, a more general theory of communication.

**Mechanism:** This is precisely the position Jauss and Iser took in their later theoretical statements. In a 1979 interview published in *New Literary History*, Jauss said that the common denominator of such different critical tendencies as Prague structuralism, semiotics and the aesthetics of reception was that they all placed the problem of interpersonal communication at the centre of their research interest. In response to the objection that reception theory deals with only one side of a larger relationship, he had repeatedly conceded since 1970 that the entire literary process — not just the reception of literary works — must be the ultimate object of study; in the interview he acknowledged the "one-sidedness" of his early work and analysed, in retrospect, the development of literary studies at Constance since 1966 as a contribution to communication theory: the theory of literary reception and effect, originally grounded in a science of texts, had increasingly developed into a theory of literary communication that tried to do justice to the functions of production, reception and their interaction. He placed this concern with literary communication within the general activity of the academy: corresponding to the rehabilitation of the reader, listener and spectator (the "recipient") in literary studies were the opening of text linguistics to the pragmatics of speech acts and communicative situations, the development of semiotics towards the concept of cultural texts, the return of the problems of role and "life-world" in social anthropology, the problem of animal and environment in biology, the return of the sociology of knowledge and of interaction theory, and the shift from formal logic to a dialogical logic. The ultimate goal — though still far from realized — was an interdisciplinary "general theory of communication" encompassed and constituted by all disciplines.

**Mechanism:** Iser reached a similar conclusion in "The current situation of literary theory", published in the same issue of *New Literary History*. Finding certain concepts recurring in contemporary approaches, he singled out three key terms — `structure`, `function` and `communication` — for a general survey of critical trends. Structure is identified with methods that describe particular texts and establish their meaning: by classifying and distinguishing the elements of a work, structural analysis makes possible an intersubjective, plausible description of the composition of subject matter; its limitation is that description and classification become ends in themselves — why meaning is produced, for what purpose it is used and by whom lie outside structuralist inquiry, which leaves a problem that even its various offshoots cannot satisfactorily solve, namely "the meaning of meaning". To examine this question a framework must be adopted that goes beyond structure itself and allows us to examine how meaning functions: functional analysis absorbs and supersedes structural analysis, dealing with the connection between the text and its meaning and extratextual reality — how the text enters into relation with its environment; but it too has limits — it can explain the recovery of historical experience through the reconstruction of past worlds, but cannot answer the question of the continuing validity of the reconstructed world and of the process of reconstruction. To deal with this, communication must be introduced: analysis based on the model of text/reader interaction includes not only structure and function but also answers the question of validity. Iser conceives literary communication as a joint activity in which reader and text interact in a "self-regulating process"; the validity of the literary work lies in the experience gained in and from reading: the text's selective use of the reader's own faculties gives him an aesthetic experience whose very structure lets him gain insight into the acquisition of experience, and lets him imagine a reality that is true as a process of experience yet can never be true in a concrete sense. *As in Jauss's more universal and utopian conception, communication is seen here as the final stage in the development of literary theory.*

**Example:** Jauss and Iser were not the only theorists to recognize the implications of reception theory for communication. The following four versions represent the main directions of the communication model.

1. **Gumbrecht: literary studies as a sociology of communication.** In an essay of the mid-1970s, **Hans Ulrich Gumbrecht**, one of Jauss's most important students, conceived the change of paradigm in terms of communication rather than effect and response. Criticizing the premature proclamation of a new paradigm, he speculated that a genuine shift would occur only as a consequence of the aesthetics of reception — when literary studies constituted itself as a branch of the sociology of communication. He outlined a theory treating literature as social action. With respect to the production of texts the task is twofold. First, to reconstruct as precisely as possible the author's intention, which he calls "the subjective meaning of the text as activity"; this does not mean reviving the outdated biographical method but stems from a fundamental flaw of earlier theories — their failure to distinguish normative from descriptive models: the former use standardized schemes of the literary process, the latter operate with a heuristic meaning of the text; because of its accessibility, reconstructibility, historicity and constancy, he chooses the meaning intended by the author as the basis of the descriptive approach. Second, production also involves factors beyond conscious authorial intention: "in-order-to motives" (*Um-zu-Motive*) focus on the subjective activity of the communicative act, while "because motives" (*Weil-Motive*) take account of the "historical, socio-structural level" — the historically enabling structures that legitimize the subjective desire for particular meanings or effects, or that generally determine the form and content of literary works. Reception forms the reverse side of the communicative process: reading and understanding are likewise treated as social action, and both kinds of motive are objects of study; but material in this area is harder to obtain — surveys may help determine contemporary responses, but evidence from the past is scarce and unreliable, and the ultimate influence of literature on "practical activity" can scarcely be determined, so one must often settle for well-grounded hypotheses based on the best available information.
2. **Stierle: the pseudo-referentiality of fiction and a double reading.** **Karlheinz Stierle**, another of Jauss's students, advocated examining the *formal* side of the communicative process: "a supplementary theory of forms of reading, whose specific criteria for receiving fictional texts derive from the concept of fictionality itself". He held that Iser did not go far enough: Iser makes the formation of illusions and images the core of reading, and Stierle agrees that this is essential to the aesthetic experience of most fiction, calling it "quasi-pragmatic" reception to distinguish it from the "pragmatic reception" of non-fictional texts; but he argues that quasi-pragmatic reading must be supplemented by "a higher form of reception that alone can do justice to the special status of fiction". He posits a `pseudo-referential` use of language lying between simple reference and self-reference: the distinguishing feature of narrative fiction is precisely this "self-reference disguised in the form of reference", so that the fictional text is "a variant of the systematic text" — if "systematic" denotes texts concerned with the conditions of use of their own terms. In other words, fiction is self-reflexive, though it looks referential. "Understanding" (*Verstehen*), identified with quasi-pragmatic reading, must be supplemented by "recognition" (*Erkennen*), the reflexive dimension, because only a reflexive turn from mimetic illusion back to the fiction and its pseudo-referential formulation can reveal the formal aspect of fiction. This double reading — first producing illusion, then thematizing the construction itself — corresponds roughly to the reader's activity plus the literary scholar's analysis.

   Stierle's main contribution appears in the collection *Text als Handlung* (1975; *Text as Action*), whose common concern is establishing a "systematic literary scholarship". He most often draws on two external impulses. One is speech act theory: using Austin and Searle to overcome the barrenness of purely text-linguistic approaches and their neglect of communication; if texts are understood as objectified schemata of linguistic performance, both production and reception can be studied through textual analysis, literary studies becomes a variant of the "science of action", and fictional texts correspond roughly to performative utterances. The other is semiotics: he particularly admires Hjelmslev (who distinguishes the planes of expression and content and so is closer to *parole* than to *langue*), the early Barthes (concerned with how signs convey ideology through systems of connotation — that is, *Mythologies* and *Elements of Semiology* rather than *S/Z*) and Greimas (who explores structures of signification). His applications are often illuminating. He treats the comic situation as "action determined by others", viewed from a third-person perspective, and from this he can also explain the unsettling effect of the theatre of the absurd: earlier comedy always ended with the restoration of a "reasonable" state of affairs, whereas the absurd problematizes normality itself as a social consensus, so the audience can no longer use laughter as an escape and loses the distancing associated with earlier comedy. On the relation between exemplum and story, he argues that only the ability to tell stories — to unify the moments of existence into a temporal sequence — lets us understand what happens and even our own lives; and although the exemplum was displaced by the historical worldview of the eighteenth century, it can still serve as a paradigm of the story, so its impossibility (as his reading of Montaigne shows) implies the impossibility of every meaningful story. On negation, he studies the negation of images and states of affairs constituted only in the reader's imagination, and uses a double reading, "combinatorial" and "pragmatic", to elucidate the reader's role that texts full of negations force on us. Yet these essays do not amount to the "systematic literary scholarship" he announced: full of suggestive insights, they lack a consistent approach; his own description of Greimas applies equally to himself — moving in the tension between essay and system, producing an extraordinary wealth of perceptual hypotheses and embryonic models, and often disorientation as well.
3. **Grimminger: one-way communication and the protest of "withdrawal into the self".** In his frequently cited outline of a theory of literary communication, **Rolf Grimminger** focuses on the "speech event" as the medium of information between two subjects. What is special about literary communication is that this speech event takes the form of a text, marking a break in direct interaction, with important consequences for the mental processes of both author and reader: the author enters an unusual relationship with the text, which is conceived as an abstract partner — reading his own work, the author compares his original intention with the objectified meaning and revises in light of this "one-way" communication; the reader's encounter with the text, for its part, is a "lonely psychic event" in which intentions are projected and revised in reading. Author and reader are both users of the text: intersubjectively intelligible signs belong to neither party and must be used or deciphered by both. The most suggestive part of his model lies in its social consequences. Unlike instrumental theories of literary effect, and even unlike the Brechtian model of alienation, he holds that social protest is inseparable from literature: as authors or readers, participants are absorbed in a monological mental process remote from, even opposed to, social action, but in this absorption the individual necessarily affirms an "ethos of inner freedom"; at least in the history of "bourgeois poetry and philosophy", protest is marked by this "intellectual-aesthetic resistance to social praxis", even without any oppositional message — "protest is already contained in the process of communication itself, that is, in the withdrawal into the self".
4. **Waldmann: communicative aesthetics and the critique of ideology.** **Günter Waldmann** is more concerned with the ideology of narrative structures. His "communicative aesthetics" appropriates a wide range of sources — Sartre, Husserl, Saussure and Charles Morris among them — but its core is Luhmann and Habermas. From Luhmann he adopts the parameters of the communication of meaning: meaning is at once a selection from various possibilities and a reference to other possibilities; meaning is constituted only within a system or a horizon of alternatives, and thereby grasps and reduces the complexity of external reality; communication is therefore not the simple transmission of information but the actualization of meaning — information can be recognized as information only within a prior system of meaning that includes both sender and receiver. Finding this account too abstract and lacking an ideological, socially determined moment, he supplements it with Habermas's concept of "discourse": unlike communicative action, which describes the relation between persons, discourse refers to the relation to the norms that determine communicative action; it makes communicative activity itself problematic and contains the conditions of possibility of communication; distortions of discourse involve ideology. He accordingly defines `ideology` as a system of meaning that gives the appearance that its normative legitimation of forms of hegemony rests on rational discourse, while systematically obstructing every genuinely rational discourse that would question or destroy it. On this basis he proposes a method of ideology critique that analyses the strategies by which rational discourse is distorted, subverted or promoted in the process of communication.

**Mechanism:** To this end Waldmann turns to the study of "textual communication systems", relying on Siegfried J. Schmidt's "text theory", which defines the text as the universal form in which language is transformed into social communicative action. But communicative action cannot be analysed at the level of concrete pragmatics: individual motives and responses are either inaccessible or unreliable or unimportant; and if author and reader are reduced to text-internal components, analysis becomes abstract speculation about "real" readers and authors. He proposes to sidestep this dilemma by taking *systems of meaning* as the object: the object of analysis in communicative aesthetics is the structurally homologous aesthetic system of meaning underlying the individual realizations of textual communication. He distinguishes four levels of textual communication, two outside the text and two within it:

| Level | Sender | Receiver | Message |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Pragmatic communication | Concrete author | Concrete recipient | Literature (i.e. the literary communication of the next level) |
| Literary communication | Author in the role of literary author | Recipient in the role of literary recipient | Text (i.e. the text-internal communication of the next level) |
| Text-internal communication | Authorial role within the text (narrator) | Recipient role within the text (reader) | Fictional (narrative) events (i.e. the fictional communication of the next level) |
| Fictional communication | Fictional character reporting | Fictional character receiving information | Fictional verbal and non-verbal interaction |

The first level comprises the real author and real reader with their intentions and expectations; the second concerns the "author's role" and "reader's role" as understood by both; the third concerns the presentation of these roles in the text, such as a novel's narrator or a reader directly addressed in the book; the fourth is communication between characters in the text. The scheme at least has the merit of neatness: each level displays a symmetrical communicative structure, and the product of each larger communication (literature, text, fictional events) feeds into the heading of the next communicative substructure.

**Limitation:** At the same time there is something artificial and alienating about such a model; more tellingly, using it is no guarantee that we will learn more about textual communication — or about literature as ideology. In the second half of the book Waldmann offers a sample analysis of a short story about war experience, "A pioneer", by the Nazi writer Erhard Wittek, without reaching any surprising conclusions: most results could either be inferred in advance from the genre and the author's ideological bias or obtained with elementary techniques of narrative theory — the theoretical excess of the first half contrasts sharply with the meagre yield of the second.

**Conclusion:** This disproportion between theory and practice perhaps points to the main difficulty facing communicative theorists of literature. On the one hand, an elaborate system developed from a multitude of sources, like Waldmann's, fails to engage effectively even with a text chosen by its designer, which calls the necessity of the enterprise into question; on the other, Stierle's "systematic literary scholarship" never got beyond a series of loosely related sketches, yet this more "casual" approach — though equally influenced by numerous outside disciplines and equally overloaded with terminology — often yields illuminating results. *Communication theory as a framework for studying literary texts thus has mixed credentials: it has yet to prove itself the panacea for the ills of earlier scholarship; but it has reminded those German reception theorists eager to throw the study of textual production and description into the dustbin of literary history that reception is only one aspect of a more complex and inclusive process.*

### The Marxist critique: the primacy of production, subjectivization and the sociological deficit

**Background:** Perhaps the strongest criticism of reception theory came from the Marxist camp. The West German left was not short of objections, but critics in the German Democratic Republic were especially quick to point out its shortcomings and contributions; after Jauss and Iser each replied to East German assessments, a minor polemic ensued, with each side accusing the other of a "false" conception of literary response. For GDR critics, Western reception theory was primarily a reaction to the crisis of bourgeois literary studies. This hypothesis appears in several important essays in the early 1970s in *Weimarer Beiträge*, the GDR's central journal of literary and cultural theory, and is restated in *Gesellschaft – Literatur – Lesen: Literaturrezeption in theoretischer Sicht* (1973; *Society – Literature – Reading*), written by a collective led by **Manfred Naumann** (Dieter Schlenstedt, Karlheinz Barck, Dieter Kliche, Rosemarie Lenzer), which offered an East German alternative to "bourgeois" reception theory. **Robert Weimann**, **Claus Träger** and Naumann, among others, placed reception theory at the end of a series of post-war methodological developments: the turn to reception was a sign of the bankruptcy of ahistorical formalism and of bourgeois alternatives to Marxism. The GDR's main target was Jauss rather than Iser: Jauss had outlined the crisis of Western methods more precisely and was more directly concerned with reintroducing history to the centre of literary study; Iser's phenomenological background made him easier to see as part of an ahistorical tradition, and at first he was regarded merely as a continuator of Ingarden. Jauss's raising of the problem of reception in terms of literary history was both more interesting and more threatening to the GDR.

**Genealogy:** The GDR's ambivalence towards Jauss presumably also stems from the ambiguous status of reception in the Marxist tradition. Two perspectives can be discerned in earlier Marxist criticism, and the more influential of them was indifferent, if not openly hostile, to audience response: Marx's and Engels's remarks on literature and art most often view culture from the side of production rather than reception. Perhaps the most influential document for the Marxist view of culture — the preface to *A Contribution to the Critique of Political Economy* (1859) — places all culture in a superstructure determined by the economic base: when the material productive forces of society come into conflict with the existing relations of production, an epoch of social revolution sooner or later begins. On the usual reading of this preface, literature, as one of the "ideological forms" in which people become conscious of this conflict and fight it out, is merely a reflection or derivative of a more fundamental economic order, a product of social forces rather than an agent of social change.

**Example:** Against this narrow reading one can cite another passage in Marx that seems to encourage attention to reception. In the introduction to the *Grundrisse* (1857), written as an introduction to the critique of political economy, Marx uses Greek art and the Homeric epic as an example: explaining the production of these cultural objects presents no difficulty for theory; the difficulty lies not in understanding that Greek art and epic are bound up with certain forms of social development, but in understanding why they still afford us artistic pleasure and in certain respects count as a norm and an unattainable model. Marx's answer — that we identify with ancient Greece because it was the "normal" childhood of humanity — is rather tentative and dubious; what matters is that he acknowledges the legitimacy of questions about the appropriation and effect of past artistic achievements.

**Distinction:** These two opposed attitudes in Marx doubtless contributed to the ambivalence of later Marxist criticism: Lukács and Mehring seem to play down the question of reception, while Brecht repeatedly stressed the importance of audience response, especially in his theory of epic theatre; Adorno considered the effect of art unimportant, while Benjamin attended increasingly to the influence of literary works in his late essays. The Marxist tradition had no consensus on the question of reception.

**Background:** After the war a traditional "representational" aesthetics — preached in its crudest form by Zhdanov in the Soviet Union and in more sophisticated form by Lukács — was established as official doctrine in the East and dominated the culture of the GDR for at least its first two decades; but other impulses persisted. Lenin's concept of the "national heritage" indirectly legitimized attention to reception: since many of the best-known works of the German past had been distorted and exploited by the Nazis, one of the new republic's most urgent tasks was to re-examine the national tradition and purge it of right-wing residues; and the ideological struggle between East and West extended into culture as a contest over the inheritance of the more progressive moments of the heritage. The result was a sometimes excessive preoccupation with the "heritage" (*Erbe*), and this preoccupation naturally led to reflection on texts having a "life" independent of their representational essence — dealing with the question of heritage meant implicitly accepting a reception-oriented problematic. The concept of the "literary society" (*Literaturgesellschaft*), introduced in the 1960s, reinforced this perspective further: first used by Johannes R. Becher, the GDR's first minister of culture, the term came to denote the cooperation and exchange among authors, critics and public — the communicative network that distinguished socialist literary life from its bourgeois counterpart.

**Distinction:** Despite these practical concerns, it would be inaccurate to portray GDR theory as "ahead" of the West in this area in the 1960s. Had East German critics already dealt satisfactorily with the questions Jauss raised, his provocation would not have been so provocative; the initially caustic response may have stemmed partly from a feeling that Marxist critics ought to have studied these questions earlier. Its sharpness may also have stemmed from residues of the "critics' cold war": in the GDR's first fifteen years almost any method remotely resembling Western theory was condemned as decadent, idealist or imperialist.

**Counterexample:** Against this background Jauss was singled out for his inadequate knowledge of Marxism. In the Constance lecture his knowledge of Marxist theory seems to have come largely from dubious secondary sources, and the revised book version of 1970 still shows huge simplifications and gaps; his assignment of Marxism to the positivist-historicist model in the 1969 "paradigm" essay would not win him friends in the East either. Barck observes in *Gesellschaft – Literatur – Lesen* that Jauss's premises simply equate Marxist literary theory with vulgar sociology, while theorists who do not fit his dogmatic model — Benjamin or Krauss, say — are treated merely as exceptions to the orthodox norm. Some East German critics grudgingly conceded that Jauss's portrayal had a kernel of truth, but his treatment of Marx and the Marxist tradition must have seemed shabby. Jauss may not have been, as one West German leftist claimed, a continuator of anti-communism in literary theory, but his challenge to literary studies was correctly read in the East as also an attack on certain aspects of Marxist theory.

**Mechanism:** One of the central criticisms of GDR scholars — the `one-sidedness` of the aesthetics of reception — is not unrelated to this perceived attack. When Jauss was accused of "absolutizing the sphere of literary consumption" (Weimann), or at least of neglecting the dialectic of production and effect in the literary process, the issue was not merely an inadequate or unbalanced model; the vehemence of the objection shows that ideological matters were also at stake: shifting the theoretical emphasis from creation or representation to reception could at the same time be read as an attack on the primacy of production in Marx's general theory, and Jauss's new "paradigm" could thus be seen as an implicit attack on the foundations of Marxist theory.

**Mechanism:** In defence of the Marxist position, Naumann's counter-model starts from the introduction to the *Grundrisse*. In Marx's account production and consumption stand in a dialectical relation, each in a sense "producing" the other. Production "produces" consumption in three ways: by creating the object of consumption, by determining the manner of consumption, and by creating in the consumer the need for consumption — thus production produces not only an object for the subject but also a subject for the object. Consumption in turn determines production in two respects: it completes the cycle of production and so in a sense creates it, and it provides the mechanism for new production by constituting the consumer's needs. Though each "produces" the other, they should not be identified: production is the starting point of realization and hence the "predominant moment" in the whole process; consumption is conceived as an inner moment of productive activity, subordinate to the act in which the whole process finally issues.

**Distinction:** Applying this scheme to art requires great care, since a facile analogy between production in general and cultural production inevitably leads to distortion; Naumann therefore distinguishes `the production of art` (*Kunstproduktion*) from `artistic production` (*künstlerische Produktion*). The former denotes the artist's activity as producer of a material product — a commodity bought and sold on the market; the latter the artist's specific activity of creating a work of art, the non-material contribution to this branch of production. The two are intertwined components of a single process and can be separated only by abstraction from production itself: artistic quality appears only in the art object, while producing for a capitalist market necessarily affects the form and content of art. What matters is that in both respects production predominates: even at the non-material level, Naumann cites Marx's remark that the art object creates a public that understands art and can enjoy beauty. In this model the effect and reception of art, though important and ultimately influencing production, are clearly subordinate.

**Counterexample:** The "one-sidedness" of Jauss's position can be challenged in another way: using the more traditional scheme of base and superstructure, the emergence of reception theory can itself be understood as an expression of a consumption-oriented society, and Jauss and others, in extolling the consumption of art, mistake a reflection for the essence of the matter. If by neglecting production Jauss was deceived by appearances, the resulting one-sidedness in turn fostered what East German observers saw as a deception about the historical process: what was most objected to in Jauss's version of reception theory was its tendency to `subjectivize` history. Given the earlier critique of his demand for "objectifying" the horizon (see P10), this objection may seem odd; but the Marxists' concern here is his hermeneutically influenced view of history, not his inadvertent residual objectivism. Most often cited is a sentence Jauss took from Collingwood: history is nothing but the re-enactment of past thought in the historian's mind. In context the sentence served against historicist historiography, but its effect — as Weimann and Barck put it — is to make history wholly dependent on individual perception: taking subjective perception as the sole basis of history denies any objective basis for evaluating historical events; in this scheme events become "eventful" only by being recognized as events. Weimann holds that Jauss posits a false choice: the choice lies not between historicism's positivist cult of facts and reception theory's hermeneutic model, but between false objectivity and subjectivity on the one side, and on the other a "genuine objectivity" that unfolds and connects phenomena in historical thought "as a process, in the reality of their movement".

**Counterexample:** The second historical problem is the alleged tendency to `relativize` literary phenomena. East German critics point out that Jauss's hermeneutic model, like Gadamer's, contains no mechanism for evaluating past judgements and thereby excluding their validity. To take an extreme example: Nazi literary scholarship may be as much a part of tradition as any other, but East German critics want some means of revoking its legitimacy; on Jauss's model, where the essence of the work lies in its reception, there seems to be no meta-principle for evaluating evaluations, and all past judgements participate equally in constituting the work's changing essence. Weimann sums up the objection in a question: how can the literary historian historicize the norms of his prehistory without hopelessly relativizing the objectivity of the work and his own historical relation to it? Behind this relativity Weimann sees Jauss making a modern virtue of historical necessity, underpinned by a false and superficial notion of parliamentary democracy; he too supports freedom of expression and the anti-authoritarianism inherent in giving voice to popular views neglected in the past, but not at the cost of "the unity of objectivity and evaluation".

**Counterexample:** The most common GDR criticism concerns the Constance School's lack of a `sociological foundation`. Barck argues that the essence of Iser's theory of communication lies in the privatization of the reading process: like bourgeois democracy, it grants the reader a false freedom of choice while ignoring the real social determinants of reception — it can be interpreted as an aesthetics-of-reception gloss on bourgeois freedom of speech, generously granting readers the right to constitute the meaning of literary texts as though there were no ruling-class ideology and no social modes of reception determined by it; Iser can preach a realm beyond subject and object only because he extols reading in its "individual form" and ignores "its socializing function". But Jauss remained the more challenged theorist, and criticism of him concentrated on two interrelated areas. The first is his individualistic conception of the reader, or the normative basis of his discussion of the reading public. Jauss's reader, Träger says, may be active in dealing with literary texts but does not seem to take part in making history: the "reader" hangs, as it were, in mid-air, because he is grasped only as a reading individual, not as a historical force, and so the whole postulate of literature's historicity as a history-forming force evaporates into a theory correct in itself but accomplishing nothing. Jauss's appeals to collectives of readers suffer from the same deficiency: in most of the modern period one can identify several very different audiences for different kinds of literature, but he repeatedly fails to differentiate them by social class or origin; the public he refers to, like the reader, is merely an idealization that lets him evade the decisive question of social function. The second is his failure to account for social mediation in his model of literary history: the attempt to connect literary history with general history through the socially formative function of literature is judged inadequate, because the experience of the reader or public, even when in turn influenced by the work, remains enclosed within the literary sphere; the horizon of expectations is conceived largely as a construct internal to literature. For Jauss, Barck sums up, there exists only a public in and for itself, characterized solely as a recipient of literature, and only in this capacity — within a horizon of expectations conceived entirely in literary rather than sociological terms — does it function as the mediation constituting the historicity of literature. Träger's conclusion: for all its postulates about effect, literary history, even understood as a history of reception, remains immanent.

### Reflection theory and the reader's freedom: mutual accusations between East and West

**Counterexample:** Jauss's and Iser's replies dealt with these objections only indirectly. Jauss conceded that literary history cannot be reconstructed from reception alone and that reception should not be raised to an "autonomous methodological paradigm"; but this apparent concession to the charge of one-sidedness is immediately followed by a sentence that restates the same problem in another form: if the historical essence of the work of art can no longer be determined for us independently of its effect, and the tradition of works no longer independently of their reception as history of art, then the customary aesthetics of production and representation must be *grounded in* an aesthetics of reception. Holub stresses that the question is precisely whether a reception-oriented model can deal with the relation between production and response without distorting the artistic process. On the East German objection that his model necessarily leads to the subjectivization and relativization of literary history, Jauss said almost nothing.

**Mechanism:** Jauss and Iser both turned instead to a counteroffensive, pointing out inconsistencies and shortcomings in the GDR theorists' own statements. Both noted an alleged contradiction in acknowledging the legitimacy of reception while retaining the concept of `reflection` (*Widerspiegelung*). Iser argues that literature's mimetic function is irreconcilable with the educational task GDR culture assigns to it: if the reader is to be educated into what he is not yet, the medium that can advance this process cannot be a representation of his existing relations; he can be educated only when something happens to him, and to bring about such an event something more than the representation of real relations is required. Jauss notes the same contradiction and traces it to an "idealist embarrassment" in Marx's work itself: citing the same passage from the *Grundrisse* introduction that Naumann uses, he argues that Marx's statement that the art object creates a public sensitive to beauty conflicts with any possible theory of reflection — if beauty were assigned only the function of reproduction in a materialist manner, the art object could hardly arouse such a need in a public initially lacking it entirely, a need the art object must first create; the aesthetic paradigm in Marx's dialectic of production and consumption implies that beauty has a transcendent ("idealist") function. Turning to Marx's statement in the *Economic and Philosophic Manuscripts of 1844* that man can produce according to the laws of beauty, Jauss finds more ammunition for a charge of idealism: if the beautiful art object arouses a new, not yet existing need, it can hardly be a copy of what already exists materially at the same time, and the "laws of beauty" can hardly be derived from what already exists materially.

**Counterexample:** Holub points out that Marxist critics could easily rebut the last point: where, if not from something "already existing materially", does Jauss think we derive our ideas of beauty? But the core difficulty with Jauss's and Iser's argument is that they hold an overly mechanical conception of reflection, understanding it as the precise "copying" of material reality. Even the most dogmatic Marxist theorist could not maintain so narrow a scheme; elevating it into a tenet of Marxist theory — even for polemical purposes — is misleading at the very least. If literary reflection is understood in a somewhat more sophisticated way — as the appropriation or extension of reality through the artistic imagination, say — it is hard to see where exactly the problem lies. *The contradiction is in fact more easily found in the Western reception theorists' attempts to remove reflection from their theoretical schemes altogether.*

**Principle:** Although almost every philosophical and aesthetic camp of the twentieth century attacked reflection theory, it has shown remarkable resilience. Its greatest asset may be that its opponents must implicitly grant it some legitimacy in attacking it. However powerful the arguments advanced to refute or belittle reflection, they could have no force without the assumption that their words in some sense reflect an actual state of affairs, a reality of which the words are at least assumed not to be part. In short, arguments against reflection necessarily become self-contradictory, and Jauss's and Iser's versions are no exception. If the mimetic function is denied entirely, it is hard to see how literature could have a socially formative role, or how it could let us ideate a world different from our own; Jauss's and Iser's theories may not be as free as they think of the "contradictions" they find in Marxist reception theory.

**Controversy:** The other main theoretical point in Jauss's and Iser's replies is more politically coloured and concerns the "freedom" granted to readers to constitute the meaning of texts. Both single out the implications of the concept of `reception specification` (*Rezeptionsvorgabe*) introduced in *Gesellschaft – Literatur – Lesen* — denoting elements in the text "given" before interpretation takes place. The problem they perceive is that the term is used to limit the possibilities of interpreting a given work, that is, to constrain the reader's freedom to produce meaning in interacting with the text; combined with `social modes of reception` (*gesellschaftliche Rezeptionsweisen*) — which determine readers' responses through historical, social and literary conditioning — the "givens of reception" tend to put the reader in a straitjacket and allow no diversity of response. Jauss and Iser therefore argue that GDR reception theory promotes a conformist model of reading that in effect negates the genuinely emancipatory role of literature. Since in Iser's description of reading the therapeutic, liberating function presupposes the reader's ability to "live another life" in the course of reading, he holds that the GDR scheme, unlike his own, obstructs the cause of freedom: the question is how far a theory of reading aimed at educating people to conform to the system — however commendable such conformity may be when it is right — must interfere with these processes; producing a "socialist mode of reading" requires internalizing the correct social norms so that the subject can adapt to society. Jauss too sees a condescending, controlling force behind East German theory, detecting "a certain mistrust of whether the socialist reader is really mature": the function of reception specifications is to eliminate indeterminacy and so compel readers to understand texts "correctly"; although Naumann occasionally allows the "given" elements to be realized in "quite different ways", the reader is active only insofar as he arrives at a "socialist" interpretation.

**Conclusion:** Jauss and Iser are surely right that the GDR model relies more on determinate structures, and perhaps right to read this as the mark of a less tolerant society — but there is equally good reason to grant the East German claim that bourgeois reception theory is pseudo-democratic. When all the political invective has been exchanged, the question of what the text demands and what the reader supplies remains. As Iser's system shows, despite the apparent freedom granted to the "active" reader, actual interpretation still limits the possibilities of concretization; Iser too implies that there are correct ways of reading texts, their correctness prescribed by structures already present. Neither Iser, nor Jauss, nor any West German reception theorist is willing to give up all constraint on the production of meaning from the side of the text; the East Germans openly advocate a principle of determinacy in their theory, and the difference from the West is one of degree rather than kind.

**Principle:** In a sense, the Eastern critics' appeal to the implicit norms of the socialist reader or to a materialist view of history actually spares them the central dilemma facing the Westerners. Without a social or historical model, non-Marxist advocates of reception find it hard to steer a course between complete relativity and the uncritical legitimation of tradition. The central problem with the concept of the horizon of expectations is precisely that it cannot be "objectified" socially or historically without contradicting the relativizing principle Jauss adopted from Gadamer's hermeneutics; any statement about the structure of expectations of a past age involves judgements shaped by theory and tradition; even acknowledging the relativity of every historical perspective implies assumptions about the historical process and our ability to understand it. Whether the Marxist interpretation of the past is more valid or accurate because it claims to derive ultimately from concrete interaction with the world (praxis) is not the issue here; the issue is that *no theory of reception can do without some grounding in historical presuppositions*. In their replies to GDR critics neither Jauss nor Iser really faced this crucial point; and although the two sides have tacitly declared a truce in recent years, the problems that sparked the controversy are far from resolved.

### Empirical reception research: what it can and cannot test

**Background:** The ceasefire between East and West does not mean that GDR theorists lost interest in questions of response and effect. On the contrary, reception studies have been as popular in the East as in the Federal Republic: *Weimarer Beiträge* has recently carried several important essays on reception theory, and the latest theoretical collection by researchers at the GDR Academy of Sciences argues for incorporating the functional aspect of literature into the hitherto sacrosanct concept of "reflection" — whether or not such a forced merger is feasible, it at least shows that the Marxist camp is still responding seriously to the challenge of reception theory.

**Example:** Accompanying this interest has been a concern to define the sociological position of readers more precisely, usually by collecting data through surveys or other statistical sources. As early as 1970 Dietrich Sommer and Dietrich Löffler published an essay examining readers' attitudes towards Hermann Kant's popular novel *Die Aula* (1965; *The School Auditorium*): positing an "in-principle possible unity" of individual and social interests, they gathered data on the reading public's needs, its views of the novel's characters and the aspects that interested it most, and concluded rather optimistically that socialist society had forged a unity of standards of quality and standards of effect, and that determining these standards was precisely the task of "effect research". In 1978, together with Walter and Scherf, they edited a large volume on socialist reading habits running to over five hundred pages, with twelve diagrams and thirty-eight statistical tables, ranging from traditional data from libraries and booksellers to readers' views on the social function of art.

**Mechanism:** The GDR has no monopoly on such research. In the past fifteen years dozens of studies have appeared in the West whose main tool is the collection and interpretation of data, usually from readers of literary works. The reasons for this turn seem related to a perceived deficiency of the Constance School's theories: as noted, Jauss and Iser were often criticized for lacking a sociological grounding with respect to readers, and many thought one way to remedy this was to analyse "real" readers, avoiding the abstractions of the "implied reader" or the "horizon of expectations". **Example:** Reinhold Viehoff tried to "objectify" the horizon of expectations empirically by sending a questionnaire to 106 West German literary critics, asking them to evaluate what German literature "actually is like" and what it "should be like". His instrument was the `semantic differential`: a series of adjectives and their supposed opposites, with a seven-point scale between them, on which critics rated actual and "ideal" literature — for example, between "intellectual" and "vital", the collective result showed actual literature regarded as "intellectual" (a rating of 2), whereas it ought ideally to be closer to "vital" (a rating of 4–5). Through a series of statistical analyses and groupings of results he sought to show the distance between ideal and reality in critics' literary expectations and thereby make the horizon of expectations objective; in the process he "discovered" that Jauss's categories are of dubious value: the ideal construct Jauss proposes neglects the extra-aesthetic dimension, which the evidence collected showed to determine the expectations of a considerable number of critics; and aesthetic distance, Jauss's criterion of evaluation, loses its force, because the horizon proves too differentiated to be measured in the way Jauss suggested. Holub notes that both criticisms could be — and in fact had been — made without the aid of statistical analysis.

**Definition:** Perhaps more important than the validity of empirical reception research's criticisms of other reception theories is its self-proclaimed rivalry with these more hermeneutically oriented theories. **Norbert Groeben**, one of the most prolific and influential advocates of empirical research, argues that the true paradigm shift Jauss announced is not the move from the immanent models of New Criticism or Formalism to functional theories of reception and effect, but the move from all hermeneutic schemes to empirically based research. Drawing on Popper's theory of falsification, Werner Stegmüller's analytic philosophy, and the concept of "text processing" developed by Götz Wienold and Siegfried J. Schmidt, he argues for reorienting literary studies along the lines of methods already current in the natural and social sciences. The fundamental flaw of earlier interpretive efforts, he holds, is their failure to separate subject and object strictly: the reader or recipient, who should be the object of literary study, is conflated with the researcher or interpreter; since the entire communicative process (sender – message – reader) is the true object of literary study, interpreting a text amounts to confusing the interpreter with the recipient of the message. Instead the two should be strictly separated: interpretation belongs to the domain of the scientific researcher, who elucidates the understanding of texts by collecting data on recipients' concretizations; the recipient functions in this process merely as a medium through which the researcher objectively observes individual concretizations and arrives at intersubjectively valid theories. Hermeneutics, which Groeben seems to equate with all previous literary theory, is relegated to a heuristic function: hypotheses based on non-empirical exegesis can be submitted to empirically grounded experiments to establish their possible validity; a hypothesis not falsified by rigorously controlled systematic procedures can be regarded as a valid statement. The techniques he mentions include many standard instruments developed by psychologists and social scientists: the semantic differential, free association, cloze procedures and free card-sorting.

**Mechanism:** Groeben's programme for empirical research can be summarized in the following sequence:

1. the researcher encounters a problem in everyday language;
2. it is reformulated within the theoretical framework of the specialized discipline (here, literary studies) as a theoretical hypothesis;
3. the theoretical hypothesis is rewritten as a working hypothesis that can be tested against reality, usually by an experiment using one of the techniques above;
4. at the same time the theoretical construct is translated into an operational model with dependent and independent variables, and data are collected by adjusting the variables and observing changes;
5. once the data have been evaluated and found significant, the results are used to verify or falsify the original theoretical hypothesis.

This procedure, Groeben holds, is designed to ensure results as objective as possible; by continually developing and testing hypotheses in this way, researchers can accumulate and refine knowledge of literature as a communicative process.

**Example:** One of Groeben's earlier studies illustrates how such research proceeds. The "everyday" problem taken as the starting point concerned the communicability of modern lyric poetry: surveying the relevant literature, he found a tendency to classify modern poetry as hermetic or monological, and he reformulated this observation as a theoretical hypothesis: modern lyric poetry, in its structural-aesthetic specificity, is *less* communicable than poetry of the classical aesthetic domain. For the experiment he linked communicability to a text's "redundancy value": communicability increases with redundancy, since redundancy means the fulfilment of expectations or the absence of surprise; and redundancy can be measured by the percentage of signs subjects guess correctly. If predictability declines in readers' responses to modern poetry, it can be considered less communicable. The experimental design was as follows: seventeen poems from various periods over the past 250 years were chosen at random from anthologies of lyric poetry; the subjects were seventeen students in their final year of secondary school; they were asked to guess in turn each sign — each letter and punctuation mark — in the first six to nine lines of each poem; after the slowest student had written down a guess for a given sign, the correct answer was written on the board and the students went on to guess the next sign. The results showed an average redundancy of about 61 per cent for classical poems and only 53 per cent for modern ones. Groeben concluded that in readers' reception modern lyric poetry is clearly less communicable than poetry of the classical aesthetic domain; the circle, he declared, had been closed, and the results justified both the form of the question and the way the results were obtained: communicability is a central category in the structure of modern poetry, and its low degree of explicitness leads to a predominance of subjective concretizations, which must be studied empirically.

**Counterexample:** In Holub's view, this example shows that the methods of empirical research either fall far short of the "scientific" standards they aspire to, or that "scientific" standards are far looser than usually supposed. The first problem arises in translating communicability into quantifiable terms: communication is not a simple process; it takes place on many levels and by many means; assuming that redundancy as defined here is an accurate measure of communication, especially with literary texts, is by no means a safe assumption. More troubling is how the conclusion is reached: one could well argue that all this experiment demonstrates is that newer poems differ from older ones. The norms of classical poetry have gradually become part of the fabric of the language, and poetry itself is habitually defined by the norms of the classical canon; the greater predictability of individual signs in eighteenth-century verse may therefore result from socialized, internalized norms of language and poetic diction rather than from any difference in communicability. To test the relative increase or decrease of communicability one would at least have to determine how classical poetry fared against *its* norms, that is, test eighteenth-century readers' responses with respect to redundancy in parallel fashion. For all its scientific appearance, the experiment raises more questions than it claims to answer, and it certainly does not "clearly" demonstrate anything about communicability.

**Limitation:** The most general criticism of Groeben's procedure is that most questions that matter in literary studies cannot be proved by empirical methods — or, if they can be "proved", perceptive literary scholars would have noticed them long ago without statistical detail. Groeben's study is a case in point: even assuming that communicability is indeed related to redundancy and that the procedures used suit their task, all the experiment does is confirm the overwhelming view of most observers of modern lyric poetry; spending so much time and effort establishing the obvious seems an enormous waste.

**Example:** Other empirical reception studies are unfortunately mostly variations on this dubious theme. Heinz Hillmann's experiment, entitled "Rezeption – empirisch" ("Reception – empirically"), stands out chiefly because it drew a response from Jauss himself. The main difference between its procedure and Groeben's is its openness to readers' responses: 300 subjects were given a very short story from Brecht's *Keuner Stories*, "The meeting" — a man who has not seen Herr Keuner for a long time greets him with the words that he has not changed at all, and Herr Keuner turns pale; the subjects were not told that the text was by Brecht, nor given any other information, but simply asked to write about the text. The result was a variety of "creative" responses in which readers filled in the "gaps" according to personal criteria, mostly interpreting the text with reference to their own life situation; the answers, Hillmann says, show clearly how extensively, if not exclusively, concretization depends on the social group and on socialization in school. Holub finds such conclusions unsurprising: Hillmann too spent a great deal of time proving common sense — assuming we are willing to grant that such spontaneous responses constitute evidence at all; worse, studies of this kind tell us almost nothing about the reader's relation to the text as a literary text and do not deal at all with readers' obvious biases — they merely confirm that when a text is torn from its context and used only as a stimulus for vaguely solicited subjective responses, a mass of ill-conceived and often absurdly naive responses can be elicited.

**Example:** Werner Faulstich, using surveys as his tool, fared somewhat better. Two of the five studies in *Domänen der Rezeptionsanalyse* (1977; *Domains of Reception Analysis*) sent detailed questionnaires to professors of English literature and to lawyers respectively, covering personal interests, working habits, attitudes towards their discipline and personal information; the second survey dealt mainly with the reading habits of what he considered a relatively homogeneous professional group, with the working hypothesis that readers do not freely choose their reading, that their preferences are determined by needs, and that needs depend largely on experiences of primary and secondary socialization. Most people's only question about this hypothesis would be: why does it need proving? More dubious than the hypothesis is the method of proof: the techniques for measuring factors such as need or socialization are often so interrelated with reading habits that one suspects the conclusions were already built into the design of the questions — as in many empirical studies, the instruments seem designed to elicit the appropriate responses. This manipulation of statistical technique is most evident in the conclusion of the first survey, which purportedly "proves" the necessity of pursuing literary studies through empirical research: Faulstich first observes a lack of coherence in English teachers' self-understanding as literary scholars; the problem, according to the data, is that they assign the same role to literary scholarship (*Literaturwissenschaft*) and literary criticism (*Literaturkritik*), whereas "in fact" the two have different demands and functions and must be clearly distinguished; the way to distinguish them, of course, is to define literary criticism as an activity operating hermeneutically, while literary scholarship analyses the hermeneutic process itself "objectively" — and so "recourse to empiricism is unavoidable". *His "objective" method not only discovers his colleagues' malaise but presents itself as the panacea for their ills.*

**Example:** The statistical overkill characteristic of so many empirical studies is best exemplified by a book-length study of a single short poem by Celan, "Fadensonnen" ("Thread suns"): *Text und Rezeption* (1972). Six researchers made an extensive analysis of responses to a questionnaire about the poem. The subjects came from primary and secondary schools (including evening schools) and university classes, aged from fourteen to seventy-four, most between seventeen and twenty-four; the questionnaire was rather lengthy and combined several empirical techniques — standard yes/no and multiple-choice questions as well as questions designed on the principles of free association and the semantic differential. Unlike the studies above, this experiment dealt mainly with responses to the poem itself, focusing on readers' associations with difficult and central words such as "thread suns" and "light-tones"; a kind of pedagogical "manipulation" was also built into the order of the questionnaire: it began with general questions about the poem's structure and meaning, then forced readers to confront individual images through the semantic differential and free association, and then asked questions about the poem as a whole again. Most readers did seem to gain a better understanding of the poem's meaning in this way — in this study not only did the researchers learn about readers' responses, but readers also gained insight into a literary work. But whether such pedagogical gains are worth the heavy statistical effort remains debatable: training people to read poetry with more attention to detail and more sensitivity to nuance can be achieved more effectively by other didactic techniques; more than 150 pages of text, charts and data bring too little educational or hermeneutic value, and an essay by a perceptive critic would undoubtedly tell us more. After reading such studies one is left with the same two major questions about "empirical" methods: do these techniques really prove what they set out to prove? And even if they provide evidence for hypotheses, could the same conclusions not be reached more simply and just as convincingly by other means?

**Distinction:** Because of the limited value of empirical reception research so far, exchange between "hermeneutic" reception theorists and empiricists has been extremely rare: neither "school" has drawn the other into extensive debate or dialogue, and neither attends to the latest work of the other camp. If the two positions agree on anything, it is presumably that the tenets underpinning the other are so mistaken that no fruitful relationship is possible — the empirical school grants its hermeneutic opponents only heuristic value, while Jauss and others would no doubt regard empirical methods as a relic of outmoded scientism. Yet there is some common ground worth exploring. Although the results so far have often been dull and disappointing, empiricism as such is not without a place in future research programmes: if empiricism is understood as an epistemological theory that fundamentally defines our acquisition of knowledge in terms of experience or sense impressions, it is hard to see how we could avoid appealing to it altogether — reading a text itself involves some sensory input. What deserves extreme scepticism is not this philosophical starting point but its abuse when it is turned into a dogmatic method claiming a monopoly on truth.

**Conclusion:** The problem with what has so far gone under the name of empirical reception theory is less an unavoidable empiricism than a `naive scientism` that has contributed nothing to literary theory and practice beyond a great many printed pages. If empirical research is to play a useful role in a larger critical programme concerned with response and effect, it must assume a more modest, auxiliary function: studying actual readings of a text can be, and has been, a useful undertaking for exploring the dynamics of texts and the sociology of readers; examining the composition and habits of different groups of readers can provide information that helps clarify the literary process as a whole; even experimental methods, used with extreme caution, can offer insight into how reading takes place — a question about which we know very little. Continuing on its present path, empirical reception theory will remain an isolated and ridiculed branch of the literary enterprise; purged of its absolutist notions of objectivity and applied judiciously, empirical research could become an aid rather than a bane to our understanding of literary texts and their reception.

*The three extensions in P12 — communicative structure, social determination, empirical data — have one thing in common: each tries, after reception theory gave up "textual determinacy", to relocate determinacy somewhere else. The communication model puts it in systems of meaning and levels of communication, Marxism in relations of production and social modes of reception, empirical research in measurable reader data; and each proves unable to do without some historical presupposition and interpretive judgement. P14 will push this situation to its limit: when text, reader, interpretation and history itself have all been problematized, where can determinacy still be placed?*

## P13 Can tradition be criticized? Hermeneutics and critical theory

### Horkheimer's programme for critical theory

**Background:** Despite differences among the major theorists of the Frankfurt School, the common core of their social criticism, as Thomas McCarthy puts it, is a "critical reconstruction of Enlightenment conceptions of reason and the rational subject" in which these are recast in sociohistorical terms rather than simply dismantled. From this point of view, the worry about Gadamer's philosophical hermeneutics is that, in its focus on historical tradition, it comes too close to simply dismantling rather than recasting these Enlightenment conceptions. Habermas's reaction to Gadamer's hermeneutics in the 1970s and Honneth's criticism of it in the 2000s were both motivated by this commitment; and the concerns they advance were set in direction by the programmatic statements of **Max Horkheimer** in the 1930s.

**Principle:** Horkheimer's recasting of critical theory is perhaps clearest in his 1939 essay "The Social Function of Philosophy". Here he compares the tasks of philosophy with those of what he calls the special sciences — the empirical natural and social sciences. The problem with these sciences is that they focus on human needs, actions and desires as they emerge within given social and historical conditions without questioning those conditions; they are concerned with human life only "in its present form". He suggests that much of contemporary philosophy has a similar purview: where it does not imagine abstract utopias divorced from any contact with human reality — what we might today call ideal theory — it sees its function as serving the sciences and accepting "the tasks set for it by the ever renewed needs of government and industry". Yet he insists that the historical thrust of philosophy has been otherwise: philosophy has always set itself against mere tradition and resignation in the decisive problems of existence, and has shouldered the unpleasant task of throwing the light of consciousness even upon those human relations and modes of action that have become so deeply rooted that they seem natural, immutable and eternal.

**Mechanism:** For Horkheimer, critical theory is precisely the continuation of this philosophical task: reflecting critically on the world in which we find ourselves, in light of the potential for social rationality that world contains. In his essays of the 1930s he follows Marx in looking to the rationality, "if in a restricted sense", embodied in the technological mastery of nature: human work contains an element of planning, and immanent in it is the idea of a rational society in which the contributions of individual members consciously and transparently complement one another. That they do not do so in existing societies is because these societies entrust technological mastery "to special, mutually opposed interests": events occur not as collective, conscious action but as the blind outcome of uncoordinated activities, and it is enough for individuals to look out for themselves. Under these conditions the social and historical world — the product of human activity and hence open to transparent collective direction — appears instead as a kind of `second nature`, indifferent to human aims, actions and desires, the "sum-total of facts; it is there and must be accepted". Horkheimer even holds that this opposition between the results of collective human activity and what appears to individuals as facts to be passively accepted explains both Kant's dualism of "purely passive sensation and active understanding" and his recourse to a transcendental subject that pre-forms sensible appearances for perception behind the individual's back. Critical theory's effort is to capture the kernel of truth in this Kantian dualism while releasing it from its ideological imprisonment in conceptual mystifications; it thereby assists in a process of Enlightenment that reconnects the "sum total of facts" to collective activity and refashions that activity as the rationally coordinated contributions of consciously acting human beings.

**Condition:** Horkheimer does not think philosophy can accomplish this process of Enlightenment alone. It needs to coordinate with empirically based, interdisciplinary analyses able to uncover the "concrete relations and tendencies" that point, on the one hand, to rational potentials and, on the other, to the factors impeding their realization. In his 1931 inaugural address as director of the Institute for Social Research he called for the "ongoing dialectical permeation and evaluation of philosophical theory and empirical-scientific praxis", a permeation that would include studies of "the economic life of society, the psychological development of its individuals and the changes within specific areas of culture" — a culture that includes not only the intellectual legacy of science, art and religion but also law, customs, fashion, public opinion, sports, entertainment, lifestyles and so on.

**Distinction:** In "Traditional and Critical Theory" Horkheimer contrasts the intent of these studies with the special sciences, which he here calls traditional theory: the latter, modelled on the natural sciences, regard themselves as a value-free exploration of a world or second nature independent of them, whereas an empirical-scientific praxis permeated by philosophical concerns is `reflexive`, aware of its implication in the same social and historical world it studies. He notes four interrelated aspects of this awareness. First, critical theory recognizes that its subject matter includes conditions and processes that influence its own projects and agendas. Second, it is aware that the ideas and concepts it uses to understand the social world themselves arise out of that world. Third, since the "sum total of facts" is produced by human activity, critical theory acknowledges that this activity includes the practice of social science itself — what the special sciences take as their value-free neutrality is, insofar as they let themselves be guided by human reality as it is, in fact a non-value-free endorsement of the established social context, whereas critical theory recognizes its own non-value-free status as a form of social practice. Fourth, it is aware that the objects of its research can become subjects of knowledge. Critical theory thus takes up a relation to the groups suffering most from the irrational organization of society; but Horkheimer denies that it can simply register that group's point of view: if critical theory consisted essentially in formulating the feelings of one class at a given moment, it would not be structurally different from the special sciences — merely a traditional social psychology taking an external, "objective" perspective on its subject matter without concern for its potential for self-enlightenment. Critical theory enters a "dynamic unity" with the oppressed class: its function is not to avoid possible conflict with it but to investigate developments in the structure of society and its role in them, so as to illuminate the possibilities of a more rational future.

**Influence:** As is well known, Horkheimer's (and Adorno's) confidence in a rational future did not survive the horror of National Socialism. *Dialectic of Enlightenment* no longer sees the reason immanent in human work as the potential for a rational society but as the problem itself: the technological mastery once associated with the possibility of a rational organization of society is now seen only as domination, and used to explain "why humanity, instead of entering a truly human state, is sinking into a new barbarism". Yet according to Habermas, Horkheimer was always aware of the limits of defining reason in terms of technological mastery: for him this "restricted sense" of reason was a by-product of the bourgeois era, though one that pointed beyond itself to a post-bourgeois form of society that would redeem the promise of substantive reason. And if by the early 1940s Horkheimer no longer believed in this promise, Habermas thinks his work of the 1950s and 1960s was more ambivalent: in his *Notizen* Habermas finds the seed of an idea that "might have restored to criticism a piece of its self-confidence" — that language, whether it wants to or not, must make a claim to truth, and that truth in speech accrues not to the isolated, naked judgement, as if printed on a piece of paper, but to the speaker's relationship to the world as expressed in judgement, focused on this place and referring to a specific object. *With these notions Horkheimer points, if vaguely, to the rational basis of speech oriented to mutual understanding — the idea Habermas pursues in his theory of communicative action, which he thinks can redeem the potential for social reason to which critical theory was originally committed. His interest in Gadamer's hermeneutics stems from this pursuit: it too attends to language and the possibility of mutual understanding, but in his view stops short of the critical reflection that is needed.*

### Habermas: hermeneutics' claim to universality and systematically distorted communication

**Background:** Like Horkheimer, Habermas is interested in how far philosophy and empirically based social science can join forces to uncover and explain deficits in social rationality; like Horkheimer, he finds the self-understanding of traditional, or what he calls positivistic, social sciences problematic, because they neglect their own reflexivity and immersion in the social and human world they study. He finds linguistic approaches to the social sciences equally problematic. Drawing on Wittgenstein, such approaches no longer equate the social sciences with the explanation of behavioural regularities but look to understanding `language games`, comprising "symbolically regulated interactions".
