They may represent an advance over positivism, since they move to the plane of shared or intersubjective meanings: explaining the causes of certain behaviours first requires determining what those behaviours are, and that requires understanding the complex of concepts, actions and normative expectations — the language game or form of life — in terms of which they have the sense they have. But in Habermas's view linguistic approaches conceive of understanding language games only as socialization within them, on the model of learning a first language; they thereby assume that we can leave our own language game behind and that our prior socialization within it has no bearing on our understanding of a new one. The social scientist becomes a free-floating linguistic analyst who is supposed to be able to "slip into the grammar of any language game without being himself bound by the dogmatism of his own". The question then arises how this free-floating is possible. Like positivistic approaches, linguistic approaches overlook the social and historical situation of social scientists themselves, already socialized into particular language games.

**Principle:** For Habermas, one virtue of Gadamer's hermeneutics is precisely that it begins from this social and historical situation and so offers an alternative to the self-understandings of social science that assume they can extract themselves from it. As Habermas explicates it, philosophical hermeneutics is a reflection on the experiences of language we gain in exercising our communicative competence: mastering a language means acquiring the capacity both to understand and to make ourselves understood; hermeneutics develops this capacity as an interpretive discipline, and philosophical hermeneutics reflects on its conditions. Its "characteristic insight", as he sees it, is the recognition that the means of natural language are, in principle, sufficient for elucidating the sense of any symbolic complex, however unfamiliar and inaccessible it may initially appear. Our ability to understand language games distant from our own depends not on liberation from our own but on `translation` into it: we learn a foreign language on the basis of the one we already possess and make sense of its contents by relating them to contents we already understand. On the one hand, translation gives priority to our own language and its interpretive resources — which is why Gadamer stresses the prejudiced character of understanding: hermeneutic understanding "is … unavoidably prepossessed by the context within which the understanding subject has initially acquired his interpretive schemes". On the other hand, translation expands our original language so that we can express what is expressed in the language we are translating, and in doing so the language being translated can illuminate the context and prejudices with which we began, offering a new perspective on them.

**Mechanism:** The same complex of prepossession, extension and illumination holds when speakers of the same language need translation because historical distance means they can no longer understand one another directly. In both cultural and historical distance, translation is less a matter of achieving one-to-one equivalence of terms than of communicating and agreeing on meaning. Whereas for Wittgenstein language games form tight complexes of concepts and actions that we must learn as natives in order to understand them at all, for Gadamer, according to Habermas, languages form open horizons: not static entities but resources and repositories of shared meaning that can expand and fuse with other horizons, making mutual understanding possible across cultures and historical generations and between ordinary partners in communication. In translation, Habermas writes, we are not concerned with a transformation that permits statements in one language system to be reduced to statements in another; the act of translation points rather to a productive accomplishment to which language always empowers those who have mastered its grammatical rules — assimilating what is foreign and thereby further developing one's own language system; this occurs every day in situations where dialogue partners must first find a "common language", which is the result of having reached an understanding.

**Mechanism:** The notion of the fusion of horizons marks a second virtue of Gadamer's hermeneutics for Habermas: it represents the productive possibilities of personal and linguistic, and also historical, distance in enlarging our understanding of meaning, and so depicts a continuing tradition in which the horizons of the present emerge out of those of the past and provide an expanded perspective on them. The extension of language systems in translation is mirrored in the extension of the meaning of historical events, actions, texts and the like through the events, actions and texts that follow them — what Gadamer calls effective history (for Habermas's example of the Thirty Years War, see P7). A third virtue is the connection Gadamer's hermeneutics establishes between hermeneutic understanding and "the articulation of an action-orienting self-understanding": just as the classical hermeneutic circle describes understanding the opening parts of a text by anticipating the meaning of the whole and continually revising that anticipation on the basis of an accumulating understanding of the parts, so we understand past historical events from a present horizon that cannot know their end, yet necessarily project that end, and hence the future, as the context of understanding, revising the projection in light of ongoing events. Habermas therefore declares that every historian is in the role of the last historian: what he can know historically cannot be grasped independently of the framework of his own life-praxis; in this context what is in the future exists only within the horizon of expectations, and these expectations form the fragments of previous tradition into a hypothetical totality of pre-understood universal history, in the light of which every relevant event can in principle be as completely described as is possible for the practically effective self-understanding of a social lifeworld.

**Limitation:** For Habermas, Gadamer's philosophical hermeneutics opens up congealed language games and accounts for the understanding of historical tradition in a way that connects it to the tradition's ongoing development and orients social action. Yet he also thinks it lacks an adequate recognition of possible rational deficits in a tradition's development. Gadamer is justified, he suggests, in emphasizing the capacity of natural languages to extend themselves in encounters with other languages so as to express whatever they want to express, thereby forging a common language and reaching agreement on meaning; and justified in insisting that understanding in natural languages can illuminate the biases and prejudices that have accumulated in them along with their interpretive resources. Less evident is the capacity of understanding in natural languages to reflect on possible `systematic distortions` *internal* to them: such distortions cannot be exposed in encounters with other languages that might reveal the biases and prejudices of one's own, because the distortion lies in the very language of the encounter. Habermas concedes that within the limits of tolerance of normal everyday communication we can determine what we do not (yet) know when we try to make sense of an incomprehensible complex of meaning; but, he continues, this hermeneutic consciousness proves inadequate in the case of systematically distorted communication, where incomprehensibility is the result of a defective organization of speech itself.

**Evidence:** As evidence of such defective organization Habermas turns to the pathologically distorted expressions examined by Freudian psychoanalytic theory. Distortions of communication occur on three levels: on the level of linguistic symbols, whose use deviates from the publicly accepted system of linguistic rules and can affect semantic content, syntax and whole fields of meaning; on the level of behaviour, which becomes rigid and compulsive; and on the level of the relation between language and behaviour, where "the usual congruence between linguistic symbols, actions and accompanying expressions has disintegrated". Gadamer can of course argue that our very capacity to detect distorted communication already depends on a background communicative consensus — Freud's detection of distortion relies on conventions of language use we generally share. But for Habermas the difficulty lies precisely with this background consensus: evidence of distorted communication means we cannot assume that the consensus is itself free of systematic distortion. We could identify a legitimate consensus with a given factual agreement, he argues, only if we could be certain that each consensus reached in the medium of linguistic tradition had been achieved without compulsion and distortion.

### Psychoanalysis and the reconstructive sciences as models of critique

**Principle:** Philosophical hermeneutics insists that our immersion in a natural language means we have no standpoint from which to verify whether it contains internal distortions; Habermas denies that critical social science must operate under this constraint. Psychoanalysis, as an example of such a science, can appeal to a theoretical reference system geared to the analytic dialogue and allowing methodological control over that dialogue. The analytic dialogue departs from ordinary communication: the analyst does not engage the patient as a communication partner capable of expanding the analyst's horizon; instead, the analyst steps outside the interaction to become the object of `transference`, the point of which is to open a space for decoding symbols that have become incomprehensible to the patient.

**Mechanism:** Here Habermas draws on Alfred Lorenzer's account of psychoanalysis as a form of linguistic analysis. Within the analytic dialogue, the theory specifies the typical pathological situation as one in which a child experiences and then represses an unbearable conflict; this repression is explained as the exclusion of the experience from public communication and the "desymbolization" of the meaning of the conflict-laden object; the gap in the semantic field the symbol once occupied is then filled by a symptom. The analyst facilitates access to the originally repressed experience by allowing the scenes in which the symptom occurs to be played out in the transference relation — a relation the analyst establishes by taking on the role of the conflict-laden primary object. The warrant for the truth of the theory lies in the patient's capacity to recover the lost meaning. What Lorenzer calls `scenic understanding` proceeds, Habermas writes, from the insight that the patient behaves in his symptomatic scenes as he does in certain transference scenes; it aims at a reconstruction of the original scene, which the patient validates in an act of self-reflection. *Habermas's account of psychoanalysis as a critical science coheres with Horkheimer's claim that critical theory assists in the Enlightenment of its subject matter by reconnecting what appears as a second nature opposed to it to possibilities of rational direction.* (On Habermas's "deprivative" reconstruction of psychoanalysis and Ricoeur's different reading, see P5.)

**Definition:** In later work Habermas moves from the example of psychoanalysis to the `reconstructive sciences`, which try to make basic intuitive competences theoretically explicit. Reconstructive sciences yield knowledge that is empirical rather than a priori, fallible rather than certain; nonetheless, Habermas holds that they can uncover invariant structures, and in particular that a reconstructive science of communicative competence can provide knowledge of the pragmatic conditions presupposed in reaching understanding with others. "Anyone acting communicatively must, in performing any speech act, raise universal validity claims and suppose that they can be vindicated." Or, as he also puts it: we understand a speech act when we know the kinds of reasons a speaker could provide to convince a hearer that he is entitled in the given circumstances to claim validity for his utterance — in short, when we know what makes it acceptable. In making assertions or raising normative claims, speakers take on the burden of being able to provide reasons for their utterances that hearers can freely accept; where at least initially they cannot, speaker and hearer can move from ordinary speech into `discourse`, in which the reasons for and against the contested claim are explicitly taken up and agreement is reached on the force of the better argument alone. This is the core of `universal pragmatics`.

**Conclusion:** In considering Gadamer's analysis, Habermas therefore insists: a critically enlightened hermeneutics that differentiates between insight and delusion incorporates the meta-hermeneutic awareness of the conditions of possibility of systematically distorted communication; it connects the process of understanding to the principle of rational discourse, according to which truth would be guaranteed only by the kind of consensus achieved under the idealized conditions of unlimited communication free from domination and capable of being maintained over time. Both psychoanalysis and the reconstructive sciences show Habermas that Gadamer's "accurate critique" of the self-understanding of certain social-scientific approaches should not have led him to oppose hermeneutic understanding to "methodical knowledge as a whole".

### Honneth: the power of the third

**Problem:** Honneth is equally suspicious of what he takes to be Gadamer's rejection of the possibilities of reflection. In "On the Destructive Power of the Third: Gadamer and Heidegger's Doctrine of Intersubjectivity" (2003) he focuses on Gadamer's account of experience, and especially on the set of parallels Gadamer establishes between the experience of historical tradition and the experience of reciprocal recognition between an "I" and a "Thou". On the one hand, these parallels are for Honneth "a methodological turning-point of the greatest utility", since they allow a normative assessment of different approaches to historical tradition; on the other, he finds it "astonishing" that Gadamer privileges an immediate form of reciprocal recognition free from the intervention of what Honneth calls "a third", the `generalized other`.

**Mechanism:** As Honneth explains, Gadamer's discussion of experience is an argument with Hegel. Gadamer endorses the insight in Hegel's dialectic into the importance of negative experience but draws different conclusions: repeatedly suffering negative experiences leads not to absolute knowledge but to insight into our continuing fallibility; knowledge is something that happens to us rather than something we control (see P7). Were Gadamer's argument with Hegel a direct one, Honneth thinks, he would move straight from these conclusions about the consequences of experience to identifying the understanding of historical tradition with experience: in experiencing tradition we do not hope reflexively to attain a determinate cognition but the converse — an expansion of our knowledge accomplishes itself in us, an expansion fundamentally open to renewed correction and so without closure. But Gadamer conceives of historical tradition as a set of voices that can address us and that we can also fail to hear; tradition thus has the character of a Thou, and our relation to it the character of an intersubjective relation. He therefore examines three possible modes of relation to a Thou and links them to three ways of understanding historical tradition (see P7): the objectifying relation corresponds to the positivist self-understanding; authoritarian solicitude to scientistic hermeneutics (which Honneth associates with Dilthey); only at the third level, where both I and Thou allow themselves a genuine connection with each other, do the "surprise value" of tradition and the experiences it provokes become possible.

**Principle:** Honneth underlines what this conclusion implies: the parallels drawn on all three levels end exactly at the point where the experiential character of all historical understanding becomes transparent — one can speak of an appropriate attitude towards historical tradition only if tradition is conceived as an interaction partner to which one must open oneself trustingly in understanding; any intrusion of reflection would only be disruptive, since it would remove the ground of the prior bond between subject and history on which the necessary trust is first premised. Contrary to what the philosophy of reflection would have it, the understanding of history is thus an accomplishment that *happens*, not a reflexive act of appropriation.

**Counterexample:** In Honneth's view this conclusion shares an assumption with Heidegger: that any move away from immediate openness to the other is a move towards the "they" (*das Man*) — "reflecting out" of a direct relation to a particular or concrete other in favour of a relation to a more generalized other who could be anyone. But Honneth argues that insisting on the former as the only authentic relation to others is plausible only with regard to those closest to us, and even there it has its limits: even in close personal relationships we can always judge our actions and interactions by socially generalized norms and standards of conduct, which can act as a corrective to merely receptive openness. This is the `power of the third`: judgement from the perspective of an impartial party or generalized other. If this perspective is available even within intimate relationships, its appropriateness is all the clearer in more distant and anonymous ones, where we cannot demand a trusting openness to others but rely on something more like mutual respect.

**Conclusion:** The conclusion for our relation to historical tradition follows: it too must be subject to two forms of understanding. One can be identified with the openness to experience corresponding to our relations with those closest to us; the other should incorporate the generalized perspective of the impartial third — a relation precisely "reflected out" of immediate engagement and able to take a more distanced stance. History, Honneth writes, is appropriately made present only when the two standpoints of the "concrete" and the "generalized" other continually and reciprocally correct one another.

### Gadamer's reply: the role and limits of reflection

**Principle:** The starting point for critical theory as Horkheimer established it is the capacity of critical reflection to disclose deficits in social reason and to assist in a process of Enlightenment that overcomes them. Following Horkheimer, both Habermas and Honneth see Gadamer's attitude towards reflection as a problem. Habermas challenges what he sees as the precedence Gadamer gives to the authority of tradition, claiming that "reflection does not wear itself out on the facticity of traditional norms without leaving a trace. It is condemned to operate after the fact; but, operating in retrospect, it unleashes retroactive power." Honneth challenges what he sees as the misplaced precedence Gadamer gives to immediate experience, emphasizing the capacity to moderate our trusting openness to tradition and to reflect on it in light of generalized norms. Gadamer was not without a response to his critics, and his reply to Habermas also suggests how he might answer Honneth.

**Counterexample:** Gadamer's first objection targets Habermas's depiction of his hermeneutics as a resource for considering the "positivistic ossification of sociological logic". In Gadamer's view this restricts philosophical hermeneutics to the methodological concerns of the social sciences and neglects what he takes to be more properly at its centre: experiences such as those we have of art. Such experiences are characterized by our being moved, stirred, shocked or otherwise engaged in ways that can be neither stimulated nor enhanced by methods or procedures. The same holds, for Gadamer, of our experiences of tradition and of other people: they have the power to affect us profoundly, to undermine our assumptions and presumptions and to transform what we thought we knew. Precisely these possibilities are lost when we insist, as Gadamer thinks Habermas does, on "raising understanding up out of a prescientific exercise to the rank of a self-reflecting activity". They would be lost too, for Gadamer, were we to try to reflect out of an immediate experience of art, tradition or the other and take up the generalized perspective of the impartial third, as Honneth recommends: in our self-involved concern with our own knowledge we split ourselves off from engagement with the very world we seek to understand — and the result, for Gadamer, can only be what he calls an "alienated understanding".

**Counterexample:** Gadamer's second objection targets the premise Habermas takes from Horkheimer: the potential for a rational society anticipated in human work or in the pragmatic structure of communication oriented to understanding. Here, as Gadamer sees it, the idea is that individuals and societies can become fully transparent to themselves, and that critical sciences can help reconnect unconscious motives and compulsions to their sources and expose ideological justifications to the light of day. On the one hand, Gadamer denies that such sources and ideologies lie beyond the grasp of hermeneutic inquiry: they represent prejudices and relations of power taken up and illuminated in our ongoing experience; he even argues that we could regard psychological compulsion and ideology as outside the scope of hermeneutic understanding only if they were outside language altogether — a possibility Habermas's own account seems to deny.

**Definition:** On the other hand, Gadamer rejects the possibility of full transparency. He distinguishes hermeneutic or `effective reflection` from the kind of reflection Habermas and Honneth emphasize, which, because it distances itself from direct experience, he can see only as "making everything an object and creating the conditions for science in the modern sense". Hermeneutic reflection differs in two ways. First, it underlines our inability to limit in advance what we can come to understand: to the extent that we are open to experience, our prejudices and pre-understandings are always in play, and what we expected or thought we knew can always be shattered by the "surprise value" of what we openly encounter. Second, "effective reflection" acknowledges that, because we are always already immersed in language, we cannot lay all its contents out before us to be exhaustively explored at once. Reflection on a given pre-understanding, Gadamer writes, brings before me something that otherwise happens behind my back — something, but not everything. We can come to grasp the unsustainability of some of our prejudices only insofar as we remain open to experience, and only on the basis of other prejudices that, at least for the time being, we do not question; and when we do question the latter, it is on the basis of the authority of still others not currently in question.

**Distinction:** Hence, while Habermas emphasizes reason over authority and Honneth reflection over experience, Gadamer maintains that these dichotomies are not as stark as they suppose: we can always question the authority of aspects of our tradition, but we do so on the basis of other aspects — inherited ideals, principles and the like — that we do not question; likewise, we can reflect on our experiences, but only if we do not begin by distancing ourselves from them. Yet even if the dichotomies are less stark than Habermas and Honneth suppose, the distance between Gadamer and his Frankfurt critics remains. In *Truth and Method* he follows his analysis of negative experience with an account of dialogue in which participants consider the challenges their interlocutors pose to their understanding of the subject matter and, together with them, rethink what they thought they had grasped; he even insists on the "discipline of questioning and inquiring", calling it "a discipline that guarantees truth" — in this respect anticipating the communicative reason Habermas develops. For Gadamer, however, questioning remains tied to the immediate experience Honneth seeks to moderate: questions, he insists, are not the product of critical reason but arise out of experiences, including experiences of others, that bring us up short. To this extent, what he sees as genuine dialogue in the participants' openness to one another Honneth finds normatively undirected, and Habermas thinks fails to find a way "to differentiate between insight and delusion". Karl-Otto Apel joined this criticism on similar grounds, using psychoanalytic theory to question the reliability of pre-understandings derived from historical tradition and doubting whether understanding can rest content with an "event of truth".

**Conclusion:** *This debate bears directly on reception theory. When GDR critics charged the aesthetics of reception with lacking a meta-principle for evaluating past judgements (P12), they were in effect repeating Habermas's critique of Gadamer at the level of reception theory: if meaning lies in effective history, what can identify Nazi literary scholarship as a distortion rather than a legitimate link in tradition? Iser's demand that readers set aside ideological positions and be "open" to the text (P11) unwittingly places him between Gadamer and critical theory: he demands openness, yet takes Enlightenment "freedom from prejudice" as his ideal — the very freedom from prejudice Gadamer denies is possible. The three positions — Gadamer's "effective reflection", Habermas's "ideal consensus in rational discourse", and Honneth's "reciprocal correction of concrete and generalized other" — offer reception theory three possible models of validity, and reception theory itself never made a clear choice among them.*

## P14 Where is meaning located? The limits of the turn to the reader

### Is the text stable? The displacement of determinacy

**Background:** The popularity of empirical research in reception studies since the mid-1970s may be a symptom of a more general crisis of reception theory: based on the experimental methods of the natural sciences, empirical research has so far represented either a retreat from hermeneutic and phenomenological starting points or a self-understanding as a supplier of data and results to more comprehensive literary models — in no case has it advanced the literary theory of the Constance School. And on the theoretical front, the best-known advocates of non-empirical methods have been equally unproductive in recent years: Stierle's and Iser's last major works appeared in 1975 and 1976 respectively, and the revised and expanded version of Jauss's *Aesthetic Experience* (1982) seems to offer no major theoretical breakthrough. This stagnation need not signal intellectual bankruptcy: it can also be seen as testimony to the richness of the initial speculative activity, since the advocates have moved on to more detailed and better-defined fields of research — in Kuhn's terms, the past five to ten years might be conceived as a period of "normal science" with no "revolution" disturbing the dominant paradigm. But the dearth of new theory can also be seen as the consequence of the difficulties reception theory encountered when its initial hypotheses were logically extended: the paths it explored were not always as open and fruitful as first imagined, and detours, dead ends and circular trails were frequent. This becomes evident when reception theory is confronted with positions in France and America associated with structuralism, post-structuralism or other "avant-garde" directions — theories that also contain a great deal of discourse challenging mainstream ways of thinking about literature, often in more radical (if not always more productive) ways. What follows examines in turn four areas — text, reader, interpretation and literary history — in which the consequences and limits of reception theory's reorientation of scholarship are most evident, touching along the way on the differences between reception theory and other currents of contemporary criticism. Between reader and interpretation an assessment of twentieth-century French contributions to hermeneutics (Barthes, Todorov, Kristeva, Derrida) is inserted: the "avant-garde" is not merely a foil for reception theory; it contains achievements that can be defended in their own right and that connect with the German hermeneutic tradition.

**Principle:** Before the advent of reception theory the text — usually understood as the verbal work of art or literary work of art — reigned supreme: influenced by Anglo-American New Criticism and aided by a native tradition of stylistic analysis, early post-war German scholarship directed attention to close reading and explication of the text. Reception theory unsettled this interpretive practice by treating the text as a function of its readers and its reception: the notion of an objective, timeless work of art with a unique structure and a single determinate meaning was replaced by models in which the essence of a work is the never-completed unfolding of its effective history and its meaning is constituted by the interaction of text and reader. In Jauss's aesthetics of reception, the text we read can never be separated from its history of reception: the horizon in which it first appeared both differs from ours and, being temporally separated from the present yet constitutive of the present horizon, belongs to ours; the text, as mediator between horizons, is therefore unstable — as the present horizon changes, so does the character of the fusion of horizons, and the understanding of the text made possible by that fusion becomes a function of history. In short, the text is grasped in its becoming rather than as a fixed entity. Iser introduced another set of considerations equally unsettling for the traditional conception of the text: for him the work of art is constituted in and by the act of reading; the essence and meaning of the literary work belong not to the text but to the process in which textual structures and the reader's ideation interact; in this interaction the reader is assigned the task of building a unique, previously unformulated work of art and of recognizing the patterns of meaning that emerge from these constructions. From different perspectives, Jauss and Iser both dismantled the old construct of the text as a stable foundation for interpretation and literary history; displaced from the centre of literary studies, the text survives in reception theory only through readers and through the history of readers' relations to it.

**Limitation:** Yet one can argue that the displacement Jauss and Iser advocated represents only a superficial shift of interpretive focus. Despite the rhetoric of "readers' rights", the text as a stable, determinate structure often manages to infiltrate the core of reception theory. Jauss's reliance on the objectification of the horizon of expectations, and his efforts to describe works through text linguistics, are two examples of determinacy being covertly reintroduced: to reconstruct past literary horizons one must assume the stability of the texts that constitute them; and the linguistic signals Jauss finds in texts recall an earlier interpretive paradigm in which a supposedly neutral observer describes the literary work as a constant structure. Iser's discussion of textual determinacy can likewise be seen as a device for giving the text new stability: even when the work of art is endowed with indeterminacy, the underlying structure that makes the various readings possible remains constant.

**Mechanism:** At some level Iser, Jauss and other reception theorists all appeal to a determinate text (or subtext) to forestall a potentially wholly subjective and arbitrary reader response. Within a traditional framework there is of course nothing wrong with claiming that texts are determinate; but in a model based on reception or effect it raises two problems. First, it may undermine the novelty and consistency of the approach: if appeal is ultimately made to the features of a knowable text, the suspicion easily arises that reception theory has often changed only the critical vocabulary, not the way literature is analysed — substituting the "objectified horizon" for the "spirit of the age", "gaps and indeterminacy" for "ambiguity and irony" in the text. Second, even granting a partial shift of focus, the problem of establishing the determinate parts of the text remains: if the essence of the text lies in its becoming, how can we describe it in terms of "signals" in anything but a provisional sense? And on what grounds do we allow variability in what the reader ideates while denying variability to the elements that — by the literary scholar's account — belong to intersubjectively verifiable enabling structures? *By unsettling the text while covertly introducing aspects of textual determinacy, reception theorists seem to have backed themselves into a theoretical corner: if they consistently based themselves on "reception" or "effect", the tools of textual analysis they use ought to be unavailable to them; if these tools are still introduced, they can be charged with merely shifting determinacy from one level of the text to another.*

**Counterexample:** Being consistently "anti-textual" is of course possible, but this theoretical postulate offers only a safe position in argument: in practice we have to pretend that texts exist and that statements can be made about them. The work of **Stanley Fish** perhaps best illustrates such theoretical anti-textualism. In his essays of the 1970s he increasingly placed meaning and interpretation in the domain of the reader: readers belong to an "interpretive community" whose conventions determine what is perceived as a text and how it is understood. In this sense one could say that the text contributes nothing to interpretation and everything depends on what readers bring to it. Asked what, then, readers read and critics interpret, Fish has no answer; but he would hold that no one else can answer either, since any attempt to establish an objective essence independent of readers — even mere black marks on a white page — is already made from within an interpretive community. The text disappears at this metacritical level, because Fish holds that any statement about it is already shaped by prior interpretive conventions (in the broadest sense). Yet in his interpretive encounters with texts — or with whatever he takes himself to encounter in reading — Fish is most often engaged in extremely detailed accounts of the production of meaning and paradox. This practice does not conflict with his theoretical position, because he claims no greater validity for his readings than for anyone else's: if we agree with his interpretation, it is because we have adopted his conventions; his work is not more correct, at most more "interesting".

**Limitation:** But this way of coping with the stubborn persistence of textual determinacy is ultimately unsatisfying. To accept Fish's position one must accept all his metacritical assumptions about conventions and communities; and, as noted (P11), his own position is by no means immune to his own metacriticism: why are Fish's statements not equally determined by an interpretive community, and so subject to the same suspension of validity as everyone else's? Moreover, even if his position could be accepted, all he achieves is a shift of determinacy from the text to another construct — whether called reader, convention or community. The apparent stability of views about certain texts, or about the "fact" that texts are stable, may result from belonging to a cohesive little interpretive circle; but where does the stability of this group come from, and how is it defined and determined? *The text may indeed vanish in Fish's model, but determinacy simply reappears in another guise: even if we agree that nothing belongs to the text and that the text is ultimately indescribable, as soon as we notice similarities in interpretation we must acknowledge that something determinate controls our interpretive agreement.*

### The proliferation of readers and the decentred reader

**Principle:** Reception theorists tend to call this controlling force the reader, and much of their work can be understood as an attempt to show that the reader is the ultimate source of meaning and literary history. Despite occasional lapses into textual objectivism, the rehabilitation of the reader must be taken seriously: no topic has been more hotly debated on the German scene in recent years. So far the debate has centred mostly on what reader-oriented research means: empirically based research insists on examining real people reading texts, while hermeneutic and phenomenological models usually opt for some heuristic construct; this basic split, and attempts to overcome it, have driven most of the discussion.

**Example:** Even within these two main camps there is often much disagreement over details. Erwin Wolff agrees with Iser on the need to develop a non-empirical category but differs from him on the precise composition of the reader and how its role is determined: against Iser's implied reader he proposes the "intended reader", the reader the author had in mind for his work. In most cases this construct overlaps with Iser's, but not entirely, because Wolff's reader is more a product of literary history than of reading pure and simple (though the distinction can never be drawn precisely): the intended reader can be determined not only from textual clues in the work being read but also from neighbouring works and even from the author's (and presumably other authors') remarks about his public. Wolff is therefore concerned with the historical unfolding of the idea of the reader; Iser is concerned only with the reader who can interact successfully with a particular text — his reader is always performing and inconceivable apart from the "act of reading", whereas Wolff's reader can be posited as an ideal type independent of any text.

**Distinction:** The point is not which reader is more useful or appropriate, but that recent theory has devoted itself to such fine distinctions, producing a seemingly endless stream of ever more "refined" concepts of the reader, distinguished from one another only by nuances: Wolff himself mentions in his essay the imagined reader, the adequate reader, the ideal reader, the idealized reader and the immanent reader; looking beyond Germany, one could add Riffaterre's superreader, Fish's informed reader or Gerald Prince's narratee. Despite their differences, what seems to unite all these constructs is their function as lightning rods with respect to the "work itself": the addressee, consumer or recipient is no longer regarded as a marginal element in literary study; especially in the German models, the reader becomes — in Jauss's words — "the arbiter of a new literary history".

**Limitation:** If the fruitfulness of an approach is measured by the amount of research and theoretical discussion it generates, the turn to the reader — whether understood as a historical person or as a heuristic construct — has been enormously successful; the novelty and appeal of reception theory are no doubt inseparable from the flexibility built into reader-oriented models. But this orientation also brings limitations that French or American "avant-garde" theorists would ascribe to most contemporary German criticism: appealing to the reader almost inevitably involves focusing criticism on a human subject who confronts an object called a literary text, to be read or interpreted in the process of reading; the reader, whether as historical entity or textual function, introduces the subject-object division so roundly condemned of late for its metaphysical underpinnings. In contrast to the decentred subject fashionable in post-structuralist circles, reception theory harks back to a conventional framework of interpretive endeavour: rather than announcing the "death of man", like French theorists from Foucault to Lacan, German criticism, despite its radical posturing, seems to be refurbishing the much-maligned "humanist" tradition.

**Counterexample:** To appreciate the "conservative" nature of reception theory relative to post-structuralist models, one need only compare Barthes's notion of the reader in *S/Z* with the various readers that most often appear in reception theory. Barthes problematizes the usual notion of reading in reflecting on the seemingly simple sentence "I read the text". Like the reception theorists, he grants the "subject" a productive role in reading: the more plural the text, the less it is written before I read it. But unlike the German critics, he extends this plurality to the "I" as well, and the process of reading itself is thereby altered: it is no longer conceived as yet another instance of a defined subject performing an operation on a knowable, pre-existing object — I do not subject the text to a predicative operation consequent upon its being, called reading, and the "I" is not an innocent subject anterior to the text who subsequently deals with it as an object to be dismantled or a site to be occupied. What distinguishes Barthes's "reader" from those of reception theory is that it is constituted by an infinity of codes or texts: not a unified centre from which meaning and interpretation issue, but a construct marked by dispersion and plurality — the "I" that approaches the text is itself already a plurality of other texts, of infinite codes, or more precisely of codes that are lost (whose origin is lost). *If we take what Barthes promotes here as roughly a "post-structuralist reader", the difference between German and French criticism is easily summarized: reception theorists shifted the interpretive focus from text to reader, whereas post-structuralists displaced every focus by textualizing the reader.*

### Structuralist reading and semiology: Barthes

**Distinction:** Twentieth-century French contributions to hermeneutics should not be summed up solely by the radical "decentring" gestures just described. Twentieth-century France took a lively interest in interpretation (though it seldom used the name "hermeneutics"); some of its developments were derivative and some misguided, but once these are discounted a considerable body of original and defensible thought remains. The approach Sartre developed and applied in *Being and Nothingness* (1943) and in his Flaubert biography *The Family Idiot* (1971) — that language is fundamental to thought and essentially a social institution, yet appropriated and transformed by individuals in original ways, so that interpreting an original author requires thorough knowledge of his historical context, background culture and background language together with deep insight into his individual use of language and way of thinking — is eminently reasonable but not original: it closely resembles the hermeneutics of Schleiermacher and his student Böckh, and nineteenth-century Germans influenced by it (Haym, Dilthey, Unger) had already written detailed biographies implementing the method. More original in Sartre is a theory of "hermeneutic radical freedom" in his early existentialism: although we do not create the world itself, we create the meanings or interpretations through which we know it, and are always free to choose them, so that we bear full responsibility for the world as we know it — an interesting position, but one that probably exaggerates our control over our own interpretations, and the freedom we do have is often only the freedom to choose *wrong* interpretations. In his Marxist period he also argued that Marxism, if it was to account adequately for ideology, must fill the gap between socio-economic classes and the individuals composing them with auxiliary disciplines such as the psychoanalysis of the individual unconscious. Ricoeur's hermeneutics, developed in *The Conflict of Interpretations* (1969), is highly eclectic (Schleiermacher, Dilthey, Heidegger, Gadamer, Hegel, Freud, structural linguistics and religion); its principles — overcoming cultural distance, the individual subject's modification of linguistic structures, attention to linguistic structure, attaining self-understanding through understanding others — are very reasonable but not original, while the introduction of religious premises into hermeneutics is a regression relative to Herder and Schleiermacher; his most important contribution remains the naming of the "hermeneutics of suspicion" (see P5).

**Counterexample:** The contribution of **Roland Barthes** to hermeneutics is more significant. One of his central ideas is the `death of the author`, put forward in 1967 (Derrida and Foucault advanced similar ideas in different forms at about the same time): it is always a mistake to attribute what a text expresses to an individual author or his intentions; what a text expresses always derives from a confluence of grammatical forms, terms, formulae, figures and the like drawn from a shared language. **Michael N. Forster** considers this a huge exaggeration and distortion: much of what texts express *can* be attributed to individual authors and their intentions — think of the many linguistic, conceptual and intellectual innovations introduced by Plato or Shakespeare. But as a counterweight to an equally one-sided author-centred position that ignores the mass of inherited linguistic convention in texts, it is at least useful. Steering between these two errors, between Scylla and Charybdis — recognizing that a text involves neither "individuality" alone nor "universality" alone but a *synthesis* of the two — was already one of the most driving and admirable ambitions of Schleiermacher's hermeneutics (see P3).

**Definition:** More fruitful for hermeneutics are two further innovations of Barthes's: `structuralist reading` of texts and, more broadly, `semiology`. In both areas he not only made important theoretical contributions but demonstrated their fruitfulness in persuasive interpretive applications. The former appears in two works of 1970: *S/Z* (taking Balzac's novella *Sarrasine* as its example) and "The structural analysis of narrative" (taking a passage from the Acts of the Apostles). In these texts Barthes oscillates between a Derridean rejection of the text's original meaning and a more traditional commitment to it (at least for "classic" texts), but seems ultimately to lean towards the latter, treating structuralist interpretation as a preparation for, rather than a replacement of, more traditional forms of interpretation — which is indeed his best position. So understood, the basic idea of such reading is to focus on deep formal features of the text that make possible or support its semantic content.

**Example:** These deep formal features include large-scale oppositions running through a text, such as death and life, male and female, active and passive (as in *Sarrasine*); patterns in which characters appear and disappear in a narrative (as in Acts); certain patterns in the use of proper names for persons, places and so on (in the classic novel, for instance, the proper name functions to embody a person as something more than a collection of described traits and activities); and the conveying of ideas through formal structures — in *Sarrasine*, the hero's name "Sarrasine" sounds feminine, and its second *s* resembles the *z* in the name of the castrato Zambinella, suggesting Sarrasine's own feminized and "castrated" character; in the American presidential campaign slogan "I like Ike", *Ike* is wrapped within *like*; Acts, through its constant use of recursive indirect speech, formally mirrors its central theme, the spreading of the word of God. This interpretive approach in fact bears only a tenuous resemblance to the structural linguistics that inspired it (the emphasis on oppositions is a resemblance, the other features are not), and it is not without precedent in German hermeneutics — Friedrich Schlegel had already pointed out that the diametrically opposed contents of Spinoza's and Fichte's philosophies (Spinoza's static single substance, Fichte's active I) are inscribed in the very form of their philosophical texts. Nonetheless, identifying such deep formal features as part of interpretation seems legitimate and fruitful; and Barthes's conception of them as cultural and variable rather than innate or common to all texts is a further merit of his position. *Forster even suggests that it might be added as a fifth aspect of interpretation to the four Böckh had already identified — historical, linguistic, individual and generic interpretation.*

**Mechanism:** Barthes's contributions to the development of a broader `semiology` are equally considerable. His most purely theoretical contribution is *Elements of Semiology* (1964), in which he takes up Saussure's idea that linguistics should be seen as only part of a broader science of signs, which he understands to include not only language but non-linguistic forms of expression such as traffic signs, fashion, cuisine and cars. This approach, which means extending hermeneutics beyond language, is very reasonable, but not really a new step in the discipline: Schleiermacher and Gadamer largely restricted interpretation to language, but many other German hermeneutic theorists did not — Herder also attended to sculpture and music, Schlegel to painting and architecture, Hegel to architecture and sculpture, and the mature Dilthey especially to a broader class of "expressions" including the kinds of cases that chiefly concern Barthes. Barthes's attempts to extend Saussure's key linguistic concepts and distinctions to other signs are not always convincing: extending the *langue*/*parole* distinction to non-linguistic cases seems implausible (partly because the famous distinction is itself vague and dubious); but some extensions are more illuminating, for example his extension to non-linguistic signs of Saussure's combination of purely positive and (especially) oppositional features constituting the meaning of linguistic signs — in fashion, the meaning of a short skirt is partly constituted by its contrast with a long one. In *The Fashion System* (1967) he further distinguishes the "genera" of fashion (reflected in generic names of types of garment) from the "variants" attached to them (properties roughly expressed by adjectives), and argues that opposition and exclusion are essential to both. The book also contains several sound observations with important implications for hermeneutics: apparently non-linguistic cases are in fact often permeated by language; and unlike language, non-linguistic signs often have merely practical functions in addition to their function as signs (food or clothing, for example).

**Example:** Perhaps more impressive than this more abstract book are several works in which Barthes both articulates his semiology and applies it in detail to the interpretation of concrete cases. One is *Empire of Signs* (1970), which in a sense continues the intense interest in cultural otherness that has dominated much French thought since Montaigne. The book officially presents itself as a kind of fictional image of Japan, but this is less an accurate description of its intention or achievement than a modest acknowledgement of the limits of its information: quite unlike the deliberate fictions of Chateaubriand and Nerval or the unintended fictions of Lévi-Strauss's structural anthropology, it is in fact a careful and sophisticated anthropological study, though based more on reading than on fieldwork. Some of its interpretations concern language, texts and discourse: Barthes argues plausibly that fundamental differences between Japanese and European languages contribute to fundamental differences of thought — the grammatical complexity of Japanese personal pronouns precludes the Western notion of subjectivity; the grammatical marking in Japanese of the animate/inanimate distinction, and fiction's usual use of the latter class of grammatical forms, make fiction a genre quite different from its Western counterpart; and the existence in Japanese of transitive verbs with neither subject nor object (modelled on the Indian *dhyana*) contributes to a metaphysical worldview very different from ours. He also argues in some detail — very much in the spirit of Herder and the Schlegel brothers on how genres vary across time and culture and thereby give rise to misunderstanding — that the Japanese haiku is precisely *not* meant, as Westerners usually interpret it, to express rich meaning concisely, but has a Zen background and aims to bring discourse and thought to a halt. But much more of the book is devoted to interpreting Japanese *non-linguistic* signs, such as the layout of a city like Tokyo, whose centre is strikingly inaccessible and empty, and the Japanese practice of elaborately wrapping utterly ordinary gifts; in Barthes's interpretation both cases, like the haiku, signify an absence rather than a plenitude.

**Example:** Another work that impressively combines semiological theory and practice is the earlier *Mythologies* (1957). It too examines many instances of linguistic and non-linguistic signification, but its object is contemporary French society, and it adds a strong socio-political dimension to the analysis. Barthes points out that once non-linguistic signs are taken into account, meaningful signs are an almost ubiquitous feature of everyday experience: how many really non-signifying fields do we cross in a day? Very few, sometimes none. He is at the seaside; the sea, admittedly, carries no message, but on the beach — flags, slogans, signals, signboards, clothes, even suntans — these are all messages to him. His interpretations of many concrete examples from the French context are fascinating: non-linguistic cases include a famous interpretation of the meaning of wrestling (as opposed to other combat sports such as boxing or judo) and an interpretation of the Tour de France as a modern epic. He stresses, moreover, that these forms of sport, entertainment, objects and so on usually serve a function of social ideology: conveying a distorted representation that rationalizes the social status quo, above all by giving it an appearance of eternity and naturalness.

**Limitation:** The book also has limitations. Its approach is less original than it seems — in particular, extending interpretation to such non-linguistic cases and interpreting them as social ideology had been strongly anticipated by Adorno's account of the "culture industry" (first presented in Adorno and Horkheimer's *Dialectic of Enlightenment*); some interpretations are too imaginative to be fully convincing as interpretations; it fails to distinguish clearly between "meaningful", "distortedly meaningful" and "distortedly meaningful for ideological reasons"; and its theoretical account of ideology, and of the relation between ideology and "myth", is too thin. Nonetheless, as a statement of Barthes's semiological approach and a demonstration of the fruitfulness of applying it to individual cases, it remains impressive. *These studies concentrate on impersonal or collective expression, which fits the dubious idea of the "death of the author"; but even without that idea the focus can be justified — it concerns a special case rather than a paradigm.*

### Genre, intertextuality and deconstruction: Todorov, Kristeva, Derrida

**Genealogy:** Two thinkers of Eastern European origin who spent most of their careers in France and wrote in French had ties to both Barthes and Derrida and both represent structural linguistics. **Tzvetan Todorov**'s work on `genre` is especially important, chiefly in *Genres in Discourse* (1978). Böckh had already recognized in the nineteenth century that identifying genre is an essential part of all interpretation, and so added a fourth kind — generic interpretation — to Schleiermacher's historical, grammatical and psychological interpretation; the twentieth-century Russian literary theorist Mikhail Bakhtin took a similar position. Todorov inherits this crucial insight and, like Bakhtin, stresses in particular that not only texts (literary and non-literary) but also oral uses of language have genres that must be identified if they are to be understood. He has several further interesting insights, for example that although genre must in general be distinguished from illocutionary force (a distinction predecessors such as Hirsch — see P15 — failed to draw), the two coincide in cases such as prayer.

**Definition:** Another French thinker of Bulgarian origin, **Julia Kristeva**, also made important contributions to hermeneutics, above all with her concept of `intertextuality`. First introduced in the 1966 essay "Word, dialogue and novel" under the influence of Bakhtin's conception of the dialogism of the novel, it refers roughly to the ways in which texts (and discourses) are largely constituted by explicit and implicit incorporation of, and other kinds of response to, other texts (and discourses). That texts have such features had long been recognized (Protagoras' discussion of Simonides' poem in Plato's *Protagoras*, for instance), but gathering them under the single term "intertextuality" is still useful, since it reminds interpreters how pervasive these features are and sharpens their eye.

**Condition:** But the concept needs revision in one respect. Kristeva first introduced it partly in the service of the kind of position, driven by structural linguistics, that Barthes championed under the name of the "death of the author"; and, as noted, that position one-sidedly distorts the interplay of communal and individual (or intentional) factors common in works. A sound concept of "intertextuality" should drop this element: to a large extent it is the *author* who determines (consciously or unconsciously) which other texts and discourses are incorporated and responded to in a work, and how.

**Definition:** The series of works **Jacques Derrida** published from 1967 onwards is also important for hermeneutics. He sums up his theory of meaning and interpretation with `iterability` (taking both "other" and "again") and `différance` (taking both "difference" and "deferral"). In its synchronic aspect this largely repeats Saussure's sound and important point that meaning arises only through a system of linguistic oppositions; in its diachronic aspect it largely repeats Gadamer's conception (Derrida's writings on the subject appeared a few years after *Truth and Method*, and he did not acknowledge the debt) that meaning is not something original and discrete but only something produced in an open-ended process of (re)interpretation.

**Counterexample:** Forster holds that this conception of Gadamer's is untenable: counter-intuitive, unfounded and even implicitly incoherent. It has two main problems. First, Gadamer vacillates between three mutually inconsistent versions of his main claim: (a) there is no original meaning; (b) there is an original meaning, but we cannot know it; (c) there is an original meaning, but it cannot be of any interest to us. Second, his central argument for the most important version, (a), is that when an author writes or says something, an interpretation arises that means something slightly different, then another that means something slightly different again, and so on indefinitely, so that there can be no original meaning, only an open sequence of interpretation-relative meanings; but this argument is not only a complete non sequitur but implicitly incoherent, since its premises presuppose the very original meaning its conclusion denies. Worse still, Derrida offers even less argument for the conception than Gadamer. *Apart from its sound Saussurean component, Derrida's theory is a house built on sand.*

**Distinction:** But Derrida also has some more promising interpretive ideas. One is the thesis that texts, especially philosophical texts, typically contain hidden contradictions that interpretation should expose — an exposure he calls `deconstruction` and practises on Rousseau, Hegel and many other traditional philosophers. The thesis is true of many texts (including philosophical ones) and very important; it is not new — Friedrich Schlegel had formulated it at the turn of the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries and had already applied it, as Derrida does, to philosophers — but Derrida's commitment to it is at least far better than the dubious ideas, currently widespread among anglophone philosophers and historians of philosophy, that interpretation must exercise "charity" and in particular avoid attributing logical inconsistency to texts. *(Such ideas often rest on a double error: a principle that it is impossible to think inconsistently, and the inference that attributing inconsistency to a text must therefore be mistaken; but the principle is itself false, and even if it were true it would apply only to explicit inconsistency, whereas what needs attributing to texts is usually implicit inconsistency.)* Anglophone historians of ideas such as Arthur Lovejoy and Quentin Skinner have done much better in this respect.

**Distinction:** Derrida is also known for advocating `decentring` in interpretation. In different contexts he means different things by this, some defensible, some not. Sometimes he means merely acknowledging that, because of the situation Saussure and Gadamer describe, there is never an original, discrete meaning available for interpretation — which, as noted above, is partly problematic. At other times he means interpreting texts by focusing on aspects the text itself presents as of only marginal importance (aspects carrying an implicit political or social ideology, for instance) — and such interpretations can indeed sometimes be legitimate, illuminating and important. Closely related is his strategy in interpreting the visual arts of focusing on seemingly marginal features of the artwork: the material medium (the "subjectile"), "traces" such as brushstrokes, and "parerga" such as the frame, the title or the signature.

### Misreading and interpretive communities: Bloom, Fish and Culler

**Principle:** Another distinction in contemporary hermeneutics appears in the notion of interpretation. Here again reception theory takes a position ostensibly opposed to traditional practice yet, from many contemporary perspectives, not very radical. Its oppositional stance is easiest to see in Jauss's adoption of Kuhn's paradigm model: applying the concept of scientific revolution to literary studies can be seen as an attempt to separate the interpretive thrust of reception theory from earlier, outmoded hermeneutic efforts; and one of the central concerns of the new paradigm is precisely to supply new interpretations of older works, thereby rescuing and revitalizing the literary canon. Interpretation, conceived as the reader's activity of understanding texts, thus remains the focus of Jauss's theory. So too with Iser, who devotes the whole first chapter of *The Act of Reading* to the question: he strongly opposes "the form of interpretation that is concerned first and foremost with the meaning of the literary work", and more specifically the "referential model" in which readers are asked to search for a truth hidden in the folds of the text's fabric. Twentieth-century criticism, he argues, tried to escape this "classical norm of interpretation" but inevitably succumbed to it; his main example is New Criticism, which rejected treating the work as "an object containing a hidden meaning of some prevailing truth", preferring to concentrate on "the elements of the work and their interaction", and so avoided the pitfalls of the earlier exegetical enterprise — but not completely, since its appeal to harmony and the elimination of ambiguity betrays its adherence to "old values". Iser's alternative attends more to process than to result: meaning is not dug out of the text or pieced together from textual clues but reached through the process of interaction between reader and text; interpretation involves not discovering a determinate meaning in the text but experiencing the work as the process unfolds.

**Counterexample:** From one vantage point reception theory does break with the old notion of interpretation: placing the reader at the centre of the interpretive enterprise makes possible an evolutionary model of literary history and an interactive theory of reading. But we should still ask whether this refocusing of critical activity retains more of the "old values" than reception theorists recognize. Reception theory's most general tendency is to shift attention from text to reader, so that the determinate text insisted on by traditional criticism is replaced by the recipient; but there is no reason to think that the consumers of literature are more stable than the mutable text. Even without accepting Barthes's textualization of the reader, why should we be able to read the reading subject, or his reading, more easily and accurately than what the reading subject reads? Faced with the ambiguity of texts, shifting the critical focus from text to reader preserves the determinacy of the critical heritage by relocating it: *we concede that the text is plural, while denying the plurality of readers and of their writing.*

**Counterexample:** Reception theorists share this critical strategy, and its limitations, with the two most popular variants of American reader-response criticism. **Jonathan Culler**'s wish to establish a "structuralist poetics" and Fish's frequent references to literary communities and their conventions are beset by similar problems: both seem to imply some realm in which the indeterminacy of the interpretive enterprise is halted by a readable and determinate institution. For Culler the difficulty arises when he speaks of `literary competence` and tries to articulate the "set of conventions for reading literary texts" that constitutes it. He is no doubt right that rules and conventions enable us to understand literature; but any attempt to formulate these enabling rules necessarily runs into the same problems that led critics to abandon the notion of a determinate text — since conventions are, after all, abstracted from texts, why should we be able to determine conventions if texts themselves remain elusive? Why should conventions not be as infinitely extensible as interpretations of texts? And why could we not find another, larger controlling institution (society or history, say) that oversees conventions and so becomes the more appropriate object of study? Fish creates a similar dilemma with his references to the `interpretive community`; and perhaps not insignificantly, his introduction of the term involves an evident circularity: literary communities exist because agreement among critics is observed, and agreement exists because critics belong to a common literary community. Fish seems unwilling to break out of this circle by proposing other criteria. When such strategies are offered as alternatives to traditional interpretation, or as aids to access to texts, one may ask: why should we be able to read readers, conventions or institutions any better than texts?

**Distinction:** Acknowledging that problems of determinacy arise in studying readers, conventions or institutions does not of course mean such activity is useless; the point here is that the problems involved in studying these areas are of the same kind as those encountered in studying literary texts, and that trying to bypass the text by shifting the critical focus to another institution only *defers* the confrontation with determinacy. The problem can be avoided entirely only by placing interpretation itself in a different framework — and this is precisely where "postmodernist" perspectives differ from models relying on reader response. In dealing with interpretation, reception theorists reinforce a model in which a subject produces a written or oral statement explaining or supplying the meaning of an original text; interpretation is thus seen as derivative, parasitic or marginal, its own textual character secondary. "Postmodernist" critics deny this generally accepted relation between text and commentary: within this frame of reference criticism is less an act of interpretation than an act of creation, the emphasis falling not on the secondariness of interpretation but on the production of a new text. In spatial imagery, commenting on a literary work means adding a layer on top of, or even beside, the text, rather than penetrating into or approaching something original.

**Example:** Most post-structuralists share this view of criticism, but perhaps its strongest formulation comes from **Harold Bloom**. Bloom's interests are mostly directed at how poets read other poets, and his central thesis — that every strong poet necessarily misreads his great precursor — aims to supply a general theory of poetic influence; but his view of reading also has implications for the activity of critics and scholars. `Misreading` — in Bloom's own vocabulary, "misprision" — is seen as constitutive of reading, interpretation and literary history: restating a poem or approaching its meaning, as traditional critics may believe possible, can never be done; at most we can write another poem, and even this reformulation is always a misunderstanding of a parent poem. What distinguishes poems from what we usually call criticism is only that the former misreading is usually more extreme than the latter — a difference of degree, not of kind. Criticism is conceived here as "a series of swerves after unique creative acts of misunderstanding", and the best interpretations — rewritten as misprision and prose poetry — consist of the most imaginative and "antithetical" texts disguised as commentary.

### Can history be interpreted? Derrida and Hayden White

**Principle:** Given such notions of interpretation, it is not surprising that reception theory diverges sharply from the "avant-garde" on the question of literary history. The German camp cannot be charged with lacking innovation here either: especially in its earliest phase, represented by Jauss's work, reception theory was concerned with rethinking the relation between literary history and interpretation, and between literary history and general history. A text can no longer be interpreted merely by placing it in its historical context; its history of interpretation is seen as an inseparable part of our ability to understand it; literary history therefore cannot be a positivist chronicle of authors and movements but must be based on an evolutionary model stressing the event character of literary works and their role in constituting history. One of the most important consequences of this redefinition is a reversal of the relation between cultural history and pragmatic history (*Historie*): previously, culture was seen as an epiphenomenon dependent on a political, economic or social base; Jauss's early programme can be read as an effort to overturn such models — through the continuous mediation of past and present art, the history of art can become the paradigm of a history meant to show "the development of this present". Jauss is opposing nineteenth-century historical objectivism here; taking art as the paradigm of general history means replacing "the substantialist notion of tradition" with "a functional conception of history".

**Counterexample:** Some shortcomings of this view of history have already been recorded in the GDR objections (see P12); but the "avant-garde" challenge is of a different kind: what is questioned is not only how we understand the past but the very idea of historical thinking. In the same year that Jauss delivered his manifesto of reception theory at Constance, Derrida published *Of Grammatology*, which seems to call for the abolition of history itself. Analysing Rousseau's *Essay on the Origin of Languages*, he asks that reading be freed, "at least in its axis", from the classical categories of history — not only those of the history of ideas and literary history but perhaps above all those of the history of philosophy. His very procedure in the book — "regressing" from an examination of Saussure to an extended study of Rousseau — shows his indifference to discussing his central themes in any traditional chronological order. But his most serious objection to history is that it too belongs to the metaphysical system he is determined to deconstruct: the concept of "history" makes sense only within "a logocentric epoch", and since he hopes to bring about the end of that epoch, his work cannot acknowledge a conception of history that may have served as a pillar of logocentric thought. *Jauss tries to reorganize history in order to challenge literary studies; Derrida wants to banish history in order to read logocentric texts more correctly. Jauss seeks a reversal of the relation between cultural and general history; Derrida sees both as symptoms and vestiges of a larger philosophical problem.*

**Definition:** Even the less radical members of the "avant-garde" seem to take more revolutionary positions than Jauss's in challenging traditional historiography. Perhaps the best-known writer on these questions in the English-speaking world is **Hayden White**. Like Jauss, White questions the existence of objective historical facts and ultimately arrives at a similarly relativizing view of history that excludes notions of truth and certainty; but the way he develops his metahistorical view shows once again the gulf between reception theory and other contemporary currents. White conceives historical writing as `narrative` and relies on poetic theory for his critique: analysing historians' narrative strategies, he observes a series of fourfold typologies according to modes of emplotment, modes of explanation and modes of ideological implication. None of these modes derives from purely individual factors, yet no particular emplotment, explanation or ideology can be given priority; reading and writing history resemble, in essential respects, writing stories — like a medieval poet, the historian is constrained more by the tropes and conventions of his craft than by the "truth" he presents.

**Mechanism:** By labelling the modes of emplotment with terms taken from poetics — romance, comedy, tragedy and satire — and assigning the rhetorical tropes — metaphor, metonymy, synecdoche and irony — to models of explanation, White suggests that our understanding of the past has a fictive dimension and, ultimately, a `linguistic determination`. Compared with Jauss, White is more differentiated, more radical and more literary: Jauss grounds his revised view of history in a dialogical relation with the past, whereas White subsumes all historiography under the category of narrative; the aesthetics of reception tries to incorporate the interpretation of the past into the unfolding and writing of history, whereas metahistory conceives all history as fundamentally an act of interpretation; Jauss proposes that cultural history can serve as the paradigm of general history, while White replies with a model in which literary tropes make historical thinking itself possible. *In short, Jauss's programme seeks to place history at the centre of literary studies; White questions history itself by placing literary studies at the centre of historiography.*

**Distinction:** Comparing White and Jauss in this area is to some extent unfair: White specializes in historiography, while Jauss is a Romanist who wanted to revitalize his discipline with a new conception of literary history. The point here, and throughout this part, is to bring out the theoretical limits reception theory encountered in establishing a "new paradigm", and partly to explain the difficulties it has had, and will continue to have, in gaining reception outside Germany — not to prove the superiority of one set of approaches.

**Conclusion:** From an "avant-garde" perspective reception theory may seem a conservative enterprise: it retains or reintroduces much of the baggage carried by the tradition it claims to overturn. Reception theorists have been slow to take up the challenge of French and American critical discourse: although Jauss specializes in French literature and Iser has close ties to American academia, neither *The Act of Reading* nor *Aesthetic Experience and Literary Hermeneutics* mentions Derrida or Lacan, and Foucault is mentioned only in passing in one of Iser's footnotes. On the whole reception theorists have avoided the questions that have been most provocative outside Germany; and post-structuralism has gained only a minimal foothold among young German scholars. Even without an internal challenge, a dialogue between reception theory and other critical currents would be desirable; it might force all parties to rethink what has become cliché in their respective discourses. *The reception of reception theory in the English-speaking world may therefore still lie in the future: if it can enter into fruitful relations with other modes of contemporary thought, it may once again offer literary studies the welcome "provocation" it once offered a generation of German critics. The pattern running through this part is that text, reader, convention, community, history — each time the location of meaning is moved from one place to another, determinacy does not vanish but is merely relocated. P15 turns to a different question: rather than asking where meaning is, it asks what distinct operations the activity of "interpretation" itself comprises, and how the validity of each is established.*

## P15 How is the validity of interpretation established?

### The dispute over authorial intention: New Criticism, historicism and Hirsch

**Background:** Until the mid-twentieth century the dominant mode of literary criticism was evaluative, and interpretation was not the central activity of literary studies (see P1). Anglo-American `New Criticism` changed this: it established new and better interpretations of individual works as the goal of literary study, while severing interpretation from the author's psychology with a famous argument. In "The Intentional Fallacy" (1946) Wimsatt and Beardsley maintained that the design or intention of the author is neither available nor desirable as a standard for judging the success of a work of literary art. The argument is often extended to a stronger version: the meaning of a poem is not the author's intention.

**Distinction:** New Criticism did not thereby claim that a poem's meaning is arbitrary. Wimsatt and Beardsley distinguish three kinds of evidence. *Internal evidence* is public, discovered through the semantics and syntax of the language, through dictionaries and through everything that makes up a literature and a culture; *external evidence* is private and personal, such as an author's statements in letters, journals or conversation about how or why he wrote the poem; between them lie the author's private associations of words or the usage of his circle.
