The first is the legitimate basis of criticism, the second is not, and the intermediate kind is a grey zone. The polemical target of this distinction was the biographical criticism then current — interpreting works through authors' lives; it directed attention to the text itself, to the core New Critical categories of `ambiguity`, `paradox`, `irony` and `organic unity`.

**Example:** The most famous clash between New Criticism and historical scholarship took place in the late 1940s and early 1950s: the dispute between the New Critic **Cleanth Brooks** and the literary historian **Douglas Bush** over Marvell's "An Horatian Ode upon Cromwell's Return from Ireland" (1650). The background: Cromwell had led the parliamentary army to victory in the English Civil War and brought about the execution of Charles I, and had just brutally suppressed the rebellion in Ireland; the poem's attitude both to Cromwell and to the recently executed Charles is subtle and hard to pin down. The dispute came to focus on one stanza:

> And now the Irish are asham'd / To see themselves in one Year tam'd: / So much one Man can do, / That does both act and know. / They can affirm his Praises best, / And have, though overcome, confest / How good he is, how just, / And fit for highest Trust.

The Irish are ashamed to have been tamed within a single year; they can best affirm his praises, and though overcome have confessed how good he is, how just, how fit for the highest trust. Is this jest, satire, irony — or a serious assertion about the Irish? Brooks argues that if the poem is a good poem, the stanza must make dramatic sense; as a straightforward assertion — that the crushed Irish acknowledge their conqueror to be good and just — it would seem foolish, a blemish on a poem that he sees as a complex, balanced portrait of praise, full of reservations and often double-edged. He therefore hears a grim irony here: the crushed Irish confess how good and just he is with the sword at their throats. Bush calls this "a desperate solution": there is not the slightest hint of irony in the wording; it is as straightforward a statement as one could wish, however much we may dislike it; we must accept the unpleasant fact that Marvell was writing as an Englishman of 1650, and that he is here indulging in wishful thinking — Cromwell is so great a conqueror that even the Irish must share the English sentiment and accept the course of history. Yet Bush's own paraphrase — "accept the course of history" — already departs from the poem's "confest how good he is, how just", which shows that he too felt the incongruity of the lines themselves.

**Distinction:** The disagreement is in fact a disagreement between two views of evidence. Bush is concerned with what historical evidence can tell us about what Marvell thought when he wrote the poem, and accuses Brooks of imposing on it the prejudices of a good modern liberal, for whom a man as intelligent and sensitive as Marvell could not have admired a ruthless, brutal man of action like Cromwell, who must have been a kind of "Puritan Stalin". Brooks finds this historical method "crude", for two reasons: (1) even if we knew what Marvell the man thought of Cromwell when he wrote the poem, Marvell the man is not Marvell the poet, who was writing a "Horatian ode"; (2) even if we knew what Marvell the poet meant to say, that would not prove that the poem actually says it — the unconscious plays a part in composition, and poets may write better than they know. The total attitude presented in the poem must be distinguished from the author's attitudes as a person and citizen; reading the poem means appealing to "the total context of the poem itself" rather than to Marvell's mind — a question of the poem's organization, not of biography or history. If one said the poem is unified because it reflects the contradictions of a hesitant, self-contradictory and at times foolish man, one might get a useful historical document, but not a poem.

**Example:** The dispute contains a telling episode. Brooks replied that he could hardly claim the title of "liberal": he was more often called a reactionary, even a proto-fascist. Bush had wrongly inferred the historical author's politics from Brooks's text — an error that itself bears out Brooks's point: one cannot assume continuity between what a text says and what its historical author believes.

**Mechanism:** This dispute ought to have summoned hermeneutic theory to adjudicate it, but twentieth-century hermeneutics could offer almost no help. A philological hermeneutics concerned with establishing the original meaning of sacred or legal texts and bridging linguistic distance through grammar and vocabulary might still have offered some guidance; a philosophical hermeneutics concerned with the structure of understanding was of almost no use. As Peter Szondi observed, hermeneutics was once entirely a system of rules and is now entirely a theory of understanding (see P1); having turned from "rules for determining meaning" to "reflection on the nature of understanding", it no longer offers guidance on how to interpret. A Gadamerian would say that Brooks and Bush are both engaged in a fusion of horizons, merely bringing different horizons of expectation to the poem and different conceptions of the relation between literary work and historical context — *a description that may be correct, but that answers precisely not the question of whose reading is more valid.* Szondi also observed that the "age of interpretation" inherited from hermeneutics little more than the idea of the "hermeneutic circle", which often became an excuse for not reflecting on the nature of one's evidence: since the circle cannot be avoided, why bother? The problem thus became acute: to support their interpretations, critics need to be able to argue that at least some interpretations are misreadings, or else they will be accused of "anything goes"; yet *Truth and Method* shows no interest in how to distinguish valid from invalid interpretations and may not even acknowledge the distinction. (On Gadamer's anti-intentionalist stance in his writing on Celan, see P7.)

**Definition:** The most prominent attempt to bring twentieth-century hermeneutics into literary criticism was **E. D. Hirsch**'s *Validity in Interpretation* (1967) (not to be confused with Julian Hirsch, the student of fame in P9). At first sight it seems designed precisely to solve the problem of validity and adjudicate disputes like that between Brooks and Bush. Hirsch sharply criticized the New Critics' doctrine of the intentional fallacy, arguing that the act of interpretation makes sense only on the assumption that the text has a meaning to be recovered, and that this meaning is the meaning the author intended; there must be something unchanging about which the interpreter can be right or wrong, or else there is chaos. He distinguished the original `meaning`, the object of `understanding`, from `significance`, the product of `interpretation` — what the text may mean to the interpreter, varying with readers and times; a text's meaning does not change, its significance does. On this basis he saw Gadamer's "application" and fusion of horizons as confusing meaning with significance and so abolishing the question of validity.

**Counterexample:** One common criticism of this distinction is that any "original meaning" posited by a contemporary interpreter is already a function of his present situation and cannot escape it — which unwittingly takes Gadamer's line that all understanding is interpretation. A more important criticism comes from **David Hoy**: Hirsch's model ultimately cannot "cash out", cannot provide guidance for interpretation, because the authorial intention he speaks of is ultimately *the intention manifested in the text*, not something that can be known independently and then used as a standard for measuring claims about the text. Hirsch himself concedes that the author whose intention constitutes the text's meaning is the "speaking subject": not the subjectivity of the author as actual historical person, but the final and most comprehensive level of awareness determinative of verbal meaning — in the case of a lie, the speaking subject assumes he is telling the truth, while the actual subject retains a private awareness of the deception; the historical individual's inner thoughts about the text's meaning are irrelevant and inaccessible. Hirsch's actual position thus differs little from that of Wimsatt, whom he attacked, and is not far from Gadamer, whom he also criticized — at least the Gadamer who wrote on Celan. Knapp and Michaels's famous polemical essay "Against Theory" (1982) pushed this point to the extreme: meaning *is* the author's intention, and no other concept of meaning is intelligible; but they stated explicitly that the claim has no consequences, since it implies nothing about how meaning is to be discovered. Most literary critics understood Hirsch as requiring them to discover the historical author's intention and use it as the standard of validity, and resisted that requirement for the same reasons that made Hirsch himself unable to hold on to the historical individual's intention. *In other words, the meaning/significance distinction is conceptually valid, but if "meaning = authorial intention" is to become an operable standard of validity, it needs a theory of how intentions are attributed and on what evidence they are established.*

### The intentionalist basis of meaning and the empirical character of interpretation

**Problem:** Gadamer held that general hermeneutics can learn from legal hermeneutics. This is persuasive: hardly any other field of hermeneutics today has comparable social importance, similarly institutionalized practices or as many professional practitioners. The legal scholar **Ralf Poscher** agrees that something can be learned from law, but not about what is learned. For Gadamer, what law teaches is that an element of application must be integrated into the concept of interpretation — hermeneutics is a `unitary` practice, an interpretation with an inherent element of application. Poscher thinks law teaches exactly the opposite: hermeneutics is *a set of different practices* whose relevance varies across hermeneutic situations, each raising different theoretical issues, most of which can be connected with particular debates in analytic philosophy. Gadamer's merit was to highlight the element of application — and not only for legal hermeneutics; but, as the Italian jurist **Emilio Betti** had already rightly pointed out, his concept of application is highly ambiguous and conflates at least four theoretically distinct aspects. The activities a lawyer relies on in applying the law to a case can be distinguished in turn as: legal interpretation, rule-following, legal construction, association, the exercise of discretion, and judgements about the significance of legal provisions. These distinctions are deeply rooted not only in legal methodology but also in legal doctrine and even institutional tradition; and they are not peculiar to law — parallels can be found in the hermeneutics of art.

**Example:** The most basic operation is identifying the applicable legal norm, and norms are usually conveyed by texts. Article 5(3) of the German Basic Law says only: art and science … are free. Suppose the police ban a performance of *Macbeth* because it is seen as criticizing the president: how does a lawyer get from this text to the norm that "the state may not interfere with artistic activity"? Semantically, the sentence does not even state a norm; it states a fact.

**Principle:** The basic operation that lets an interpreter derive meaning from a set of signs can be explained, along the lines of **H. P. Grice** and **Donald Davidson**, in `intentionalist` terms: in the fundamental sense, meaning can rest only on `communicative intentions`; no non-natural meaning can exist without the presupposition of a communicative intention. Lines washed into the sand by the waves that look exactly like letters have no meaning; only by presupposing some hypothetical speaker can we give them meaning — if the waves wrote "I love you", we might imagine a couple walking along the beach, one declaring love to the other. The "meaning" of natural signs derives from non-intentional causal relations between objects and their environment; the meaning of non-natural signs derives from the intentions speakers or authors attach to their utterances. Smoke and the cry "Fire!" both indicate fire, but the latter does so not because of a causal relation between the cry and the fire but because of the intention of the person crying out. Meaningful utterance is thus a special kind of intentional action: conveying propositional content by sounds, signs or any other communicative means.

**Condition:** Intentionalism is a `mentalist` theory of meaning: it explains meaning through communicative intentions, which are mental representations, and so still needs to be supplemented by a theory of mental content; but however that theory is worked out, it must be able to account for intentions in general and communicative intentions in particular. It follows that attributing meaning to an artefact for theoretical purposes requires attributing intentions. These need not be actual intentions, but we cannot understand how an artefact has meaning without at least implicitly presupposing an intentional subject — even a fictional or hypothetical one. Legal texts are no different: `legislative intent` appears in every classical canon of legal interpretation, though its status is contested; "art and science are free" has the meaning it has because we attribute to those who ratified it the intention of communicating a legal *norm*, not merely stating a fact. Attributing intentions to collectives such as parliaments or constitutional conventions raises the problem of `collective intentionality`, but this shows only two things: that even where collective intentions are hard to understand we cannot dispense with communicative intentions; and that legal hermeneutics must draw on broad theoretical resources — it can use reductive accounts of collective intention in action theory to reconstruct ordinary talk of legislative intent.

**Mechanism:** This explains the nature of interpretation in its most basic form: *interpretation is a special case of empirical explanation*, concerning intentional phenomena — beliefs, desires, intentions, hopes, actions — and their products, such as tools and, most prominently in the hermeneutic tradition, texts. Seeing someone pick up an umbrella, we explain the action by his believing it will rain, not wanting to get wet and intending to go out — that is, we interpret his action. Interpretation is not limited to actions: a piece of wood attached to a metal bar is interpreted as a hammer (Heidegger's famous example) because someone made it with the intention that it serve this use; if we believed an objective spirit guided the course of history, we could even interpret the course of history, since we would conceive the objective spirit as an intentional subject. Intentional phenomena supervene on non-intentional phenomena (neurophysiological states, even interactions of subatomic particles), with token identity but no type identity between them, so that explanations at different levels are not mutually reducible (Davidson's "anomalous monism"). But interpretation as intentional explanation differs from non-intentional explanation not only in level but in the *standards* employed: interpretation relies on standards of rationality such as the `principle of charity` — in general, an agent's beliefs are to be interpreted charitably with respect to truth; without presupposing some degree of rationality it is impossible to reconstruct another's intentions. For this reason a "mind-reading machine" that reconstructed a speaker's mental contents solely from natural causes such as brain waves would not produce *interpretations*. Yet intentional explanation remains *empirical*: whether someone acted with a particular intention is always an empirical question — did she pick up the umbrella to keep off the rain, to take it for repair, or to wrap it as a present? Communicative intentions are simply the intentions attached to the special kind of action called speech acts, and so the same holds: when she asked him to meet her at the *bank*, whether she meant the riverbank or the savings bank is an empirical question.

**Mechanism:** The goal of communicative interpretation is `speaker meaning` in Grice's sense. Semantic meaning — what Grice calls `sentence meaning` — is the main clue for inferring a speaker's communicative intentions; semantic meaning itself supervenes on the vast diachronic and synchronic use of a word to convey particular meanings, and it allows us to infer that speakers generally use words in accordance with their semantic meaning. But semantic meaning alone may be insufficient, as in the *bank* example; speakers may also fail to follow standard usage (what Davidson calls malapropism) or use words to convey different intentions (Grice's conversational implicature, or metaphor). Since Savigny formulated his canons of interpretation, the semantic ("grammatical") meaning of the law has been one of the canons for deciphering the legislator's intent. The intentionalist view of interpretation therefore raises no questions beyond those empirical inquiry generally has to answer: it provides not only a basic concept of meaning but also an entirely unmysterious basic concept of interpretation, and it can define interpretation precisely with "empirical explanation" as its proximate genus. In law, the first step of hermeneutics is to form an empirical hypothesis about the communicative intention the legislator attached to the legal text: in the case of Article 5(3), the semantics yields a statement of fact, while the context of the utterance supports the hypothesis that the constitutional convention intended to communicate the normative principle that "the state may not interfere with artistic activity". *This lies on the same line as Schleiermacher's understanding of "divination" as a hypothesis grounded in evidence yet going beyond it (see P3), and as Droysen's and Dilthey's understanding of understanding as intellectual inference (see P4).*

**Distinction:** In art, the counterpart of this communicative interpretation is the approach that stresses `authorial intention`, as Hirsch did. It is controversial, and its limits are precisely the subject discussed by the New Critics and by the post-structuralists who proclaimed the "death of the author" (Barthes, 1967). But that authorial intention may be hard to establish, that literary hermeneutics goes beyond it, even that the most important part of literary hermeneutics may not depend on it — none of this makes authorial intention irrelevant: in art, "what was the author's intention?" remains a meaningful question that admits meaningful answers. To say that Picasso wanted to convey the horrors of the civil war with *Guernica* is a legitimate hermeneutic thesis, even if it does not exhaust the hermeneutic potential of the painting. And that reconstructing authorial intention cannot exhaust hermeneutics is precisely the point of the "compound concept of hermeneutics": hermeneutics comprises a whole set of practices, of which communicative interpretation is only one — but it is foundational in a theoretical sense, since interpretation presupposes attributing non-natural meaning to an utterance issued by an actual or presupposed fictional author. *Intentionalism here is therefore not a global claim about "all interpretation" but a precise claim about one of its operations; it differs from Hirsch in not treating authorial intention as the sole standard of all hermeneutic activity.*

**Condition:** Communicative interpretation is empirical and therefore fallible; but fallible does not mean arbitrary. When the evidence is insufficient to decide between several hypotheses, or when the legislator simply had no relevant intention about the case at hand, communicative interpretation reaches its limit — and what continues from there is already a different operation.

### Rule-following and the power of judgement

**Problem:** Through communicative interpretation the lawyer knows that the text "art is free" was enacted and promulgated to communicate the constitutional norm that "the state may not interfere with artistic activity". The next step is to apply the norm to her case — the *first* kind of application suggested by Gadamer's ambiguous concept of "application".

**Genealogy:** The theoretical background to what happens when a norm is applied to cases it covers is the long discussion prompted by **Wittgenstein**'s remarks on `rule-following` and **Saul Kripke**'s sceptical reading of them. Wittgenstein rightly pointed out that no interpretation, in the sense of substituting one expression of a rule for another, can bridge the "gap" between a rule and its application; trying to bridge it by paraphrasing the rule leads only to an endless and fruitless series of interpretations. In intentionalist terms: no interpretive restatement of a communicative intention can bridge the gap between the communicated norm and its application. There must therefore be a way of grasping a rule that is *not an interpretation*, but is exhibited in what we call "obeying the rule" and "going against it" in actual cases. What applying a rule requires is a capacity different from inferring others' intentions — best conceived as a shared, basic human capacity.

**Mechanism:** *Understanding* a rule is a complex capacity that includes the ability to paraphrase the rule's formulation; but first and foremost it is the ability to apply it to standard cases in a variety of interwoven practices, including judging others by the rule — as courts judge the conduct of defendants and parties by the law. This capacity-based view of rule-following resembles Kant's analysis of `judgement` (*Urteilskraft*): if understanding is explained as the faculty of rules, judgement is the faculty of subsuming under rules; if logic wanted to show in general how to subsume, it could do so only by means of another rule, and so on ad infinitum — hence, though understanding can be instructed by rules, judgement is a special talent that can only be practised, not taught. So there may be said to be a gap between a rule and its application, but there is no gap between *understanding* a rule and knowing how to apply it to standard cases: being able to apply it to standard cases is the most important sense of understanding in Kant's and Wittgenstein's sense. Exercising the capacity to follow rules is like a swimmer exercising the capacity to swim on various occasions — in a lake on a hot summer day, in a pool for exercise. Contemporary pragmatism (**Robert Brandom**) reverses the two more radically: rules are only attempts to make explicit our practices in standard cases; application is not derived from rules, rather rules supervene on applications — which also explains why we sometimes cannot formulate a rule covering all our practice in particular cases, as with the Gettier problem for the concept of knowledge. But however the relation between rule and application is conceived, the problem it addresses differs from inferring communicative intentions from utterances: the meaning of an utterance is determined by inferring the communicative intention attached to it, whereas the application of a communicated rule is not extracted from the rule by interpretation or other inferential means — the rule does not "contain" its applications, any more than the capacity to swim "contains" a swim in a particular lake.

**Distinction:** In so-called `easy cases`, communicative interpretation and rule-following are all that is needed. Banning a performance of *Macbeth* for political reasons is an easy case of violating Article 5(3): once the legislator's intention to establish the rule "no governmental interference with art" has been reconstructed, the lawyer need only exercise her rule-following capacity to apply the rule to the case — a theatrical performance is a paradigm of art, banning a performance a paradigm of state interference; if the lawyer "understands" the rule, she can apply it to this case. German even has a word for this capacity in legal contexts: `Judiz`, the experienced lawyer's ability to arrive intuitively at the right judgement of a case. Note, though, that even easy cases require two operations in applying the law: inferring communicative intention from the text, and exercising the capacity to follow rules. The performing arts also involve an element of rule application: "interpreting" a piece of music includes following the instructions given by the markings in the score — if the first movement is marked *adagio* and the second *presto*, the former must be played more slowly than the latter.

**Conclusion:** Rule-following is not interpretation but a further operation that occurs directly, in easy cases, after communicative interpretation; its correctness lies not in correct attribution of intention but in agreement with a shared practice. Judgement cannot be reduced to rules, but it is not arbitrary: it is a capacity that can be trained, and tested and corrected within a community of practice.

### Legal construction, association and discretion: three activities beyond communicative content

**Background:** Quantitatively, easy cases make up almost all of legal practice: of the billions of contracts concluded every year, only a tiny fraction ever come to the attention of professional lawyers, and of these only a small part are not easy cases — in the rest, lawyers simply enforce a price, a mortgage or an eviction. But the cases in which communicative interpretation and rule-following are insufficient to apply the law, though quantitatively negligible, are at the core of law as a professional practice, especially so-called `hard cases`: cases for which even further methodological resources yield no determinate legal answer. As Hart put it, hard cases are "the daily diet of the law schools", and they are the cases decided by the higher courts and reported in the law reports.

**Mechanism:** Cases are hard for several kinds of reason: (1) epistemic — semantic or syntactic indeterminacy arising from ambiguity, polysemy, multidimensionality, standard-relativity or vagueness, or a conflict between the pragmatic context of the legislative process and an otherwise determinate text; (2) `systemic indeterminacy` — the texts of the relevant provisions are each determinate but point in different directions; with priority rules such as *lex superior*, *lex posterior* and *lex specialis*, application may simply be more complex yet still an easy case, but if these rules do not help, the communicative meaning of a provision may have to give way to the legal meaning that emerges from resolving the systemic conflict; (3) applying a rule conflicts not with other legal norms but with other intentions of the legislator, or with the demands of reasonableness or justice — whether such cases can be handled by teleological reduction, the absurdity rule or even the Radbruch formula is highly controversial; (4) situations the legislator did not, or could not, foresee. Gadamer captured this last aspect with the difference between the horizon in which a text arose and the horizon in which it is applied: in the distance between the two horizons, cases arise about which it is uncertain whether they fall under the rule.

**Example:** Is a search engine's answer to the query "best politician of our time" protected by the free speech clause of the First Amendment to the US Constitution? When the framers thought about freedom of speech they could not imagine computers, let alone the internet; it is reasonable to assume that faced with this case they would be as puzzled as we are — the resulting indeterminacy is not merely epistemic. In all cases of indeterminacy, lawyers must resort to another activity: `legal construction`.

**Genealogy:** Since the earliest modern reflections on legal hermeneutics, jurists have been aware of the distinction between interpretation and construction. In his early lectures on methodology of 1809, **Friedrich Carl von Savigny** distinguished legal interpretation from the "further development of law" (*Fortbildung des Rechts*), the doctrinal development of the law; three years before Savigny published his methodology in 1840, the German-American jurist **Francis Lieber** published a pioneering essay, "On Political Hermeneutics, or On Political Interpretation and Construction" (1837), distinguishing legal interpretation from construction. The distinction has remained a lively topic in debates on legal methodology ever since.

**Definition:** Legal construction patches up a law that is indeterminate for the case at hand with a rule covering it, so that it can be applied by simple rule-following. This distinguishes it from legal interpretation: construction *creates new law*; it is not an empirical but a `normative` enterprise. What keeps it a hermeneutic activity is that its justification must follow the structure of communicative interpretation: a court may not patch up the law arbitrarily with a rule of its own choosing, like a legislator acting solely on its own convictions of political reasonableness; it must justify the rule as an intention that a — fictional — `reasonable legislator` *could* have attached to the text. This imposes a semantic limit on construction, since a reasonable legislator would not attach to a text an intention the interpreter cannot connect with it: *semantic meaning guides legal interpretation and limits legal construction.* Beyond the requirement of a justification connectable with the text, the specific standards for justifying construction vary between legal cultures and even between areas of law; but maintaining a hermeneutic relation to the text distinguishes construction from legislation, though both create new law. Construction is not merely reconstructive; it admits of creativity and choice: search engine results can be either included in or excluded from the free speech protection of the German constitution or the First Amendment, and both solutions can be connected with the text of the clause — a reasonable legislator might have intended to protect those who write algorithms producing opinionated content, and connected that intention with the broad wording of the free speech guarantee. Through construction the law adapts to the changing needs of its time; it concerns the *second* aspect of Gadamer's concept of application, presumably the one he noticed when he turned his attention to law: construction connects the horizon of the past legislator with the horizon of the present court that must apply the law to a new situation. The classic phrase in the legal literature is that "the law can be wiser than the legislator" — bolder than Kant's remark about his own interpretation of Plato (that we often understand an author better than he understood himself), since it hides the interpreter's agency behind the personification of the text as a rational intentional subject.

**Distinction:** Construction differs from `association`. In association, the object merely provides an occasion for the perceiver to produce meaning; there is only a causal relation between object and association — the object triggers the association but is not conceived as representing it, or even as containing any meaning. The famous madeleine can trigger associations of childhood, but these associations can be explained without attributing those meanings to the cake: it caused them without intending to; the same goes for a song a mother used to sing to her child, which need not be about childhood to trigger associations of childhood. Hermeneutic justification cannot be satisfied with a merely associative relation; it must concern a textual meaning that is structurally prior to the act of interpretation: *unlike association, in interpretation the text is not only the trigger of meaning but also its measure* — it is not enough that the text evokes an idea in the recipient; the meaning connected with the text must also be one intended by its actual or implied fictional author. In law, association can play a part in the `context of discovery`: in the search engine case, such a query might remind the lawyer of some great historical statesman and the way he dealt with hard problems, prompting ideas about how best to adapt the free speech guarantee to the digital age. But for reasons of legitimacy, association does not appear in the hermeneutic `context of justification` of legal decisions: legal decisions depend on the legitimacy of their sources, and their justification must be traced back to the source of the applied law — an actual, fictional or, formerly, often divine legislator; the content of association, by contrast, is not intentionally tied to its source but issues from the associating subject. In art, however, association is often at the centre of reception — beauty is in the eye of the beholder; artists often create precisely in order to produce objects of association that engage beholders far beyond what the artist intended. *It is this non-interpretive component of hermeneutics that makes the reception of art a more personal and more subjective experience.*

**Mechanism:** `Discretion` concerns the *third* aspect of Gadamer's concept of application. Applying a norm means not only judging others' rule-following conduct but also that lawyers are themselves bound by rules and must act in "compliance with the rule" — a criminal court must not only judge whether the defendant broke the law but, if so, comply with the rule requiring it to pass sentence. Application in this sense involves another theoretical aspect of legal hermeneutics: because of the ontological density of the world, concrete objects and events are `descriptively inexhaustible`. The Latin maxim *individuum est ineffabile* goes back to ancient Greek philosophy and became famous in German Romanticism through Goethe's repeated use of it. No linguistic representation of concrete objects and events can describe all their properties by its predicates: every individual object and event is already related to countless others in space and time and so cannot, even in theory, be completely described; our descriptions are limited by perception, reaching roughly only middle-sized goods; and most importantly, we limit our descriptions for pragmatic reasons, according to our purposes — even definite descriptions do not require completeness; identification relies on the uniqueness of the combination of properties described.

**Example:** In the tradition of legal methodology, **Hans Kelsen** emphasized the limits of descriptive means in order to highlight the inevitability of discretion: the determination of the act applying a higher norm by that norm can never be complete; there always remains a greater or lesser range of discretion; even the most detailed command must leave the executor some discretion — if organ A orders organ B to arrest a person, B must decide at its own discretion when, where and how to carry out the arrest. Since the purpose of law is to guide its addressees' conduct in respects relevant to the interests and rights of others, it need only describe the main properties of conduct affecting those interests and rights: the law commands the buyer to pay the agreed price but does not describe which notes or coins to use, at what time of day to hand them over or what to wear on the day of payment. In all aspects not prescribed, the addressee of the law has discretion; the same holds where the required action is described only in very general terms — German police codes empower the police to take "measures" to avert danger without specifying them except that they must serve to avert danger; faced with an environmental hazard, the police can compel the landowner to investigate its source, order a third party to do so, or do it themselves. The law sometimes sets explicit boundaries to discretion, as when penal norms usually specify a sentencing range such as "one to five years of prison" rather than a fixed sentence; sometimes it even grants discretion over whether to take the legally conditioned action at all: under German law, prosecutors and courts in principle have no discretion whether to prosecute a known crime and must prosecute every crime — the `legality principle`; whereas the police, faced with a danger to public safety, in principle have discretion whether to intervene — the `opportunity principle`: when demonstrators endanger public safety the police may intervene, but may also judge it wiser not to, since a police intervention could turn a menacing but still peaceful demonstration into an uncontrollable riot.

**Distinction:** Whether discretion concerns aspects the law does not address, the generality of the law's description of aspects it does address, or explicitly granted discretion, its exercise differs categorically from legal interpretation or construction: where the law provides discretion, its addressees are *not bound by hermeneutic standards* — they need not justify their choice by reference to the text, as in construction; they need not argue that an actual or fictional reasonable legislator intended to communicate that this rather than that 50-euro note be used to pay a debt, or intended two years when prescribing "one to five years". Roughly, *construction addresses the law's indeterminacy, discretion its generality*: where the law's description is indeterminate as to whether it applies to a case, construction is needed to make it precise; where the law clearly applies but its scope is general, discretion is exercised to choose one of the many forms of application falling under its general description. Discretion is limited, on the one hand, by the general description of the applied rule — if the law prescribes one to five years, a court cannot impose six; on the other hand it is usually bound by secondary legal standards such as proportionality — five years for a first offender who caused only minimal harm would be disproportionate. But to the extent that it is not bound by secondary standards, the person exercising discretion is not bound by the law, and there is no hermeneutic relation between the decision and the law. The exercise of discretion cannot be justified by hermeneutic standards, but it may have to be justified by other standards — general reasonableness, efficiency or political appropriateness. German administrative law draws the line precisely here: an administrative agency's interpretation of the law is reviewed by the courts according to the standards of legal interpretation and construction, while the exercise of discretion is controlled only within the political hierarchy of the administration according to standards of political reasonableness. *The distinction between construction and discretion is thus even written into legal institutions.* Though discretion and construction share some functions, they are entirely different in nature: the latter is hermeneutic, the former is not. Like association, discretion need not be justified by reference to a communicative intention already attached to the text; unlike association, discretion must stay within the boundaries of the text, whereas association can range far beyond it; and discretion must usually be justified by some other standard of reason, whereas association need not follow any rational pattern at all.

### Meaning and significance: the fourth aspect of application

**Distinction:** The last aspect had already been discussed in detail by Betti: Gadamer conflates `meaning` and `significance`, a conflation facilitated by the German *Bedeutung*, which carries both senses. Gadamer insists that even the legal historian never reconstructs historical facts purely for their own sake; any sensible historiography will try to understand the meaning of historical facts in terms of their relevance from a contemporary perspective — which, for him, shows that a contemporary interest in the text is part of all textual interpretation. But the argument conflates meaning with significance: the significance of a legal provision may be assessable only from changing present perspectives, but this does not automatically affect its meaning. A speed limit on certain roads does not change its meaning because long-standing severe congestion has made it irrelevant. As Betti insisted, meaning and significance must be distinguished; legal interpretation and construction concern the meaning of the law, not the significance of that meaning. (This agrees with Hirsch's meaning/significance distinction; see the first section of this part.)

**Example:** Law too has its discussions that conflate meaning and significance. German constitutional scholarship has carried on a debate for over a century about `constitutional change` (*Verfassungswandel*): whether, and how, constitutional provisions change their "meaning" as the political environment changes. Constitutional scholars of the late nineteenth century (Laband, Jellinek) were fascinated by the way the actual workings of the newly founded German Empire's constitution diverged sharply from what its text seemed to suggest. A striking example is the growth of a federal bureaucracy out of the Imperial Chancellery: the chancellor, representing dominant Prussia, presided over the Federal Council — the body representing the princes of the member states — and was responsible for countersigning federal laws and overseeing their execution in the Council; nowhere did the constitution provide for federal ministries, yet Bismarck built around the chancellor's office a vast federal supervisory administration whose departments became de facto federal ministries. The provisions on the Chancellery thereby acquired an unforeseen significance, discussed at the time under the heading of "constitutional change" as a hermeneutic phenomenon. *By the distinction above, what changed was the provisions' significance, not their communicative meaning; calling it a "change of meaning" is precisely an instance of conflating the two.*

**Example:** In art, changes in a work's significance are among the most common experiences of its reception. For decades no German theatre bothered to stage Aeschylus' *The Suppliants* (*Supplement:* the plot concerns a band of young women fleeing forced marriage who seek asylum from the city of Argos); but after the so-called refugee crisis peaked in the summer of 2015, even provincial theatres such as Freiburg's put it on. A play's significance can change dramatically within a single summer, even after more than two and a half thousand years.

**Conclusion:** This shows the ambiguity of Gadamer's concept of "application": it fuses rule-following, legal construction, the exercise of discretion and judgements of significance into a unified "understanding as application". The fusion lets philosophical hermeneutics capture the situatedness of understanding, but it also prevents it from answering the question of validity for each operation separately. Law is already intuitively aware of these distinctions in many of its doctrinal and methodological aspects: it distinguishes interpretation from construction, interpretation from legal judgement (*Judiz*), construction from association (context of discovery and context of justification), interpretation from the exercise of discretion, and meaning from historical significance. The following table sets these distinctions side by side:

| Operation | When it occurs | What it does | Standard of legitimacy or validity | Which aspect of Gadamer's "application" |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Communicative interpretation | All texts and utterances | Infers the communicative intention of author or legislator | Empirical hypothesis: best explains the evidence, constrained by standards of rationality (principle of charity) | — |
| Rule-following | Easy cases: communicative content clear, case at the core of the rule | Applies the rule directly to the case | Agreement with a shared practice (judgement, *Judiz*) | First aspect |
| Legal construction | Hard cases: communicative content indeterminate or absent | Creates a new rule covering the case | Must be justifiable as an intention a fictional reasonable legislator could have attached to the text; semantic meaning limits it | Second aspect |
| Association | Any stage | The object triggers new ideas and lines of thought | Belongs only to the context of discovery, never enters justification | — |
| Discretion | The law clearly applies but its description is general, or discretion is expressly granted | Chooses within the range of the general description | Not bound by hermeneutic standards; limited by the rule's boundaries and secondary standards such as proportionality; justified by reasonableness, efficiency or political appropriateness | Third aspect |
| Judgement of significance | After meaning has been established | Judges the text's import and relevance for the present | A judgement varying with perspective that does not change meaning | Fourth aspect |

### Parallel structures in law, art and literature

**Principle:** This compound structure is not peculiar to law. We have already seen the counterparts in art of communicative interpretation (*Guernica*), rule-following (the score), association and judgements of significance (*The Suppliants*); the counterparts of construction and discretion in art deserve particular development, since they bear directly on the core concepts of reception theory.

**Example:** Construction in art can be illustrated with **Robert Stecker**'s work on interpretation and construction in the hermeneutics of art. Watteau's famous painting *The Embarkation for Cythera* has long been controversial: are the people in it arriving at the island where Venus was born, or about to leave? Watteau deliberately left the answer open. Whether the difficulty is merely epistemic — Watteau had a communicative intention but did not disclose it — or whether he had no settled intention on this point at all, anyone wishing to give an interpretation here cannot rely on the painter's intention; he must *construct* the scene as arrival or departure, and thereby implicitly presuppose an intentional subject who painted with this or that intention. What Stecker calls radical constructivists hold that every interpretation of art is a construction by the interpreter; moderate constructivists allow only that construction may legitimately supplement or replace interpretation relying on the artist's communicative intention. Either way, the distinction between communicative interpretation and construction applies equally to the hermeneutics of art. But there is a striking difference: *in law, construction is mandatory* — every case brought before a court must be decided, even where there is no legislative intention to be found at all, and refusing to construct would amount to denial of justice; *in art, whether construction is worth undertaking is up to the beholder* — one can enjoy Watteau's painting just as well without constructing anything.

**Example:** Discretion in art shares its origin with the "places of indeterminacy" of literary aesthetics. Ingarden emphasized the "places of indeterminacy of the represented objects" in literary works, some of which are filled by the reader's imagination (see P9); in the performing arts, discretion has the same source: even if a playwright wished to, she could not determine every aspect of its staging — she must leave room for discretion, and usually leaves a great deal, so that the work can vary interestingly and adapt to different local and temporal contexts. In the performing arts discretion also serves to connect the horizons Gadamer was concerned with, providing room for a play to adapt to — or, in Gadamer's terms, to be `applied` to — different times and places in history: when John Dew staged Wagner's *Das Rheingold* in Wiesbaden in 2003, he set Valhalla in a nuclear power plant; he could do so because Wagner's text did not specify what Valhalla should look like on stage, leaving it to the director's discretion and imagination.

**Conclusion:** *The same set of distinctions runs through law, art and literature, which shows that they are not a special technique of jurisprudence but the structure of hermeneutic activity in general. This offers reception theory a belated clarification. Ingarden's "concretization" and Iser's "filling of blanks" belong, in this framework, roughly to discretion — the text sets the frame but does not uniquely determine the outcome; Iser's insistence that the reader's freedom extends only to details (see P11) corresponds exactly to the requirement that discretion "stay within the boundaries of the text". Jauss's "fusion of horizons" and "chain of receptions" mix construction, association and judgements of significance; his early attempt to "objectify" the horizon of expectations in order to establish a work's original meaning belongs to communicative interpretation. Reception theory's claim that "meaning is realized in reception" is largely right if read as a claim about discretion, association and judgements of significance; read as a claim about communicative interpretation, it falls into the dilemma of determinacy exposed in P14.*

## P16 The domain of hermeneutics and its self-reflection

### Is philosophy the meta-discipline of the human sciences?

**Problem:** The history of hermeneutics can be read as the history of the human sciences' self-understanding: what they study, how they study it, and what status their knowledge has. At the end of this history a question arises about hermeneutics itself: what role does philosophy play in the human sciences? Is it a meta-discipline that grounds the various humanistic disciplines and legislates for them, or one discipline among others, standing beside them and learning from them?

**Principle:** Gadamer's *Truth and Method* gave an influential answer. It is framed by two pairs of distinctions — methodological versus philosophical hermeneutics, special versus general hermeneutics (see P1) — and within this frame it tells a history of hermeneutics: from the methodological errors of the Romantics, through Dilthey's historicist predicament, to Heidegger's ontological turn and the completion of philosophical hermeneutics (see P7). In this narrative, philosophical hermeneutics is not concerned with the method of any particular discipline but discloses the conditions of possibility of all understanding — and hence of all the human sciences, and even the natural sciences; in this sense it precedes and grounds the individual disciplines.

**Counterexample:** **Kristin Gjesdal** argues that this answer contains a worrying tendency: first to identify philosophy with hermeneutics, then to establish hermeneutics as the master discourse of the human sciences. Her critique has three layers. First, Gadamer's narrative of the history of hermeneutics is polemical (see P1, P7): to justify the ontological option he must portray the methodological tradition as a mixture of Kantian subjectivism and objectivism, a portrayal resting on a misreading of Schleiermacher (see P3). Second, his two pairs of distinctions are not as neutral as they look: opposing "methodological" to "philosophical" presupposes that any concern with method must amount to submission to the model of the natural sciences, and so cannot see another kind of hermeneutics that does not take the natural sciences as its model yet is equally concerned with method and validity. Third, Gadamer's ontological framework leaves him almost uninterested in the different challenges arising within the various fields of the human sciences: philology, history, art history, law and anthropology each have their particular objects, evidence and difficulties, whereas for him the human sciences matter chiefly as "studies of interpretation", and their internal differences — even those between branches of philosophy, or between approaches to textual interpretation — go unacknowledged.

**Distinction:** Gjesdal further points out that Gadamer's philosophical hermeneutics ultimately commits itself to a softened, historicized transcendental claim: it still takes as its task the search for the a priori conditions of experience, merely moving those conditions from the transcendental subject to tradition. *Philosophical hermeneutics thus, while criticizing Kant, retains the most ambitious side of Kant's philosophy — the ambition to ground all knowledge; it merely replaces reason with history as the grounding agent.*

**Conclusion:** Another answer to the question is that philosophy is one discipline among the human sciences, not their meta-discipline. Its task is to reflect on the conditions and validity of understanding, but that reflection must start from the actual practice of the humanistic disciplines and learn from them rather than legislate for them. This answer is not a new invention: it has a precedent in the history of hermeneutics that Gadamer's narrative obscures.

### Another history of hermeneutics: the Herder–Schleiermacher–Dilthey line

**Genealogy:** On Dilthey's own genealogy, hermeneutics should be traced back not to Kant but to Herder's response to the formalism of rationalist school philosophy. In an early essay, "How Philosophy Can Become More Universal and Useful for the Benefit of the People" (1765), Herder already argued that philosophy must "descend" from scholastic abstraction to anthropology — to the study of human beings in their concrete existence in history, language and culture; the value of philosophy is to be measured by its contribution to human life. From this starting point came a hermeneutic line running through P2 to P4: Herder, Schlegel, Schleiermacher, Humboldt, Böckh, Droysen, Dilthey.

**Background:** Several factors were especially important in bringing about the breakthrough of the Herder–Schleiermacher line in the philosophy of interpretation: (a) the debate between Lessing and Winckelmann over how to approach historical and artistic material — Lessing worried about Winckelmann's deductive approach, while Winckelmann was dissatisfied with Lessing's lack of first-hand experience of the works he described and called for a more empirical study of sculpture; (b) the widespread interest of German philosophers in Hume's essays and historical writings, and in the larger empiricist circle of Shaftesbury, Kames and others, as well as in poets and theologians such as Young and Lowth; (c) the shift of the methodological paradigm of the natural sciences from physics to biology; (d) unease about the formalism, ritualization and class coding of the French classicist aesthetics favoured by rationalist philosophers, which disparaged all folk culture and non-classical works, Shakespeare's plays included (see P3); (e) unease about European colonialism — its violence, and its failure to recognize that the concept of "humanity" extends far beyond the inhabitants of modern Europe. These movements crystallized in the 1760s, before Kant's Copernican turn, and transcendental philosophy played little part in them; they were accompanied by a broad programme of practical hermeneutics: collecting endangered literary forms, translation, cross-cultural mediation, and the revaluation of various aesthetic hierarchies — European and non-European art, ritualized and formal high art and folk songs and ballads — even turning to the art forms of indigenous peoples such as the Sámi or the Inuit. It is no historical accident that linguistics and anthropology took early shape at precisely this time in the work of Herder and Humboldt.

**Mechanism:** This line shares several features, which correspond precisely to what Gadamer's narrative neglects:

- **Bottom-up and empirically oriented**: it starts not from a priori principles but from concrete texts, works, historical events and languages; Herder's "explaining one thing by another", Schleiermacher's comparative method, Humboldt's comparative linguistics and Droysen's source research all rest on extensive empirical work (see P3, P4).
- **Attentive to the individual and particular**: it demands individualized, context-sensitive interpretation of historical and artistic material, acknowledging deep differences between genres, periods, peoples and individuals (see the anti-universalism and the historicity of genre in P2).
- **Methodologically plural and fallibilist**: it does not make success depend on a single method but combines grammatical, historical, psychological and generic interpretation; it treats understanding as a hypothesis grounded in evidence yet going beyond it, fallible and revisable (see divination and the hermeneutic circle in P3, understanding as intellectual inference in P4).
- **Sympathetic but not mystical**: it demands putting oneself in another's place, but this "sympathy" is an activity of the imagination tethered to evidence, not mystical empathy or a fusion of minds (see the rebuttals of the "empathy" caricature in P3 and P4).
- **Interdisciplinary**: it is in constant exchange with philology, biblical criticism, history, natural history, linguistics and psychology, and hermeneutic methods also influenced the study of nature (see Herder's concept of "world" and geography in P3).

**Distinction:** This line is not the "naive objectivism" Gadamer portrays: it acknowledges the situatedness and historicity of understanding, that misunderstanding is an ever-present danger and that interpretation is never complete; nor is it the "mystical empathy theory" portrayed by analytic philosophy: evidence, comparison and revision are its basic procedures of understanding. *It occupies the place left empty by Gadamer's dichotomy of method versus truth: a hermeneutics concerned with method and validity that does not take the natural sciences as its model.*

**Distinction:** Like later hermeneutic positions, this line seeks a non-formalist, non-objectifying attitude to interpretation; but in its view tradition (and its unifying tendencies) does not only disclose a world but can also be conservative and stifling. Herder has a metaphor later echoed in the Marxist critique of religion: tradition is a cultural opium — cultures drink deep of this pleasant poison and then pass the cup to others. Herder was also a philosopher who stressed genealogy: he attended not only to the historical past but to how our narratives about the past often serve to justify present ideologies, most famously in his discussion of slavery and colonialism in *This Too a Philosophy of History*. A fruitful interpretation — one that produces new knowledge and understanding and helps overcome prejudice — cannot therefore find its measure in the "happening" or "play" of tradition, as in the hermeneutic experience Gadamer characterizes metaphorically through the classical work of art; it lies in combining, and moving back and forth between, the interpretive strategies of historical contextualization, critique, linguistic analysis and a basic sympathy for the views involved. *This corresponds exactly to critical theory's objection to Gadamer in P13 — but it comes from within the hermeneutic tradition itself, and a century and a half before Gadamer.*

**Conclusion:** In its Heidegger–Gadamer form, "the relation of philosophy to the other sciences" receives a comprehensive, almost a priori answer: philosophy conceived hermeneutically explains the deeper understanding through which our world is disclosed as a world of meaning — not merely an environment but a human world. Once the equation "philosophy = hermeneutics, hermeneutics = ontology" is questioned, the question no longer has an absolute answer; but that does not mean it has none. No longer regarding philosophy as the master discipline, philosophers can, in the spirit of Herder, Schleiermacher and Dilthey, reflect on the role of their discipline in continuing interaction with practitioners of other fields of knowledge — these thoroughly historicized philosophers suggest that philosophy's role among the sciences varies with culture and period. As Herder put it, the philosopher must be familiar with historical knowledge and show that historical knowledge does not shame the philosopher (see P4); Dilthey's "critique of historical reason" took the actual practice of the human sciences as its starting point for the same reason. *Philosophy is neither the queen of the sciences nor their handmaid*: it turns to human self-understanding, thinking about thinking and understanding understanding, but always with reference to how understanding and interpretation take shape in the various human sciences. This may not be much, but it is enough to make philosophy a member of the broad family of the humanities and social sciences — drawing on their results and repaying them with its own findings — and to make hermeneutics an irreplaceable contribution to that family. *From this perspective, Poscher's fine-grained distinctions within "interpretation", drawn from legal practice in P15, are precisely a contemporary continuation of this line: rather than deriving the structure of the disciplines from an ontology of understanding, it starts from the actual operations of the disciplines and reflects on the different forms of understanding and their validity.*

### Must hermeneutics be Western? Non-Western traditions of interpretation

**Problem:** Interpretive practices exist in every civilization: as soon as people could talk about dreams, they needed to interpret those fleeting images; oracle bones, eclipses, earthquakes and storms required their meaning to be determined; the obscure utterances of kings and priests needed intrepid interpreters to elucidate them. Later, written documents — laws, chronicles, sacred texts — called for sophisticated techniques of textual analysis, giving rise to commentaries, compilations, editions, glosses and translations, and interpreters often addressed difficult religious, metaphysical and ethical questions in their commentaries. This was so in ancient Greece, Egypt and Israel, and equally in Mesopotamia, Persia, the Maya world, India, China and many cultures across the globe. Yet when philosophers in the Western academy think about the task of hermeneutics, they read almost exclusively Plato's dialogues, Aristotle's *De interpretatione*, Augustine's *De doctrina christiana*, Schleiermacher's lectures and *Truth and Method*; few study Wang Bi's (226–249) reflections on the art of interpretation, the interpretive techniques for the Torah listed by Hillel (c. 110 BCE – 10 CE), al-Ghazali's (1058–1111) *Revival of the Religious Sciences*, or Motoori Norinaga's (1730–1801) methodological reflections on interpreting ancient Japanese poetry. The question, then, is whether modern hermeneutics is necessarily a Western phenomenon. Gadamer himself was doubtful: in his view, the profound and wise enigmatic sayings developed by other cultures, especially those of the Far East, stand in an ultimately incommensurable relation to so-called Western philosophy, not least because the science in whose name we ask is itself a Western discovery. But how far he actually understood these perspectives is unclear.

**Condition:** Discussing "non-Western hermeneutics" raises several difficulties. Many non-Western traditions of interpretation were marginalized in the modern period — often during Western colonial rule — with the introduction of new systems of knowledge; they are often thought to be religious or of merely local significance, and so fit only for study in national philologies or "area studies", not as part of a universal inquiry into the problem of understanding; and Western-trained philosophers often suspect that "comparative" or "global philosophy" lacks methodological foundations, or is simply irrelevant. Such judgements usually rest not on solid knowledge but on prejudice and vague assumptions: familiarity with Aristotle is taken for granted in Western culture, while complete ignorance of the eighth-century Indian Buddhist thinker Śāntarakṣita is generally accepted. On the other hand, words such as "Western" and "non-Western" are themselves highly dubious — "non-Western" is an awkward shorthand for a host of cultures and worldviews with little in common, and it forms no part of the self-understanding of many people in Colombo, Tokyo or Muscat; at the same time one must beware the pitfalls of Orientalist fantasy, and the temptations of political correctness as well.

**Example:** The traditions followed different trajectories. In India, Sanskrit scholars from the fourth century BCE produced a vast literature on grammar and lexicography, poetics (*alaṅkāraśāstra*) and hermeneutics (`Mīmāṃsā`). In Jewish culture, `midrash` exegesis recovered meaning from the Torah with highly specialized methods, and Rabbi Akiva and Rabbi Ishmael sought to establish rules for interpreting scripture. In the Arab world, interpretive traditions developed around the Qur'an: scholars studied its language, theologians debated the nature of the divine teaching and sought to recover both the literal meaning (*zahir*) and the inner spiritual meaning (*haqiqa*) of its passages; later philosophers, as in Jewish culture, explored the tension between reason and revelation. In early Japan, scholars of language and literature studied mainly Chinese texts, turning only in the seventeenth century to Japanese texts such as the *Nihon shoki* (720) and the oldest Japanese poetry anthology, the *Man'yōshū*. These traditions seem to share certain features: an emphasis on the sacredness and absolute authority of certain texts; an interweaving of philological, religious and philosophical questions; the belief that texts (and the authority to interpret them) must be transmitted from master to disciple; the belief that readers can have direct access to a text's original meaning; and a rejection of the present in the name of an idealized past. But such generalizations easily lead to overgeneralization and misunderstanding — the very idea of *comparing* these practices from "our" standpoint may mislead: comparative judgements are unavoidable, but should be made only after long engagement with the other. What can be said with more confidence is that these traditions all have some affinity with the philological work familiar from the European *Geisteswissenschaften*.

**Distinction:** Take China. Chinese culture is often seen as the epitome of "otherness", and a long tradition of Orientalist scholarship portrayed it as the polar opposite of everything Western readers knew — what the sinologist **Andrew Plaks** aptly called the `antipodal fallacy`. But it is equally mistaken to assimilate Chinese perspectives entirely to our own worldview, or to ignore certain deep cultural and historical differences between the West and China on questions of reading and understanding. **James Legge**, the famous nineteenth-century translator of the Chinese classics, writing the introduction to the first volume of *The Chinese Classics* in 1861, was rather disappointed by Confucius' teachings, finding in them nothing of religion or universal concern as he understood them; only after more personal contact with Chinese scholars did he learn to appreciate Confucian thought *on its own terms*. *The antipodal fallacy and assimilation are the two symmetrical errors of cross-cultural understanding: the first reads the other as our opposite, the second reads the other as ourselves — precisely the two forms of "measuring one culture by the standards of another" against which Herder warned in judging Shakespeare (see P3).*

**Background:** The Chinese have always given priority to texts, especially those associated with Confucius (551–479 BCE) and canonized by the emperors from the Han dynasty onward. A basic idea of the premodern Chinese worldview — shared by Confucianism, Buddhism and even some Daoism — is that certain texts contain ultimate values and must therefore be respected by everyone. The traditional Confucian canon of the "Five Classics" comprises the *Changes*, the *Rites*, the *Odes*, the *Documents* and the *Spring and Autumn Annals*, a chronicle of Confucius' home state; later it came to include the *Analects*, recording conversations between Confucius and his disciples. The *ru* were both guardians of the canon and administrators and ministers; until the abolition of the civil service examinations in 1905, doctrinal disputes and interpretive questions surrounding these *jing* ran through Chinese intellectual life.

**Mechanism:** The *Analects* is the most important *jing* of Chinese civilization and one of the most influential texts in human history; it and its countless commentaries show several features of Chinese interpretive practice.

The first is `performativity`. Like many Chinese thinkers, Confucius expressed his thought in teaching; all communication, oral or written, was understood to be closely bound up with a person's character, so the meaning (and validity) of Confucius' ethical advice to his disciples cannot be separated from the occasions on which it was spoken. *Supplement:* In *Analects* 11.22, Zilu and Ran You each ask the same question — "Should one act at once on what one hears?" Confucius tells Zilu, "You have a father and elder brothers to consult", and tells Ran You, "Act on it at once." Puzzled, Gongxi Hua asks why, and Confucius explains that Ran You holds back, so he urged him forward, while Zilu has the energy of two men, so he held him back. Two opposite answers to the same question make sense only when returned to their occasions and interlocutors.

The second is `dialogical understanding`. On the view of many later commentators, the *Analects* must be read through a dialogical process of understanding: by engaging closely with the "Master" through the text, learners make the disciples' questions their own and are educated by Confucius' answers. Yet Confucius never tries to exhaust a question or impose a fixed meaning on his disciples; he suggests a space for thought and stimulates their imagination — elaborate rhetoric would be an obstacle; this attitude is widespread and can be found in Chan Buddhism too. The ideal Chinese classical text, says the sinologist **Christoph Harbsmeier**, plants the seeds of meaning in the reader's mind rather than explicitly delivering the fruits of thought, and so fosters an implicit mutual understanding.

Third, precisely because of this fundamental fluidity of meaning, it is a misunderstanding to ascribe an "intentionalist hermeneutics" to the Confucian tradition. When literati over the centuries reflected on how to understand texts, they often cited Mencius' (c. 370–c. 290 BCE) words on reading the *Odes*: `yi yi ni zhi` — "using one's own understanding to meet the (poet's) intent". On this reading Mencius asks his disciples to understand literary texts with reference to their own existential situation rather than merely recovering the author's intention; each interpreter must read the text anew, continually and creatively transforming the past — personal engagement matters more than mere intellectual understanding. *Supplement:* The phrase comes from *Mencius* 5A.4, where it is preceded by "those who explain the *Odes* should not let the words obscure the phrase, nor the phrase obscure the intent"; Mencius takes the line from "Yunhan" in the "Greater Odes", "Of the remaining black-haired people of Zhou, not one was left": read literally, it would mean that not a single person of Zhou survived, whereas the poet is only expressing the extremity of the drought. Whether *yi* refers to the reader's understanding or the poet's has been debated by later commentators (Zhao Qi and Zhu Xi both take it as the reader's); *it is precisely this ambiguity that lets "yi yi ni zhi" include a search for the author's intent while leaving room for the reader's situation — closer to Gadamer's fusion of horizons than to Hirsch's authorial intention.*

**Mechanism:** Later thinkers — many of whom scholars today would not hesitate to call "philosophers" — used highly sophisticated exegetical techniques to explain obscure passages in the *Analects* and other classics, often expressing their own philosophical, metaphysical or ethical understanding in the genre of interlinear commentary. **Wang Bi** represents an important shift in the history of Chinese interpretation: he expressed a heightened awareness of the instability of meaning. Under Daoist influence, Wang Bi held that language is a rather unreliable tool: the "intent" of the sage Confucius embodied in texts such as the *Analects* cannot easily be transmitted to later generations and must be recovered through a non-discursive insight into the *dao*; ultimately, any particular perspective derived from particular words must be transcended. *Supplement:* His most famous formulation is in the "Clarifying the Images" chapter of his *General Remarks on the Zhouyi*: words serve to clarify the images, images serve to preserve the meaning; once the meaning is grasped, forget the images; once the images are grasped, forget the words — language is a tool for grasping the hexagram images, the images a tool for grasping meaning, and once meaning is attained one should not cling to the tools. *Setting this thesis beside Hamann's and Herder's metacritique of language (see P2) shows the two traditions taking different paths on the same question: the former seeks to pass beyond language to meaning, the latter holds that thought cannot pass beyond language.*

**Mechanism:** **Zhu Xi** (1130–1200), the most important representative of Neo-Confucianism, often expressed scepticism about claims to canonical authority: he held that the authority to evaluate texts should pass from the court and other political authorities to Neo-Confucian scholars. Even texts from the remote past, he believed, could be thoroughly understood — readers need only apply them to their own existential situation and overcome their prejudices, which he called `emptying the mind` (*xuxin*). Zhu Xi's `method of reading` (*dushu fa*) is often praised as premodern China's most systematic attempt to deal with the problem of understanding: it makes great demands on readers, describing reading as ethical self-cultivation (`gongfu`), while placing high value on the reader's autonomy — it never simply calls for passive acknowledgement of tradition or authorial intention. For Zhu Xi, reading is not only a way of connecting with the world of past sages such as Confucius but also gives the reader a holistic insight into the present world, enabling an appropriate response, in accordance with Confucian virtue, to whatever situation arises. As the sinologist **John Makeham** observes, although Zhu Xi's hermeneutics has no phenomenological programme (let alone a concept of Being), its goal is likewise less understanding as a way of knowing than understanding as a way of experiencing — learning to become a sage. *Supplement:* Zhu Xi's disciples later summed up his teaching on reading in six maxims: proceed step by step in proper order; read thoroughly and think carefully; empty the mind and immerse oneself; examine the text against one's own experience; apply oneself with urgency and effort; maintain reverence and hold to one's aim. *"Emptying the mind" is close to Schlegel's and Schleiermacher's demand not to project oneself into the field of interpretation, and "examining the text against one's own experience" close to Gadamer's "application"; Zhu Xi's distinctiveness lies in placing both within a practice of reading aimed at self-cultivation.*

**Background:** Returning to the history of European hermeneutics, one can see that its horizon was once wider. Medieval theologians formulating their hermeneutic insights mostly did not consider how the contingencies of history and time limit the understanding of meaning; in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries the problem of historicity grew increasingly prominent — the discovery of Asia and its religions fundamentally challenged the Judaeo-Christian worldview: Sanskrit was thought to be an older language than Hebrew, the Bible had older rivals from China and India, and European civilization suddenly appeared as merely a local phenomenon in a vastly expanded and increasingly complex world. Responding to Kant's project of rebuilding modern rationalism, Hamann, Herder and Schlegel insisted on the radical diversity of cultures, the dependence of thought on language and the limits historical development places on human agency; it was against this background that Schleiermacher developed his general hermeneutics (see P2, P3). The young Herder praised Holwell's *Shastah* in 1772 and later introduced his readers to the Indian Brahmins in his search for human origins; Humboldt developed his own theory of the Chinese language in letters to the French sinologist Abel-Rémusat.

**Limitation:** Since the late eighteenth century, however, the horizon of modern hermeneutics has narrowed. Most representatives of twentieth-century hermeneutics no longer took non-European cultures seriously and instead emphasized the Greek roots of European culture. One obvious reason is the powerful nineteenth-century influence of Hegel's model of the history of philosophy, which left only a marginal place for Chinese and Indian ways of thinking; the growing professionalization of the humanities and the rise of specialized philologies also made philosophers less willing to think about non-European languages or to study Arabic, Indian or Chinese texts. Heidegger was one of the few twentieth-century philosophers willing to step outside the European history of philosophy: to free himself from the European "history of Being" he occasionally cited the *Daodejing* and sought dialogue with Buddhist monks, Chinese translators and Japanese philosophers, as in the famous "From a Dialogue on Language between a Japanese and an Inquirer" (written 1953–54, published 1959). *Supplement:* In 1946 he also attempted, together with the Chinese scholar Paul Shih-yi Hsiao, to translate several chapters of the *Daodejing*. But he was also convinced that Asian languages were ultimately inaccessible and never made any effort to learn Sanskrit or classical Chinese; he repeatedly pointed to a future dialogue between East and West but never thought of revising his core ideas in light of insights from other traditions, convinced that a fundamental renewal of philosophy could come only through a reversal of the European tradition. Gadamer, for his part, was concerned mainly with the European history of understanding: unlike Heidegger, who wanted to leave Western metaphysical thinking behind once and for all, he turned to Plato and stressed how far "we" (if we happen to be European or belong to the Anglo-American world) are bound to our heritage; he spoke of the need for engagement with non-Western thought, yet at times regarded it as irrelevant.
